RML Clinical Psychology, CET-166 · Study Material from Scratch

ABVIMS & Dr. Ram Manohar Lohia Hospital · MA Clinical Psychology (RCI) via GGSIPU · the whole entrance syllabus taught from zero, tuned to how the RML paper actually behaves. Built for Sihan, 2026-07-02.

You flagged RML as the emergency, so this is the first RML drop: a complete, from-scratch study text that covers every concept the CET-166 clinical paper tests, built off the syllabus in your RML Task brief and off how past RML / IPU clinical papers actually behave. Read it top to bottom and you have covered the subject core. Every theorist is given by full name (not just a surname), every test names its author, and every therapy names who developed it, because that is exactly what this paper rewards. Now expanded to the full syllabus neighborhood (2026-07-03): not just the PYQ hot topics, but the foundational build-up, the related theories, and the adjacent subtopics the examiner can swing into, so the high-yield material still gets the most room while nothing is left as a blind spot. Every one of the 12 sections gained new ground: the surrounding reasoning types and the full mental-health-law stack, sensation and cognition and language, brain-imaging methods and behaviour genetics, prenatal-to-ageing-to-death development, the self and social cognition and attraction, biological and cognitive and Indian personality models, the history of abnormality plus culture-bound syndromes, the mental status examination and rehabilitation psychology, group and play and specific behavioural therapies, Indian test adaptations and clinical rating scales, qualitative methods and ANOVA and non-parametric statistics, and the six darshanas with Yoga and Buddhist psychology in depth.

How to use this. This is a teaching text, not a cheatsheet: each section builds a topic from the ground up, then flags the exact traps and matching-items RML fishes for. The green Key boxes are the must-knows, the red Trap boxes are the confusions the paper exploits, the teal Who → what boxes are the theorist / founder pairings, and the purple RML tilt boxes tell you how this specific paper phrases the question. Read the 6-year PYQ map first to set your priorities, then after each section sit the matching part of any of the six live PYQ mocks (2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025) and the weak spots map straight back here.
What makes the RML paper different, baked into every section below: (1) lean harder on Indian Psychology, tested past AND present; (2) treat abnormal as ICD-deep and factual, including the older signs and eponyms DSM has dropped; (3) drill who developed which therapy and the acronyms (REBT, DBT, ACT, EMDR, MI) until reflexive; (4) by the six-year PYQ count the paper is actually carried by General/basic psychology, Assessment & tests, Statistics/Research methods, Abnormal, and Biopsychology (the top five = 65% of marks; see the PYQ map); (5) no negative marking, so attempt everything.
The syllabus, 12 sections: ★ The 6-year PYQ map (read first) 1 · Reasoning, Research Aptitude & General Awareness 2 · General & Basic Psychology 3 · Biopsychology 4 · Developmental 5 · Social 6 · Personality 7 · Abnormal (ICD-deep) 8 · Clinical Psychology 9 · Counselling & Psychotherapy 10 · Assessment & Tests 11 · Research Methods & Statistics 12 · Indian Psychology

★ What actually repeats: the 6-year PYQ map (2020–2025)

Everything below this heading is built from the real RML papers, all six of them: 2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024 and 2025, which is 570 actual questions (100 each year except 2023, which had 70). Every one of those questions was tagged to a topic, the topics were counted across the six years, and the concepts that keep coming back were pulled out. So this is not a guess about what the examiner likes; it is the paper's own track record. Read this section first, let it set your priorities, then go into the twelve teaching sections knowing exactly where the marks actually live.

How the marks distribute. The single table below is the whole weighting in one view: how many questions each topic drew in each of the six years, the six-year total, its share of the 570 marks, and which teaching section covers it. The bottom four rows of the ranking (cognitive, social, schools, and the rest) are still worth marks, but the top five topics, basic/general, assessment, statistics, abnormal, and biopsychology, are 65% of the entire paper on their own. That is the order to study in.

Attack order, straight off the six-year data: (1) General & basic psychology and (2) Assessment & tests and (3) Research methods & statistics are the three biggest buckets, about 42% of the paper between them, and they are the most learnable (definitions, who-built-which-test, stats vocabulary), so bank them first. (4) Abnormal and (5) Biopsychology are the next tier and together add another 24%. Only after those five are solid should you spend heavily on (6) developmental, (7) therapy, (8) cognitive, (9) social, and (10) schools/systems. Personality looks like a zero in the table only because RML rarely labels a question "personality"; its content (Sigmund Freud's structure, Raymond Cattell's 16PF, Hans Eysenck's dimensions, the Big Five) is tested inside the general, abnormal and assessment buckets, so section 6 is not optional, it is spread across the others.

RML tilt, confirmed by the data: two things carry across almost every year, so make them reflexive. First, "who developed what": the paper repeatedly names a test, therapy, or theory and asks for its author (Hermann Rorschach, Henry Murray, Lee Cronbach, Albert Ellis, Aaron Beck, Jean Piaget, Erik Erikson). Second, the projective tests and the defense mechanisms, which appear in four of the six years each. If you drill only two things, drill those.

The rest of this section is the concept-by-concept breakdown, topic by topic, ordered biggest bucket first. Each callout names the concept, the years it actually appeared, and the exact fact or distinction the paper fishes for. Every recurring item here has a fuller treatment in the numbered teaching sections below.

Schools & Systems of Psychology, 21 questions over six years

This is a small but revealing bucket. It is not "systems of psychology" in the textbook sense (structuralism, functionalism, and so on live in the General bucket). Here the recurring core is organisational / industrial behaviour, systems & family therapy, and, from 2023 onward, research software (Excel and SPSS). Learn the named theories and the named theorists, because RML asks them by name.

Repeats (2020, 2023): Organisational & industrial psychology is the steadiest thread here. 2020 probed dysfunctional organisational cultures and case management premises; 2023 asked about a decentralised, empowerment-focused structure (the flat / organic structure) and about Herzberg's Two-Factor Theory. Know the two families of motivators at work.

Who → what: Frederick Herzberg → Two-Factor (Motivation-Hygiene) Theory. Motivators (achievement, recognition, the work itself, responsibility, advancement) drive genuine job satisfaction; hygiene factors (salary, supervision, working conditions, company policy) only prevent dissatisfaction and never satisfy. The 2023 question keys on "motivators that lead to job satisfaction," so pick the growth-of-the-job factors, not pay.

Repeats (2020): Group development ran across several 2020 items. Bruce Tuckman's stages are the must-know: forming → storming → norming → performing → adjourning. The 2020 stem "struggle with each other over leadership and group definition" = storming; "clarified its purpose and strategy for achieving its goals" = norming. Also seen: common group properties, formed-group characteristics, and William Shulman's group-role language.

Who → what: Gregory Bateson → the double-bind theory of schizophrenia. The 2021 mother-and-son vignette (she stiffens at his embrace, then blames him for withdrawing, with no escape from the contradiction) is the classic double-bind illustration: conflicting messages on different logical levels with no way to comment on or leave the situation. The 2021 "individuals, families, and cultures given different power as creators of their realities" stem points to constructivist / narrative family therapy.

Repeats (2023): Research software is a 2023 cluster worth easy marks. SPSS = the standard statistical-analysis package (tabulation, graphing, statistical tests) in psychology research; MS-Excel uses PivotTables for tabulating data and the AVERAGE function for a mean of a range. Expect one or two verbatim "which software / which function" items.

General & Basic Psychology, 92 questions over six years (the biggest bucket)

This is the single largest slice of the RML paper and the most scatter-shot: research methodology, motivation, memory, perception, learning, the schools of psychology, stress and health, cognitive biases, and Indian psychology all live here. The good news is that it is theorist-heavy and fact-checkable, so a map of the repeats converts directly into marks. Each year has a centre of gravity: 2022-2023 lean research methods, 2024 is a memory-perception-learning avalanche, and 2025 is almost entirely motivation, schools, and Indian psychology.

Repeats (2020, 2022, 2023): Research methodology and design is the largest recurring theme in this bucket. Recurring hooks: the double-blind design (controls both participant and experimenter expectancy), confounding variables (the barbecue-vs-plain-chips flavour and the radio-on/off items both test uncontrolled confounds), sampling methods (stratified = divide into subgroups then sample each; cluster = sample whole groups), field vs lab experiments, the literature review, hypothesis formulation, plagiarism, and referencing / citation ethics.

Trap: Deductive vs inductive reasoning is asked repeatedly (2023, 2024) and students flip them. Deductive = theory first, then test with observations, general → specific; its conclusion's validity depends on the truth of its premises. Inductive = gather data, find patterns, then build a theory, specific observations → broad generalisation (qualitative research leans inductive). If the stem says "starts with a theory," it is deductive.

Repeats (2021, 2024, 2025): Motivation theory is a top-three repeat and dominates 2025. Know each by its one-line signature. Abraham Maslow's hierarchy (self-actualisation = realising full potential; belonging / love must be met before esteem) appears in both 2021 and 2025.

Theory & theoristCore claim (RML one-liner)
Hierarchy of Needs (Abraham Maslow)Lower needs first; self-actualisation = full potential at the top
Self-Determination Theory (Edward Deci & Richard Ryan)Three needs: autonomy, competence, relatedness; intrinsic motivation = internal reward & growth
Expectancy Theory (Victor Vroom)Effort → performance → outcome, and the outcome must be valued (valent)
Goal-Setting Theory (Edwin Locke)Specific and difficult (challenging) goals motivate best
Drive-Reduction Theory (Clark Hull)Motivation reduces internal tension to restore homeostasis
Incentive TheoryBehaviour pulled by external rewards / incentives
Social Cognitive Theory (Albert Bandura)Self-efficacy and observational learning drive motivation

Trap: Self-Determination Theory's three needs are autonomy, competence, relatedness. The 2025 "which is NOT one of the three needs" item plants a decoy (often "achievement" or "esteem"). Achievement belongs to McClelland, not to SDT.

Repeats (2020, 2024): Memory is the 2024 heavyweight and links back to 2020. The founders are the answers, so memorise the name-to-concept map below. The 2024 paper alone tests the magic number, the serial-position effect, decay theory, filter / attenuation theories, tip-of-the-tongue, the PQRST study method, and the method of reconstruction.

ConceptTheorist (the answer)
"Magic number" 7 ± 2 (span of short-term memory)George A. Miller
Primacy-recency / serial-position effectHermann Ebbinghaus
Filter theory of attentionDonald Broadbent
Filter-attenuation theoryAnne Treisman
Self-reference effectT. B. Rogers (Rogers, Kuiper & Kirker)
Decay theory of forgettingEdward Thorndike (law of disuse)

RML tilt: "Tip of the tongue" is a retrieval failure (the trace is stored but momentarily inaccessible), not encoding or storage. In the PQRST study method (Preview, Question, Read, Self-recitation, Test), S = Self-recitation (actively recalling in your own words). And the 2020 "what do Hermann Ebbinghaus, Francis Galton and Sigmund Freud share" item keys on their common contribution to the early study of memory / association.

Repeats (2024): Perception and Gestalt organisation form a dense 2024 block. Dark adaptation = the eyes growing more sensitive to light (dim light) over time; closure = the Gestalt tendency to complete an incomplete figure; the Stroop effect = interference / selective-attention breakdown (colour word vs ink colour). Depth cues recur as a mini-family (below).

2024 depth-cue stemMonocular cue
Rail tracks appear to convergeLinear perspective
Density of stones increases with distanceTexture gradient
Painted stripes on a freeway off-rampRelative size / texture gradient

Repeats (2024, 2025): Learning and conditioning span both years. Insight learning (sudden "aha" grasp of a solution) is Wolfgang Kohler's Gestalt work; shaping = reinforcing successive approximations; extinction = weakening a habit by removing all reward; stimulus generalisation vs discrimination (the furry-black-cat vs short-haired-tabby item = discrimination); a token that must be traded (five stickers) is a secondary / conditioned reinforcer. 2025 names the parent school directly: stimulus-response conditioning = behaviourism.

Repeats (2020, 2024, 2025): The schools of psychology and their history recur across three years. 2020 asked who first used "clinical psychology" in print (Lightner Witmer, 1896-1907, who also opened the first psychology clinic). 2024 asked who gave "folk psychology" / Volkerpsychologie (Wilhelm Wundt). 2025 asked you to name whole approaches from a description.

Approach (2025 stems)Signature & key figures
BehaviourismLearning via stimulus-response conditioning; John B. Watson, B. F. Skinner
Psychodynamic / psychoanalyticUnconscious forces & early childhood; inner drives beyond control; Sigmund Freud
HumanisticFree will, growth, self-actualisation; Abraham Maslow, Carl Rogers
Comparative psychologyStudy of animal behaviour
Structuralism / founder of the fieldWilhelm Wundt (first lab, 1879); "folk psychology"

Repeats (2024): Stress and health cluster in 2024. Hans Selye's General Adaptation Syndrome runs in strict order: alarm → resistance → exhaustion. Type A behaviour is the pattern most directly linked to higher coronary-heart-disease risk. Distinguish an acute stressor (short, sharp, time-limited) from a chronic one.

Trap: 2024 mixes several cognitive-bias / problem-solving terms that look alike. Functional fixedness = seeing an object only in its usual use; hindsight bias = the "I knew it all along" overestimate of past predictability; a heuristic = a rule-of-thumb shortcut for efficient search. Do not swap functional fixedness for mental set.

RML tilt: Indian psychology is a live 2025 theme (five questions) and is easy to underprepare. Core points: it is rooted in Vedanta and the Indian philosophical traditions and centres on consciousness and the self; Atman = the true self / soul (distinct from ego); it treats mental health holistically (mind-body-spirit, practices such as yoga and meditation) rather than through a purely Western symptom-and-diagnosis lens.

Assessment & Tests, 74 questions over six years

This topic is dominated by a handful of named tests and the reliability-validity vocabulary: the Rorschach, the MMPI, the TAT and projective logic, the reliability and validity families, and the IQ line reappear in year after year, so the recurring names below carry most of the marks.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2025): The Rorschach inkblot test is the single most-repeated item. Built by Hermann Rorschach (Swiss psychiatrist, 1921), it has 10 cards total: 5 achromatic (black and grey), 2 black-and-red, and 3 fully multicoloured, so 5 cards are coloured. It is a projective test of personality and unconscious process. RML also asks its procedure: the Enquiry (Inquiry) Phase is the second stage where the examiner returns to each blot to ask what was seen and what made it look that way (location, determinants).

Who → what: Hermann Rorschach → inkblot test; Henry Murray & Christiana Morgan → Thematic Apperception Test (TAT), 1930s (1935), Harvard; Starke Hathaway & J. Charnley McKinley → MMPI; John Buck → House-Tree-Person test; Edgar Doll → Vineland Social Maturity Scale; Lee Cronbach → coefficient alpha; Alfred Binet & Theodore Simon → first intelligence test (1905); David Wechsler → WAIS/WISC.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022): The MMPI (Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory) is asked almost every early year. It is an objective, self-report inventory that measures psychopathology and personality (clinical scales plus validity scales), NOT a projective test and NOT an intelligence test. That objective-self-report identity is the fact RML fishes for.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2025): Projective tests and their techniques. A projective test uses ambiguous stimuli to draw out unconscious thoughts, motives and personality. Learn the technique labels the paper uses: the Rorschach is an association technique; the TAT is a construction technique (the participant constructs a story); word-association is also association-based. The TAT's stories are analysed to infer needs, motives and inner conflicts (Murray's need-press theory).

Trap: TAT vs Rorschach. Both are projective, but the Rorschach uses inkblots (perception/association) while the TAT uses picture cards of people in scenes and asks for a story (apperception/construction). RML asks directly for the "key difference" and for how many TAT cards are used: the full set is 31, and roughly 20 are shown in a typical session.

Who → what: the reliability family. Test-retest = same test, same people, two occasions (stability over time). Inter-rater = agreement between two scorers/observers. Split-half = correlate two halves of one test, best split by the odd-even method and corrected by Spearman-Brown. Cronbach's alpha (Lee Cronbach) = internal consistency across all items. RML has asked each of these by definition in 2021, 2022 and 2025.

Repeats (2021, 2024, 2025): Validity types. Face validity = looks like it measures the trait, on the surface. Content validity = covers the whole domain. Criterion-related validity splits into concurrent (against a criterion measured now) and predictive (against a future criterion). Construct validity = the test truly captures the theoretical construct.

Trap: the 2024 and 2025 items ask which type is NOT criterion-based. Only concurrent and predictive are criterion-based; construct and content validity are separate categories, so they are the correct "NOT criterion" answers.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2025): Intelligence and IQ. Memorise the ratio-IQ formula IQ = (Mental Age ÷ Chronological Age) × 100 (MA 8, CA 10 → IQ 80). Know the test-to-author map: WAIS/WISC are Wechsler individual intelligence scales; Raven's Standard Progressive Matrices measure nonverbal fluid intelligence; the first intelligence test was built by Alfred Binet (with Theodore Simon, 1905).

RML tilt: RML likes attitude-scale and rating-scale wording. A Likert scale (Rensis Likert) is a summated rating scale: agree-to-disagree items are summed into one score. It appears in 2020, 2022 and 2023 phrased as "summated rating scale," "attitude measurement scale," or "a Likert scale is best described as."

Research Methods & Statistics, 72 questions over six years

Stats repeats cluster tightly on five spines: sampling methods, scales of measurement, the ANOVA/F machinery, central tendency, and the correlation-is-not-causation logic, with hypothesis testing errors and research ethics close behind.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2023, 2025): Sampling methods, the top stats repeat. Split probability from non-probability. Convenience/accidental (whoever is easy to recruit), snowball (participants refer others), and quota/purposive are non-probability. Systematic sampling = selecting every nth person from a list. Random sampling matters because it gives every member an equal chance and makes the sample representative, which is what licenses generalisation.

Repeats (2023, 2024, 2025): Scales of measurement (S.S. Stevens). Nominal = unordered categories (car colours). Ordinal = ranked. Interval = equal intervals but an arbitrary/no true zero (temperature). Ratio = equal intervals plus a true zero and counting (number of books).

Trap: zero is the discriminator. The 2024 "arbitrary zero" item is the interval scale; the 2023 "equal intervals AND a true zero point" item is the ratio scale. Do not answer ratio when it says arbitrary zero.

Repeats (2020, 2022, 2025): Measures of central tendency are mean, median and mode; standard deviation is a measure of dispersion, NOT central tendency (a common "which is NOT" answer). In a negatively skewed distribution the order is mean < median < mode, so the mean sits to the left of (below) the median. For skewed data such as anxiety scores, the median is the preferred summary because it resists outliers.

Who → what: the ANOVA family (2021, 2022). Ronald Fisher → ANOVA and the F-ratio, where F = between-groups variance ÷ within-groups (pooled/error) variance, so the denominator is the pooled within-group variance. As chance (error) variance falls, F rises. Post-hoc tests (Tukey's HSD, Scheffe, Duncan's multiple range, Newman-Keuls) are run only after a significant ANOVA. ANCOVA adds one assumption ANOVA lacks: a covariate statistically controlled for.

Repeats (2021, 2025): Correlation is not causation. A correlational or observational study (regularly exercising adults being less depressed, meditators being less stressed) cannot establish cause because there is no manipulation and no random assignment, so third variables and reverse causation stay open. This exact reasoning is RML's favourite "why is this conclusion not appropriate" item.

Who → what: matching a test to the data. Pearson's r → two continuous variables (stress vs sleep). Biserial/point-biserial → one continuous and one dichotomous variable. Non-parametric equivalents (2023): Mann-Whitney U → independent-samples t-test; Kruskal-Wallis → one-way ANOVA (3+ independent groups); Friedman → repeated-measures design.

Repeats (2022, 2023): Hypothesis-testing errors. A Type I error = rejecting a true null (false positive, controlled by alpha). A Type II error = failing to reject a null that is actually false (false negative). The 2023 stem "fails to reject the null when it is actually false" is by definition Type II.

RML tilt: two smaller but reliable repeats. Informed consent (2021, 2025) = telling participants the study's purpose and procedures so they can freely decide to take part. And the normal-distribution line (2021, 2022): the z-table is derived from the standard normal curve, and the bell curve was first described by Abraham de Moivre and popularised by Carl Friedrich Gauss (hence "Gaussian").

Abnormal & Psychopathology, 70 questions over six years

This is the single heaviest cluster on the CET-166 paper, and it rewards flat memorisation over reasoning. RML sits close to the ICD text, loves the older eponyms and signs (Bleuler, Schneider, Janet), and returns to the same handful of anchors every year: Sigmund Freud's defense mechanisms, schizophrenia signs, the anorexia-versus-bulimia contrast, mood disorders, and dissociation. Learn these as fact lists, not as concepts to derive under time pressure.

Repeats (2020, 2022, 2024, 2025): Defense mechanisms are the number-one recurring item, almost always as a clinical vignette ("name the mechanism"). Sigmund Freud's ego defenses you must recognise on sight: sublimation (channeling an unacceptable urge into an acceptable outlet, e.g. anger into kickboxing), projection (attributing your own unacceptable thoughts or impulses to others, asked twice in 2025), displacement (redirecting a feeling from a threatening target to a safer one, e.g. yelling at the dog when angry at your partner), repression (involuntary blocking of painful material from awareness), and rationalisation (excusing failure by blaming an external cause, e.g. Clare blaming a co-worker for being fired).

MechanismCore movePaper cue
SublimationUrge → socially valued outletAnger → kickboxing class
ProjectionOwn impulse attributed to others"attributing unacceptable thoughts to others"
DisplacementFeeling shifted to a safer targetYells at dog / argues with husband
RepressionInvoluntary blocking from awarenessCannot bring anxiety-provoking material to consciousness
RationalisationSelf-serving excuse for a failureBlames co-worker for being fired
SplittingAll-good / all-bad, no middleSeen in borderline personality disorder
Projective identificationMelanie Klein, paranoid-schizoidEgo defense "in paranoia"

Who → what: Sigmund Freud → structural model (id runs on the pleasure principle, ego on the reality principle, superego is morality) and the topographic model (the preconscious holds accessible memories recalled at will). Asked in 2020, 2022 (Freud on alcohol dependence = oral-stage fixation) and 2025 ("component that operates on the reality principle" = the ego).

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2024): Schizophrenia recurs every one of those years. Know the positive versus negative symptom split (positive = added experiences: delusions, hallucinations; negative = lost function: flat affect, avolition, alogia, anhedonia), identical-twin concordance of roughly 50 percent, and expressed emotion (EE) = a family climate of criticism, hostility and emotional over-involvement that predicts relapse. RML also asks the ICD inclusion/exclusion criteria directly.

Who → what: Kurt Schneider → First-Rank Symptoms of schizophrenia (2022 "who gave the concept", 2024 "which is NOT a first-rank symptom"). The list: audible thoughts / voices commenting or arguing, thought withdrawal-insertion-broadcasting, made (passivity) feelings, impulses and acts, and delusional perception. Anything negative-symptom (e.g. flat affect) is the classic "NOT first-rank" trap answer.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2024): Eating disorders, always framed as anorexia-versus-bulimia. Shared feature = intense fear of weight gain plus disturbed body image; the discriminator = anorexia nervosa carries markedly low body weight (and, in older criteria, amenorrhea = absence of menstrual periods), whereas bulimia nervosa runs at near-normal weight with binge eating plus compensatory behaviour (self-induced vomiting, laxatives, fasting, over-exercise).

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2024): Mood / affective disorders. Bipolar is the disorder where mood "swings from one extreme to another"; a manic episode's core pair = elevated/expansive mood plus increased energy or activity. Major depressive disorder is cued by loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia) and appetite change, NOT euphoria or specific phobias. Dysthymia (persistent depressive disorder) = milder but longer-lasting than MDD. Depression is the one the paper nicknames the "common cold of psychiatry" and the usual cause of pseudodementia.

Repeats (2022, 2024, 2025): Dissociative disorders. Multiple distinct personalities within one person = dissociative identity disorder (DID). Psychodynamic theory reads dissociative symptoms as defense against trauma; dissociative amnesia is usually localised or selective, whereas generalised amnesia wipes the entire life history. RML pairs this with hysteria/conversion signs (see eponyms below).

Repeats (2023, 2025): The diathesis-stress model asks the same thing twice: mental disorders arise from the interaction of a predisposing vulnerability (the diathesis, genetic or biological) with environmental stress, not from either alone.

Repeats (2021, 2022, 2025): Personality disorders. They are stable, long-term, pervasive patterns. Antisocial PD = manipulation, lack of remorse, irresponsibility; obsessive-compulsive PD = driven by a need for order and control (not the same as OCD).

RML tilt: The 2024 paper in particular is dense with eponyms and older signs, so bank them cold: "ambivalence" (one of Eugen Bleuler's four A's) was coined by Eugen Bleuler; "la belle indifférence" in hysteria is attributed to Pierre Janet; delusion of infidelity = Othello syndrome (delusional jealousy); First-Rank Symptoms = Kurt Schneider; kleptomania is a classic French-alienist eponym. RML rewards knowing the name attached to the term, not just the term.

Counselling & Psychotherapy, 48 questions over six years

The therapy section is built around two obsessions RML returns to every year: who developed which therapy (founder attribution) and the mechanics of behaviour therapy and CBT. Learn the acronym-to-founder map below cold; it converts several guaranteed marks. Note that ego defense mechanisms reappear inside this section too, so the Freud grid above is doing double duty.

Who → what: This is the paper's favourite therapy question type (2021, 2022, 2024 all ask it). Memorise the founder map:

Therapy / conceptDeveloper (full name)
Cognitive therapy / CBT, Socratic questioningAaron Beck
REBT and the ABC modelAlbert Ellis
DBT (distress tolerance)Marsha Linehan
ACT (acceptance & commitment)Steven Hayes
EMDRFrancine Shapiro
Motivational Interviewing (MI)William Miller & Stephen Rollnick
Interpersonal Psychotherapy (IPT)Gerald Klerman & Myrna Weissman
Client-centred therapyCarl Rogers
Self-efficacyAlbert Bandura
Systematic desensitisationJoseph Wolpe
Progressive deep muscle relaxationEdmund Jacobson
Individual psychology, inferiority complexAlfred Adler
Collective unconsciousCarl Jung

Repeats (2021, 2022, 2024, 2025): The cognitive family. Aaron Beck's cognitive therapy is drilled through its methods (Socratic questioning, collaborative identification of cognitive errors such as overgeneralisation and arbitrary inference, thought records for depression). Albert Ellis's REBT is drilled through the ABC model: Activating event → Beliefs → Consequences. Keep Beck (errors/distortions) and Ellis (irrational beliefs, ABC) separate in your head; the paper baits you to swap them.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022): Behaviour therapy built on relaxation. The technique where a patient relaxes while visualising a graded hierarchy of anxiety-provoking scenes (the public-speaking vignette in 2021) is systematic desensitisation, developed by Joseph Wolpe on the principle of reciprocal inhibition / counterconditioning; the relaxation half is Edmund Jacobson's progressive deep relaxation.

Repeats (2021, 2022): Operant-based methods. "Behaviour analysis" and "functional analysis" (asked verbatim, twice each) rest on the principles of operant conditioning (B.F. Skinner): behaviour is shaped by its consequences. Shaping = reinforcing successive approximations; the "Lovaas programme" = applied behaviour analysis for autism.

Who → what: EMDR (2021, 2022) → Francine Shapiro, and it was developed specifically for PTSD / trauma; the technique pairs recall of the distressing memory with bilateral eye movements. DBT and its distress tolerance module (2024) → Marsha Linehan.

Trap: Defense mechanisms show up inside the therapy section, not just abnormal (2024 asked four in a row). Match them precisely: displacement (harassed at work → argues at home), projective identification in paranoia (Melanie Klein), isolation / undoing / reaction formation in OCD, and splitting in borderline personality disorder. Also do not confuse OCD's exposure treatment ERP (exposure and response prevention) with EMDR.

Biopsychology, 65 questions over six years

Biopsychology is the single biggest physiological load in the RML paper, and it repeats along a small set of predictable axes: which neurotransmitter goes with which disease, which lobe or structure does which job, and how the classic theories of emotion order the events. Learn the tables below cold and you clear most of the section on recognition alone.

Repeats (2022, 2023, 2024, 2025): Neurotransmitter → function/disease is asked almost every year. Memorise the pairings: acetylcholine loss → Alzheimer's dementia (2022), dopamine degeneration in the substantia nigra → Parkinson's disease (2023), endorphins are the "feel-good" neurotransmitters (2024), and alcohol acts as an agonist at GABA receptors (2022).

NeurotransmitterSignature link RML tests
AcetylcholineDeficiency → Alzheimer's disease; muscle activation
DopamineDegeneration → Parkinson's; blocked by neuroleptics
GABAMain inhibitory NT; alcohol is an agonist
Endorphins"Feel-good" NT, pain relief and pleasure
Serotonin / DopamineMood; targets of antidepressants and antipsychotics

Repeats (2020, 2024): Lobe → function is a perennial. The frontal lobe handles cognitive/intellectual functioning and voluntary movement (2020, 2024), the parietal lobe interprets sensory information (2024), the occipital lobe processes vision, and the temporal lobe processes hearing and language.

LobeMain domain
FrontalVoluntary movement, planning, intellect, personality
ParietalSomatosensory, interpreting sensory input, spatial sense
OccipitalVision
TemporalHearing, language comprehension, memory

Repeats (2020, 2023, 2024, 2025): Subcortical structure → job is tested constantly. Fix these: hippocampus → consolidation of long-term memory and the site damaged in anterograde amnesia (2023, 2024); amygdala → emotion, fear and aggression, the "seat of emotion" (2023, 2025); hypothalamus → hunger/satiety centre and homeostasis (2021, 2024); medulla (brainstem) → breathing and heart rate (2023); pineal gland → melatonin and the circadian sleep-wake clock (2022, 2023).

StructureFunction RML fishes for
HippocampusLong-term memory consolidation; damaged in anterograde amnesia
AmygdalaFear, aggression, emotional modulation of memory ("seat of emotion")
HypothalamusSatiety/hunger centre, homeostasis, links to pituitary
Medulla oblongataVital autonomic functions: breathing, heart rate
Pineal glandMelatonin; circadian rhythm and sleep-wake cycle

Trap: The "satiety centre" is the ventromedial hypothalamus (destroy it and the rat overeats), while the lateral hypothalamus is the hunger/feeding centre (destroy it and the rat stops eating). RML has flipped these both ways (2021, 2024).

Who → what: Theories of emotion recur every cycle (2020, 2025). William James and Carl Lange (James-Lange theory): stimulus → bodily/physiological arousal → then the felt emotion ("we are afraid because we tremble"). Walter Cannon and Philip Bard (Cannon-Bard theory): arousal and emotion occur simultaneously. Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer (two-factor theory): arousal plus a cognitive label, which explains misattribution of arousal, feeling attraction when actually just aroused by fear (2020, 2025).

Who → what: Paul Ekman is tied to the six universally recognised basic emotions: happiness, sadness, fear, anger, surprise, and disgust (2025 asks which one is NOT on the list, so anything like "contempt", "pride", or "love" is the answer).

Repeats (2021, 2022, 2024): Alcohol-related amnesia recurs. Chronic alcoholism → Korsakoff's syndrome (thiamine/B1 deficiency), classically involving profound retrograde amnesia and confabulation (2021, 2024). Distinguish anterograde amnesia (cannot form NEW memories, hippocampal damage) from retrograde amnesia (loss of OLD memories).

Who → what: Hans Selye's General Adaptation Syndrome (GAS) describes stress in three stages: alarm, resistance, exhaustion (2020). Pair this with the fight-or-flight response, run by the sympathetic branch of the autonomic nervous system with the adrenal glands (2024).

Repeats (2022, 2024): Sleep architecture appears repeatedly. A normal night runs in roughly 90-minute cycles, giving about 4 to 6 NREM-REM cycles per major sleep period (2022, 2024). Know the NREM to REM progression and that REM is when vivid dreaming occurs.

Who → what: Aphasia (loss of language) is the umbrella term (2020), and RML repeatedly probes Broca's aphasia: slow, laboured, non-fluent, telegraphic speech using only keywords, from damage to Broca's area in the left frontal lobe (2022, 2025). Contrast Wernicke's aphasia (fluent but meaningless speech, comprehension lost). Prosopagnosia is the specific inability to recognise faces (2024).

Repeats (2024, 2025): Sensory systems and thresholds recur. The vestibular system (semicircular canals of the inner ear) senses spin and balance (2024); rods are the receptors for scotopic (dim-light) vision (2024); the human ear responds to roughly 20 Hz to 20,000 Hz (2024); and the absolute threshold is the minimum stimulus intensity detectable 50% of the time (2025).

RML tilt: The 2024 and 2025 papers lean on split-brain and imaging facts. Cutting the corpus callosum severs communication between the two hemispheres (Roger Sperry's split-brain work); the left hemisphere is typically dominant for language and analytic processing. For imaging, EEG gives the highest temporal resolution, while CT/MRI are used to view brain structure after a head injury (2025).

RML tilt: The pituitary is repeatedly framed as the "master gland" that controls the other endocrine glands and regulates growth (2024). Neuroleptic (antipsychotic) drugs treat psychosis/schizophrenia and can cause tardive dyskinesia as a long-term side effect (2022, 2025).

Cognitive Psychology, 37 questions over six years

Cognitive Psychology in RML clusters tightly around three areas: the architecture of memory (stores, working memory, memory types), how and why we forget, and perception with its named theorists and biases. Almost every named-psychologist question in the section comes from perception or decision-making, so bind each name to its one idea.

Who → what: The stage/multi-store model (Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin) runs sensory memory → short-term/working memory → long-term memory. Sensory memory holds information for the shortest duration (2020), and RML asks which store is NOT part of this model (2025). Encoding is the process that moves new information into memory (2025).

StoreDuration / capacity
Sensory memoryShortest, under ~1 second; large raw capacity
Short-term / working~15-30 sec; ~7 items (George Miller)
Long-term memoryPotentially unlimited and long-lasting

Who → what: Working memory (Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch) is a repeat favourite. Know its four components: the central executive (attention controller, disrupted by a demanding secondary task, 2022), the phonological loop (verbal/auditory), the visuospatial sketchpad (visual/spatial), and the episodic buffer (2025 lists all four and asks for the model's name).

Repeats (2020, 2022, 2023, 2025): Memory phenomena and types are asked every year. Lock in: the tip-of-the-tongue state is temporary retrieval failure of a known word (2020); retrieval-induced forgetting is when recalling some items suppresses related ones (2020); flashbulb memory is a vivid recollection of an emotionally important event (2025); semantic memory produces scripts of familiar routines (2025); the engram is the physical memory trace (Karl Lashley / Richard Semon, 2025); and dual-coding theory (Allan Paivio) says information coded both verbally and visually is best remembered (2023).

Trap: Explicit (declarative) memory is conscious, includes episodic and semantic; implicit (non-declarative) memory is unconscious, includes procedural skills and priming. RML asks which statement does NOT fit one of these terms (2020, 2025), so watch for a description that swaps the two.

Who → what: Classical conditioning (Ivan Pavlov) recurs across sections. In the dog experiment the bell/metronome starts as a neutral stimulus and becomes the conditioned stimulus after pairing (2020); a conditioned response eliminated by withdrawing reinforcement is called extinction (2020). Taste aversion (John Garcia effect) is single-trial learning with a long delay, an exception to normal conditioning rules (2022).

Who → what: Perception theory questions are all name-driven (2020, 2022, 2023). Bind these: Gestalt psychology → perceiving a whole organised form rather than parts (2020); Hermann von Helmholtz → unconscious inference in perception (2023); James Gibson → affordances, the action opportunities the environment offers, and direct perception (2023); the Bayesian/probabilistic approach → perception as prior knowledge combined with sensory evidence (2023). The Muller-Lyer illusion is weaker in people from non-"carpentered" (non-Western) environments (2022).

Who → what: Judgment and decision-making repeats through named biases (2023). Herbert Simon → bounded rationality, decisions limited by information and cognitive resources (2023). Confirmation bias is linked to the availability heuristic; the representativeness heuristic causes errors by ignoring base rates (Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman, 2023).

Repeats (2021, 2023, 2025): Attention and recall effects surface regularly. The recency effect is better recall of the last items in a list (part of the serial position curve, 2021); change blindness is failing to notice obvious changes in a scene (2023); the misinformation effect is post-event information distorting a memory (Elizabeth Loftus, 2023); and the Stroop effect demonstrates automatic processing interfering with controlled attention (2025).

Who → what: Intelligence models appear in this section too. Joy Paul Guilford's Structure of Intellect model proposes 120+ (up to 150/180) dimensions from operations x contents x products (2021). Raymond Cattell distinguished crystallised intelligence (learned knowledge and skills, an "acquired" ability) from fluid intelligence (2021).

Developmental & Child Psychology, 54 questions over six years

Development is the single heaviest topic on this paper, and it is dominated by the four great stage theorists. Roughly one question in three across the six years is just "match the theorist to the stage or the age," so the fastest marks here come from having Jean Piaget, Erik Erikson, Sigmund Freud, and Lev Vygotsky memorised cold. The paper then layers on a steady supply of research-design items (longitudinal vs cross-sectional), temperament, moral reasoning, and, in 2025, a fresh cluster on learning difficulties.

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2025): Jean Piaget's four cognitive-development stages are the most-tested single idea in the whole fragment. 2020 asked for object permanence (the marker of the sensorimotor stage) and quizzed the conservation task; 2021 asked the age band for the preoperational child; 2022 and 2025 both went after formal operational reasoning (the stage that adds abstract, hypothetical thought). Know the order and the one-line signature of each stage.

Who → what: Jean Piaget → four cognitive stages: Sensorimotor (0 to 2, object permanence), Preoperational (2 to 7, egocentrism, no conservation), Concrete operational (7 to 11, conservation and reversibility), Formal operational (11+, abstract and hypothetical reasoning).

Stage (Piaget)AgeMust-know marker
Sensorimotor0 to 2Object permanence develops
Preoperational2 to 7Egocentrism, cannot conserve
Concrete operational7 to 11Conservation, reversibility
Formal operational11+Abstract, hypothetical thought

Repeats (2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024): Erik Erikson's eight psychosocial stages appear in five of the six years, making them tied as the paper's most reliable theme. 2020 fished for the mid-life crisis of stagnation (the feeling of "having achieved nothing for the next generation," i.e. Generativity vs Stagnation); 2021 asked when Industry vs Inferiority occurs (school-age, ~6 to 12); 2022 and 2023 both asked the adolescent crisis (Identity vs Role Confusion); 2024 asked who coined "identity crisis" (Erikson himself).

Who → what: Erik Erikson → eight psychosocial crises across the lifespan. The three the paper repeatedly targets: Industry vs Inferiority (school age), Identity vs Role Confusion (adolescence, source of the "identity crisis"), and Generativity vs Stagnation (middle adulthood).

Repeats (2021, 2022, 2025): Lev Vygotsky recurs three years running, always contrasted with Piaget. 2021 asked the core difference between Piaget and Vygotsky on language development (Vygotsky sees language and social interaction as driving thought, not merely reflecting it); 2022 tested his link between conceptual thinking and language; 2025 asked directly for the Zone of Proximal Development, the gap between what a child can do alone and what they can do with guidance.

Who → what: Lev Vygotsky → sociocultural theory: the Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD), scaffolding, and the primacy of language and social interaction in cognitive growth.

Repeats (2021, 2022): Lawrence Kohlberg's stages of moral reasoning show up two years in a row. 2021 asked which option is NOT one of his stages and tested the preconventional level (right and wrong judged by reward and punishment, the consequences of an act). 2022 tested Carol Gilligan's critique that Kohlberg's model was built on males and neglects a female "ethic of care."

Who → what: Lawrence Kohlberg → three levels of moral development: Preconventional (reward and punishment), Conventional (social approval and law), Postconventional (universal ethical principles). Carol Gilligan → the "ethic of care" critique.

Trap: On the Kohlberg items the paper offers plausible-sounding stage names that are not his. Preconventional morality is about consequences to the self (reward or punishment), NOT about following the law; that legal orientation belongs to the Conventional level.

Repeats (2020, 2024): Sigmund Freud's psychosexual stages and complexes surface at both ends of the window. 2020 tied adult sarcasm, overeating, and nail-biting to oral-stage fixation, and asked the girl-boy complex pairing (Electra complex to girls as Oedipus complex to boys). 2024 asked the clinical term for lack of bowel control in children (encopresis), an anal-stage adjacent item.

Who → what: Sigmund Freud → five psychosexual stages (Oral, Anal, Phallic, Latency, Genital); fixation at a stage leaves adult traits. Oral fixation → nail-biting, overeating, sarcasm; the Phallic stage carries the Oedipus complex (boys) and Electra complex (girls).

Repeats (2020, 2025): Research-design items reward an easy point. Both 2020 ("studying people across time repeatedly") and 2025 ("the same group of individuals over a 10-year period") are simply asking you to name the longitudinal design. Do not overthink these.

Trap: Longitudinal follows ONE group over time; cross-sectional compares DIFFERENT age groups at ONE time. The paper words the longitudinal stem as "same group over X years" to see if you confuse it with cross-sectional.

Repeats (2021, 2022): Temperament research is asked by named authors. 2021 credited Thomas and Chess with the nine dimensions of temperament (and their easy, difficult, slow-to-warm-up types); 2022 asked what is NOT in Mary Rothbart and John Bates's structure of temperament. Attach the names to the models.

Repeats (2021, 2024): The critical/sensitive period appears twice. 2021 framed it as irreversible limits when infants get no early language exposure; 2024 asked where the strongest evidence for the critical-period hypothesis is found (again, language acquisition). Language is the paper's go-to example for critical periods.

RML tilt: Attachment loaded almost entirely into 2021, which ran a mini-cluster: the child who seeks yet rejects the parent (resistant/ambivalent, Type C), the "internal working model" as the schema-theory prototype for later attachments, and Type D (disorganised) infants. Attribute the classification system to Mary Ainsworth's Strange Situation and the underlying theory to John Bowlby. 2025 also opened a new learning-difficulties strand (dyslexia, dyscalculia); expect it to recur.

Social Psychology, 37 questions over six years

Social psychology on this paper splits into two personalities. The 2020 paper is dominated by a large social-work cluster (case work, group work, and their pioneers), while 2021 onward shifts to mainstream social psychology: group processes, attitudes, conformity, and "name the theorist." Cross-year repeats are fewer here than in development, so the reliable earners are social identity theory, the attitude family, and conformity/influence.

Repeats (2020, 2023): Social identity theory is the one social concept the paper returns to in different years. 2020 asked where we derive a large part of our identity and self-feelings from (our group memberships); 2023 asked what determines self-concept and behaviour within a group (again, the group we belong to). Same answer, same theory.

Who → what: Henri Tajfel and John Turner → Social Identity Theory: part of the self-concept comes from group membership, producing in-group favouritism and out-group bias.

Repeats (2021, 2023): Attitudes and their link to behaviour form a mini-family. 2021 gave the classic cognitive-dissonance scenario (a student who hates smoking then smokes at a party, feeling the tension of dissonance) and asked when attitudes least predict behaviour. 2023 asked, under the Theory of Planned Behavior, what drives the intention to act on a health behaviour (attitude, subjective norms, and perceived behavioural control).

Who → what: Leon Festinger → Cognitive Dissonance Theory (tension from holding conflicting attitudes and behaviours drives attitude change). Icek Ajzen → Theory of Planned Behavior (intention is set by attitude, subjective norms, and perceived behavioural control).

Repeats (2021, 2024): Conformity and social influence recur. 2024 defined conformity outright ("changing your behaviour to match that of others in a group") and asked what Solomon Asch's classic line-judgement experiment tested (the limits of conformity to group pressure). 2021's groupthink item sits in the same influence family.

Who → what: Solomon Asch → line-judgement conformity experiments (people yield to a unanimous wrong majority). Pair with Stanley Milgram → obedience to authority, the influence experiment most likely to appear alongside Asch.

RML tilt: 2021 ran an unusually dense group-processes cluster that is worth pre-loading as a set. Watch for: social facilitation (an audience improves easy tasks but impairs hard or unpractised ones, so it hurts the novice dancer), social loafing (people slack in groups; fix it by making individual contributions identifiable), groupthink (reduce it by encouraging dissent and appointing a devil's advocate), deindividuation (loss of self-awareness in anonymous online or crowd settings, as with cyberbullies), and bystander apathy / diffusion of responsibility (the crowd that films but does not help).

Who → what: 2022 was a pure "name the theorist" year, so bank these. Alice Eagly → Social Role Theory of gender; Leonard Berkowitz → reformulated frustration-aggression hypothesis; Serge Moscovici → Social Representation Theory; Robert Cialdini → six principles of compliance (reciprocity, commitment, social proof, authority, liking, scarcity); John French and Bertram Raven → bases of social power.

Trap: The 2022 compliance and power items are framed as "which is NOT included." You must know Cialdini's six principles and the French and Raven power bases well enough to spot the invented seventh option, not just recognise the real ones.

RML tilt: The 2020 paper carries a heavy social-work block found on no other year: schools of social work, the social case-work process and its techniques, group work as a method, and H. B. Trecker's principles of social group work. It reflects RML's applied, service-oriented framing of "social." Treat these as pure factual recall (pioneers, process steps, and the odd-one-out principle) rather than theory to reason through.

Recap of the map: 570 real questions, six years. Top five topics (general, assessment, statistics, abnormal, biopsychology) = 65% of the paper, study them first. The two evergreen question shapes are "who developed what" and the projective-tests / defense-mechanisms pair. Everything flagged above is taught in full in the twelve sections that follow.

1 Section A, Reasoning, Research Aptitude & General Awareness

This ~20-mark block is scored, not skimmed. It rewards speed on reasoning, precision on research methods vocabulary, and rote accuracy on the Indian mental-health system. Treat it as free marks: the answers are unambiguous once you know the definition.

1. Logical & Analytical Reasoning

The CET-166 paper (and the older GGSIPU CET = Guru Gobind Singh Indraprastha University Common Entrance Test pattern it inherits) uses a fixed menu of item types. Learn the trick behind each.

Series

Find the rule linking consecutive terms, then extend it. Rules are arithmetic (+/−), geometric (×/÷), squares/cubes, or alternating (two interleaved series).

2, 6, 12, 20, 30, ?, differences are 4, 6, 8, 10, so next gap is 12 → 42.

Analogies

"A is to B as C is to D." Name the exact relationship in A:B, then force the same relationship on C:D.

Doctor : Hospital :: Teacher : School (worker : workplace).

Syllogisms

From scratch: A syllogism gives you premises ("All A are B", "Some B are C") and asks which conclusion necessarily follows. Draw overlapping circles (Venn diagrams). A conclusion is valid ONLY if it is true in every possible diagram, not just one. "Some" means at least one and possibly all; "All A are B" does NOT let you say "All B are A".

All cats are animals; all animals are mortal → All cats are mortal (valid).

Trap: "Some A are B" never guarantees "Some A are not B". Existence of an overlap does not prove a non-overlap. The paper plants this "illicit converse" repeatedly.

Odd-one-out (classification)

Four items share a property; one breaks it. Rose, Lotus, Lily, Mango, Mango is the odd one (it is a fruit/tree, the rest are flowers).

Coding-Decoding

A word is enciphered by a rule (shift letters, reverse, number-for-letter). Discover the rule from the given pair, apply it to the target.

If CAT = DBU (each letter +1), then DOG = EPH.

Basic Data Interpretation

A table/bar/pie gives numbers; questions ask totals, differences, percentages, or ratios. If a pie shows 90° for "Clinical", that slice is 90/360 = 25% of the total. Read the axis and units first, compute second.

RML tilt: Reasoning items are non-verbal-friendly and answerable in under a minute; do not burn exam time deriving elegant formulas. Spot the pattern, mark, move.

2. Research Aptitude

Types of research

  • Basic (pure) vs Applied, theory-building vs solving a real problem.
  • Descriptive (describes "what is") vs Experimental (manipulates to test cause).
  • Qualitative (meaning, themes) vs Quantitative (numbers, statistics).
  • Exploratory, correlational, longitudinal, cross-sectional designs.

Hypotheses

The null hypothesis (H₀) states "no effect / no difference / no relationship." The alternative hypothesis (H₁) states there IS one. A directional (one-tailed) hypothesis predicts the direction (Group A scores higher); a non-directional (two-tailed) hypothesis predicts only that a difference exists.

Key. You never "prove" H₁, you reject or fail to reject H₀. A Type I error is rejecting a true H₀ (false positive, α); a Type II error is failing to reject a false H₀ (false negative, β).

Variables

VariableRole
Independent (IV)Manipulated / presumed cause
Dependent (DV)Measured / the effect
ConfoundUnwanted variable that co-varies with the IV and offers a rival explanation
ModeratorChanges the strength/direction of the IV→DV link (a "for whom / when")
MediatorThe mechanism through which IV affects DV (a "how / why")
Trap: Moderator vs mediator. A moderator sits alongside and interacts; a mediator sits in the causal chain (IV → mediator → DV). Stress is a mediator if IV causes stress which then lowers DV.

Sampling

Probability sampling (every unit has a known, non-zero chance; allows generalisation):

  • Simple random, pure lottery.
  • Systematic, every k-th unit from a random start.
  • Stratified, divide into strata, sample within each proportionally.
  • Cluster, sample whole groups (e.g. clinics), study all within.

Non-probability sampling (chance not known; cheaper, weaker generalisation):

  • Convenience, whoever is available.
  • Purposive (judgmental), chosen for a trait.
  • Quota, fill fixed counts per category.
  • Snowball, participants refer others (hidden populations).

Reliability & Validity

Reliability = consistency. Types: test-retest (same test, two times), inter-rater (two scorers agree), internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha), parallel/alternate forms, split-half.

Validity = measures what it claims. Types: content (covers the domain), construct (measures the trait; includes convergent and discriminant), criterion (concurrent and predictive), plus face validity (looks right).

Key. A test can be reliable but NOT valid (a scale consistently 2 kg heavy). It cannot be valid without being reliable. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity.

Basic designs

Between-subjects (different people per condition) vs within-subjects/repeated-measures (same people, all conditions). Randomised control trial (RCT) is the gold-standard experiment. Quasi-experiment lacks random assignment; case study is single-unit depth.

3. General Awareness, Indian Mental-Health System

Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI)

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) is a statutory body created by the RCI Act, 1992. It regulates training of rehabilitation professionals (including clinical psychologists) and maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register.

Key. To practise as a Clinical Psychologist in India you must hold RCI registration. RML's CET-166 admits to an RCI-recognised MA Clinical Psychology, which is why this is the "RCI-licensing route."

Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (MHCA 2017)

Replaced the Mental Health Act, 1987. Rights-based. Key provisions:

  • Advance directive, a person may state in advance how they wish (or do not wish) to be treated.
  • Nominated representative, a person appointed to take decisions on their behalf.
  • Decriminalised suicide attempt, a person attempting suicide is presumed to be under severe stress and shall not be punished under the IPC.
  • Right to community living, protection from cruel/degrading treatment, and free treatment for the poor/homeless.
Trap: The advance directive belongs to MHCA 2017, NOT to the RCI Act or the RPwD Act. Do not confuse MHCA 2017 with the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016.

National & District programmes

The National Mental Health Programme (NMHP) was launched in 1982 to make mental-health care accessible and integrate it into general health services. Its field arm, the District Mental Health Programme (DMHP), began in 1996 (Bellary model) to deliver care at the district level.

Major institutes

InstituteLocation
NIMHANS (National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences)Bengaluru
IHBAS (Institute of Human Behaviour and Allied Sciences)Delhi
CIP (Central Institute of Psychiatry)Ranchi
Dr. Ram Manohar Lohia Hospital (RML)New Delhi
AIIMS (All India Institute of Medical Sciences)New Delhi

Landmark figures (brief)

India's first psychology laboratory and department were established at the University of Calcutta; Girindrasekhar Bose founded the Indian Psychoanalytical Society (1922). (Fuller treatment is in the Indian-psychology section.)

Recap: Reasoning = spot the rule, then apply it; watch the syllogism "some/all" trap. Research aptitude = H₀/H₁, IV/DV plus moderator-vs-mediator, probability vs non-probability sampling, reliable≠valid. General awareness = RCI Act 1992 (your licence), MHCA 2017 (advance directive, nominated representative, decriminalised suicide, community living), NMHP 1982 / DMHP 1996, and the big institutes.

4. More Reasoning Item Types

The paper can rotate across many logical-reasoning families. Below are the ones most likely to appear beyond the basics already covered, each with the quick method that cracks it.

4.1 Blood Relations

Decode a family chain by replacing each link with a symbol as you read it. Draw a small tree: put males and females on rows and connect with lines.

From scratch: "Pointing to a man, Ravi said, 'His mother is the only daughter of my mother.'" Only daughter of Ravi's mother = Ravi's sister (or Ravi's own generation female). So the man's mother is Ravi's sister, making the man Ravi's nephew.
Tip: "only daughter of my mother" often resolves to the speaker's sister or the speaker herself; test both.

4.2 Direction Sense

Track movement on a compass grid (North up). Sum the net East–West and North–South displacement; final distance from start uses the Pythagorean shortcut when the two legs are perpendicular.

Walk 3 km North, then 4 km East: straight-line distance = √(3²+4²) = 5 km, and you are North-East of start.
Trap: Left/right turns depend on the facing direction, not on the page. Turning "right" while facing South sends you West, not East.

4.3 Seating Arrangement (Linear & Circular)

  • Linear: everyone faces the same way; "left" and "right" are the observer's, unless stated otherwise.
  • Circular, facing centre: a person's right hand points anticlockwise; facing outward reverses this.
Key. Fix one definite clue first (an absolute position), then place relative clues around it. Keep a light pencil diagram; do not solve circular puzzles purely in your head.

4.4 Calendar & Clock

Calendar: use odd days (remainder after dividing total days by 7). An ordinary year has 1 odd day, a leap year 2. A century leap-year rule: divisible by 400 is a leap year (2000 yes), a plain century (1900) is not.

Clock: the minute hand gains 5.5° per minute over the hour hand. Hands overlap 11 times in 12 hours, and form a right angle 22 times in 12 hours.

4.5 Series Depth & Venn Set-Counting

Number/alphabet series may mix two interleaved patterns (alternate terms) or use differences of differences. For letters, convert to position numbers (A=1 … Z=26) and read the gaps.

Venn set-counting: for two sets, |A∪B| = |A| + |B| − |A∩B|. For three, add the three singles, subtract the three pair-overlaps, add back the triple overlap.

In a class of 40, 25 play cricket, 20 play hockey, 10 play both: at least one sport = 25 + 20 − 10 = 35; neither = 5.

4.6 Statement-Based & Other Visual Types

TypeWhat you decide
Statement & AssumptionIs the unstated thing taken for granted necessarily implied?
Statement & ConclusionDoes the conclusion definitely follow from the given facts alone?
Statement & Course of ActionIs the proposed action a sensible, practical response to the problem?
Cause & EffectWhich event is the cause, which the effect, or are they independent?
Cubes & DiceOpposite faces of a standard die sum to 7; painted-cube corner pieces show 3 faces.
Input-OutputFind the step rule (shift, sort, swap) applied once per step, then extend it.
Ranking / OrderingTotal = (rank from top) + (rank from bottom) − 1.
Trap: In assumptions, an option that merely restates the statement, or is too strong ("all", "never"), is usually not a valid assumption. Stick to what is strictly necessary.

5. Research Aptitude: Going Deeper

5.1 Purpose-Based Types of Research

  • Fundamental (basic/pure): builds theory and general knowledge, no immediate application (testing a memory model).
  • Applied: solves a specific practical problem (does a coping programme cut relapse?).
  • Action research: a practitioner studies and improves their own practice in cycles of act → observe → reflect.

By objective, research is also exploratory (map an unfamiliar area, generate hypotheses), descriptive (describe what exists, the "what"), or explanatory (test cause, the "why").

5.2 Steps of the Research Process

  1. Identify and define the problem.
  2. Review the existing literature.
  3. Formulate hypotheses.
  4. Choose the research design and sample.
  5. Collect the data.
  6. Analyse and interpret the data.
  7. Draw conclusions and report.

5.3 A Good Hypothesis and Research Ethics

Key. A good hypothesis is clear and specific, testable (falsifiable), states a relationship between variables, is grounded in theory, and is stated before data collection.
  • Informed consent: participants agree freely after understanding the purpose, procedure, and risks; they may withdraw anytime.
  • Confidentiality & anonymity: protect identities and keep data secure.
  • Plagiarism: presenting others' work as one's own; avoid by citing every borrowed idea.
  • Referencing awareness: psychology uses APA style (author, year, in-text; full list at the end). Institutional Ethics Committee approval is required for research on humans.

6. Indian Mental-Health System: High-Yield GK

6.1 Programmes and Committees

Who → what: Joseph Bhore → Bhore Committee (1946), blueprint for India's public-health system; A. L. Mudaliar → Mudaliar Committee (1959, Health Survey and Planning Committee), reviewed progress since Bhore.
  • National Mental Health Programme (NMHP), 1982: aims to ensure minimum mental-health care for all, integrate it into general health services, and promote community participation.
  • District Mental Health Programme (DMHP), 1996: the NMHP's district-level arm; began from the Bellary (Karnataka) model, delivering care through district hospitals and primary health centres.
  • National Mental Health Policy, 2014: India's first, rights-based, stresses universal access and reducing stigma.
  • Tele-MANAS, 2022: the national tele-mental-health helpline (14416) under Ayushman Bharat; Ayushman Bharat also adds mental-health services at Health and Wellness Centres.

6.2 Key Legislation

LawCore point
Mental Health Act, 1987Replaced the Indian Lunacy Act 1912; now superseded.
Mental Healthcare Act (MHCA), 2017Rights-based; decriminalises suicide attempts, allows an Advance Directive and a Nominated Representative, sets up Mental Health Review Boards.
National Trust Act, 1999For autism, cerebral palsy, intellectual disability, and multiple disabilities; enables guardianship.
Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPWD) Act, 2016Lists 21 specified disabilities; replaced the 1995 Act; aligns with the UN Convention (UNCRPD).
RML tilt: Expect a one-liner such as "Which Act decriminalised the attempt to suicide?" (answer: MHCA 2017) or "Number of disabilities under RPWD 2016?" (answer: 21). The RPWD list includes mental illness, intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities, and autism spectrum disorder.

6.3 Institutes, Cities, and Global Anchors

InstituteCity
NIMHANSBengaluru
Central Institute of Psychiatry (CIP)Ranchi
RINPASRanchi
IHBASDelhi
LGBRIMHTezpur (Assam)

WHO mhGAP (Mental Health Gap Action Programme, 2008) scales up care for mental, neurological, and substance-use disorders in low-resource settings using non-specialist health workers.

Recap: Reasoning now spans blood relations, directions, seating, calendar/clock, series, Venn counting, and statement/visual types; research aptitude adds the basic/applied/action split, the seven-step process, hypothesis quality, and ethics; and the GK core is NMHP 1982 → DMHP 1996, MHA 1987 → MHCA 2017, RPWD 2016 (21 disabilities), National Trust 1999, Bhore and Mudaliar committees, Tele-MANAS, mhGAP, and the institute-to-city map.

7. Cause-and-Effect Reasoning

From scratch: A cause-and-effect item gives you two statements and asks how they are related. Your job is to decide whether one statement is the reason and the other is the result, whether both spring from a common third cause, or whether they are simply two unrelated facts. This tests whether you can separate correlation from causation, a skill that is also core to research methodology, so RML likes it.

The five standard options in this family are: (a) statement I is the cause and II is its effect; (b) statement II is the cause and I is its effect; (c) both are effects of independent causes; (d) both are effects of a common cause; (e) both are independent. The trick is that the order in which the statements are printed is not the order of causation. You must reason about what could physically or logically come first in time.

7.1 The method that cracks it

Ask three questions in sequence. First, could one event have produced the other? If yes, the earlier-in-time event is the cause. Second, if neither can produce the other, is there an obvious background factor that produced both? If yes, choose "common cause". Third, if no link survives, choose "independent". Always test the time arrow: an effect cannot precede its cause.

Example: (I) The government raised the price of petrol sharply. (II) The transport unions announced a strike. Here I is a plausible trigger for II, and I comes first in time, so I is the cause and II is the effect.
Trap: When both statements describe consequences of a monsoon, an election, or an economic slump that is not stated, the answer is usually "common cause", not "I causes II". Do not force a direct link just because the two statements sit next to each other.

8. The Three "Statement" Families: Assumption, Argument, Conclusion

From scratch: These three item types all begin with a statement, but each asks a different question, and mixing them up is the single most common way candidates lose marks in this block. Learn the one-line definition of each and the specific test each one demands.

8.1 Statement and Assumption

An assumption is something the speaker takes for granted, an unstated belief without which the statement would make no sense. The test is the negation test: negate the assumption; if the statement then collapses, the assumption is implicit (valid). If the statement still stands, it is not an assumption.

Example: Statement, "Please switch off the lights when you leave the room." Assumption, "The lights can be switched off." Negate it (the lights cannot be switched off) and the request becomes meaningless, so the assumption holds.

8.2 Statement and Argument

Here you are given a proposal and one or more arguments for or against it, and you judge whether each argument is strong (directly relevant and weighty) or weak (irrelevant, trivial, or merely an opinion). A strong argument addresses the core issue with a real consequence; a weak one appeals to emotion, restates the question, or raises a side point.

Rule: An argument that begins "Yes, because it will save money and lives" is usually strong; one that begins "No, because we have never done it before" or "Yes, because every other country does it" is usually weak.

8.3 Statement and Conclusion

A conclusion is something that must follow from the given statement alone, using no outside knowledge. Treat the statement as the only truth in the universe and ask whether the conclusion is guaranteed. If the conclusion adds any information not derivable from the statement, it does not follow.

Trap: Do not bring in real-world knowledge. A conclusion can be factually true in the real world yet still "not follow" because the given statement does not support it. Judge only against the printed statement.
TypeQuestion askedWinning test
AssumptionWhat is taken for granted?Negation test
ArgumentIs this reason strong or weak?Relevance + real consequence
ConclusionWhat must follow?Derivable from statement alone

9. Data Sufficiency

From scratch: A data-sufficiency question gives a problem and two numbered statements, then asks not for the answer but whether the statements give enough information to answer it. You are testing sufficiency, not computing the final number. This saves time once you master it because you often stop before doing any arithmetic.

9.1 The five-option ladder

The standard options are: (a) statement I alone is sufficient; (b) statement II alone is sufficient; (c) both together are sufficient but neither alone; (d) either alone is sufficient; (e) both together are still insufficient. Evaluate each statement separately first, covering the other with your hand, then only combine them if neither alone works.

Example: What is the two-digit number? (I) The sum of its digits is 9. (II) The digit in the tens place is twice the units digit. Statement I alone gives many numbers; II alone gives many numbers; together, tens = 2 times units and digits sum to 9 gives 63 uniquely, so the answer is "both together".
Trap: Never actually solve for the value once you know a single unique answer exists. Deciding "this yields exactly one value" is enough, and computing it wastes the seconds this format is designed to save.

10. Data Interpretation in Depth: Tables, Bars, Pies, and Percentage Change

From scratch: Data interpretation gives you a table or chart and a cluster of questions. The chart never changes; only the questions do. The examiner is testing whether you can read the axis, pick the right cell, and run three arithmetic operations quickly: percentage, ratio, and percentage change.

10.1 Reading each chart type

A table is the most information-dense and needs careful row-column matching. A bar chart compares magnitudes across categories; read the height against the axis. A pie chart shows parts of a whole, where the whole is 360 degrees or 100 percent, so a 90-degree slice is one quarter of the total. A line graph shows a trend over time; its slope, not its height, signals growth.

10.2 The percentage-change formula

Rule: Percentage change = (new value minus old value) divided by old value, times 100. The denominator is always the original value, never the new one. A rise from 200 to 250 is a 25 percent increase (50/200), but a fall from 250 back to 200 is only a 20 percent decrease (50/250).
Example: A pie chart shows a family spends 25 percent of a 40,000 rupee budget on rent. Rent = 0.25 times 40,000 = 10,000 rupees. If a bar chart then shows rent rose to 12,000 next year, the increase is (12,000 minus 10,000)/10,000 times 100 = 20 percent.
Trap: Percentage points and percentages are not the same. A share that moves from 20 percent to 25 percent has risen by 5 percentage points but by 25 percent in relative terms. DI questions exploit this difference constantly.

10.3 Two more habits that save time

First, before touching any question, read the units and the footnote: a bar labelled "in thousands" or a table with a "figures in lakh" note quietly rescales every answer. Second, when a question asks for an average across categories, do not average the percentages; convert each to an absolute value first, add them, then divide. Averaging percentages of different bases is one of the most reliable ways the paper manufactures a wrong option that looks clean.

Example: two shops sell at 20 percent and 40 percent profit, but on different cost bases of 100 and 400 rupees. The combined profit is not 30 percent; it is (20 + 160)/(100 + 400) times 100 = 36 percent.

11. Approaches to Research: Qualitative, Quantitative, and by Aim

From scratch: Beyond the basic-versus-applied split, RML aptitude items ask you to classify a study by its aim and by its type of data. Knowing the vocabulary lets you answer these in seconds.

11.1 By aim

Exploratory research probes a poorly understood problem to generate ideas and hypotheses, often using open interviews. Descriptive research paints an accurate picture of a group or situation (surveys, case studies) but does not manipulate anything. Explanatory or experimental research tests cause and effect by manipulating an independent variable and controlling others.

11.2 By type of data

Quantitative research collects numbers and uses statistics, prizing objectivity, large samples, and generalisability. Qualitative research collects words, meanings, and observations, prizing depth and context; it uses methods such as thematic analysis and grounded theory. Mixed-methods research combines both.

DimensionQuantitativeQualitative
DataNumbersWords, meanings
LogicDeductive, tests theoryInductive, builds theory
SampleLarge, randomSmall, purposive
GoalGeneraliseUnderstand in depth
RML tilt: Expect a one-line vignette ("a researcher interviews 8 survivors to understand their lived experience") and a demand to name the approach. Depth-plus-small-sample-plus-meaning equals qualitative; that stem is a giveaway.

12. The Research Report and APA Referencing

12.1 Standard structure of a report

A research report or thesis follows a fixed skeleton, and aptitude items ask you to order or identify its parts. The core body runs: Introduction (states the problem and objectives), Review of Literature (what is already known and the gap), Method (participants, tools, procedure, design), Results (findings and statistics, no interpretation), Discussion (interpretation, limitations, implications), and References. The abstract sits at the front but is written last.

Rule: Results state what was found; Discussion states what it means. Any interpretation placed in the Results section is a structural error, and examiners test exactly this boundary.

12.2 APA referencing basics

The American Psychological Association (APA) style is the standard in psychology. In-text citations use the author-and-year form, for example (Sharma, 2019). A journal reference lists author, year in parentheses, article title, italicised journal name, volume, and pages. APA is now in its 7th edition (2019/2020), a fact worth remembering for a factual GK item.

Example: A book reference reads, Author, A. A. (Year). Title of the book. Publisher. Note the year comes right after the author, a hallmark that distinguishes APA from MLA.

13. Thesis, Dissertation, and Academic Terms Tested in Aptitude

13.1 Thesis versus dissertation

In the Indian and British convention, a dissertation is the shorter research document submitted for a Master's degree (as in an MA or M.Sc.), while a thesis is the longer, original-contribution document submitted for an M.Phil. or PhD. (The American convention reverses the two words, which is why examiners sometimes flag the ambiguity, but for RML use the Indian sense.)

13.2 Sampling and design terms as tested

Aptitude items reuse research vocabulary as pure recognition questions. Know that a population is the whole group of interest and a sample is the studied subset; a parameter describes a population while a statistic describes a sample. Probability sampling (simple random, systematic, stratified, cluster) allows generalisation; non-probability sampling (convenience, purposive, snowball, quota) does not. A pilot study is a small trial run to test the tools before the main study.

Trap: Stratified sampling divides the population into groups and samples within each group; cluster sampling divides into groups and samples whole groups. Candidates swap these two constantly.

14. Teaching Aptitude Essentials

From scratch: Some RML and allied entrance papers slip in a few teaching-aptitude items borrowed from the UGC-NET pattern. You do not need pedagogy training, only the standard vocabulary of levels, objectives, and evaluation.

14.1 Levels and objectives

Teaching is often described at three levels: the memory level (recall of facts), the understanding level (grasp of relationships), and the reflective level (problem-solving and critical thought). Learning objectives are commonly framed with Bloom's taxonomy, which ranks cognitive skills from remembering, understanding, applying, analysing, and evaluating, up to creating.

14.2 Formative versus summative evaluation
