Rule: Formative evaluation happens during learning to give feedback and improve teaching (class quizzes, assignments). Summative evaluation happens at the end to certify achievement (the final exam). A quick memory hook: formative "forms" the learner along the way; summative "sums up" at the finish.
RML tilt: A one-line stem like "a teacher gives weekly ungraded quizzes to adjust her lessons" points straight at formative evaluation. Watch the word "during" versus "at the end".

15. Global and National Bodies Beyond RCI and NIMHANS

From scratch: The GK block rewards a clean map of who does what. RCI and NIMHANS are covered elsewhere; here are the other bodies that recur.

15.1 The core acronyms

BodyWhat it isRole
WHOWorld Health Organization (UN, Geneva)Global health standards; publishes the ICD classification
DGHSDirectorate General of Health ServicesTechnical health advice to the Union health ministry
ICMRIndian Council of Medical ResearchApex body for biomedical research and ethics guidelines
NHRCNational Human Rights CommissionProtects rights, including of persons in mental-health institutions
NMHCNational Mental Health Commission / Authority contextOversight linked to MHCA 2017 (Central Mental Health Authority)
Who → what: WHO publishes the ICD (currently ICD-11), while the APA publishes the DSM (currently DSM-5-TR). India officially uses the ICD system, a fact examiners love to check.

15.2 Diagnostic manuals in one line

The ICD (International Classification of Diseases) is the WHO system used officially in India; the DSM (Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders) is the APA system used widely in research and the United States. Knowing "WHO to ICD, APA to DSM" answers a whole family of questions.

16. Rights Guaranteed under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017

From scratch: The MHCA 2017 replaced the Mental Health Act of 1987 and shifted the frame from control to rights. The Act came into force in 2018 and treats access to mental healthcare as a right. RML tests the specific rights it grants.

16.1 The headline rights

  • Right to access mental healthcare run or funded by the government, of affordable and good quality.
  • Decriminalisation of suicide: a person who attempts suicide is presumed to be under severe stress and is not to be punished under the penal code.
  • Advance Directive: a person may state in advance how they wish to be treated, and whom they nominate as their representative.
  • Nominated Representative: the right to appoint a person to take decisions on one's behalf.
  • Right to confidentiality, dignity, and protection from cruel or degrading treatment.
  • Restrictions on procedures: electro-convulsive therapy without muscle relaxants and anaesthesia is banned, ECT is banned for minors, and sterilisation and chaining are prohibited.
Rule: The MHCA 2017 defines "mental illness" but pointedly excludes mental retardation / intellectual disability from that definition, since disability is covered by the RPWD Act. This exclusion is a favourite one-mark trap.
Trap: Do not confuse the Advance Directive (patient states future wishes) with the Nominated Representative (a named person who decides). Both are new to the 2017 Act, and stems deliberately blur them.

17. The RPWD Act 2016 and the National Institutes

17.1 Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016

The RPWD Act 2016 replaced the older Persons with Disabilities Act of 1995 and expanded the recognised disabilities from 7 to 21, now including mental illness, autism spectrum disorder, intellectual disability, and specific learning disabilities. It mandates reservation in higher education (5 percent) and government jobs (4 percent) and defines "benchmark disability" as 40 percent or more.

Who → what: The RPWD Act is administered by the Department of Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities (DEPwD) under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, the same ministry under which the RCI sits.

17.2 National institutes worth naming

Several National Institutes serve specific disabilities and often appear as "match the institute to the city" items. Key ones include NIMHANS (Bengaluru, mental health and neurosciences), the Central Institute of Psychiatry or CIP (Ranchi), IHBAS (Delhi), NIEPID (Secunderabad, intellectual disabilities), NIEPMD (Chennai, multiple disabilities), Ali Yavar Jung National Institute (Mumbai, hearing), and the National Institute for the Visually Handicapped (Dehradun).

RML tilt: CIP Ranchi and IHBAS Delhi appear repeatedly because both run their own clinical-psychology entrances, so an RML aspirant is expected to know them cold.

18. Important Days and Landmark Reports

18.1 The calendar of health days

DayDate
World Mental Health Day10 October
World Health Day7 April
World Suicide Prevention Day10 September
World Autism Awareness Day2 April
International Day of Persons with Disabilities3 December
World Alzheimer's Day21 September

18.2 Programmes and the policy trail

The National Mental Health Programme (NMHP) was launched in 1982 to make mental-health care available at the community level; its field arm, the District Mental Health Programme (DMHP), began in 1996 under the Bellary model in Karnataka. The National Mental Health Policy was adopted in 2014, and the MHCA followed in 2017. Reading these as a timeline (1982 to 1996 to 2014 to 2017) turns a memory task into a story.

Recap: cause-and-effect turns on the time arrow and the hidden common cause; the three "statement" families each demand their own test (negation, strength, must-follow); data sufficiency asks only "is it enough", never the final number; percentage change always divides by the original value. On the GK side, WHO owns the ICD and APA owns the DSM; the MHCA 2017 grants access, decriminalises suicide, and adds the Advance Directive and Nominated Representative, while excluding intellectual disability, which the RPWD 2016 (21 disabilities, 40 percent benchmark) covers instead. Anchor the policy timeline at 1982 to 2017 and the day at 10 October.

2 General & Basic Psychology

From scratch: Psychology is the scientific study of behaviour and mental processes. This section builds the "core" academic base the RML CET-166 paper draws on: the schools, then the big four content areas (learning, memory, attention/perception, motivation/emotion) and intelligence. Learn each theorist as a who → what pair; the exam almost always tests matching.

1. Schools & History of Psychology

Modern psychology begins in 1879, when Wilhelm Wundt opened the first psychology laboratory at Leipzig, Germany. Each school below is a reaction to the one before it.

Who → what:
  • Wilhelm Wundt & Edward Titchener → Structuralism: break conscious experience into basic elements (sensations, feelings) using introspection. Titchener coined the term.
  • William James → Functionalism: don't ask what the mind is, ask what it is for, how consciousness helps us adapt. Wrote Principles of Psychology (1890).
  • Max Wertheimer, Wolfgang Köhler, Kurt Koffka → Gestalt: "the whole is different from the sum of its parts." Wertheimer's phi phenomenon (apparent motion) launched it; Köhler showed insight learning in chimpanzees.
  • John B. Watson & B.F. Skinner → Behaviourism: study only observable behaviour, not the mind. Watson's 1913 "behaviourist manifesto" is the founding statement.
  • Sigmund Freud → Psychoanalysis: behaviour driven by the unconscious and early childhood conflict.
  • Carl Rogers & Abraham Maslow → Humanistic ("third force"): free will, growth, self-actualization.
  • Ulric Neisser & George A. Miller → Cognitive revolution (1950s–60s): the mind is back, studied as information processing. Neisser's 1967 book Cognitive Psychology named the field; Miller's "magical number seven, plus or minus two" set short-term memory span.
Trap: Titchener (not Wundt) coined "structuralism," and Titchener was American-based; Wundt is the founder of experimental psychology. Also, Gestalt is perception-focused, behaviourism is learning-focused, don't swap.

2. Learning

Learning = a relatively permanent change in behaviour due to experience.

Classical conditioning, Ivan Pavlov

Ivan Pavlov (Russian physiologist) discovered that a neutral stimulus, paired with one that already triggers a reflex, comes to trigger that reflex itself.

TermMeaningPavlov's dog
US (unconditioned stimulus)triggers a reflex naturallyfood
UR (unconditioned response)the natural reflexsalivation to food
CS (conditioned stimulus)was neutral, now triggers responsebell
CR (conditioned response)learned response to CSsalivation to bell
  • Acquisition: learning the CS–US link.
  • Extinction: CS repeatedly without US → CR fades.
  • Spontaneous recovery: after a rest, the extinguished CR briefly reappears.
  • Generalization: similar stimuli also trigger the CR (a different bell).
  • Discrimination: responding only to the specific CS, not similar ones.
  • Higher-order conditioning: a well-learned CS acts as a US to condition a new CS.

Operant conditioning, Thorndike & Skinner

Edward L. Thorndike's Law of Effect: responses followed by satisfaction are strengthened; those followed by discomfort are weakened (from his puzzle box cats). B.F. Skinner extended this: behaviour is shaped by its consequences, studied in the Skinner box.

Key. Reinforcement always increases a behaviour; punishment always decreases it. "Positive" = add a stimulus, "negative" = remove one.
  • Positive reinforcement: add something pleasant (give a treat).
  • Negative reinforcement: remove something unpleasant (seatbelt beep stops).
  • Positive punishment: add something aversive (a scolding).
  • Negative punishment: remove something pleasant (take away the phone).
  • Primary reinforcer: satisfies a biological need (food, water). Secondary reinforcer: learned value (money, praise).
  • Shaping: reinforcing successive approximations toward a target behaviour.

Schedules of reinforcement:

ScheduleRuleFeature
Fixed Ratio (FR)after a set number of responseshigh rate, post-reward pause
Variable Ratio (VR)after an unpredictable numberhighest, steadiest rate
Fixed Interval (FI)first response after a set timescalloped pattern
Variable Interval (VI)first response after variable timeslow, steady
Key. Variable Ratio (VR) produces the highest response rate and is most resistant to extinction (why gambling is so persistent).

Observational learning, Albert Bandura

Albert Bandura's Bobo doll experiment showed children imitate aggression they merely watch, learning by modelling, no direct reinforcement needed. His broader social learning theory adds thought between stimulus and response.

3. Memory

Who → what:
  • Richard Atkinson & Richard Shiffrin → multi-store model: sensory memory → short-term memory (STM) → long-term memory (LTM), with rehearsal moving info along.
  • Alan Baddeley & Graham Hitch → working memory: replaces passive STM with an active system, central executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad (later, the episodic buffer).
  • Fergus Craik & Robert Lockhart → levels of processing: deeper semantic (meaning) processing is remembered better than shallow (looks, sound).
  • Endel Tulving → memory types: episodic (personal events), semantic (facts), both declarative; plus procedural (skills, non-declarative).
  • Hermann Ebbinghaus → the forgetting curve: memory drops sharply then levels off; also the spacing effect.

Forgetting theories: decay (unused traces fade), interference, proactive (old learning blocks new) vs retroactive (new learning blocks old), and retrieval failure.

Key. Serial position effect: we best recall the first items (primacy, rehearsed into LTM) and last items (recency, still in STM); the middle is worst.
Trap: Proactive = pro/forward, old interferes forward onto new; retroactive = new acts backward on old. Easy to reverse under time pressure.

4. Attention & Perception

Who → what:
  • Donald Broadbent → filter (bottleneck) model: an early filter selects one channel by physical features; unattended info is blocked.
  • Anne Treisman → attenuation model: the filter turns unattended input down rather than off, so a salient word (your name) can still break through.

Gestalt laws of perceptual organization: proximity, similarity, closure, continuity (good continuation), figure–ground, common fate.

Depth cues: monocular (one eye, relative size, interposition, linear perspective, texture gradient) and binocular (two eyes, retinal disparity, convergence). Perceptual constancies keep size, shape, and colour stable despite changing retinal images.

5. Motivation & Emotion

Drive-reduction theory (Clark Hull): a need creates a drive that we act to reduce, restoring homeostasis. Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs stacks motives: physiological → safety → love/belonging → esteem → self-actualization; lower needs dominate until met.

Theories of emotion (a classic RML matching item):

TheoryCore claim
James–Langebody first: we feel afraid because we tremble
Cannon–Bardsimultaneous: arousal and emotion occur at once
Schachter–Singer two-factorarousal + a cognitive label for it
Richard Lazarus appraisala cognitive appraisal comes first and causes emotion
Trap: James–Lange = bodily response precedes emotion; Cannon–Bard = they are simultaneous. Reversing these two is the most common error.

6. Intelligence

Who → what:
  • Charles Spearman → two-factor theory: a general factor g underlies all abilities plus task-specific s factors (from factor analysis).
  • Louis Thurstone → seven primary mental abilities (verbal, numerical, spatial, memory, reasoning, word fluency, perceptual speed), no single g.
  • Raymond B. Cattell → fluid intelligence (reasoning with novel problems, declines with age) vs crystallized intelligence (learned knowledge, holds up).
  • Howard Gardner → multiple intelligences (linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily-kinesthetic, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic).
  • Robert Sternberg → triarchic theory: analytical, creative, practical.
  • J.P. Guilford → Structure of Intellect: up to 150+ abilities from operations × contents × products; source of divergent thinking.
RML tilt: The paper leans on who-said-what matching, theorist to concept, school to founder, emotion theory to claim. Memorize the Pavlov four-letter set (US/UR/CS/CR), the VR = most extinction-resistant fact, proactive vs retroactive interference, and the intelligence theorists cold; these recur almost every year.
Recap: Schools: Wundt/Titchener structuralism, James functionalism, Wertheimer/Köhler/Koffka Gestalt, Watson/Skinner behaviourism, Freud psychoanalysis, Rogers/Maslow humanistic, Neisser/Miller cognitive. Learning: Pavlov classical (US/UR/CS/CR), Thorndike law of effect, Skinner reinforcement/punishment & schedules (VR resists extinction), Bandura observational. Memory: Atkinson–Shiffrin multi-store, Baddeley–Hitch working memory, Craik–Lockhart levels, Tulving episodic/semantic/procedural, Ebbinghaus forgetting curve, serial position. Attention: Broadbent filter, Treisman attenuation. Emotion: James–Lange, Cannon–Bard, Schachter–Singer, Lazarus. Intelligence: Spearman g, Thurstone, Cattell fluid/crystallized, Gardner, Sternberg, Guilford.

7. Sensation & Psychophysics

Everything you know about the world arrives first as raw energy hitting a receptor. Sensation is the pickup of that physical energy (light, sound, pressure, chemicals) by specialised receptor cells; perception is the brain's organisation and interpretation of those signals into meaningful objects and events. Sensation asks "is something there?"; perception asks "what is it?".

From scratch: a receptor cannot pass physical energy directly into the nervous system. It must convert light or sound or pressure into nerve impulses (action potentials). That conversion is called transduction, and it is the single most important idea in sensation: every sense is a transducer.

7.1 Thresholds

Not every stimulus is strong enough to notice. Psychophysics (founded by Gustav Fechner) measures the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations they produce.

  • Absolute threshold: the smallest amount of stimulus energy detectable 50% of the time (e.g. a candle flame seen on a dark clear night from about 30 miles).
  • Difference threshold (just-noticeable-difference, JND): the smallest change in a stimulus that can be detected 50% of the time. Also called the difference limen.
  • Subliminal stimulus: one below the absolute threshold, not consciously detected.

7.2 The three classic laws

LawTheoristCore statement
Weber's lawErnst WeberThe JND is a constant proportion of the original stimulus, not a fixed amount. A heavier weight needs a bigger change to feel different.
Fechner's lawGustav FechnerSensation grows as the logarithm of stimulus intensity (built on Weber's constant). Big physical increases yield smaller sensory increases.
Stevens' power lawStanley Smith StevensSensation is a power function of intensity; the exponent varies by sense (electric shock grows faster than brightness). Often considered more accurate than Fechner's.
Who → what: Weber → constant fraction (JND/stimulus = k); Fechner → logarithmic sensation; Stevens → power-function sensation.

Weber fraction for weight is about 1/50: you can just detect a 2g change on a 100g load, but need a 20g change on a 1000g load.

7.3 Signal detection theory

Detection is not just about the stimulus; it also depends on the observer's expectations, motivation and the background "noise". Signal detection theory (SDT) replaces the fixed threshold with two independent factors: sensitivity (how well signal is told from noise) and response criterion / bias (how willing the person is to say "yes"). Outcomes fall into hits, misses, false alarms and correct rejections.

RML tilt: expect a one-liner match item: "detection depends on both stimulus intensity and decision criterion" → signal detection theory. Know that SDT explains why a tired radiologist reports more false alarms.

7.4 Sensory adaptation and the senses

Sensory adaptation is the reduced sensitivity to an unchanging stimulus (you stop smelling a room, stop feeling your clothes). It keeps us tuned to change.

  • Vision: rods and cones in the retina transduce light (photoreception).
  • Audition: hair cells on the basilar membrane (cochlea) transduce sound vibration.
  • Gustation and olfaction: chemoreceptors on the tongue and in the nose transduce dissolved and airborne chemicals.
  • Somatosensation: mechanoreceptors, thermoreceptors and nociceptors handle touch, temperature and pain; the vestibular and kinaesthetic senses handle balance and body position.

8. Thinking, Cognition & Problem Solving

8.1 Concepts and categories

A concept is a mental grouping of similar objects, events or ideas; it lets us handle the world without treating every item as brand new. We organise concepts around a prototype: the best, most typical example of a category (a robin is a more prototypical "bird" than a penguin). Faster categorisation of items close to the prototype is a classic exam fact (Eleanor Rosch's work).

8.2 Reasoning

  • Deductive reasoning: from general premises to a specific, logically certain conclusion (top-down). If the premises are true, the conclusion must be true.
  • Inductive reasoning: from specific observations to a probable general rule (bottom-up). Conclusions are likely, not guaranteed.

8.3 Problem-solving strategies

StrategyWhat it is
AlgorithmStep-by-step procedure that guarantees a solution but can be slow.
HeuristicMental shortcut / rule of thumb: fast, but can fail.
Means-end analysisReduce the gap between current state and goal by breaking it into subgoals.
InsightSudden "aha" grasp of the solution (Wolfgang Kohler's ape Sultan; Gestalt view).

Two classic blocks to problem solving:

  • Mental set: the tendency to keep using a strategy that worked before, even when a simpler one exists.
  • Functional fixedness: inability to see a novel use for an object (Karl Duncker's candle problem: not seeing the matchbox as a shelf).
From scratch: the Gestalt school treated problem solving as restructuring the whole problem, so insight was a perceptual reorganisation, not trial and error. This is why insight and functional fixedness sit together in the syllabus.

8.4 Judgement and decision-making heuristics

Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky showed that everyday judgement leans on heuristics that produce predictable errors (biases).

  • Availability heuristic: judging likelihood by how easily examples come to mind (plane crashes feel common because they are vivid).
  • Representativeness heuristic: judging by how well something matches a stereotype, ignoring base rates.
  • Anchoring and adjustment: starting from an initial value and adjusting insufficiently.
  • Framing effect: the same choice pulls different decisions depending on wording ("90% survive" vs "10% die").
  • Confirmation bias: seeking evidence that fits an existing belief and ignoring the rest.
Trap: availability = ease of recall; representativeness = resemblance to a stereotype. Exams swap the two. Kahneman won the 2002 Nobel in Economics for this work (Tversky had died).

8.5 Creativity

J.P. Guilford distinguished convergent thinking (narrowing to one correct answer, what IQ tests measure) from divergent thinking (generating many possible answers, the engine of creativity). Divergent thinking is judged on fluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration.

9. Language & Psycholinguistics

Language is a rule-governed system for combining symbols to communicate meaning. Psycholinguists break it into levels:

  • Phonology: the sound system; phonemes are the smallest distinctive sound units.
  • Morphology: word structure; morphemes are the smallest meaning units.
  • Syntax: rules for combining words into grammatical sentences.
  • Semantics: meaning of words and sentences.
  • Pragmatics: use of language in social context (tone, turn-taking, implied meaning).

9.1 How is language acquired? The great debate

Who → what: Noam Chomsky → nativist / innate view; B.F. Skinner → learning / behaviourist view.

B.F. Skinner (in Verbal Behavior, 1957) argued that language is learned like any behaviour, through imitation, reinforcement and shaping. Noam Chomsky attacked this, arguing that children produce novel sentences they were never taught and learn grammar too fast for reinforcement alone. He proposed:

  • Language acquisition device (LAD): an innate brain mechanism for grasping grammar.
  • Universal grammar: a common grammatical blueprint underlying all human languages.
  • Transformational grammar: deep structure (meaning) transformed into surface structure (spoken form).

9.2 Linguistic relativity

Benjamin Lee Whorf and Edward Sapir proposed the linguistic relativity hypothesis (the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis): the language we speak shapes how we think and perceive the world. The strong form (language determines thought) is largely rejected; the weak form (language influences thought) has some support.

9.3 Stages of language acquisition

  1. Cooing (around 2 months): vowel-like sounds.
  2. Babbling (around 4 to 6 months): consonant-vowel repetitions, initially universal.
  3. One-word / holophrastic (around 12 months): single words carry whole meanings.
  4. Two-word / telegraphic (around 18 to 24 months): "want milk", grammar words dropped.
  5. Sentences (from about 2.5 years): rapid grammar growth, including overregularisation ("goed").

10. Consciousness & Cognitive Psychology as a Field

Consciousness is our awareness of ourselves and our environment. Psychologists describe varied states of consciousness: normal waking awareness, sleep and dreaming, hypnosis, meditation, and drug-altered states.

10.1 The cognitive revolution

From roughly the 1950s, psychology turned away from strict behaviourism and back to the study of internal mental processes. This cognitive revolution borrowed the computer as a metaphor and gave rise to the information-processing model: the mind takes in information (input), encodes and transforms it (processing / storage), and produces behaviour (output), much like a computer.

10.2 Cognitive psychology as a school

Who → what: Ulric Neisser → "father of cognitive psychology"; his 1967 book Cognitive Psychology named and organised the field.

Cognitive psychology studies how people perceive, attend, remember, think, reason and use language, treating these as information-processing operations that can be modelled and tested experimentally.

Key. Sensation transduces energy; perception interprets it. Weber = constant fraction, Fechner = log, Stevens = power. Kahneman and Tversky = heuristics and biases. Chomsky (innate LAD, universal grammar) vs Skinner (learned verbal behaviour). Neisser = father of cognitive psychology; the information-processing model is its master metaphor.
Recap: This block bridged the raw senses (psychophysics, thresholds, three laws, signal detection) to higher cognition (concepts and prototypes, reasoning, problem-solving blocks, judgement heuristics, creativity), then to language (five levels, the Chomsky-Skinner debate, Sapir-Whorf, acquisition stages) and finally to consciousness and the cognitive revolution that made cognitive psychology a school.

11. Memory Architecture: How Information Is Stored

Memory is not a single box in the head; it is a set of interacting systems that differ in capacity, duration, and coding. The modern account grew out of a stage model and was later refined into a workspace model. Learn both, because RML has repeatedly asked candidates to name the store, its capacity, and the psychologist who proposed it.

From scratch: Three verbs organise all of memory. Encoding is turning an experience into a memory trace. Storage is holding that trace over time. Retrieval is pulling it back out. A memory failure can happen at any of the three, and good exam answers say which stage failed.

11.1 The Atkinson–Shiffrin multi-store model

Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin (1968) proposed that information flows through three separate stores in sequence. First is the sensory register, which holds a near-exact copy of sensory input for a fraction of a second (iconic memory for vision, about 250 milliseconds, demonstrated by George Sperling; echoic memory for sound, about 2 to 4 seconds). If attention is paid, information passes to short-term memory (STM), which holds roughly 7 plus or minus 2 items (George Miller's magic number) for about 15 to 30 seconds unless rehearsed. Maintenance rehearsal keeps items in STM; elaborative rehearsal transfers them to long-term memory (LTM), which is effectively unlimited in capacity and duration.

Who → what: Atkinson & Shiffrin → the "modal" multi-store model (sensory register, STM, LTM as separate stores linked by attention and rehearsal).
Trap: The magic number "7 plus or minus 2" is George Miller (1956), not Atkinson or Shiffrin. Later work by Nelson Cowan revised the true capacity of the focus of attention down to about 4 chunks. Do not attribute the number to Miller's students.

11.2 Baddeley and Hitch: the working memory model

Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch (1974) argued that STM is not a single passive store but an active working memory with separate components. The central executive is an attention-directing controller that has no storage of its own; it allocates resources to two slave systems. The phonological loop holds speech-based information and has two parts: a phonological store (the "inner ear") and an articulatory rehearsal process (the "inner voice"). The visuospatial sketchpad (the "inner eye") holds visual and spatial information. In 2000 Baddeley added a fourth component, the episodic buffer, a limited store that binds information from the loop, the sketchpad, and LTM into single coherent episodes.

ComponentHandlesEveryday sign
Central executiveAttention control, coordinationDeciding what to focus on
Phonological loopVerbal / acoustic materialRepeating a phone number under your breath
Visuospatial sketchpadImages, layout, locationPicturing the route to a shop
Episodic bufferBinding, linking to LTMCombining a face and a name into one memory
RML tilt: A favourite item asks which component was added later. Answer: the episodic buffer (Baddeley, 2000). Another asks which component has no storage capacity: the central executive.

11.3 Levels of processing

Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart (1972) rejected the store metaphor. They argued that memory strength depends not on where information sits but on how deeply it is processed. Shallow, structural processing (how a word looks) produces weak traces; phonemic processing (how it sounds) is intermediate; deep, semantic processing (what it means) produces the strongest, most durable traces. This is why relating new material to your own life (self-reference effect) aids recall.

Example: asked "Is DOG in capitals?" you barely remember the word later; asked "Does DOG fit the sentence 'The ___ chased the cat?'" you remember it well, because you processed its meaning.

11.4 Types of long-term memory

Endel Tulving (1972) split LTM into episodic memory (personally experienced events tied to a time and place, "what I ate yesterday") and semantic memory (general facts and concepts detached from any learning episode, "Paris is the capital of France"). Both are declarative (explicit, consciously recalled and stated). Against these stands procedural memory, a non-declarative (implicit) store of skills and habits such as cycling or typing, which is expressed through performance rather than conscious recall.

Rule: Declarative = "knowing that" (episodic + semantic), can be verbalised. Procedural = "knowing how", shown by doing. Amnesic patients often lose declarative memory while procedural learning stays intact.

11.5 The serial position effect

When people recall a list, they remember the first items well (the primacy effect, because early items got the most rehearsal and reached LTM) and the last items well (the recency effect, because they are still in STM). Middle items are recalled worst. Delaying recall wipes out recency but leaves primacy, which is strong evidence for separate short-term and long-term stores.

11.6 Why we forget

Forgetting is not one process. Several mechanisms have been proposed and each has a named champion.

  • Decay theory: traces fade with the mere passage of time if unused (a very old idea, strongest for sensory and short-term memory).
  • Interference theory: other memories block the target. Proactive interference is old learning disrupting new (your old phone number intrudes on the new one). Retroactive interference is new learning disrupting old (a new number makes you forget the old one).
  • Cue-dependent forgetting: Endel Tulving argued the trace is intact but the retrieval cue is missing (the encoding specificity principle: recall is best when retrieval cues match encoding conditions). This covers context-dependent and state-dependent memory.
  • Motivated forgetting: Sigmund Freud's repression, the active pushing of threatening memories out of awareness.
Trap: Proactive = old harms new (moving forward in time it interferes); retroactive = new harms old (acting backward). Students routinely swap these. Anchor on "pro = prior".

11.7 Ebbinghaus and the forgetting curve

Hermann Ebbinghaus (1885), using himself and nonsense syllables, plotted the first forgetting curve: retention drops sharply within the first hour and the first day, then levels off. He also described the spacing effect (distributed practice beats massed cramming) and savings (relearning is faster than first learning).

11.8 Reconstructive memory and schemas

Frederic Bartlett (1932), using his story "The War of the Ghosts", showed that memory is not a photocopy but an active reconstruction. Participants distorted the unfamiliar story to fit their own cultural expectations, a process he explained through schemas (organised packets of prior knowledge). We remember the gist and rebuild the details, often wrongly.

11.9 Loftus and the misinformation effect

Elizabeth Loftus demonstrated that memory is malleable and vulnerable to post-event information. In the classic study, the verb used to describe a filmed car crash changed speed estimates: cars that "smashed" were rated faster than cars that "hit", and a week later "smashed" witnesses falsely recalled broken glass. This misinformation effect is the empirical backbone of doubts about eyewitness testimony.

RML tilt: Link Loftus to the courtroom: misleading questions can implant false details, so eyewitness confidence is a poor guide to accuracy. Contrast this with a flashbulb memory, a vivid, confidently held snapshot of the moment one heard shocking news (Roger Brown & James Kulik, 1977); such memories feel accurate but are also prone to error.

11.10 Amnesia

Anterograde amnesia is the inability to form new long-term memories after brain injury (the famous patient H.M., after hippocampal removal, could not lay down new declarative memories yet still learned new motor skills). Retrograde amnesia is the loss of memories formed before the injury. The dissociation in H.M. is the strongest single case for separating declarative and procedural memory.

Memory recap: sensory register → STM (Miller's 7±2) → LTM, via attention and rehearsal (Atkinson & Shiffrin). STM is really working memory with four parts (Baddeley & Hitch). Depth beats duration for durability (Craik & Lockhart). LTM splits into episodic/semantic/procedural (Tulving). Forgetting = decay, interference, missing cues, or repression. Memory is reconstructive (Bartlett) and suggestible (Loftus).

12. Models of Attention

Attention is the cognitive process of selecting some inputs for deeper processing while filtering others. The history of attention research is essentially a debate over where in the processing stream the filter sits: early (before meaning) or late (after meaning).

12.1 Early-selection: Broadbent's filter

Donald Broadbent (1958) proposed the first information-processing model of attention. Inputs enter a sensory buffer, then a selective filter passes only one channel (selected by physical features such as which ear or which voice) to a limited-capacity system; the rest are blocked early, before meaning is extracted. This explains the cocktail-party finding that people notice little from an unattended channel.

12.2 Attenuation: Treisman

Anne Treisman (1964) softened the filter. Instead of blocking the unattended channel completely, the filter attenuates it (turns its volume down). Important words with low thresholds, such as your own name, can still break through. This explains why we hear our name spoken across a noisy room.

12.3 Late-selection: Deutsch and Norman

J. Anthony Deutsch and Diana Deutsch (1963), later refined by Donald Norman, argued that all inputs are processed for meaning and the selection happens late, at the point of response or memory, based on importance. On this view nothing is filtered out early; the bottleneck is at the exit, not the entrance.

12.4 Capacity model: Kahneman

Daniel Kahneman (1973) reframed attention as a pool of limited mental effort or capacity that can be flexibly allocated across tasks rather than a fixed structural filter. Arousal raises available capacity; task difficulty and enduring dispositions govern how it is spent. This model handles divided attention (two easy tasks can share the pool, two hard ones cannot).

TypeMeaning
Selective attentionFocusing on one source, ignoring others
Divided attentionSplitting resources across tasks (multitasking)
Sustained attentionMaintaining focus over long periods (vigilance)
Rule: Automatic processing is fast, effortless, parallel, and needs no attention (reading a familiar word). Controlled processing is slow, effortful, serial, and capacity-limited (reading in a new language). The classic evidence is the Stroop effect: automatic word reading interferes with controlled colour naming.

13. Perception: Organising the Sensory World

From scratch: Sensation is the raw detection of a stimulus by a sense organ. Perception is the brain's interpretation of that raw data into a meaningful experience. The same light can be sensed identically yet perceived differently, which is why illusions exist.

13.1 Gestalt laws of grouping

The Gestalt psychologists (Max Wertheimer, Kurt Koffka, Wolfgang Köhler) argued that "the whole is different from the sum of its parts" and that the mind organises sensory elements by innate laws. Key laws: proximity (near items group together), similarity (alike items group), closure (we complete incomplete figures), continuity or good continuation (we follow smooth paths), and figure–ground (we split a scene into an object and its background).

13.2 Depth perception

We build a three-dimensional world from a two-dimensional retinal image using depth cues. Binocular cues need two eyes: retinal disparity (the two eyes get slightly different images, and the brain reads the difference as depth) and convergence (the inward turn of the eyes for near objects). Monocular cues work with one eye: relative size, interposition (overlap), linear perspective, texture gradient, aerial perspective, light and shadow, and motion parallax.

13.3 Perceptual constancies

Constancy is perceiving an object as stable despite changing sensory input. Size constancy (a car looks the same size as it drives away, though its retinal image shrinks), shape constancy (a door stays rectangular as it swings open), and colour constancy (a red apple looks red under different lighting).

13.4 Illusions

Illusions reveal the rules the brain uses. In the Müller-Lyer illusion, a line with outward "arrowhead" fins looks shorter than an equal line with inward "feather" fins, often explained by misapplied size constancy learned from carpentered rooms. In the Ponzo illusion, two equal lines placed over converging railway tracks look unequal because the "farther" line is scaled up by linear-perspective depth cues.

13.5 Bottom-up versus top-down, and the two great theorists

Bottom-up processing builds perception directly from the sensory data upward. Top-down processing uses prior knowledge, context, and expectations to interpret data. James Gibson championed direct (ecological) perception: the optic array is so rich that perception is bottom-up and needs no inference; we directly pick up affordances (what an object offers for action). Richard Gregory championed the constructivist view: perception is top-down, an active hypothesis or "best guess" that the brain constructs from incomplete data, which is why illusions fool us.

RML tilt: Pair the names cleanly. Gibson = direct, data-rich, bottom-up, affordances. Gregory = constructivist, inference, top-down, perception as hypothesis. Items often give a one-line definition and ask for the theorist.

14. Theories of Emotion

From scratch: An emotion has three ingredients: a physiological arousal (racing heart), a behavioural expression (running), and a subjective feeling (fear). The theories differ on the order in which these occur and which one causes which.

14.1 The classic sequence debate

William James and Carl Lange (independently, the James–Lange theory) held that the bodily reaction comes first and the emotion is our perception of it: we are afraid because we tremble, we do not tremble because we are afraid. Walter Cannon and Philip Bard (the Cannon–Bard theory) objected that the body's responses are too slow and too similar across emotions; they proposed that the arousal and the felt emotion occur simultaneously and independently, both triggered by the thalamus.

14.2 Two-factor theory

Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer (1962) proposed the two-factor theory: emotion equals physiological arousal plus a cognitive label. Arousal is general and undifferentiated; we scan the situation to decide what to call it. Their adrenaline study showed that identically aroused participants reported euphoria or anger depending on the confederate's behaviour. This is the origin of misattribution of arousal.

14.3 Appraisal and the cognition-versus-affect debate

Richard Lazarus argued that cognitive appraisal of an event (is this a threat or a challenge?) is necessary and comes before emotion; no appraisal, no emotion. Robert Zajonc disagreed, arguing that affect can precede cognition ("preferences need no inferences"), shown by the mere-exposure effect where liking rises for stimuli seen too briefly to recognise. Joseph LeDoux supplied the neuroscience: a fast, subcortical "low road" from thalamus to amygdala can trigger fear before the slower cortical "high road" identifies the stimulus.

14.4 Basic emotions and facial feedback

Paul Ekman's cross-cultural studies identified a small set of basic emotions with universal facial expressions: happiness, sadness, anger, fear, disgust, and surprise. The facial-feedback hypothesis holds that our facial muscles feed back to intensify or even generate emotion, so forcing a smile can lift mood.

TheoryCore claim (order of events)
James–LangeBody reacts first, feeling follows
Cannon–BardArousal and feeling occur at once
Schachter–SingerArousal plus cognitive label
LazarusAppraisal (cognition) comes first
ZajoncAffect can precede cognition

15. Theories of Motivation

15.1 Drive-reduction and arousal

Clark Hull's drive-reduction theory says a physiological need creates an aroused drive state that motivates behaviour to restore homeostasis (balance); reducing the drive is reinforcing. But drive reduction cannot explain why we seek stimulation (roller coasters, curiosity). Arousal theory fills the gap: we act to keep arousal at an optimal level. The Yerkes–Dodson law (Robert Yerkes & John Dodson) states that performance is best at a moderate level of arousal and falls off when arousal is too low or too high, and that the optimal level is lower for difficult tasks than for easy ones.

15.2 Maslow's hierarchy of needs

Abraham Maslow arranged needs in a pyramid that must be climbed from the bottom up: physiological (food, water) → safety → love and belonging → esteem → self-actualisation (realising one's full potential). The lower four are deficiency needs; self-actualisation is a growth need. Maslow later added transcendence at the top.

15.3 McClelland's needs

David McClelland proposed three learned social motives measured by the Thematic Apperception Test: the need for achievement (nAch), the drive to master challenging goals; the need for affiliation (nAff), the drive for warm relationships; and the need for power (nPow), the drive to influence others. High-nAch people prefer moderately difficult, feedback-rich tasks.

15.4 Self-determination theory

Edward Deci and Richard Ryan's self-determination theory holds that humans have three innate psychological needs: autonomy (feeling in control of one's actions), competence (feeling effective), and relatedness (feeling connected). It distinguishes intrinsic motivation (doing something for its own reward) from extrinsic motivation (doing it for an outside reward). A key finding is the overjustification effect: paying people for an already-enjoyed activity can undermine intrinsic interest.

Rule: Incentive theory says behaviour is pulled by external goals (a "carrot"), whereas drive theory says it is pushed by internal needs. Expectancy-value approaches add that effort depends on how likely success seems multiplied by how much the outcome is valued.

16. Theories of Intelligence

16.1 Factor theories

Charles Spearman, using factor analysis, found that all mental tests correlate positively and proposed a general intelligence factor, "g", plus test-specific factors ("s"); this is his two-factor theory. Louis Thurstone disagreed with a single g and identified seven relatively independent primary mental abilities (verbal comprehension, word fluency, number, spatial, memory, perceptual speed, reasoning). Raymond Cattell split g into fluid intelligence (Gf), the ability to reason with novel problems (peaks early, declines with age), and crystallised intelligence (Gc), accumulated knowledge and skills (holds or rises with age).

16.2 Structure and multiple models

J.P. Guilford's Structure of Intellect model rejected g entirely and proposed up to 150 distinct abilities arranged on three dimensions: operations (what you do), contents (what you do it to), and products (the result). He is also known for distinguishing convergent from divergent thinking. Howard Gardner's theory of multiple intelligences proposed several relatively independent intelligences (linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily-kinaesthetic, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic). Robert Sternberg's triarchic theory named three: analytical (problem-solving), creative (novel ideas), and practical ("street smarts", adapting to real contexts).

16.3 The PASS model and emotional intelligence

The PASS model of J.P. Das and Jack Naglieri, built on Alexander Luria's brain theory, describes intelligence as four cognitive processes: Planning, Attention, and Simultaneous and Successive processing. Emotional intelligence was formally defined by Peter Salovey and John Mayer as the ability to perceive, understand, use, and regulate emotions, and popularised by Daniel Goleman.

RML tilt: The PASS model (Das & Naglieri) and its Luria roots are a favourite of Indian entrance papers. Also remember the Flynn effect (James Flynn): average IQ scores have risen steadily across the twentieth century, roughly 3 points per decade, forcing periodic re-standardisation of tests.
TheoristContribution
Charles Spearmang and two-factor theory
Louis ThurstoneSeven primary mental abilities
Raymond CattellFluid vs crystallised intelligence
J.P. GuilfordStructure of Intellect (150 abilities)
Howard GardnerMultiple intelligences
Robert SternbergTriarchic (analytic, creative, practical)
Das & NaglieriPASS model
Salovey & MayerEmotional intelligence

17. Learning: Deeper Mechanisms

The core paradigms (classical conditioning, operant conditioning, observational learning) already appear earlier in this book. This section adds the finer mechanisms that exam papers test but the introduction skips.

17.1 Schedules of reinforcement

B.F. Skinner showed that when reinforcement is delivered shapes behaviour as much as whether it is. Continuous reinforcement (reward every response) produces fast learning but fast extinction. Partial (intermittent) schedules produce slower extinction. There are four:

ScheduleReward afterResponse pattern
Fixed ratio (FR)A set number of responsesHigh rate, brief post-reward pause
Variable ratio (VR)An unpredictable number of responsesVery high, steady, extinction-resistant (gambling)
Fixed interval (FI)First response after a set timeScalloped: speeds up near the deadline
Variable interval (VI)First response after an unpredictable timeSlow, steady, stable
Trap: Ratio schedules (based on number of responses) always produce higher response rates than interval schedules (based on time). Variable ratio is the most resistant to extinction, which is why slot machines use it.

17.2 Shaping, generalisation, and discrimination

Shaping is reinforcing successive approximations toward a target behaviour, used to build complex acts that would never occur spontaneously. Stimulus generalisation is responding to stimuli similar to the trained one (Little Albert feared many furry things). Discrimination is learning to respond only to a specific stimulus and not to similar ones, trained by reinforcing one and not the other.

17.3 Cognitive challenges to strict behaviourism

Several findings showed that learning involves internal representation, not just stimulus-response bonds. Edward Tolman demonstrated latent learning: rats that explored a maze without reward still learned its layout (a cognitive map) and used it the moment reward appeared, proving learning can occur without reinforcement and stay hidden until needed. Wolfgang Köhler, studying chimpanzees, described insight learning: a sudden reorganisation of the problem (the "aha" moment) rather than gradual trial and error.

17.4 Biological constraints on learning

John Garcia discovered taste aversion (the "Garcia effect"): rats learned to avoid a flavour paired with nausea even when the illness came hours later, and only with taste, not with lights or sounds. This violated two rules of classical conditioning (contiguity and equipotentiality) and proved that organisms are biologically prepared to associate certain stimuli. Martin Seligman named this preparedness and also demonstrated learned helplessness: dogs given inescapable shocks later failed to escape even when they could, a model that became foundational for understanding depression.

RML tilt: Learned helplessness (Seligman) and its reformulation into attributional style is a bridge item between the learning and clinical sections; expect it to reappear under depression. Taste aversion (Garcia) is the standard example that biology limits what can be conditioned.
Learning recap: reinforcement timing matters (four schedules; VR is stickiest). Complex behaviour is built by shaping. Learning generalises and can be discriminated. Tolman (latent learning, cognitive maps) and Köhler (insight) show learning is cognitive, not just S-R. Garcia (taste aversion) and Seligman (preparedness, learned helplessness) show biology constrains it.

3 Biopsychology & Physiological Psychology

From scratch: Biopsychology asks how the brain and body produce behaviour and mental life. Every thought, mood, and symptom you will study in clinical work has a physical substrate: cells that fire, chemicals that cross gaps, and structures that specialise. This section builds that substrate from the single cell upward.

3.1 The Neuron & the Synapse

The neuron is the basic signalling cell of the nervous system. Santiago Ramón y Cajal established the neuron doctrine: the nervous system is made of discrete individual cells (not a continuous net), for which he shared the 1906 Nobel Prize with Camillo Golgi.

Parts of a neuron. Dendrites are branching fibres that receive incoming signals. The soma (cell body) contains the nucleus and integrates inputs. The axon carries the signal away toward other cells. The myelin sheath is a fatty insulating layer (made by Schwann cells in the periphery, oligodendrocytes centrally) with gaps called nodes of Ranvier; it speeds conduction via saltatory conduction. The axon terminals (terminal buttons) release chemical messengers.

Resting potential. At rest the inside of the axon is about −70 mV relative to outside, kept negative by the sodium-potassium pump and selective permeability. This is polarisation.

Action potential. When input pushes the membrane past its threshold (about −55 mV), voltage-gated sodium (Na+) channels open and Na+ floods in: depolarisation. Then K+ exits, causing repolarisation and a brief hyperpolarisation. Key properties: it is all-or-none (a spike either fires fully or not at all, so intensity is coded by rate, not size); during the absolute refractory period no new spike can fire, and during the relative refractory period only a stronger-than-usual stimulus can.

Synaptic transmission. The spike reaches the terminal, triggering calcium influx, which makes vesicles fuse and release neurotransmitter into the synaptic cleft. The transmitter binds receptors on the next (postsynaptic) neuron. It is then cleared by reuptake, enzymatic breakdown, or diffusion. An EPSP (excitatory postsynaptic potential) depolarises and makes firing more likely; an IPSP (inhibitory postsynaptic potential) hyperpolarises and makes it less likely. The neuron sums all EPSPs and IPSPs (spatial and temporal summation).

Key. Resting −70 mV → threshold −55 mV → Na+ in (depolarise) → K+ out (repolarise). All-or-none; strength is coded by frequency.

3.2 Neurotransmitters

TransmitterFunctionClinical link
Acetylcholine (ACh)Muscle movement, learning, memoryDepleted in Alzheimer's disease
DopamineReward, motivation, movementExcess/overactivity in schizophrenia; loss in Parkinson's disease
Serotonin (5-HT)Mood, sleep, appetiteLow levels linked to depression and anxiety
NorepinephrineArousal, alertness, fight/flightImplicated in depression and stress
GABAMain inhibitory transmitterLow activity in anxiety and seizures
GlutamateMain excitatory transmitter; learningExcess causes excitotoxicity; NMDA role in schizophrenia
EndorphinsPain relief, pleasureInvolved in addiction and analgesia

GABA = gamma-aminobutyric acid. The dopamine hypothesis of schizophrenia holds that overactivity of dopamine (especially at D2 receptors in mesolimbic pathways) produces the positive symptoms: hallucinations, delusions, disordered thought. Evidence: antipsychotics block dopamine and reduce these symptoms, while amphetamines (which raise dopamine) can induce psychosis.

Trap: The dopamine hypothesis explains positive symptoms best. Negative symptoms (flat affect, avolition) are less well accounted for and are linked to glutamate and prefrontal underactivity.

3.3 Brain Anatomy

Hindbrain. The medulla controls vital autonomic functions (heartbeat, breathing). The pons bridges the halves and relays signals, and helps regulate sleep. The cerebellum governs balance, coordination, and motor timing.

Midbrain. Contains relay centres for vision and hearing and dopamine pathways; with parts of the hindbrain it forms the reticular activating system for arousal and attention.

Forebrain. The largest region, housing the thalamus, hypothalamus, limbic system, and the cerebral cortex. The cortex is divided into four lobes:

  • Frontal lobe, voluntary movement, planning, and personality. The prefrontal cortex handles executive function, judgment, and impulse control. Broca's area (usually left frontal) controls speech production; damage causes non-fluent Broca's aphasia.
  • Parietal lobe, the somatosensory cortex processes touch, temperature, and body position.
  • Temporal lobe, hearing and language comprehension. Wernicke's area (usually left) handles language understanding; damage gives fluent-but-meaningless Wernicke's aphasia. The hippocampus (deep temporal) forms new long-term memories.
  • Occipital lobe, visual processing.

Limbic system, the emotion-and-memory network: the amygdala (fear, aggression, emotional salience), the hippocampus (memory formation), the hypothalamus (drives, hormones, homeostasis), and the thalamus (the sensory relay station, routing all senses except smell to the cortex).

Lateralization. The left hemisphere is typically dominant for language, logic, and analysis; the right hemisphere for spatial ability, faces, and holistic processing. The hemispheres communicate through the corpus callosum (studied via Roger Sperry's split-brain work).

RML tilt: The Phineas Gage case (1848) is a near-certain factual item. A railroad tamping iron destroyed his frontal lobe; his intellect survived but his personality and impulse control changed drastically ("no longer Gage"). It became early evidence that the frontal lobe governs personality and social judgment.

3.4 Neuroendocrine System

Hormones are chemical messengers released by glands into the blood. The pituitary ("master gland") is directed by the hypothalamus; the adrenal glands release stress hormones; the thyroid sets metabolism; the pancreas regulates blood sugar; the gonads produce sex hormones.

The HPA axis (HPA = hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal) is the core stress system. Under stress the hypothalamus releases CRH → the pituitary releases ACTH → the adrenal cortex releases cortisol. Cortisol mobilises energy and suppresses non-essential functions. It also gives negative feedback to shut the axis off. Chronic HPA activation and high cortisol are linked to depression, anxiety, and PTSD (post-traumatic stress disorder).

3.5 Autonomic Nervous System

The autonomic nervous system (ANS) controls involuntary organs and has two opposing branches.

SympatheticParasympathetic
Fight or flightRest and digest
Heart rate up, pupils dilate, digestion halts, adrenaline releasedHeart rate down, pupils constrict, digestion resumes
Key. Sympathetic = arousal/expend energy; parasympathetic = calming/conserve energy. They restore homeostasis between them.

3.6 Sleep

Sleep cycles through stages roughly every 90 minutes, tracked by the EEG (electroencephalogram).

StageEEG waveFeature
Awake, alertBetaFast, low amplitude
Relaxed, eyes closedAlphaCalm wakefulness
N1 (light)ThetaDrifting off; hypnic jerks
N2Theta + sleep spindles, K-complexesTrue sleep onset
N3 (deep/slow-wave)DeltaRestorative; hardest to wake
REMBeta-like (paradoxical)Dreaming; rapid eye movement; muscle atonia

REM = rapid eye movement. It is called paradoxical sleep because the brain looks awake while the body is paralysed. The circadian rhythm is the ~24-hour body clock set by the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the hypothalamus and cued by light, driving melatonin (from the pineal gland) release.

Trap: Delta waves mark deep (N3) sleep, not dreaming. Vivid dreaming happens in REM, whose EEG looks fast like waking.

3.7 Psychopharmacology Basics

  • Antipsychotics, dopamine antagonists (block D2 receptors), reducing positive symptoms of schizophrenia. Typicals like haloperidol; atypicals like risperidone (also block serotonin).
  • Antidepressants, SSRIs (SSRI = selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor) block serotonin reuptake to raise its level (fluoxetine); TCAs (tricyclic antidepressants) raise serotonin and norepinephrine but have more side effects; MAOIs (monoamine oxidase inhibitors) block the enzyme that breaks these transmitters down (need dietary tyramine caution).
  • Anxiolytics, benzodiazepines enhance GABA (the main inhibitory transmitter), calming the nervous system (diazepam, lorazepam).
  • Mood stabilisers, lithium is first-line for bipolar disorder, needing blood-level monitoring for a narrow therapeutic window.
RML tilt: Match drug to mechanism to transmitter: antipsychotic→dopamine block, SSRI→serotonin, benzodiazepine→GABA, lithium→bipolar. These one-line pairings are frequent MCQ fodder.
Recap: Neurons fire all-or-none action potentials (−70→−55 mV, Na+ in) and talk chemically across synapses via EPSPs/IPSPs. Cajal gave the neuron doctrine. Key transmitters: dopamine (schizophrenia positive symptoms), serotonin/norepinephrine (mood), GABA (inhibitory/anxiety), glutamate (excitatory), ACh (memory), endorphins (pain). Brain builds from hindbrain (medulla, pons, cerebellum) to forebrain and four lobes (frontal/Broca's, parietal/somatosensory, temporal/Wernicke's + hippocampus, occipital); limbic system runs emotion and memory; Gage showed the frontal lobe shapes personality. The HPA axis pumps cortisol under stress; the ANS balances sympathetic fight-or-flight against parasympathetic rest-and-digest. Sleep runs N1–N3 (theta→delta) plus REM, on a circadian clock. Drugs map to transmitters: antipsychotics→dopamine, SSRIs→serotonin, benzodiazepines→GABA, lithium→bipolar.

3.8 Methods of Studying the Brain

How do we know what a brain region does when we cannot open a living person's skull and watch? Physiological psychology answers this by pairing older lesion logic with modern imaging. The methods split into two families: those that measure activity (recording) and those that disrupt or destroy tissue to see what is lost.

From scratch: Two opposite questions drive brain science. "What happens if this part stops working?" is answered by lesions and stimulation. "What lights up while a person does a task?" is answered by EEG, PET and fMRI. Structure questions ("what does it look like?") are answered by CT and MRI.

Lesion and ablation studies

A lesion is damaged tissue; ablation is the deliberate surgical removal or destruction of a region (in animals, or observed in human injury). The reasoning: if removing area X abolishes behaviour Y, area X is implicated in Y. This is the oldest tool (recall Paul Broca's patient "Tan" and speech production). Limits: damage is rarely clean, and loss of a function does not prove the region alone produced it.

Electrical recording and stimulation

  • Electroencephalography (EEG): scalp electrodes record summed electrical activity of large neuron populations. Excellent time resolution (milliseconds), poor spatial resolution (cannot localise deep sources). Reads sleep stages, seizures, wakefulness rhythms (alpha, beta, delta, theta).
  • Event-related potentials (ERP): EEG signals averaged across many repeats of a stimulus, cancelling background noise to reveal the response locked to that event (for example the P300 wave to a surprising target).
  • Single-cell / microelectrode recording: a fine electrode records firing of one neuron. Highest precision, but invasive (mostly animals). Gave us David Hubel and Torsten Wiesel's feature-detector cells in the visual cortex.
  • Transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS): a magnetic coil over the scalp induces current that briefly excites or silences a cortical patch, creating a temporary "virtual lesion" in a healthy person. Establishes that a region is causally necessary, not just correlated.

Structural and functional imaging

MethodWhat it showsTrade-off
CT (computed tomography)X-ray slices → brain structure, gross damage, bleedsStructure only, uses radiation, low detail
MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)Detailed structural images via magnetic fields, no radiationStructure only; expensive, loud, no metal
fMRI (functional MRI)BOLD signal → blood-oxygen changes = activity, good spatial detailSlow (seconds); measures blood flow, not firing directly
PET (positron emission)Radioactive tracer → metabolism, blood flow, neurotransmitter mapsInjects radiation, poor time resolution, costly
Key. EEG and ERP win on time (milliseconds); fMRI and PET win on space (localisation). No single method is best at both.
Trap: fMRI does not measure neural firing directly; it measures the haemodynamic (blood-oxygen, BOLD) response that lags activity by a few seconds. CT shows structure, not function.

3.9 Behaviour Genetics

Behaviour genetics asks how much of a trait's variation is inherited. Start with the physical units. DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) is the double-helix molecule coiled into chromosomes (humans have 23 pairs, 46 total). A gene is a segment of DNA coding for a protein. The 23rd pair are the sex chromosomes: XX female, XY male.

  • Genotype: the actual genetic code a person carries.
  • Phenotype: the observable trait that results (genotype plus environment).
  • Dominant allele: expressed even with one copy. Recessive: expressed only with two copies.
  • Heritability: the proportion of variation in a trait, within a population, attributable to genetic differences. It is a group statistic, not a personal percentage.

Twin and adoption studies

Monozygotic (identical) twins share ~100% of genes; dizygotic (fraternal) twins share ~50%. If identical twins resemble each other more than fraternal twins on a trait, genes are implicated. Adoption studies separate nature from nurture: an adopted child resembling biological parents points to genes, resembling adoptive parents points to environment.

Trap: High heritability does not mean "unchangeable." Height is highly heritable yet responds to nutrition. Heritability describes variation in a population, never the fate of one individual.

Clinically important genetic and chromosomal conditions

ConditionCauseFeature
Down syndromeTrisomy 21 (extra chromosome 21)Intellectual disability, characteristic features
Fragile XMutation on X chromosome (FMR1)Commonest inherited intellectual disability
Phenylketonuria (PKU)Recessive; cannot metabolise phenylalaninePreventable retardation via diet; screened at birth
Turner syndromeSingle X (45, X0) in femalesShort stature, no ovarian development
Klinefelter syndromeExtra X in males (47, XXY)Reduced fertility, tall stature
Huntington's diseaseDominant gene on chromosome 4Late-onset motor and cognitive decline
RML tilt: PKU is a favourite because it proves a genetic disorder can be environmentally managed (a phenylalanine-free diet prevents the disability). Note Down syndrome = trisomy 21 and Huntington's = dominant.

3.10 Hemispheric Lateralization and the Split Brain

The two cerebral hemispheres are joined by the corpus callosum, a thick band of fibres carrying information between them. Each hemisphere controls the opposite side of the body: this is contralateral control (the left hemisphere moves the right hand, and vice versa).

Who → what: Roger Sperry (Nobel Prize, 1981) and his student Michael Gazzaniga studied "split-brain" patients whose corpus callosum was surgically cut to control severe epilepsy. With the bridge severed, the two hemispheres could no longer share information, revealing what each does alone.

Findings: an object shown only to the left visual field (right hemisphere) could be picked out by the left hand but could not be named, because speech is usually a left-hemisphere function. This demonstrated lateralization: functions are not evenly spread across both sides.

Left hemisphere (typical)Right hemisphere (typical)
Language, speech, readingSpatial ability, faces
Logic, analysis, calculationMusic, emotion, holistic patterns
Trap: The "left-brained vs right-brained personality" idea is pop-psych myth. Lateralization means relative specialisation, not that a person uses only one hemisphere.

3.11 Physiology of Motivation and Biological Rhythms

Hypothalamic control of hunger

The hypothalamus regulates eating through two centres:

  • Lateral hypothalamus (LH): the "start eating" centre. Stimulation induces eating; damage causes an animal to stop eating (aphagia).
  • Ventromedial hypothalamus (VMH): the "stop eating" (satiety) centre. Damage causes overeating and obesity (hyperphagia).
Key. LH = Lights on (start). VMH = Very much full (stop). Lesioning them produces the opposite of their normal job.

Thirst and sexual behaviour

The hypothalamus also monitors thirst, triggered by cellular dehydration (osmotic) and by drops in blood volume. Sexual behaviour is driven by hormones: androgens such as testosterone and oestrogens, released under hypothalamic and pituitary control. Motivation here is both hormonal (internal drive) and neural (brain circuits).

Pain: gate-control theory

Who → what: Ronald Melzack and Patrick Wall (1965) proposed the gate-control theory: a neural "gate" in the spinal cord can open or close to let pain signals reach the brain. Rubbing an injury or psychological factors can "close the gate," explaining why pain is not a simple one-to-one readout of tissue damage.

Biological rhythms and the circadian clock

Circadian rhythms are roughly 24-hour cycles (sleep, temperature, hormones). The master clock is the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) in the hypothalamus, which reads light through the eyes. The SCN signals the pineal gland to release melatonin in darkness, promoting sleep.

RML tilt: Remember the chain: light → SCN → pineal gland → melatonin. The SCN is the pacemaker; the pineal gland only secretes the hormone.

3.12 Stress Physiology

When a stressor is perceived, the body mounts a coordinated response. Two systems fire. The fast one is the sympathetic nervous system (adrenaline, "fight or flight"). The slower, sustained one is the HPA axis.

The HPA axis

  • Hypothalamus releases CRH (corticotropin-releasing hormone).
  • CRH signals the Pituitary to release ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone).
  • ACTH signals the Adrenal cortex to release cortisol.

Cortisol mobilises energy and dampens non-essential functions. Chronically high cortisol harms immunity, memory (hippocampus) and mood, linking stress to illness.

General Adaptation Syndrome

Who → what: Hans Selye described the General Adaptation Syndrome (GAS), the body's stereotyped physiological response to any prolonged stressor, in three stages.
  • Alarm: initial shock, sympathetic activation, resources mobilised.
  • Resistance: the body adapts and copes; cortisol stays elevated to sustain the effort.
  • Exhaustion: resources deplete, resistance collapses, and illness or breakdown can follow.
Key. GAS order is Alarm → Resistance → Exhaustion. Selye called stress "the non-specific response of the body to any demand."
Recap: Brain methods trade time against space (EEG fast, fMRI/PET localised). Behaviour genetics separates genotype from phenotype using twin and adoption studies, with PKU, Down (trisomy 21) and Huntington's as key conditions. Sperry and Gazzaniga's split-brain work showed lateralization across the corpus callosum. The hypothalamus runs hunger (LH start, VMH stop) and the SCN drives circadian rhythm via pineal melatonin. Stress runs through the HPA axis and cortisol, unfolding as Selye's alarm, resistance and exhaustion.

The Resting Potential: Why a Neuron Sits at –70 mV

A neuron at rest is not electrically silent; it is a charged battery holding its charge, ready to fire. The inside of the cell membrane carries a slightly negative charge relative to the outside, and the standard measured value is –70 millivolts (mV). This steady difference is called the resting membrane potential. Understanding how it is built and maintained is the single most important foundation in physiological psychology, because every signal the brain sends is a temporary, controlled collapse of this resting state.

From scratch: "Potential" here means stored electrical energy, exactly like the potential energy of water held behind a dam. A voltage is simply a difference in charge across a barrier (the membrane). If the two sides were identical, the voltage would be zero. The neuron spends energy to keep the two sides different, and that stored difference is what it later "spends" to send a message.

The four forces that set the resting potential

Two ions do most of the work: sodium (Na+) and potassium (K+), both positively charged, plus large negatively charged proteins (anions, A–) trapped inside. The resting potential is the outcome of a tug of war between diffusion (particles spreading from high to low concentration) and electrostatic pressure (like charges repel, opposite charges attract).

  • Concentration gradient: Na+ is far more concentrated outside the cell; K+ is far more concentrated inside. Each ion "wants" to diffuse down its own gradient.
  • Electrostatic gradient: The negative interior pulls positive ions inward.
  • Selective permeability: At rest the membrane is roughly 40 times more permeable to K+ than to Na+ (potassium leak channels stay open). K+ therefore leaks out until the growing internal negativity nearly balances its outward diffusion.
  • The sodium–potassium pump: A protein pump, discovered by Jens Christian Skou (Nobel Prize 1997), uses ATP to push 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ it brings in. Because it moves more positive charge out than in, it is electrogenic and keeps the gradients from running down.
Who → what: Jens Christian Skou identified the Na+/K+ ATPase pump, the molecular engine behind the resting potential. Alan Lloyd Hodgkin and Andrew Fielding Huxley (Nobel Prize 1963) worked out the ionic mechanism of the action potential using the giant axon of the squid.

The Action Potential: The Neuron's All-or-None Signal

When a neuron is stimulated enough to push its membrane potential from –70 mV up to the threshold of excitation (about –55 mV), it fires an action potential: a brief, self-propagating reversal of voltage that travels down the axon. This is the fundamental unit of neural communication.

The sequence, step by step

  • Depolarization: At threshold, voltage-gated sodium channels snap open. Na+ floods in, driven by both concentration and electrostatic forces. The interior rapidly swings from –55 mV toward +40 mV. The inside is now momentarily positive.
  • Repolarization: Sodium channels close and inactivate; voltage-gated potassium channels open. K+ rushes out, dragging the charge back down toward negative.
  • Hyperpolarization (undershoot): Potassium channels close slowly, so a little too much K+ escapes and the membrane briefly dips below –70 mV before the pump and leak channels restore the resting state.
Rule: The action potential obeys the all-or-none law. Once threshold is reached, the neuron fires at full amplitude every time; a stronger stimulus does not produce a bigger spike. Intensity is coded by the frequency of firing and by the number of neurons firing, not by spike size.

Refractory periods

Immediately after firing, the neuron cannot fire again no matter how strong the input; this is the absolute refractory period (sodium channels are inactivated). It is followed by the relative refractory period, when a stronger-than-usual stimulus is needed because the cell is hyperpolarized. These periods (a) set an upper limit on firing rate and (b) force the impulse to travel in one direction only, since the patch behind it is temporarily unexcitable.

Saltatory conduction and myelin

Many axons are wrapped in myelin, a fatty insulating sheath produced by oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system and Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system. Myelin is interrupted by gaps called the nodes of Ranvier (named after Louis-Antoine Ranvier). Because ions can only cross at the nodes, the action potential appears to jump from node to node, a mode called saltatory conduction (from the Latin saltare, to leap). This dramatically speeds transmission and saves energy.

RML tilt: Demyelinating diseases test well. In multiple sclerosis the myelin of the CNS is attacked, slowing or blocking saltatory conduction; in Guillain-Barre syndrome the peripheral myelin is affected. Expect an item pairing "loss of myelin" with "slowed nerve conduction".

Resting potential = –70 mV, maintained by the Na+/K+ pump and K+ leak. Action potential = depolarization (Na+ in) → repolarization (K+ out) → hyperpolarization. It is all-or-none, one-directional (refractory periods), and sped up by myelin via saltatory conduction.

Synaptic Transmission in Full Detail

The action potential travels down the axon but cannot cross the tiny gap (the synaptic cleft, about 20 nanometres wide) between one neuron and the next. Communication across this gap is chemical, carried by neurotransmitters. The term "synapse" was coined by Charles Scott Sherrington.

The step-by-step chain of events

  • 1. Arrival and calcium entry: The action potential reaches the axon terminal and opens voltage-gated calcium (Ca2+) channels. Calcium floods in.
  • 2. Exocytosis: Calcium causes synaptic vesicles (tiny sacs holding neurotransmitter) to fuse with the presynaptic membrane and dump their contents into the cleft. This release is called exocytosis.
  • 3. Diffusion and receptor binding: The transmitter diffuses across the cleft and binds to receptor proteins on the postsynaptic membrane, fitting like a key in a lock.
  • 4. Postsynaptic effect: Binding opens ion channels, changing the postsynaptic cell's charge (see EPSP/IPSP below).
  • 5. Termination: The signal must be switched off. This happens by reuptake (the transmitter is pumped back into the sending neuron for reuse, e.g. serotonin and dopamine) or by enzymatic degradation (an enzyme breaks it down in the cleft, e.g. acetylcholinesterase destroying acetylcholine).
From scratch: Reuptake is why SSRIs (selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors) work. By blocking the reuptake pump, the drug leaves more serotonin sitting in the cleft, prolonging its action. This is a direct exam-relevant consequence of step 5.

EPSPs, IPSPs, and summation

A single synapse does not decide whether the neuron fires. Instead each produces a small graded change:

  • EPSP (excitatory postsynaptic potential): depolarizes the postsynaptic cell (moves it toward threshold), typically by letting Na+ in.
  • IPSP (inhibitory postsynaptic potential): hyperpolarizes the cell (moves it away from threshold), typically by letting Cl– in or K+ out.

The neuron adds these up at the axon hillock. This adding is called summation, and it comes in two forms:

  • Spatial summation: many synapses at different locations fire at the same time, and their effects add together.
  • Temporal summation: a single synapse fires rapidly in succession, so its effects pile up before they fade.
Rule: A neuron fires only if the net sum of all EPSPs minus IPSPs, arriving close enough in time and space, pushes the membrane to threshold. The neuron is therefore a tiny decision-making integrator, not a simple relay.

Neurotransmitters Mapped to Disorders

Each major transmitter is linked to specific functions and to the disorders that arise when it is too high or too low. This mapping is one of the highest-yield tables for the entrance.

TransmitterMain roleClinical link
Dopamine (DA)Reward, movement, motivationExcess in mesolimbic pathway → positive symptoms of schizophrenia; deficiency in the substantia nigra → Parkinson's disease
Serotonin (5-HT)Mood, sleep, appetiteLow levels linked to depression, OCD, and anxiety; target of SSRIs
Acetylcholine (ACh)Muscle action, memory, attentionLoss of ACh neurons in Alzheimer's disease; blocked at the junction in myasthenia gravis
GABAMain inhibitory transmitterLow activity → anxiety and seizures; benzodiazepines boost GABA
GlutamateMain excitatory transmitter, learningOveractivity → excitotoxicity (cell death after stroke); implicated in schizophrenia
Norepinephrine (NE)Arousal, alertness, fight-or-flightDysregulation in depression and anxiety; part of the stress response
EndorphinsNatural painkillers, pleasureReduce pain; involved in the "runner's high"

Agonists versus antagonists

Drugs act on synapses in two opposite directions.

  • An agonist mimics or boosts a transmitter's action (it may bind the receptor directly, block reuptake, or block the degrading enzyme). Example: L-DOPA raises dopamine activity in Parkinson's; nicotine is an ACh agonist.
  • An antagonist blocks or reduces a transmitter's action (it may occupy the receptor without activating it, or prevent release). Example: antipsychotics are dopamine antagonists; curare blocks ACh receptors, causing paralysis.
Trap: An antagonist that occupies a receptor without triggering it is still "binding"; do not equate binding with activation. Only agonists activate. Also note: blocking reuptake makes a drug an agonist (more transmitter stays active), which students often reverse.

Deeper Brain Anatomy: Nuclei, Circuits, and Cortex

Beyond the gross divisions, the entrance tests specific nuclei and functional zones. Work outward from the brainstem to the cortex.

Thalamus and hypothalamus

The thalamus is the great sensory relay station: nearly all sensory information (except smell) passes through it on the way to the cortex. Key nuclei include the lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) for vision and the medial geniculate nucleus (MGN) for hearing. The hypothalamus, though tiny, governs the "four Fs" (feeding, fighting, fleeing, and reproduction). Named nuclei to remember: the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) is the master clock; the lateral hypothalamus (LH) is the hunger "on" centre; the ventromedial hypothalamus (VMH) is the "off" (satiety) centre; the supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei govern hormone release.

Basal ganglia

The basal ganglia (caudate nucleus, putamen, globus pallidus, plus the associated substantia nigra and subthalamic nucleus) control the initiation and smoothing of voluntary movement and habit learning. Their degeneration causes movement disorders: Parkinson's disease (too little dopamine, producing rigidity and tremor) and Huntington's disease (loss of caudate neurons, producing involuntary jerky movements).

The limbic system
