The limbic system is the emotional and memory core, wrapping the brainstem.

  • Amygdala: the fear and threat detector; central to emotional learning. Damage produces reduced fear (studied via Kluver-Bucy syndrome).
  • Hippocampus: forms new explicit (declarative) long-term memories. Its role was revealed by patient H.M. (Henry Molaison), whose bilateral removal left him unable to form new memories.
  • Cingulate cortex: links emotion to behaviour and attention; the anterior cingulate handles conflict monitoring and pain's emotional component.

Reticular formation, cerebellum, and brainstem

The reticular formation, a net of neurons running through the brainstem, contains the reticular activating system (RAS) that controls arousal, wakefulness, and the sleep–wake transition; damage can cause coma. The cerebellum ("little brain") coordinates balance, posture, and finely timed movement, and stores procedural motor memories; alcohol impairs it, causing the classic staggering gait. The brainstem (midbrain, pons, and medulla oblongata) runs automatic survival functions: the medulla controls heartbeat and breathing, and the pons bridges signals and helps regulate sleep.

Cortical lobes and functional areas

The cerebral cortex is divided into four lobes with well-mapped functions.

LobeKey functions and landmarks
FrontalPlanning, judgement, personality; the motor cortex (precentral gyrus); Broca's area for speech production
ParietalTouch and body sense; the somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus)
TemporalHearing; Wernicke's area for language comprehension
OccipitalVision (primary visual cortex)

The motor and somatosensory strips are laid out as a homunculus ("little man"): body parts needing fine control or fine sensation (hands, lips, tongue) get disproportionately large cortical space. This map was charted by neurosurgeon Wilder Penfield using electrical stimulation.

Who → what: Paul Broca linked damaged left frontal tissue to loss of speech production (Broca's aphasia, non-fluent). Carl Wernicke linked left temporal damage to loss of speech comprehension (Wernicke's aphasia, fluent but meaningless). Wilder Penfield mapped the motor and sensory homunculus.

Trap: Broca = production (broken, halting speech but understanding intact). Wernicke = comprehension (words flow fluently but make no sense). Students routinely swap these; anchor "Broca is broken speech".

Neuroplasticity, Long-Term Potentiation, and Neurogenesis

The brain is not fixed hardware; it reshapes itself with experience, a property called neuroplasticity. Three linked ideas matter for the exam.

  • Long-term potentiation (LTP): the long-lasting strengthening of a synapse after repeated, high-frequency use. First described by Terje Lomo and Timothy Bliss, LTP is the leading cellular model of learning and memory and relies heavily on glutamate acting on NMDA receptors.
  • Hebbian learning: summarized by the slogan "cells that fire together, wire together", from Donald Hebb. Synapses that are active at the same time as the postsynaptic cell fires get strengthened.
  • Neurogenesis: the birth of new neurons in the adult brain, occurring notably in the hippocampus (dentate gyrus). Once thought impossible, it is now accepted and is reduced by chronic stress and boosted by exercise and enriched environments.
From scratch: "Potentiation" simply means making something more potent (stronger). LTP is the synapse becoming a stronger, more reliable connection because it was used a lot. This is the physical trace that abstract "learning" leaves in tissue.

Methods of Studying the Brain, in Depth

Each technique answers a different question and carries a different trade-off between structure and function, and between spatial and temporal detail.

MethodWhat it measuresStrength / limit
EEG (electroencephalography)Electrical activity (brain waves) from scalp electrodesExcellent time resolution (milliseconds); poor at locating the source
ERP (event-related potential)Averaged EEG time-locked to a stimulusIsolates the brain's response to a specific event; same poor spatial detail
PET (positron emission tomography)Metabolic activity via a radioactive tracer (e.g. glucose)Shows function and can track neurotransmitters; involves radiation, slow
fMRIBlood oxygen level (BOLD signal) as a proxy for activityGood spatial detail, no radiation; slower than EEG (seconds), indirect measure
MRIDetailed static structureBest structural images; shows anatomy, not function
CT (computed tomography)X-ray-based structural slicesFast, good for tumours/bleeds; uses radiation, lower detail than MRI
TMSMagnetic pulse that briefly excites or silences a cortical regionCan test causation (a "virtual lesion"); limited to surface areas
Lesion / ablationBehaviour after tissue is destroyed or removedShows what a region is necessary for; damage is imprecise and irreversible
MicroelectrodeFiring of a single neuronFinest possible detail; invasive, one cell at a time
OptogeneticsLight switching genetically light-sensitive neurons on/offPrecise causal control of specific cell types; requires genetic modification (mostly animals)
Rule: Match the tool to the question. Structure only → MRI or CT. Function → fMRI or PET. Fast timing → EEG or ERP. Proving a region causes a behaviour → lesion, TMS, or optogenetics (correlational methods like fMRI cannot).

Biological Rhythms and the Architecture of Sleep

The body runs on internal clocks. The most important is the roughly 24-hour circadian rhythm, governed by the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) of the hypothalamus. The SCN reads light through the eyes and signals the pineal gland to release melatonin in darkness, promoting sleep. When these cues are disrupted, we get jet lag and shift-work fatigue.

The stages of sleep

Sleep is not uniform; it cycles through stages defined by EEG patterns, discovered in the modern form by researchers including Nathaniel Kleitman and Eugene Aserinsky (who identified REM).

  • NREM Stage 1: light drifting sleep; slow "theta" waves; easily woken; may feel hypnic jerks.
  • NREM Stage 2: clear sleep onset; marked by sleep spindles and K-complexes; largest share of the night.
  • NREM Stage 3: deep slow-wave sleep with large "delta" waves; hardest to wake; when growth hormone is released and the body repairs itself. (Older systems split this into stages 3 and 4.)
  • REM sleep: rapid eye movement, vivid dreaming, brain activity resembling waking, but with muscle atonia (paralysis). Called "paradoxical sleep" because the brain is active while the body is still.

A full sleep cycle lasts about 90 minutes and repeats 4 to 6 times a night. As the night goes on, deep NREM shrinks and REM periods lengthen.

Functions of sleep and effects of deprivation

Leading theories say sleep serves restoration (repairing the body and clearing metabolic waste), memory consolidation (NREM for facts, REM for procedural and emotional memory), and energy conservation. Deprivation impairs attention, mood, immune function, and metabolism; severe or prolonged loss causes microsleeps and hallucinations.

RML tilt: Expect items pairing "90-minute cycle", "REM = paradoxical sleep + atonia", and "slow-wave (delta) sleep = Stage 3, deepest". Also know that REM proportion is highest in infancy and that alcohol and many antidepressants suppress REM.

Physiology of Hunger, Thirst, and Body Temperature

Hunger regulation

Two hypothalamic centres form a dual-control system. The lateral hypothalamus (LH) is the hunger centre: stimulate it and an animal eats, destroy it and the animal starves (aphagia). The ventromedial hypothalamus (VMH) is the satiety centre: stimulate it and eating stops, destroy it and the animal overeats to obesity (hyperphagia). Two hormones fine-tune this: ghrelin, released by the empty stomach, signals hunger ("ghrelin = growling"); leptin, released by fat cells, signals fullness and long-term energy stores.

Thirst and thermoregulation

Thirst comes in two kinds: osmotic thirst (cells lose water and shrink, detected by osmoreceptors) and hypovolemic thirst (loss of blood volume, as in bleeding). Both are monitored by the hypothalamus, which triggers drinking and the release of antidiuretic hormone (ADH / vasopressin) to conserve water. The hypothalamus is also the body's thermostat: it drives shivering and vasoconstriction to warm up, and sweating and vasodilation to cool down, holding core temperature near 37 C.

The Endocrine System in Detail

The endocrine system is the body's slower, chemical communication network. Glands release hormones into the bloodstream to act on distant targets.

Hormones versus neurotransmitters

FeatureNeurotransmittersHormones
TravelAcross the synaptic cleftThrough the bloodstream
SpeedMillisecondsSeconds to hours
DurationBriefLong-lasting
TargetAdjacent neuronDistant organs with receptors
Trap: Some molecules are both. Norepinephrine and dopamine act as neurotransmitters in the brain and as hormones from the adrenal gland. The category depends on where it is released and how it travels, not on the chemical itself.

Key glands

  • Pituitary (the "master gland"): sits below the hypothalamus and directs other glands. The anterior pituitary releases growth hormone, ACTH, TSH, and the gonadotropins; the posterior pituitary stores oxytocin and ADH made by the hypothalamus.
  • Thyroid: releases thyroxine, setting metabolic rate. Too little causes sluggishness and, in infants, cretinism; too much causes agitation and weight loss.
  • Adrenal glands: the adrenal cortex (outer) releases cortisol (the stress hormone) and aldosterone; the adrenal medulla (inner) releases adrenaline (epinephrine) and noradrenaline for the fast fight-or-flight surge.
  • Gonads: ovaries and testes release the sex hormones (oestrogen, progesterone, testosterone).

The HPA axis

The hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis is the body's central stress circuit. Under stress the hypothalamus releases CRH, which tells the pituitary to release ACTH, which tells the adrenal cortex to release cortisol. Cortisol then feeds back to shut the loop down. Chronic activation keeps cortisol high, which harms the hippocampus, weakens immunity, and is implicated in depression.

The Physiology of Pain

Pain is not a simple wire from injury to brain. The dominant account is the gate-control theory, proposed in 1965 by Ronald Melzack and Patrick Wall.

Who → what: Ronald Melzack and Patrick Wall proposed that a neural "gate" in the spinal cord (the dorsal horn) can open or close to let pain signals reach the brain. Large-fibre touch input and top-down signals from the brain (attention, mood, expectation) can close the gate; this explains why rubbing a bump eases it and why anxiety worsens pain.

The body also makes its own opioids, the endorphins ("endogenous morphine"), which bind opioid receptors to blunt pain and produce pleasure. They explain placebo analgesia, acupuncture effects, and the runner's high.

Genetics and Behaviour

Behaviour genetics asks how much of a trait comes from genes versus environment, and how the two interact.

  • Genotype vs phenotype: the genotype is the full set of genes inherited; the phenotype is the observable result (traits, behaviour), produced by genes interacting with environment.
  • Twin studies: compare monozygotic (identical, 100% shared genes) with dizygotic (fraternal, ~50% shared) twins. Higher concordance in identical twins points to a genetic contribution.
  • Adoption studies: compare adopted children with both their biological and adoptive parents to separate nature from nurture.
  • Heritability: the proportion of the variation in a trait within a population attributable to genetic differences. It is a population statistic, not a statement about any one individual.
  • Epigenetics: the study of how experience (stress, diet, toxins) switches genes on or off without changing the DNA sequence, sometimes across generations.
Trap: Heritability of 0.5 does not mean half of one person's trait is genetic. It means half the differences among people in that population trace to genes. Applying a population figure to an individual is a classic exam error.

Signals: resting potential (Na/K pump) → all-or-none action potential (myelin speeds it) → chemical synapse (vesicles, receptors, reuptake/enzymes) → EPSP/IPSP summed to threshold. Chemistry: each transmitter maps to a disorder; agonists boost, antagonists block. Structures: thalamus relays, hypothalamus regulates drives and the endocrine system via the HPA axis, limbic system handles emotion and memory, cortex maps to a homunculus with Broca (production) and Wernicke (comprehension). Plasticity (LTP, neurogenesis), sleep (90-min cycles, REM paradoxical), hunger (LH on / VMH off, ghrelin/leptin), pain (gate control, endorphins), and genetics (genotype/phenotype, twins, heritability, epigenetics) complete the biological picture.

4 Developmental & Child Psychology

Developmental psychology studies how thinking, emotion, morality, and relationships change across the lifespan. For the RML CET-166, the examiners lean on the classic stage theorists (who moves through what, and in what order) and on the attachment experiments. Learn the names, the sequence, and the one-line "signature" idea for each.

4.1 Jean Piaget, Cognitive Development

From scratch: Jean Piaget argued that a child is not a "small adult" but a little scientist who actively constructs knowledge. Hence his theory is called cognitive constructivism. The child builds mental structures called schemas (organised patterns of knowing).

Two processes drive change. Assimilation = fitting new information into an existing schema (a child calls a cat "doggy" because it has four legs and fur). Accommodation = changing the schema to fit new information (learning that cats and dogs are different categories). The push toward balance between the two is equilibration; a mismatch causes disequilibrium, which motivates learning.

StageAge (approx.)Signature achievement / limit
Sensorimotor0–2 yrsKnows the world through senses & action; gains object permanence (objects exist when out of sight)
Preoperational2–7 yrsEgocentrism, animism, fails conservation; symbolic/pretend play, no logical operations
Concrete operational7–11 yrsAchieves conservation, reversibility, classification; logic tied to concrete objects
Formal operational11+ yrsAbstract & hypothetical-deductive reasoning; "what if" thinking

Object permanence is tested by hiding a toy; before ~8 months, "out of sight is out of mind." Egocentrism means the child cannot take another's viewpoint (the three-mountains task). Animism = attributing life to objects ("the sun follows me"). Conservation = understanding quantity stays the same despite a change in appearance (the same water poured into a tall thin glass "looks like more" to a preoperational child).

Trap: Conservation is failed in the preoperational stage and gained in the concrete operational stage. Object permanence belongs to sensorimotor, not preoperational. Do not swap these.

4.2 Lev Vygotsky, Sociocultural Theory

Lev Vygotsky stressed that cognition grows out of social interaction and culture, not solo discovery. His key idea is the Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD): the gap between what a child can do alone and what she can do with help. Learning happens in that zone through scaffolding (temporary, tailored support that is withdrawn as competence grows), provided by a More Knowledgeable Other (MKO), a parent, teacher, or abler peer.

Private speech (children talking aloud to themselves) is, for Vygotsky, a tool for self-guidance that later becomes silent inner speech, a positive sign of thinking, not mere immaturity.

Who → what: Piaget = child as lone scientist, development drives learning, universal stages. Vygotsky = child as social apprentice, learning drives development, culture-dependent, ZPD + scaffolding.

4.3 Erik Erikson, Psychosocial Stages

Erik Erikson extended Freud across the whole lifespan. Each of eight stages poses a psychosocial crisis; resolving it yields a virtue (basic strength).

Stage / ageCrisisVirtue
Infancy (0–1)Trust vs MistrustHope
Toddler (1–3)Autonomy vs Shame & DoubtWill
Preschool (3–6)Initiative vs GuiltPurpose
School (6–12)Industry vs InferiorityCompetence
Adolescence (12–18)Identity vs Role ConfusionFidelity
Young adultIntimacy vs IsolationLove
Middle adultGenerativity vs StagnationCare
Late adultIntegrity vs DespairWisdom
Key. Adolescence = Identity vs Role Confusion (Erikson's most famous stage; source of the term identity crisis). Old age = Integrity vs Despair.

4.4 Sigmund Freud, Psychosexual Stages

Sigmund Freud held that libido (sexual energy) centres on a different erogenous zone at each stage. Being over- or under-gratified causes fixation, energy "stuck" at that stage, shaping adult personality.

  • Oral (0–1): mouth; fixation → dependency, smoking, overeating.
  • Anal (1–3): bowel control; fixation → anal-retentive (orderly, stingy) or anal-expulsive (messy).
  • Phallic (3–6): genitals; the Oedipus complex in boys (with castration anxiety) and the Electra complex in girls; resolved by identifying with the same-sex parent → superego forms.
  • Latency (6–puberty): sexual urges dormant; focus on skills, same-sex friends.
  • Genital (puberty on): mature adult sexuality.
Trap: The phallic stage carries the Oedipus/Electra conflict and forms the superego, not the anal or latency stage.

4.5 Lawrence Kohlberg, Moral Development

Lawrence Kohlberg, using the Heinz dilemma, proposed three levels (six stages) of moral reasoning, judged by the reasoning given, not the choice.

  • Pre-conventional: morality = avoiding punishment (Stage 1) and self-interest/reward (Stage 2). Typical of children.
  • Conventional: morality = social approval, being a "good boy/girl" (Stage 3) and upholding law and order (Stage 4). Most adults.
  • Post-conventional: morality = social contract (Stage 5) and universal ethical principles (Stage 6). Reached by few.
Who → what: Carol Gilligan critiqued Kohlberg for a male-biased "justice" emphasis built on all-male samples, and proposed an ethic of care (morality as responsibility and relationships) alongside his ethic of justice.

4.6 Attachment

From scratch: Attachment is the enduring emotional bond between infant and caregiver, a survival system, not just "spoiling."

John Bowlby founded attachment theory: the infant is biologically primed to seek proximity to one primary figure (monotropy), and early experience builds an internal working model, a mental template of relationships that guides later bonds. Prolonged separation, he warned, causes lasting harm (maternal deprivation).

Mary Ainsworth tested this with the Strange Situation (structured separations and reunions), classifying three patterns: secure (distressed at separation, comforted at reunion), anxious-ambivalent/resistant (very distressed, hard to soothe, clingy-and-angry), and avoidant (little distress, ignores caregiver). Later, Mary Main added a fourth: disorganized (confused, contradictory behaviour, often linked to frightening care).

Harry Harlow's rhesus monkey studies showed infants clung to a soft cloth "mother" over a wire mother that fed them, proving that contact comfort, not food, is the basis of attachment, a direct blow to the behaviourist "cupboard love" view.

RML tilt: A favourite matching item: Bowlby = theory/monotropy, Ainsworth = Strange Situation & the three styles, Main = disorganized, Harlow = monkeys/contact comfort. Keep the person-to-contribution links crisp.

4.7 Diana Baumrind, Parenting Styles

Diana Baumrind crossed two dimensions, demandingness (control) and responsiveness (warmth), to describe parenting.

  • Authoritative: high warmth + high control; firm but reasoning. Best child outcomes.
  • Authoritarian: low warmth + high control; strict, "because I said so."
  • Permissive (indulgent): high warmth + low control; few demands.

Maccoby & Martin added a fourth style: neglectful/uninvolved (low warmth + low control), associated with the poorest outcomes.

Trap: Authoritative (best, balanced) is easily confused with authoritarian (harsh, controlling). The neglectful style was added by Maccoby & Martin, not Baumrind.

4.8 Arnold Gesell, Maturation & Milestones

Arnold Gesell championed maturation: development unfolds in a fixed, biologically pre-programmed sequence, relatively independent of environment. From detailed observation he produced developmental norms (age-based milestones) still echoed in modern screening, sits ~6 months, stands ~9–10 months, walks ~12 months, first words ~12 months, two-word phrases ~24 months. His stance is the classic nature pole against Watson's environmentalism.

4.9 Common Developmental Disorders

  • Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD): persistent deficits in social communication plus restricted, repetitive behaviours; onset in early childhood.
  • Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD): age-inappropriate inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity across settings.
  • Intellectual Disability (ID): significantly below-average intellectual functioning and adaptive deficits, with onset in the developmental period.
Recap: Piaget = 4 cognitive stages (object permanence → conservation), schema/assimilation/accommodation. Vygotsky = ZPD, scaffolding, MKO, private speech (social > solo). Erikson = 8 psychosocial crises with virtues (Trust→Integrity). Freud = 5 psychosexual stages, phallic = Oedipus/Electra, fixation. Kohlberg = pre-/conventional/post-conventional; Gilligan = ethic of care. Attachment: Bowlby (monotropy, internal working model), Ainsworth (Strange Situation: secure/ambivalent/avoidant), Main (disorganized), Harlow (contact comfort). Baumrind parenting (+ neglectful by Maccoby & Martin). Gesell = maturation & norms.

4.10 Nature vs Nurture and Developmental Research Designs

From scratch: Development is the study of how a person changes across the lifespan. Before we can study any theory, we need two foundations: what drives change (biology or experience), and how we actually measure change over time.

Nature vs Nurture (Heredity-Environment Interaction)

The oldest question in the field asks whether behaviour is shaped mainly by nature (genes, heredity, biological maturation) or by nurture (environment, learning, culture, upbringing). The modern answer rejects the either/or framing: nearly every trait emerges from an interaction of both.

  • Range of reaction: genes set a range of possible outcomes; the environment decides where within that range a person lands.
  • Canalization: some traits (for example motor milestones like walking) are so strongly guided by genes that they follow a narrow path despite wide environmental variation.
  • Gene-environment correlation: genes and environments are not independent, for example a musically gifted child is often given music lessons (an environment that matches the gene).
  • Epigenetics: environment can switch genes on or off without altering the DNA sequence itself.

Key. The exam-safe stance is interactionism: heredity and environment work together, not in opposition.

Developmental Research Designs

DesignMethodStrengthWeakness
Cross-sectionalDifferent age groups studied at the same timeFast, cheap, no dropoutCohort effects (age differences may be generational, not developmental)
LongitudinalSame people followed over many yearsShows true change and individual patternsSlow, costly, attrition (participant dropout), practice effects
SequentialSeveral cohorts followed longitudinally at onceSeparates age effects from cohort effectsComplex, expensive

Trap: A cohort effect is the confound of cross-sectional designs, not longitudinal ones. Longitudinal studies instead suffer from attrition.

Continuity vs Discontinuity, and Critical vs Sensitive Periods

  • Continuity view: development is gradual and quantitative, like a smooth ramp (learning theorists).
  • Discontinuity view: development moves through distinct qualitative stages, like steps on a staircase (Piaget, Freud, Erikson).
  • Critical period: a rigid window during which a specific input must occur or the outcome is lost forever (Konrad Lorenz imprinting in geese).
  • Sensitive period: a softer, optimal window when the organism is especially receptive, but learning can still happen later (much of human language).

4.11 Prenatal Development and Teratogens

From scratch: Development begins at conception, not birth. The prenatal period runs about 40 weeks across three stages.

StageTimingKey events
GerminalWeeks 0–2Zygote forms, rapid cell division, implantation in the uterine wall
EmbryonicWeeks 3–8Major organs and body systems form (organogenesis); most vulnerable stage
FetalWeek 9 to birthGrowth, refinement, brain development, viability, movement

Teratogens are environmental agents that cause harm during prenatal development: alcohol, nicotine, certain drugs, radiation, and infections such as rubella. Their damage is worst during the embryonic stage when organs are forming.

Who → what: Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS) results from maternal alcohol use. Signs include facial abnormalities, low birth weight, and intellectual disability. There is no known safe level of alcohol in pregnancy.

4.12 Temperament: Thomas and Chess

From scratch: Temperament is the biologically based, early-appearing style of emotional and behavioural reactivity, the raw material on which personality is later built.

Who → what: Alexander Thomas and Stella Chess, in the New York Longitudinal Study, identified three temperament types.

  • Easy child (~40%): regular routines, positive mood, adapts readily to new situations.
  • Difficult child (~10%): irregular routines, intense negative reactions, slow to accept change.
  • Slow-to-warm-up child (~15%): mild, low activity, withdraws at first but warms up gradually with gentle exposure.

Key. Goodness of fit is the match between a child's temperament and the demands and parenting style of their environment. Good outcomes depend on fit, not on the temperament type alone.

4.13 Bronfenbrenner's Ecological Systems Theory

Who → what: Urie Bronfenbrenner proposed the ecological (later bioecological) systems theory, which places the developing child inside a set of nested environmental layers.

SystemWhat it isExample
MicrosystemImmediate settings the child directly interacts withFamily, school, peers
MesosystemConnections between microsystemsParent-teacher relationship
ExosystemSettings that affect the child indirectlyParent's workplace, local government
MacrosystemOverarching culture, values, laws, economyCultural attitudes to childrearing
ChronosystemDimension of time and change across the lifespanDivorce, historical events, aging

RML tilt: The chronosystem (time) is the most frequently missed layer. Remember it captures transitions and historical change over the person's life.

4.14 Language Development

From scratch: Language unfolds in a predictable universal sequence in the first years, regardless of the specific language being learned.

  • Cooing (~2 months): vowel-like sounds, the first playful vocalisation.
  • Babbling (~6 months): repeated consonant-vowel strings (ba-ba-ba); becomes tuned to the native language.
  • Holophrastic / one-word stage (~12 months): single words carry whole-sentence meaning (milk means "I want milk").
  • Telegraphic speech (~18–24 months): two-word utterances with only content words, function words dropped (want cookie).

Trap: Do not confuse cooing (vowels) with babbling (consonant-vowel repetition). Cooing comes first.

4.15 Adolescence: Storm, Identity, and Egocentrism

Who → what: Granville Stanley Hall, the father of the psychology of adolescence, described this period as one of "storm and stress" (Sturm und Drang), marked by conflict, mood disruption, and risk behaviour.

Puberty is the biological onset of sexual maturity, driven by hormones, and includes primary sex characteristics (reproductive organs) and secondary sex characteristics (body hair, voice change, breast development).

Marcia's Four Identity Statuses

Who → what: James Marcia expanded Erikson's identity-versus-role-confusion stage using two dimensions, exploration (crisis) and commitment.

StatusExplorationCommitment
Identity diffusionNoNo
ForeclosureNoYes (adopted from others, no crisis)
MoratoriumYes (in crisis)No
Identity achievementYesYes

Trap: Foreclosure means commitment without exploration (values borrowed from parents). Moratorium is the active crisis with no commitment yet. Students often swap these two.

Adolescent Egocentrism (Elkind)

Who → what: David Elkind described two distortions of adolescent thought. The imaginary audience is the belief that others are constantly watching and judging you. The personal fable is the belief that you are unique and invulnerable ("it won't happen to me").

4.16 Adulthood and Aging

Who → what: Daniel Levinson, in "The Seasons of a Man's Life", described adult development as alternating stable structure-building periods and transitional periods, including the midlife transition around ages 40 to 45.

Who → what: Robert Havighurst proposed developmental tasks, age-linked challenges (for example choosing a career, marriage, adjusting to retirement) that a person must master at each life stage for healthy development.

Intelligence in Aging

Who → what: Raymond Cattell and John Horn distinguished two kinds of intelligence.

  • Fluid intelligence: reasoning, speed, and novel problem solving; declines with age.
  • Crystallized intelligence: accumulated knowledge, vocabulary, and skills; stays stable or rises with age.

From scratch: Broad theories of aging include the disengagement theory (older adults and society mutually withdraw), the activity theory (staying active supports wellbeing), and the socioemotional selectivity theory (with less time perceived ahead, older adults prioritise emotionally meaningful relationships).

4.17 Death, Dying, and Bereavement

Who → what: Elisabeth Kubler-Ross, in "On Death and Dying", described five stages of coping with terminal illness and grief.

StageCore reaction
Denial"This can't be happening to me."
Anger"Why me? It's not fair."
Bargaining"If I do X, let me live longer."
DepressionSadness, withdrawal, mourning the loss
AcceptanceCalm coming-to-terms with the reality

RML tilt: The stages are not a fixed order. Kubler-Ross herself said people may skip stages, revisit them, or experience them out of sequence. Exam items that call them rigid and sequential are testing this caveat.

Recap: Development rests on heredity-environment interaction, studied via cross-sectional, longitudinal, and sequential designs. Life begins prenatally (germinal, embryonic, fetal) where teratogens like alcohol cause FAS. Thomas and Chess mapped temperament (easy, difficult, slow-to-warm-up) with goodness of fit; Bronfenbrenner nested the child in five systems from micro to chrono. Language runs cooing to telegraphic; adolescence brings Hall's storm and stress, Marcia's four identity statuses, and Elkind's imaginary audience and personal fable. Adulthood adds Levinson's seasons, Havighurst's tasks, and the fluid-versus-crystallized shift, closing with Kubler-Ross's five stages of dying.

Information-Processing Approach to Cognitive Development

Jean Piaget pictured the child as a little scientist who reorganises whole stages of thought. A rival family of theories, the information-processing approach, drops the idea of grand stages and instead treats the mind like a computer: information flows in through the senses, is held briefly, encoded, stored, and later retrieved. Development, on this view, is not a jump from one logic to another but a gradual increase in the efficiency of the machinery.

From scratch: Think of the mind as having a limited desk (working memory) and a large filing cabinet (long-term memory). A young child has a small desk and slow hands. As the child grows, three things improve: the desk holds more at once (capacity), the hands move faster (processing speed), and the child learns filing tricks (strategies). No new "logic" is needed to explain the change, only better use of the same system.

What actually improves with age

  • Processing speed: the sheer rate at which the brain handles information rises steeply through childhood, largely because of myelination (fatty insulation on nerve fibres) and synaptic pruning.
  • Working-memory capacity: older children can juggle more items at once, so they can hold a problem's parts in mind while working on it.
  • Strategies: children learn deliberate tricks such as rehearsal (repeating a phone number), organisation (grouping a shopping list into categories), and elaboration (linking new facts to what is already known).
  • Metacognition: "thinking about thinking", the growing ability to monitor and plan one's own mental work, to know what one knows and does not know.
  • Knowledge base: the more a child already knows about a topic, the more easily new information sticks. A chess-expert child can remember board positions better than a novice adult.

Robbie Case and the Neo-Piagetian view

Robbie Case and other Neo-Piagetian theorists tried to keep the best of Piaget while borrowing from information processing. They accepted that thinking changes in broad stages, but explained the changes through growth in working memory rather than through mysterious logical structures. Case argued that as processing becomes more automatic and efficient, it frees up mental "space" (which he called M-space, a term originally from Juan Pascual-Leone) so the child can hold more in mind and reach the next level. The stage-like appearance of development, in this account, is a by-product of expanding mental workspace.

Who → what: Robbie Case, Juan Pascual-Leone, Kurt Fischer → Neo-Piagetian theories that recast Piaget's stages as products of growing working-memory capacity and processing efficiency.

Critiques of Piaget: The Competent Infant

Modern research suggests Piaget systematically underestimated infants and young children. His tasks often demanded a motor response (reaching, searching) or verbal explanation that the child could not yet produce, so a child who knew something might still fail the task. When researchers used gentler measures, hidden abilities appeared far earlier than Piaget claimed.

Renee Baillargeon and early object permanence

Renee Baillargeon used the violation-of-expectation method. Infants look longer at events that surprise them. She showed babies a solid screen rotating like a drawbridge; then she placed a box behind the screen. If the screen appeared to rotate straight through the hidden box (an impossible event), infants as young as three to four months stared longer than when it stopped at the box (a possible event). Longer looking implies the babies still represented the box even when they could not see it, that is, a form of object permanence months before Piaget's proposed eight-month mark.

Trap: Do not say Baillargeon "disproved" object permanence. She showed it emerges much earlier than Piaget thought. The concept is real; Piaget's timetable and his reliance on active search were the problem.

Theory of Mind

Theory of mind is the understanding that other people have their own beliefs, desires, and intentions, and that these mental states can differ from reality and from one's own. It is the foundation of everyday social life: deception, empathy, teaching, and persuasion all rest on it.

The false-belief task

Heinz Wimmer and Josef Perner designed the classic false-belief task, popularly called the Sally-Anne task. Sally puts a marble in a basket and leaves. While she is gone, Anne moves the marble to a box. When Sally returns, where will she look for her marble? The correct answer is the basket, because Sally still holds the false belief that the marble is where she left it. Children below about four years usually answer "the box", reporting reality rather than Sally's outdated belief. Around age four to five, most children pass, marking a milestone in mental-state understanding.

From scratch: To pass, the child must hold two versions of the world at once: the true state (marble in box) and someone else's mistaken picture (marble in basket), and predict behaviour from the mistaken picture, not the truth. Younger children collapse the two and answer from their own knowledge.

The autism link

Simon Baron-Cohen, Alan Leslie, and Uta Frith proposed that children with autism spectrum disorder show a specific delay or deficit in theory of mind, sometimes called "mindblindness". In their study many autistic children who had the general reasoning ability to pass still failed the false-belief task, whereas children with Down syndrome of lower general ability passed. This suggested theory of mind is a somewhat separate ability, not just overall intelligence.

RML tilt: Expect a direct match item pairing the "Sally-Anne / false-belief task" with "theory of mind" and with "Baron-Cohen, autism". Also remember the pass age is roughly four years.

Attachment in Depth

The earlier section introduced attachment. Here is the fuller story of the theorists and the classic experiments the RML has drawn on.

John Bowlby: the evolutionary and internal-working-model view

John Bowlby, the founder of attachment theory, argued that the infant's tie to the caregiver is an evolved behavioural system that promotes survival by keeping the vulnerable infant close to protection. Crying, clinging, and following are attachment behaviours that draw the caregiver near. From repeated experiences of care, the child builds an internal working model: a mental template of whether the self is worthy of love and whether others can be relied upon. This model, Bowlby held, quietly shapes relationships across the whole lifespan.

Bowlby also described phases of attachment: an early pre-attachment phase (indiscriminate signalling), attachment-in-the-making (preferring familiar people), clear-cut attachment (separation protest and use of the caregiver as a secure base, roughly from six to eight months), and finally a goal-corrected partnership in which the toddler begins to grasp the caregiver's own plans. He coined the terms secure base (the caregiver as a launch-pad for exploration) and safe haven (the caregiver as a refuge when frightened).

Mary Ainsworth: the Strange Situation

Mary Ainsworth turned Bowlby's ideas into a laboratory test, the Strange Situation. A twelve-to-eighteen-month-old and caregiver enter an unfamiliar playroom; across a scripted series of episodes a stranger enters, the caregiver leaves twice, and the caregiver returns twice. The key data are the child's use of the caregiver as a secure base for exploration and, above all, the child's behaviour at reunion.

ClassificationBehaviour in Strange SituationTypical caregiving
Secure (Type B)Explores freely, upset at separation, actively seeks and is soothed by caregiver at reunionSensitive, responsive
Insecure-avoidant (Type A)Little distress at separation, ignores or avoids caregiver at reunionRejecting, intrusive or dismissive
Insecure-resistant / ambivalent (Type C)Very distressed, hard to settle, both seeks and angrily resists comfort at reunionInconsistent, unpredictable
Disorganised (Type D)Contradictory, confused, freezing or fearful behaviour with no coherent strategyFrightening, frightened, or abusive
Who → what: Mary Main and Judith Solomon → added the fourth category, disorganised attachment (Type D), for infants whose behaviour did not fit Ainsworth's original three patterns and which is linked to maltreatment and unresolved caregiver trauma.

Harry Harlow's monkeys

Harry Harlow reared infant rhesus monkeys with two artificial "mothers": a bare wire mother that dispensed milk and a soft cloth mother that gave no food. The infants clung to the cloth mother and ran to it when frightened, going to the wire mother only to feed. This overturned the older "cupboard love" idea that attachment is simply learned because the mother provides food. Harlow's phrase for the cloth mother's role was contact comfort. Monkeys reared in isolation later showed severe, lasting social and emotional damage.

Konrad Lorenz and imprinting

Konrad Lorenz, an ethologist, showed that newly hatched greylag geese would follow and become attached to the first moving object they saw, often Lorenz himself. He called this imprinting and argued it happens within a narrow critical period shortly after hatching and is then hard to reverse. Imprinting influenced Bowlby's thinking that attachment has biological, time-sensitive roots.

Trap: Keep the animal studies straight: Harlow = rhesus monkeys, cloth vs wire mother, contact comfort. Lorenz = geese, imprinting, critical period. Do not swap the two names.

Cross-cultural patterns

The Strange Situation was designed in the United States, and cross-cultural work shows the proportions of each type vary. Studies in Germany reported more avoidant infants (valuing early independence), whereas studies in Japan and Israeli kibbutzim reported more resistant infants (children rarely separated from mothers found the procedure especially stressful). Secure attachment is the majority pattern everywhere, but researchers such as Marinus van IJzendoorn caution that a procedure's meaning can shift across cultures.

Moral Development Beyond Kohlberg

Lawrence Kohlberg's stages were introduced earlier. Three later voices reshaped the field.

Carol Gilligan: an ethic of care

Carol Gilligan, once Kohlberg's collaborator, argued that his theory carried a male bias because it was built from interviews with boys and prized abstract justice and rules. Women, she proposed, more often reason from an ethic of care, emphasising relationships, responsibility, and not hurting others, and could therefore be scored "lower" on Kohlberg's scale despite reasoning maturely. Gilligan described a progression in care reasoning from concern for self, to concern for others, to a balanced care for both self and others. Later research finds smaller gender differences than she claimed, but her stress on care as a genuine moral orientation was influential.

Elliot Turiel: domain theory

Elliot Turiel argued that children do not lump all rules together as Kohlberg implied. Instead they distinguish separate domains from an early age:

  • Moral domain: matters of harm, fairness, and rights (hitting, stealing). Children judge these wrong even if no rule or authority forbids them, and wrong everywhere.
  • Social-conventional domain: agreed customs that keep society running (table manners, forms of address). These are seen as changeable and specific to a group.
  • Personal domain: matters of individual choice (which friends to have, what to wear) that are nobody else's business.

Even young children treat hitting as more seriously wrong than, say, wearing pyjamas to school, showing that moral and conventional understanding develop on separate tracks.

Nancy Eisenberg: prosocial moral reasoning

Nancy Eisenberg studied prosocial moral reasoning, how children reason about helping others when there is a personal cost and no clear rule (for example, whether to stop and help an injured child and miss a party). She traced a progression from hedonistic reasoning (help only if it benefits me), through needs-oriented reasoning (recognising the other's need), to internalised, empathy-based reasoning. She placed empathy and sympathy at the heart of moral action, not just cold logical judgement.

Emotional Development

Emotions themselves develop on a schedule. Basic emotions (joy, anger, sadness, fear, surprise, disgust) appear in the first months and are recognisable across cultures. Later, once the child has a sense of self and of standards, the self-conscious emotions emerge: embarrassment, envy, pride, shame, and guilt. These require the toddler to be aware of the self as an object others can evaluate, so they typically arrive in the second year and deepen as the child internalises rules.

Alan Sroufe and the organisation of emotion

Alan Sroufe, in the Minnesota longitudinal study, showed that early attachment security predicts later emotional and social competence, and described how emotions become organised around the attachment relationship across infancy. Securely attached children tend to regulate emotion better and form healthier peer relationships years later.

Emotion regulation

Emotion regulation is the growing ability to manage the intensity and expression of feeling. Infants depend on caregivers to soothe them (external regulation); with age children develop their own strategies, from self-soothing and looking away in infancy to distraction, reappraisal, and talking things through in later childhood. The child's grasp of display rules (when it is appropriate to show or mask a feeling, such as smiling at a disappointing gift) also matures through the school years.

Gender Development

How do children come to understand and enact being a boy or a girl? Three accounts compete and combine.

Kohlberg's gender constancy

Lawrence Kohlberg applied a cognitive stage view to gender. Children move through gender identity (labelling self and others correctly, about age two to three), gender stability (understanding gender stays the same over time, that a boy becomes a man), and finally gender constancy (understanding gender is fixed despite superficial changes in dress or activity, about age six to seven). Only once constancy is reached, Kohlberg argued, does the child actively seek out same-gender models.

Sandra Bem's gender-schema theory

Sandra Bem combined cognition with social learning. Children build a gender schema, an organised network of ideas about what goes with each sex, and then use it as a lens to filter experience, remembering schema-consistent information and shaping their own behaviour to fit. Bem is also known for the concept of androgyny (possessing both masculine and feminine traits) and the Bem Sex Role Inventory that measures it.

Social learning of gender

From the social learning view (Albert Bandura, Walter Mischel), gender behaviour is shaped by reinforcement (a boy praised for toughness, discouraged from dolls) and by observation and imitation of same-sex models, including parents, peers, and media. This account stresses the environment's direct hand, whereas Kohlberg and Bem stress the child's active mental construction.

Self-Concept, Self-Esteem, and Identity in Childhood

The self-concept is the set of beliefs a person holds about who they are. It changes shape with development. Young children describe themselves in concrete, observable terms (name, age, possessions, "I can run fast"). By middle childhood, descriptions become more psychological and comparative ("I am kind, better at maths than art"), reflecting social comparison with peers.

Susan Harter and domain-specific self-esteem

Susan Harter showed that self-esteem is not one global lump but is made of separate domains, for example scholastic competence, athletic competence, social acceptance, physical appearance, and behavioural conduct, plus an overall sense of self-worth. Crucially, global self-worth depends on competence in the domains the child values: a child who cares little for sport is not hurt by being unathletic, but a child who longs to be popular suffers if socially rejected. Harter also found physical appearance is the domain most strongly tied to overall self-worth from childhood onward.

Rule: Self-esteem = perceived competence in valued domains + social support. Doing badly at something the child does not care about barely dents self-worth.

Perceptual and Motor Development

Eleanor Gibson and Richard Walk: the visual cliff

Eleanor Gibson and Richard Walk built the visual cliff: a glass-topped table with a shallow-looking side and a deep-looking "drop" beneath the glass. Crawling infants (around six months and older) would cross the shallow side to their mothers but hesitated or refused to crawl over the apparent cliff, even though the glass was safe. This showed that depth perception is present by the time babies can crawl and helped establish that perception is far more developed in infancy than once assumed.

Reflexes and milestones

Newborns arrive with a set of automatic reflexes that are markers of neural health and, in some cases, evolutionary leftovers: the rooting reflex (turning toward a cheek touch), sucking, the grasping (palmar) reflex, the Moro or startle reflex (flinging out arms when support is lost), and the Babinski reflex (toes fanning when the sole is stroked). Most primitive reflexes fade over the first months as the cortex matures. Motor milestones then unfold in a broadly fixed order, following cephalocaudal (head-to-tail) and proximodistal (centre-to-extremities) trends: head control, then rolling, sitting, crawling, standing, and walking near the first birthday.

Esther Thelen and dynamic systems theory

Esther Thelen's dynamic systems theory challenged the idea that motor milestones simply unfold from a maturational program. She argued that each new skill (reaching, walking) is softly assembled in the moment from many interacting components: muscle strength, body weight and proportions, the infant's goal, and the specific environment. For example, the newborn stepping reflex "disappears" not because the brain switched it off but because the legs grow heavier than the young muscles can lift; hold the baby in water and the stepping returns. Development, in this view, is self-organising and highly variable, not a fixed staircase.

Who → what: Eleanor Gibson → visual cliff, depth perception, and the theory of perceptual differentiation. Esther Thelen → dynamic systems theory of motor development, skills softly assembled from many components.

The Development of Play

Play is a serious business of childhood through which children practise motor, cognitive, and social skills. Mildred Parten described a sequence of social stages of play based on how much children engage with one another:

Stage of playWhat the child does
UnoccupiedNot really playing; watching whatever catches the eye
SolitaryPlays alone, absorbed, ignoring others
OnlookerWatches other children play and may talk to them, but does not join
ParallelPlays alongside others with similar toys but not with them
AssociativePlays with others, sharing and talking, but no shared goal or roles
CooperativeOrganised play toward a common goal, with assigned roles and rules

Alongside this social sequence run cognitive types of play: functional (simple repeated movements), constructive (building and making things), make-believe or pretend play (a stick becomes a sword; important for symbolic thought and, Vygotsky argued, for self-regulation), and games with rules (which appear once children can grasp and follow shared conventions).

Developmental Psychopathology: Risk and Resilience

Developmental psychopathology is a framework, not a single disorder, that studies how normal and abnormal development unfold together over time. It insists that to understand a disorder you must understand the typical developmental pathway it departs from. Two of its guiding ideas are worth memorising:

  • Equifinality: different starting points and paths can lead to the same outcome (many routes into depression).
  • Multifinality: the same starting point can lead to very different outcomes (two abused children, one resilient, one disturbed).

Risk, protective factors, and resilience

Risk factors (poverty, maltreatment, parental mental illness) raise the odds of poor outcomes; protective factors buffer against them. Resilience is good adaptation despite serious adversity. Emmy Werner's famous longitudinal study on the Hawaiian island of Kauai followed children born into high-risk circumstances and found that about a third thrived; these resilient children tended to have easy temperaments, at least one stable, caring adult relationship, and supportive community ties. Ann Masten later called resilience "ordinary magic", arguing it arises not from rare special traits but from ordinary human protective systems (competent caregiving, self-regulation, motivation) working normally.

Attachment disorders

Severe disruption of early care, as in institutional neglect, can produce clinical attachment disorders. Reactive attachment disorder involves a child who is emotionally withdrawn, rarely seeking or responding to comfort even when distressed. Disinhibited social engagement disorder shows the opposite surface: an indiscriminately friendly child who wanders off with strangers without the normal wariness. Both are traced to a lack of consistent attachment figures in early life.

RML tilt: Match Emmy Werner with "Kauai study, resilience" and Ann Masten with "ordinary magic". Distinguish reactive attachment disorder (withdrawn) from disinhibited social engagement disorder (over-familiar with strangers).

Vygotsky in Depth: Scaffolding and Private Speech

Lev Vygotsky's zone of proximal development (the gap between what a child can do alone and what they can do with help) was introduced earlier. Three deeper ideas complete the picture.

The more knowledgeable other and scaffolding

Learning is driven by the more knowledgeable other (a parent, teacher, or more able peer) who guides the child through the zone of proximal development. David Wood, Jerome Bruner, and Gail Ross coined the term scaffolding for the support this expert provides: like a builder's scaffold, help is heavy at first and gradually withdrawn as the child gains competence, until the scaffold can be removed and the child stands alone. Good scaffolding is sensitive, offering just enough help and no more.

Trap: "Zone of proximal development" is Vygotsky's own term, but "scaffolding" was coined by Wood, Bruner, and Ross to describe the teaching that happens within it. The exam may ask specifically who named "scaffolding".

Private speech

Vygotsky observed children talking aloud to themselves while working ("put the red one here, now the blue"). He called this private speech and, against Piaget (who dismissed it as immature "egocentric speech" that simply disappears), argued it is a vital tool for self-guidance. Over time private speech goes underground, becoming the silent inner speech that adults use to plan and regulate their own behaviour. For Vygotsky, thought itself grows out of social language that is first spoken, then whispered, then internalised.

Vygotsky's chain: social speech (with others) → private speech (aloud to self) → inner speech (silent self-talk that guides thinking). Higher mental functions appear first between people, then within the individual.

The Lifespan Perspective: Paul Baltes

Paul Baltes championed the lifespan developmental perspective, insisting that development does not stop at adulthood but continues from conception to death. Its key assumptions are that development is lifelong, multidimensional (biological, cognitive, social strands), multidirectional (some capacities grow while others decline), plastic (open to change), and shaped by history and culture.

Selective optimisation with compensation

Baltes captured successful ageing in the model selective optimisation with compensation (SOC). Because resources shrink with age, the older person copes by three coordinated strategies:

  • Selection: narrowing goals to the ones that matter most.
  • Optimisation: pouring practice and energy into those chosen domains to keep them strong.
  • Compensation: using aids or new means to offset losses.

Example: the ageing concert pianist Arthur Rubinstein reported that he played fewer pieces (selection), practised them more (optimisation), and slowed down before fast passages so the contrast made them seem quicker (compensation).

Baltes also drew a useful distinction between the mechanics of intelligence (raw processing, akin to fluid intelligence, which declines with age) and the pragmatics of intelligence (accumulated knowledge, skills, and wisdom, akin to crystallised intelligence, which can grow into old age). This is why wisdom, in Baltes's research, is one of the gains that can offset the losses of ageing.

New in this expansion: information processing and Neo-Piagetians (Case); the competent infant (Baillargeon); theory of mind and the false-belief task; attachment in depth (Bowlby, Ainsworth, Main and Solomon, Harlow, Lorenz, culture); care ethic (Gilligan), domain theory (Turiel), prosocial reasoning (Eisenberg); emotional development (Sroufe); gender (Kohlberg, Bem, social learning); self-concept and Harter's self-esteem; the visual cliff and dynamic systems (Gibson, Thelen); Parten's play stages; developmental psychopathology, risk and resilience (Werner, Masten); Vygotsky's scaffolding and private speech; and Baltes's lifespan SOC model.

5 Social Psychology

Social psychology is the scientific study of how our thoughts, feelings, and behaviour are shaped by the real, imagined, or implied presence of others. It sits between the individual (personality) and the group (sociology): the same person behaves very differently alone versus in a crowd, and that difference is the subject matter here.

From scratch: Three questions organise the whole field. How do we evaluate the social world (attitudes)? How do we explain it (attribution)? And how does it change us (influence, groups, prejudice, aggression, helping)? Keep those buckets in mind and the classic studies slot in cleanly.

Attitudes

An attitude is a learned, relatively stable evaluation of a person, object, or idea. The classic tri-component (ABC) model breaks it into Affect (feelings), Behaviour (action tendencies), and Cognition (beliefs). "I feel disgust at smoking (A), I refuse cigarettes (B), and I believe it causes cancer (C)."

Attitudes form through classical conditioning (pairing), operant conditioning (reward/punishment), observational learning, and mere exposure (Robert Zajonc: familiarity breeds liking). But attitudes predict behaviour weakly, the attitude-behaviour gap. Richard LaPiere's 1934 study is the classic demonstration: a Chinese couple was served at nearly every establishment they visited, yet those same places later said in writing they would refuse Chinese guests.

Persuasion: the Elaboration Likelihood Model

Richard Petty and John Cacioppo proposed two routes to persuasion. The central route works through careful thinking about argument quality (used when motivation and ability are high) and produces lasting change. The peripheral route relies on surface cues, source attractiveness, length of message, number of arguments, and produces flimsy, temporary change.

Cognitive dissonance

Leon Festinger argued that holding two inconsistent cognitions creates an uncomfortable tension (dissonance) that we reduce by changing an attitude. In the famous Festinger & Carlsmith (1959) study, people paid only $1 to lie that a boring task was fun later rated it as more enjoyable than those paid $20, because the $1 group had no external justification and had to change their attitude to resolve the tension.

Trap: Lower reward produces more attitude change in dissonance (insufficient justification). Bigger incentive gives an external excuse, so no internal change is needed.

Attribution

Attribution is how we infer the causes of behaviour. Fritz Heider, the founder, distinguished internal (dispositional) from external (situational) causes, calling ordinary people "naive scientists."

Who → what: Heider → naive-scientist attribution; Jones & Davis → correspondent inference; Harold Kelley → covariation model.

Edward Jones and Keith Davis's correspondent inference theory asks when an act reflects a stable trait: we infer disposition when behaviour is freely chosen, unexpected (low social desirability), and produces non-common effects. Harold Kelley's covariation model says we weigh three kinds of information: consensus (do others act this way?), consistency (does this person always act this way?), and distinctiveness (only towards this target?). High consensus + high distinctiveness + high consistency → external attribution; low consensus + low distinctiveness + high consistency → internal.

Three biases recur:

  • Fundamental attribution error (FAE): over-crediting disposition and under-weighting situation when explaining others (Lee Ross's term).
  • Actor-observer bias: we explain our own behaviour situationally but others' dispositionally. "I failed because the exam was unfair; he failed because he is lazy."
  • Self-serving bias: we attribute our successes internally and our failures externally, protecting self-esteem.

Social influence

Conformity is yielding to real or imagined group pressure. Solomon Asch's line-judgement study had participants match a line to one of three comparisons; with confederates giving obviously wrong answers, about 75% conformed at least once, and roughly a third of responses matched the majority. Two motives underlie it: informational influence (others are a source of truth, especially in ambiguity) and normative influence (wanting acceptance).

Obedience is following a direct order from authority. Stanley Milgram's shock experiments found 65% of ordinary people delivered the maximum 450 volts to a screaming "learner" when instructed by an experimenter, demonstrating the power of situational authority over conscience.

Compliance techniques exploit consistency and contrast: the foot-in-the-door (a small request first makes a later large one likely) and the door-in-the-face (an unreasonable request first makes a modest follow-up seem fair).

Philip Zimbardo's Stanford Prison Experiment (1971) assigned students to be guards or prisoners; guards turned cruel and the study was halted after six days, showing how roles and situations override individual character. Muzafer Sherif earlier used the autokinetic effect (an illusory moving dot) to show groups converge on a shared norm, and his Robbers Cave study created intergroup hostility between boys' camps, then dissolved it through superordinate goals (shared tasks needing cooperation).

Group processes

Norman Triplett (1898) ran what is usually called the first social psychology experiment: cyclists rode faster with others present. This is social facilitation, the presence of others improves performance on easy/well-learned tasks but impairs it on hard ones (Zajonc explained it via heightened arousal boosting the dominant response).

Its mirror image is social loafing: people exert less effort in a group when individual contributions cannot be identified (Max Ringelmann's rope-pulling). Irving Janis coined groupthink, the drive for consensus in cohesive groups that suppresses dissent and produces bad decisions (symptoms: illusion of invulnerability, self-censorship, pressure on dissenters). Group polarization is the tendency for group discussion to push members towards a more extreme version of their initial leaning. Deindividuation is the loss of self-awareness and restraint in crowds and anonymity.

The bystander effect, that people are less likely to help as the number of onlookers grows, was studied by John Darley and Bibb Latané after the 1964 Kitty Genovese case. The mechanism is diffusion of responsibility: with many present, each person assumes someone else will act.

Prejudice

Distinguish three levels: a stereotype is a belief (cognition), prejudice is a negative attitude/feeling (affect), and discrimination is unfair behaviour. Realistic conflict theory traces prejudice to competition over scarce resources (Sherif's Robbers Cave is the evidence). Henri Tajfel and John Turner's social identity theory shows that merely categorising people into groups, even arbitrary "minimal groups," produces in-group favouritism, because we derive self-esteem from group membership. Gordon Allport's contact hypothesis holds that prejudice falls when groups meet under four conditions: equal status, common goals, cooperation, and institutional support.

Aggression

John Dollard and Neal Miller's frustration-aggression hypothesis stated that frustration (a blocked goal) always leads to aggression, later softened by Leonard Berkowitz to "frustration creates readiness for aggression, triggered by cues." Albert Bandura's Bobo doll study showed social learning: children who watched an adult beat an inflatable doll imitated that aggression, proving behaviour is learned by observation without direct reinforcement.

Prosocial behaviour

Prosocial behaviour is any act intended to benefit another; altruism is helping with no expectation of reward. Daniel Batson's empathy-altruism hypothesis argues that when we feel genuine empathy for someone, we help for their sake, not merely to relieve our own distress, supporting the existence of true altruism against purely egoistic accounts.

Classic experiments at a glance

Study / conceptWhoWhat it showed
Cyclists / social facilitationNorman Triplett (1898)First social psych experiment; others' presence speeds easy tasks
Autokinetic norms; Robbers CaveMuzafer SherifGroup norm formation; realistic conflict & superordinate goals
Line-judgement conformitySolomon Asch~75% conform once; normative & informational influence
$1/$20 boring taskLeon Festinger & CarlsmithCognitive dissonance; insufficient justification
Obedience shock studyStanley Milgram65% obey authority to 450 V
Prison rolesPhilip ZimbardoSituation/roles override character
Bystander / GenoveseDarley & LatanéDiffusion of responsibility
Minimal groupsTajfel & TurnerSocial identity; in-group bias
Bobo dollAlbert BanduraAggression learned by observation
Key. Attitudes = ABC (Affect, Behaviour, Cognition). Persuasion routes = central (thinking) vs peripheral (cues). Kelley's covariation = consensus, consistency, distinctiveness. Conformity motives = normative (acceptance) vs informational (accuracy).
RML tilt: Expect one-liners pairing a psychologist with a term (Festinger → dissonance, Milgram → obedience, Tajfel → social identity, Batson → empathy-altruism, Triplett → first experiment) and definitional MCQs separating stereotype vs prejudice vs discrimination, or FAE vs actor-observer vs self-serving bias. Memorise the headline figures: Asch ~75%, Milgram 65%.
Trap: Do not confuse the two theorist pairs of attribution: Jones & Davis = correspondent inference (single act → trait); Harold Kelley = covariation model (multiple observations). And social facilitation (others present, individual identifiable, effort up) is the opposite of social loafing (group effort, individual hidden, effort down).
Recap: Attitudes are ABC evaluations that predict behaviour weakly (LaPiere), change through the ELM's central/peripheral routes (Petty & Cacioppo) and dissonance (Festinger). We explain behaviour as naive scientists (Heider) via correspondent inference (Jones & Davis) and covariation (Kelley), skewed by the FAE, actor-observer, and self-serving biases. Influence runs from conformity (Asch) and norm formation (Sherif) through obedience (Milgram), compliance tricks, and roles (Zimbardo). Groups show facilitation (Triplett), loafing, groupthink (Janis), polarization, deindividuation, and the bystander effect (Darley & Latané). Prejudice grows from competition (realistic conflict) and categorisation (Tajfel & Turner) and shrinks through Allport's contact conditions. Aggression is frustration-driven (Dollard & Miller) and learned (Bandura), while empathy fuels genuine altruism (Batson).

The Self

Social psychology treats the self as both the knower (the "I" who experiences) and the known (the "me" that is thought about). The topics below cover how people build a picture of themselves, evaluate it, and manage it in front of others.

From scratch: A self-concept is the total set of beliefs a person holds about who they are. It is organised into self-schemas: mental frameworks about specific traits (for example "I am athletic" or "I am shy") that guide how quickly and confidently we process self-relevant information. If a trait is central to you, you are said to be schematic on it; if it is irrelevant, you are aschematic.

Self-esteem is the evaluative and emotional part of the self: how positively or negatively a person judges their own worth. It differs from self-concept (the description) by adding a value judgement (the appraisal). Self-awareness is the state of focusing attention on the self as an object; it rises in front of a mirror, a camera, or an audience and tends to make behaviour match personal standards more closely.

Key theorists of the self

Who → what: Daryl Bem → self-perception theory: when internal cues are weak or ambiguous, people infer their own attitudes and feelings by observing their own behaviour, exactly as an outside observer would ("I have eaten three biscuits, so I must like them"). This is the reverse of the usual assumption that attitudes drive behaviour, and it is the main rival explanation to cognitive dissonance.
Who → what: Leon Festinger → social comparison theory: people evaluate their own opinions and abilities by comparing themselves with others, especially when objective yardsticks are missing. Upward comparison (with someone better) can motivate or deflate; downward comparison (with someone worse) can protect self-esteem.
Who → what: Mark Snyder → self-monitoring: a personality dimension describing how much people adjust their behaviour to fit the social situation. High self-monitors read the room and change accordingly; low self-monitors act from inner attitudes regardless of audience.

Self-serving biases and strategies

  • Self-serving bias: the tendency to take credit for successes (internal attribution) but blame failures on external causes. It protects self-esteem.
  • Self-fulfilling prophecy: an initially false expectation that causes the very behaviour that makes it come true. Demonstrated by Robert Rosenthal and Lenore Jacobson in the "Pygmalion in the classroom" study, where teachers' raised expectations lifted pupils' actual performance.
  • Self-handicapping: creating an obstacle to one's own success (for example not studying, or drinking before a test) so that failure can be blamed on the obstacle rather than on low ability, while success looks even more impressive.
Trap: Do not confuse self-serving bias (protecting the self after an outcome) with the fundamental attribution error (misjudging other people). Self-serving bias is about you; the attribution error is about them.

Social Cognition & Perception

Social cognition is the study of how people select, store, and use information about other people and social situations. Because we cannot process everything, we rely on schemas (organised knowledge structures) and mental shortcuts.

Impression formation

Who → what: Solomon Asch → classic impression-formation work. He showed a primacy effect: traits presented first weigh more heavily than later ones. He also identified central traits (such as "warm" versus "cold") that reshape the whole impression, versus peripheral traits (such as "polite" versus "blunt") that change it little.

The halo effect is a related bias: one positive quality (often physical attractiveness) leads us to assume other unrelated positive qualities, so good-looking people are rated as kinder or more competent without evidence.

Heuristics in social judgement

Heuristics are quick rules of thumb, described by Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, that usually work but can bias judgement.

HeuristicRule of thumbExample error
AvailabilityJudge frequency by how easily examples come to mindOverrating plane-crash risk after news coverage
RepresentativenessJudge by similarity to a typical case, ignoring base ratesAssuming a quiet man is a librarian, not a farmer
AnchoringStay near an initial number when estimatingA high starting price inflating a final offer

Stereotypes can be seen as cognitive shortcuts: category-based beliefs that save effort but overgeneralise and feed prejudice. Priming is the process by which a recent or frequent concept becomes temporarily more accessible and shapes how the next person or event is interpreted, often outside awareness.

Attribution, re-framed as social cognition

Two well-known attribution biases are really products of how we process social information:

  • Fundamental attribution error: overestimating personality and underestimating the situation when explaining others' behaviour.
  • Actor-observer bias: attributing our own behaviour to the situation, but others' behaviour to their character, partly because we literally see the situation from our own viewpoint.
RML tilt: Expect a matching-type item pairing each heuristic or bias with its definition, and a single-line question naming the theorist (Asch for central traits, Bem for self-perception, Snyder for self-monitoring). Learn the names, not just the ideas.

Interpersonal Attraction & Close Relationships

Determinants of attraction

  • Proximity and mere exposure: we like those who are physically near, largely through the mere exposure effect (Robert Zajonc): repeated contact with a stimulus increases liking.
  • Physical attractiveness: a powerful first-stage cue, amplified by the halo effect.
  • Similarity: shared attitudes, values, and background predict attraction far more than the folk idea that "opposites attract".
  • Reciprocity: we tend to like those who like us.

Theories of love and bonding

Who → what: Robert Sternberg → the triangular theory of love. Three components combine into different kinds of love: intimacy (closeness, warmth), passion (physical and emotional arousal), and commitment (the decision to maintain the relationship). All three together give consummate love.

A common distinction is between passionate love (intense, arousing, often early in a relationship) and companionate love (steady affection and deep friendship that endures).

Who → what: Cindy Hazan and Phillip Shaver → extended attachment theory to adults, proposing that adult romantic bonds echo infant attachment. Adults show secure, anxious (ambivalent), or avoidant styles that shape trust and intimacy.

Economic models of relationships

Social exchange theory analyses relationships as a ledger of rewards minus costs, judged against a comparison level (what we expect) and a comparison level for alternatives (what else is available). Equity theory adds that people are most satisfied when the ratio of their own outcomes to inputs feels roughly fair compared with the partner's.

Key. Attraction is driven by proximity, similarity, physical cues, and reciprocity; love is best split into Sternberg's intimacy, passion, and commitment; long relationships run on companionate love, fair exchange, and secure attachment.

Applied Social Psychology (Brief)

Applied social psychology uses these principles to solve real problems in specific settings.

  • Health: attitude-change and social-norm campaigns promote vaccination, exercise, and safe behaviour; perceived social support buffers stress and improves recovery.
  • Environment: feedback, commitment, and descriptive norms ("most guests reuse their towels") are used to cut energy use and encourage conservation.
  • Law: research on eyewitness memory, line-up construction, jury decision-making, and conformity within juries informs fairer legal procedures.
Recap: The self is described by self-concept and self-schemas, valued through self-esteem, and inferred through Bem's self-perception and Festinger's social comparison, then defended by self-serving bias and self-handicapping. Social cognition runs on schemas, Asch's primacy and central traits, the halo effect, and the availability, representativeness, and anchoring heuristics. Attraction rests on proximity, similarity, and reciprocity; Sternberg's triangle and Hazan and Shaver's adult attachment explain love; and applied social psychology carries all of this into health, environment, and law.

Attitude Formation and the Deep Theories of Attitude Change

An attitude is a learned, relatively enduring evaluation of a person, object, or idea that predisposes a person to respond in a favourable or unfavourable way. Psychologists describe attitudes with the ABC model: an Affective component (feelings), a Behavioural component (action tendencies), and a Cognitive component (beliefs). The existing chapter introduced the elaboration likelihood model and dissonance in outline. This section teaches the full family of change theories from scratch so that no exam question on the topic can catch you.

From scratch: The core puzzle of attitude research is consistency. Humans dislike holding beliefs, feelings, and behaviours that clash with one another. Almost every classic theory below is a different answer to one question: what does a person do when two mental elements do not fit together?

Fritz Heider: Balance Theory

Fritz Heider (1946, 1958) proposed balance theory, the earliest of the cognitive-consistency theories. It concerns a triad of relations between a Person (P), an Other (O), and an attitude object (X). Each link between two elements is either positive (liking, agreement) or negative (disliking, disagreement). A triad is balanced when the product of the three signs is positive, and unbalanced when the product is negative. Balanced states are stable and pleasant; unbalanced states create tension that pushes the person to change one relation to restore balance.

Example: You (P) like a friend (O). Your friend praises a film (X) that you dislike. The signs are P–O positive, O–X positive, P–X negative; the product is negative, so the triad is unbalanced. You can restore balance by starting to like the film, by deciding you dislike the friend, or by deciding the friend does not really care for the film.

Rule: "A friend of my friend is my friend; an enemy of my enemy is my friend." Balanced triads have an even number (zero or two) of negative links.

Leon Festinger: Cognitive Dissonance and the Classic $1 versus $20 Study

Leon Festinger (1957) argued that when a person holds two psychologically inconsistent cognitions, an uncomfortable drive state called cognitive dissonance arises, and the person is motivated to reduce it by changing a cognition, adding new consonant cognitions, or trivialising the conflict. The landmark demonstration is Festinger and James Carlsmith (1959). Participants performed a genuinely boring peg-turning task, then were paid either $1 or $20 to tell a waiting participant that the task was enjoyable. Counter-intuitively, the $1 group later rated the task as more enjoyable than the $20 group.

From scratch: The $20 participants had a large external justification ("I lied for the money"), so little dissonance. The $1 participants had insufficient justification for lying, so they resolved the dissonance between "the task was dull" and "I said it was fun" by genuinely revising their attitude: "it actually was quite interesting." This is induced compliance, and it shows that attitudes follow behaviour when external justification is weak.

Dissonance also explains effort justification (Aronson and Mills, 1959: people who endured a severe initiation valued a dull group more), post-decision dissonance (chosen alternatives become more attractive after a decision, the "spreading of alternatives"), and the free-choice paradigm.

Daryl Bem: Self-Perception Theory

Daryl Bem (1967) offered a rival explanation for the same findings. Self-perception theory holds that people do not always have privileged access to their own attitudes; instead, when internal cues are weak or ambiguous, they infer their attitudes by observing their own behaviour and the situation, exactly as an outside observer would.
