The $1 participant reasons, "I said it was interesting for almost no money, so I must have found it interesting." No tension or drive is required.

Trap: Do not treat dissonance and self-perception as simply "right versus wrong." The modern resolution (Fazio, Zanna, Cooper) is that dissonance operates when behaviour is discrepant with a clearly held attitude (producing real arousal), while self-perception operates in the ambiguous middle range where the person has no strong prior attitude.

Carl Hovland: The Yale Attitude-Change Approach

Carl Hovland and the Yale Communication and Attitude Change Program (1950s) launched the systematic study of persuasion, framed as "who says what to whom." They isolated three sets of variables. Source factors: credibility (expertise and trustworthiness) and attractiveness. Hovland and Weiss demonstrated the sleeper effect: a message from a low-credibility source has little immediate impact, but its persuasive power can grow over time as the message dissociates from its discredited source. Message factors: one-sided versus two-sided arguments, order of presentation (primacy versus recency), and emotional appeals. Audience factors: intelligence, self-esteem, and initial position.

Fear Appeals

A fear appeal is a persuasive message that arouses fear about a threat to motivate protective behaviour (anti-smoking, road-safety, health campaigns). Early work suggested moderate fear works best, and that fear appeals succeed only when they also provide a clear, achievable recommendation for avoiding the threat. Ronald Rogers systematised this in Protection Motivation Theory: persuasion depends on perceived severity of the threat, perceived vulnerability, response efficacy (the action works), and self-efficacy (I can do the action). Very high fear without an efficacy route triggers defensive avoidance rather than action.

The Attitude–Behaviour Gap: Reasoned Action and Planned Behaviour

Attitudes often predict behaviour poorly (the famous LaPiere study of the 1930s found restaurateurs served a Chinese couple in person yet said in writing they would not). Martin Fishbein and Icek Ajzen addressed this with the Theory of Reasoned Action (TRA): the immediate predictor of behaviour is behavioural intention, which is determined by (1) one's attitude toward the specific behaviour and (2) subjective norms (perceived social pressure). Ajzen later extended it into the Theory of Planned Behaviour (TPB) by adding perceived behavioural control (how easy or hard the person believes the behaviour is), which improves prediction for behaviours not fully under voluntary control.

Rule: Attitudes predict behaviour best when they are specific to the behaviour, strong and accessible, based on direct experience, and measured close in time to the action (the principle of compatibility).

Attribution: How People Explain Behaviour, in Depth

Attribution is the process of inferring the causes of one's own and others' behaviour. The existing chapter summarised the topic; here each formal model is taught in full.

Fritz Heider: Naive Psychology and the Dispositional–Situational Split

Fritz Heider (1958), often called the father of attribution theory, described ordinary people as "naive scientists" who seek stable causes for events. His key distinction is between internal (dispositional) causes, located in the person (ability, effort, personality), and external (situational) causes, located in the environment (luck, task difficulty, social pressure).

Harold Kelley: The Covariation Model and Causal Schemata

Harold Kelley (1967) proposed the covariation model for cases where we have information across time and situations. We attribute an effect to the factor with which it covaries, using three kinds of information:

InformationQuestion it answersHigh value points to
ConsensusDo other people react the same way to this stimulus?External (stimulus) cause
DistinctivenessDoes this person react this way only to this stimulus?External (stimulus) cause
ConsistencyDoes this person react this way every time?Needed for any stable attribution

High consensus + high distinctiveness + high consistency yields an external attribution; low consensus + low distinctiveness + high consistency yields an internal (person) attribution. Kelley added causal schemata for single-observation cases, including the discounting principle (the role of a given cause is discounted if other plausible causes are present) and the augmenting principle (a cause is weighted more heavily if it produced an effect despite an inhibitory factor).

Edward Jones and Keith Davis: Correspondent Inference Theory

Edward Jones and Keith Davis (1965) explained how we infer that a behaviour corresponds to a stable disposition from a single act. Correspondent inferences are stronger when the behaviour is freely chosen, produces non-common effects (outcomes unique to that choice), is low in social desirability (unexpected behaviour is more informative), and is high in hedonic relevance to the perceiver.

Bernard Weiner: Attribution of Achievement

Bernard Weiner (1972, 1985) applied attribution to success and failure in achievement settings. He classified causes along dimensions: locus (internal versus external), stability (stable versus unstable over time), and controllability. The four classic causes are ability (internal, stable), effort (internal, unstable), task difficulty (external, stable), and luck (external, unstable). The stability dimension governs expectancy of future outcomes; the locus and controllability dimensions govern emotions such as pride, shame, and gratitude. Attributing failure to lack of effort (unstable, controllable) is adaptive; attributing it to lack of ability (stable) fosters learned helplessness.

Systematic Attribution Biases

Fundamental attribution error (Lee Ross, 1977): observers overestimate dispositional causes and underestimate situational ones when explaining others' behaviour. Actor–observer bias (Jones and Nisbett): we attribute our own behaviour to the situation but others' identical behaviour to their disposition, partly because of differing perceptual focus. Self-serving bias: we attribute our successes internally and our failures externally, protecting self-esteem. Just-world hypothesis (Melvin Lerner, 1980): the belief that the world is fair and people get what they deserve, which leads observers to blame innocent victims ("she must have done something").

RML tilt: Expect a matching item pairing the theorist to the model: Heider → naive psychology/balance, Kelley → covariation (consensus/distinctiveness/consistency), Jones and Davis → correspondent inference, Weiner → achievement attribution, Lerner → just-world.

Social Influence: Conformity, Obedience, and Minority Power in Depth

Muzafer Sherif and Solomon Asch: Two Faces of Conformity

Muzafer Sherif (1935) used the autokinetic effect (a stationary point of light appears to move in a dark room). Individual estimates of the movement converged over trials into a shared group norm that persisted even when participants were later tested alone, demonstrating informational social influence in an ambiguous situation. Solomon Asch (1951) removed the ambiguity: participants judged which of three comparison lines matched a standard, an easy perceptual task. With confederates unanimously giving a wrong answer, about 75% of participants conformed at least once and roughly a third of responses overall were conforming, revealing normative social influence, conforming to be accepted despite knowing the answer.

Rule: Informational influence produces genuine private acceptance and rises with ambiguity; normative influence produces public compliance to gain approval and falls sharply when even one ally breaks the unanimity (Asch found a single dissenter cut conformity dramatically).

Stanley Milgram: Obedience and Its Variations

Stanley Milgram (1963) studied destructive obedience to authority. Participants ("teachers") were ordered by an experimenter to deliver escalating "shocks" to a learner. In the baseline, 65% went to the maximum 450 volts. Milgram ran many variations that reveal the causes: obedience fell when the experimenter gave orders by telephone, when the study moved from Yale to a run-down office, when the learner was in the same room (proximity), and above all when a peer confederate defied the experimenter. Milgram explained results through the agentic state, in which a person sees themselves as an instrument carrying out another's wishes and shifts responsibility upward.

Philip Zimbardo: The Stanford Prison Experiment

Philip Zimbardo (1971) randomly assigned students to be "guards" or "prisoners" in a simulated prison. The study, planned for two weeks, was halted after six days because guards became abusive and prisoners showed extreme stress. Zimbardo argued that situational forces and assigned social roles, plus deindividuation, can override individual dispositions. (Note the modern critique: demand characteristics and explicit guard coaching mean the effect was less "spontaneous" than first claimed.)

Serge Moscovici: Minority Influence

Serge Moscovici (1969) showed that a numerical minority can influence a majority through consistency (holding firmly to a position over time and within the group). In his blue-green colour study, a consistent minority calling blue slides "green" led some majority members to shift. Minority influence tends to produce conversion (deep, private, delayed attitude change through the validation process) rather than the surface compliance produced by majorities.

Compliance Techniques and Reactance

Foot-in-the-door (Freedman and Fraser): agreeing to a small request raises the chance of agreeing to a larger one later, via the consistency principle. Door-in-the-face (Cialdini): a large request is refused, then a smaller one is accepted, via reciprocal concessions. Reactance (Jack Brehm, 1966): when people feel a freedom is threatened or removed, they experience an aroused motivational state to reassert it, sometimes doing the very opposite of what was demanded.

Group Processes in Depth

Norman Triplett and Robert Zajonc: Social Facilitation

Norman Triplett (1898) ran what is often called social psychology's first experiment, finding cyclists rode faster when racing others. Robert Zajonc (1965) resolved contradictory findings with drive theory: the mere presence of others raises arousal, which strengthens the dominant response. On easy or well-learned tasks the dominant response is correct, so presence improves performance (social facilitation); on hard or novel tasks the dominant response is often wrong, so presence impairs performance (social inhibition).

Bibb Latane: Social Loafing

Bibb Latane, Kipling Williams, and Stephen Harkins (1979) documented social loafing: individuals exert less effort on a collective task (rope-pulling, clapping, shouting) when their individual contribution cannot be identified. It is reduced by making contributions identifiable, increasing task meaningfulness, and keeping groups small.

Irving Janis: Groupthink

Irving Janis (1972) coined groupthink: a mode of thinking in highly cohesive, insulated groups under a directive leader where the drive for unanimity overrides realistic appraisal of alternatives. Symptoms include the illusion of invulnerability, collective rationalisation, belief in the group's morality, stereotyping of out-groups, self-censorship, an illusion of unanimity, direct pressure on dissenters, and self-appointed "mindguards." Historical cases include the Bay of Pigs invasion.

Group Polarization, Risky Shift, and Deindividuation

Group polarization is the tendency for group discussion to strengthen the members' initially dominant leaning, producing a more extreme group decision; the risky shift is its earliest-noticed form (Stoner). Deindividuation (built on Le Bon and developed by Festinger, Pepitone, Newcomb, and Zimbardo) is a loss of self-awareness and individual accountability in groups or anonymous conditions, lowering restraints on impulsive and antisocial behaviour.

Muzafer Sherif: The Robbers Cave Study

Muzafer Sherif (1954) ran the Robbers Cave field study with two groups of boys at a summer camp. Competition for scarce resources rapidly produced hostility (support for realistic conflict theory), and simple contact did not reduce it. Only superordinate goals, shared aims that required both groups to cooperate (fixing the water supply, pulling a stalled truck), reduced intergroup conflict.

Kurt Lewin: Leadership Styles

Kurt Lewin, Ronald Lippitt, and Ralph White (1939) compared autocratic, democratic, and laissez-faire leadership in boys' clubs. Democratic leadership produced the most satisfaction and sustained productivity; autocratic leadership produced high output only under supervision plus aggression or apathy; laissez-faire produced the least work.

Prejudice and Intergroup Relations in Depth

Realistic Conflict Theory

Realistic conflict theory (Sherif; Donald Campbell) holds that intergroup hostility arises from real competition over limited resources (jobs, land, status). Robbers Cave is its central demonstration.

Henri Tajfel: Social Identity Theory and the Minimal Group Paradigm

Henri Tajfel and John Turner (1979) proposed social identity theory: part of our self-concept comes from group memberships, and we boost self-esteem through favourable in-group/out-group comparisons. In the minimal group paradigm, Tajfel showed that merely categorising strangers into arbitrary groups (even by a coin toss or a preference for one painter) was enough to trigger in-group favouritism in resource allocation, with no competition or prior contact required. The process runs categorisation → identification → comparison.

Gordon Allport: The Contact Hypothesis

Gordon Allport (1954) argued that contact between groups reduces prejudice only under four conditions: equal status within the situation, common goals, intergroup cooperation, and support of authorities, law, or custom. Mere contact without these can worsen prejudice.

Claude Steele: Stereotype Threat

Claude Steele and Joshua Aronson (1995) demonstrated stereotype threat: when a negative stereotype about one's group is made salient, the fear of confirming it disrupts performance. African-American students scored lower on a test framed as diagnostic of ability, but not when the same test was framed as non-diagnostic; the effect also appears for women in mathematics.

Scapegoat Theory and the Authoritarian Personality

Scapegoat theory links prejudice to displaced frustration onto a weaker out-group. Theodor Adorno and colleagues (1950) proposed the authoritarian personality, measured by the F-scale, a syndrome of rigid conventionalism, submission to authority, and hostility to out-groups, rooted in harsh childhood discipline.

Aggression: Theories from Scratch

John Dollard, Neal Miller and colleagues (1939) proposed the frustration–aggression hypothesis: frustration (blocked goal-directed behaviour) always produces a readiness to aggress, and aggression is always preceded by frustration. Leonard Berkowitz revised it: frustration produces anger, which yields aggression only when aggressive cues are present (the weapons effect). Albert Bandura's Bobo doll studies (1961) showed aggression is learned through observation and imitation of models, especially when the model is rewarded (social learning theory). The General Aggression Model (Anderson and Bushman) integrates person and situation inputs acting through arousal, cognition, and affect. The catharsis hypothesis, the idea that venting aggression discharges it, is largely a myth: acting aggressively tends to increase, not decrease, later aggression.

Prosocial Behaviour in Depth

John Darley and Bibb Latane: The Bystander Effect

Spurred by the Kitty Genovese case, John Darley and Bibb Latane (1968, 1970) showed the bystander effect: the more bystanders present, the less likely any one of them helps. Their five-step decision model requires the bystander to (1) notice the event, (2) interpret it as an emergency (blocked by pluralistic ignorance, where each person, seeing others calm, decides it is not serious), (3) assume responsibility (blocked by diffusion of responsibility across the group), (4) know how to help, and (5) decide to act.

Models of Helping

The arousal: cost–reward model (Piliavin) holds that witnessing distress creates unpleasant arousal that the bystander is motivated to reduce, weighing the costs of helping against the costs of not helping. Daniel Batson's empathy–altruism hypothesis argues that feeling genuine empathy for a sufferer produces truly altruistic motivation to help for the other's sake, not merely to relieve one's own distress. Evolutionary accounts add kin selection (helping relatives preserves shared genes) and reciprocal altruism (helping non-kin who may help back).

Interpersonal Attraction and Close Relationships in Depth

Robert Sternberg: The Triangular Theory of Love

Robert Sternberg (1986) proposed that love has three components: intimacy (closeness and warmth), passion (physical and romantic arousal), and commitment (the decision to maintain the relationship). Their combinations yield seven kinds of love: liking (intimacy alone), infatuation (passion alone), empty love (commitment alone), romantic love (intimacy + passion), companionate love (intimacy + commitment), fatuous love (passion + commitment), and consummate love (all three).

Cindy Hazan and Phillip Shaver: Adult Attachment Styles

Cindy Hazan and Phillip Shaver (1987) extended Bowlby and Ainsworth's infant attachment to adult romantic relationships, describing three styles: secure (comfortable with closeness and trust), anxious/ambivalent (craves closeness, fears abandonment, clingy), and avoidant (uncomfortable with intimacy and dependence). Adult attachment style predicts jealousy, trust, and relationship stability.

Economic and Perceptual Determinants

Social exchange theory (Thibaut and Kelley) treats relationships as an exchange in which people seek to maximise rewards and minimise costs, judging satisfaction against a comparison level and commitment against the comparison level for alternatives. Equity theory (Walster/Hatfield) adds that people are most satisfied when the ratio of each partner's outcomes to inputs feels fair, and both under-benefited and over-benefited partners feel distress. The matching hypothesis holds that people tend to form relationships with others of similar physical attractiveness. The mere exposure effect (Robert Zajonc) shows that repeated exposure to a stimulus, including a person, increases liking, which is why proximity is such a strong predictor of friendship.

Consistency theories: Heider (balance), Festinger (dissonance, $1 > $20), Bem (self-perception). Persuasion: Hovland/Yale, Petty and Cacioppo (ELM), Rogers (protection motivation). Attitude–behaviour gap: Fishbein and Ajzen (reasoned action, planned behaviour). Attribution: Heider, Kelley (covariation), Jones and Davis (correspondent inference), Weiner (achievement), Lerner (just-world). Influence: Sherif (informational), Asch (normative), Milgram (obedience/agentic state), Zimbardo (roles), Moscovici (minority/consistency), Brehm (reactance). Groups: Triplett and Zajonc (facilitation), Latane (loafing/bystander), Janis (groupthink), Sherif (Robbers Cave/superordinate goals), Lewin (leadership). Prejudice: Tajfel and Turner (social identity/minimal group), Allport (contact), Steele (stereotype threat), Adorno (authoritarian personality). Aggression: Dollard (frustration), Bandura (Bobo doll), catharsis is a myth. Prosocial: Darley and Latane (bystander/diffusion), Batson (empathy–altruism). Relationships: Sternberg (triangular love), Hazan and Shaver (adult attachment).

6 Personality

Personality is the enduring, organised pattern of thoughts, feelings and behaviours that makes a person distinctive and relatively consistent across time and situations. The word comes from the Latin persona (the mask worn by an actor). For the RML CET-166 paper the safest way to master this domain is by theorist → construct: they rarely ask "what is personality?" and very often ask "who proposed the inferiority complex?" This section is organised into four families plus a short assessment link.

From scratch: Two research styles run through all trait work. The nomothetic approach (from Greek nomos, law) seeks universal dimensions that apply to everyone; the idiographic approach studies the unique individual. Trait psychologists differ mainly in how many universal dimensions they claim exist.

1. Trait & Type Theories

Who → what: Gordon Allport → the first modern trait theory. He distinguished cardinal traits (a single dominating disposition that rules a life, e.g. a "Scrooge-like" greed, rare), central traits (the 5–10 core descriptors we would list in a reference letter) and secondary traits (situational preferences, e.g. anxiety only in exams). Allport stressed the idiographic study of the individual and coined the term functional autonomy (a motive can outlive its original cause).
Who → what: Raymond B. Cattell → used factor analysis to reduce Allport's list to 16 source traits. He separated surface traits (clusters of behaviour we observe directly) from source traits (the deeper underlying factors that cause them). His test is the 16PF (Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire).
Who → what: Hans Eysenck → argued personality reduces to just a few biologically-based dimensions. His PEN model has three: Psychoticism, Extraversion and Neuroticism (originally only E and N, plus Psychoticism added later). He gave each a biological basis: Extraversion reflects low cortical arousal (extraverts seek stimulation to reach an optimum), and Neuroticism reflects a labile autonomic/limbic system. His measures include the EPQ (Eysenck Personality Questionnaire).
Who → what: Paul Costa & Robert McCrae → the Big Five / Five-Factor Model (FFM), remembered by the acronym OCEAN: Openness to experience, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness and Neuroticism. Their test is the NEO-PI-R (Revised NEO Personality Inventory).
Who → what: William Sheldon → somatotypes, a constitutional (body-type) theory linking physique to temperament: the round endomorph (viscerotonic, sociable, food-loving), the muscular mesomorph (somatotonic, assertive, energetic) and the thin ectomorph (cerebrotonic, restrained, introverted).
Key. Count the dimensions: Cattell = 16, Eysenck = 3 (PEN), Costa & McCrae = 5 (OCEAN). Only Neuroticism and Extraversion appear in all the modern models.
Trap: The 16PF belongs to Cattell, not Eysenck; the NEO-PI belongs to Costa & McCrae; the EPQ belongs to Eysenck. Examiners love to swap the test onto the wrong theorist.

2. Psychodynamic Theories

From scratch: Psychodynamic theories assume behaviour is driven by unconscious forces and early experience. They begin with Freud, and the "neo-Freudians" are those who trained in or reacted against him and then softened his emphasis on sexuality.
Who → what: Sigmund Freud → the founder of psychoanalysis. His structural model has three parts: the id (unconscious, pleasure principle, primary process), the ego (reality principle, mediator), and the superego (conscience and ego-ideal, morality). His topographical model divides the mind into conscious, preconscious (accessible) and unconscious (repressed). The energy driving all this is the libido (sexual/life energy). When the ego is threatened it deploys defence mechanisms such as repression, projection, denial, displacement, reaction formation, rationalisation and sublimation.
TheoristSchoolKey idea
Carl JungAnalytical psychologyCollective unconscious & archetypes (persona, shadow, anima/animus, self); coined introversion/extraversion
Alfred AdlerIndividual psychologyInferiority complex, striving for superiority, birth order, style of life
Karen HorneyFeminine/social psychoanalysisBasic anxiety, ten neurotic needs (moving toward/against/away), womb envy
Erik EriksonPsychosocial (ego psychology)Eight psychosocial stages across the whole lifespan; identity crisis
Harry Stack SullivanInterpersonal theoryPersonality = the pattern of interpersonal relationships; the "self-system"
Erich FrommHumanistic psychoanalysisFreedom vs security; "escape from freedom"; social/character orientations
Trap: Penis envy is Freud's term; womb envy (men's envy of women's capacity to bear children) is Karen Horney's rebuttal. Do not confuse them. Also, introversion/extraversion as personality attitudes originate with Jung, even though Eysenck later measured Extraversion.
Key. "Collective unconscious & archetypes" = Jung. "Inferiority & birth order" = Adler. "Psychosocial stages" = Erikson. "Interpersonal" = Sullivan. Lock these one-liners.

3. Humanistic Theories

From scratch: The "third force" (after psychoanalysis and behaviourism) rejected determinism and stressed free will, growth and the healthy person's potential.
Who → what: Carl Rogers → person-centred theory. The self-concept is our organised view of ourselves; wellbeing depends on congruence between the real self and the ideal self. Growth requires unconditional positive regard (acceptance without conditions of worth); the goal is the fully functioning person.
Who → what: Abraham Maslow → the hierarchy of needs, usually drawn as a pyramid: physiological → safety → love/belonging → esteem → self-actualization at the top. Lower deficiency needs must be reasonably met before the higher growth need to fulfil one's potential emerges.
Trap: Self-actualization as the peak of the need hierarchy is Maslow; the fully functioning person is Rogers. Both are humanists, but the labelled endpoints differ.

4. Social-Cognitive & Behavioural Theories

Who → what: Albert Bandura → social-cognitive theory. His central concept is reciprocal determinism: person, behaviour and environment continuously influence one another. He is also known for self-efficacy (belief in one's ability to succeed at a task) and observational learning (the Bobo doll studies).
Who → what: Julian Rotter → locus of control (internal: outcomes depend on my own effort; external: outcomes depend on luck, fate or powerful others) and expectancy (behaviour potential = expectancy × reinforcement value).
Who → what: Walter Mischel → launched the person–situation debate (in Personality and Assessment, arguing behaviour is less cross-situationally consistent than traits imply), and ran the marshmallow test on delay of gratification in children.
Who → what: George Kelly → personal construct theory. People are "scientists" who anticipate events through bipolar personal constructs (e.g. kind–cruel); assessed with the Rep Test (Role Construct Repertory Test).
Key. Self-efficacy = Bandura; locus of control = Rotter; marshmallow / person-situation debate = Mischel; personal constructs = Kelly.

5. Assessment Link (brief)

Self-report inventories present structured items scored against norms: the MMPI (Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory) for clinical psychopathology, the 16PF (Cattell) and the NEO-PI-R (Costa & McCrae). Projective tests present ambiguous stimuli onto which the person "projects" inner content: the Rorschach inkblot test (Hermann Rorschach) and the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) (Henry Murray & Christiana Morgan).

Trap: Self-report inventories are objective/structured; the Rorschach and TAT are projective. Do not label the MMPI "projective." Full scoring and validity detail sits in the Assessment section.
RML tilt: Expect one-mark "match the theorist to the construct" items far more than essay-style definitions. Drill the acronyms and their owners: PEN → Eysenck, OCEAN/FFM → Costa & McCrae, 16PF → Cattell. Recurrent RML-style hooks: id/ego/superego and defence mechanisms (Freud), collective unconscious (Jung), inferiority complex & birth order (Adler), womb envy (Horney), unconditional positive regard (Rogers), hierarchy of needs (Maslow), self-efficacy (Bandura) and locus of control (Rotter).
Recap: Four families. Trait/type: Allport (cardinal/central/secondary, idiographic), Cattell (16 source traits, 16PF), Eysenck (PEN, biological arousal), Costa & McCrae (Big Five/OCEAN, NEO-PI), Sheldon (endo/meso/ecto somatotypes). Psychodynamic: Freud (id/ego/superego, conscious–preconscious–unconscious, libido, defences), then Jung (collective unconscious, archetypes), Adler (inferiority, birth order), Horney (basic anxiety, womb envy), Erikson (psychosocial), Sullivan (interpersonal), Fromm (freedom vs security). Humanistic: Rogers (real vs ideal self, unconditional positive regard, fully functioning person) and Maslow (hierarchy of needs, self-actualization). Social-cognitive/behavioural: Bandura (reciprocal determinism, self-efficacy), Rotter (locus of control, expectancy), Mischel (person–situation debate, marshmallow test), Kelly (personal constructs). Assessment: self-report (MMPI, 16PF, NEO-PI) vs projective (Rorschach, TAT).

6. Biological & Genetic Approaches

Where trait theories describe personality and psychodynamic theories root it in early conflict, biological approaches ask a blunter question: what in the nervous system and the genome makes one person reliably calmer, bolder, or more anxious than another? The answer is that stable individual differences have measurable physiological and inherited substrates.

From scratch: A "biological substrate" simply means a bodily mechanism (a brain circuit, a chemical, an inherited tendency) that a psychological trait sits on top of. If a trait is biological, it should appear early, be fairly stable, and run in families.

Hans Eysenck's PEN model and cortical arousal

Hans Eysenck argued that personality reduces to a small number of biologically based dimensions. His mature model, PEN, has three: Psychoticism (tough-minded, aggressive, cold), Extraversion (sociable, stimulation-seeking), and Neuroticism (emotionally unstable, anxious). Each is a continuum on which everyone falls somewhere.

His signature claim was about the biology of extraversion. The ascending reticular activating system (ARAS), a network in the brainstem, regulates cortical arousal (how "switched on" the cortex is). Eysenck proposed that introverts have chronically higher baseline cortical arousal, so they are already near their comfortable ceiling and avoid extra stimulation. Extraverts have lower baseline arousal, so they seek stimulation (company, noise, risk) to top themselves up. Neuroticism he tied to the limbic system and its threshold for emotional reactivity.

PEN dimensionHigh-scorerProposed biology
ExtraversionSociable, sensation-seekingLow ARAS arousal → seeks stimulation
NeuroticismAnxious, moodyReactive limbic system, low emotional threshold
PsychoticismCold, impulsive, aggressiveLinked (loosely) to testosterone, low serotonin
Trap: Introverts are the high-arousal group, not the low. Students often reverse this because introverts seem "quieter." The logic is that they are already aroused, so they retreat from more stimulation.

Jeffrey Gray's Reinforcement Sensitivity Theory (BIS/BAS)

Jeffrey Gray, a student of Eysenck, proposed that arousal is the wrong axis. He described two brain systems sensitive to reward and punishment. The Behavioural Activation System (BAS) responds to signals of reward and drives approach, impulsivity, and positive affect. The Behavioural Inhibition System (BIS) responds to signals of punishment, novelty, and non-reward, producing anxiety, caution, and stopping. (Gray later added a Fight-Flight-Freeze System for outright threat.)

Gray rotated Eysenck's axes: high BAS activity underlies impulsivity (roughly extraversion plus neuroticism) and high BIS activity underlies anxiety (roughly introversion plus neuroticism). Crucially, Gray's theory is about sensitivity to reinforcement, not general arousal, which is why it is called Reinforcement Sensitivity Theory.

Who → what: Eysenck → PEN, cortical arousal, ARAS. Gray → BIS/BAS reinforcement sensitivity. Friedman & Rosenman → Type A/B behaviour pattern.

Behaviour genetics and heritability

Behaviour genetics uses twin and adoption studies to partition trait variance into genetic and environmental sources. Heritability is the proportion of variation in a trait, within a population, attributable to genetic differences. For the Big Five and Eysenck's dimensions, heritability estimates typically fall around 0.40 to 0.60, meaning roughly half of the variation is genetic.

Trap: Heritability describes variation across a population, not how much of one person's personality is "genetic." A heritability of 0.5 does not mean half of your extraversion came from your genes.

A robust and counter-intuitive finding is that the shared environment (growing up in the same home) contributes little to adult personality, while the non-shared environment (unique experiences, peers, chance) matters much more. Siblings resemble each other mainly through shared genes, not shared upbringing.

Temperament as the biological substrate

Temperament is the set of early-appearing, biologically based individual differences in emotional reactivity and self-regulation, visible in infancy before personality proper forms. Alexander Thomas and Stella Chess described three infant patterns, easy, difficult, and slow-to-warm-up, and stressed goodness of fit between a child's temperament and the caregiving environment. Temperament is best seen as the raw biological material that experience later shapes into personality.

Type A and Type B behaviour pattern

Cardiologists Meyer Friedman and Ray Rosenman described a behaviour pattern, not a trait dimension, from clinical observation. Type A people are competitive, time-urgent, hard-driving, and easily provoked to hostility. Type B people are relaxed, patient, and less hostile. Their long-term data linked Type A to higher coronary heart disease risk; later work narrowed the toxic ingredient to hostility and anger rather than ambition or busyness as such.

RML tilt: Expect one-liners pairing a theorist to a construct: Eysenck-ARAS, Gray-BIS/BAS, Friedman/Rosenman-Type A. Also know that Type A's cardiac danger is specifically hostility.

7. Cognitive Approaches

Cognitive approaches locate personality in how a person thinks: the categories they use to interpret events, their expectancies about outcomes, and the mental representations that guide behaviour. Personality is a knowledge system, not a bundle of drives or reinforced habits.

George Kelly's Personal Construct Theory

George Kelly saw every person as a "scientist" who forms hypotheses about the world and tests them against experience. His core idea is constructive alternativism: reality can always be construed (interpreted) in more than one way, so people are not prisoners of the facts but of their own constructs. A personal construct is a bipolar dimension of judgment (for example kind versus cruel) that a person uses to make sense of people and events. The system of constructs a person carries is their personality.

Kelly built the Repertory Grid (Rep Test) to elicit these constructs: the person compares triads of significant people (mother, boss, self) and states how two are alike and different from the third, revealing the personal dimensions they habitually use.

Julian Rotter: locus of control and expectancy

Julian Rotter, a social-learning theorist, argued that behaviour depends on expectancy (the belief that an action will lead to a given outcome) times reinforcement value (how much that outcome is wanted). His famous individual-difference variable is locus of control: whether a person generally believes outcomes are controlled by their own actions (internal locus) or by luck, fate, and powerful others (external locus). Internals tend to take initiative and cope actively; externals are more prone to helplessness.

Walter Mischel: the person-situation debate

Walter Mischel lit the fuse of a decades-long debate. In 1968 he pointed out that trait scores predict behaviour across situations only modestly (correlations often around 0.30), the so-called "personality coefficient." He argued that behaviour is heavily situation-dependent, challenging the assumption of broad, stable traits.

This produced the consistency paradox: people intuitively feel that personality is consistent, yet measured cross-situational consistency is low. Mischel's resolution was that consistency is real but lives in if-then situation-behaviour signatures (Priya is aggressive if criticised by a peer, but not if criticised by a boss). Aggregated across situations, behaviour is stable; situation by situation, it varies meaningfully.

He and Yuichi Shoda formalised this as the Cognitive-Affective Personality System (CAPS): personality is a network of cognitive-affective units (encodings, expectancies, goals, competencies, self-regulatory plans) that different situations activate differently, generating the stable if-then patterns.

Key. Mischel did not deny personality; he relocated consistency from "the same behaviour everywhere" to "the same distinctive if-then pattern across situations."

8. Indian Approaches

Indian psychology frames personality not as fixed traits but as a dynamic balance of underlying qualities, and it treats the self as layered rather than singular. These models are constitutional and spiritual, aimed at self-transformation, where Western trait models are descriptive and aimed at prediction.

The Triguna theory

From Samkhya philosophy and the Bhagavad Gita, all of nature (prakriti) is woven from three gunas (qualities) present in everyone in varying proportion: Sattva (purity, clarity, calm, balance), Rajas (activity, passion, restlessness, ambition), and Tamas (inertia, dullness, ignorance, lethargy). Personality reflects the shifting dominance among the three; the ideal is to cultivate sattva. Because the balance can change through diet, conduct, and practice, the triguna model is dynamic, unlike a fixed Western trait score.

GunaQualityBehavioural flavour
SattvaClarity, harmonyCalm, disciplined, wise
RajasActivity, passionDriven, restless, desirous
TamasInertia, darknessLethargic, dull, confused

The Panchakosha (five sheaths) model

From the Taittiriya Upanishad, the self is described as five concentric koshas (sheaths) wrapping the innermost being: Annamaya (the physical/food sheath), Pranamaya (the vital-energy/breath sheath), Manomaya (the mental-emotional sheath), Vijnanamaya (the intellect/discernment sheath), and Anandamaya (the bliss sheath). Personality and wellbeing arise from the harmony of these layers, with the true self lying beyond identification with any single sheath.

The Ayurvedic tridosha (constitutional types)

Ayurveda classifies constitution (prakriti) by three doshas: Vata (air/space: quick, changeable, anxious), Pitta (fire: sharp, driven, irritable), and Kapha (earth/water: steady, calm, slow, attached). A person's dominant dosha describes a body-mind type, an early trait-like typology of temperament grounded in physiology.

Trap: Do not confuse the three gunas (sattva/rajas/tamas, mental qualities) with the three doshas (vata/pitta/kapha, bodily constitution). Different systems, different triads.

9. Culture, and the Nomothetic vs Idiographic Debate

Culture and personality

The culture-and-personality tradition holds that a society shapes typical personality through childrearing and shared meanings. A central contrast is between individualist cultures (self as independent, autonomous, defined by inner traits) and collectivist cultures (self as interdependent, defined by relationships and roles). Indian selfhood is often described as relational and contextual, which is one reason Western trait inventories can miss culturally central qualities.

Nomothetic vs idiographic

Gordon Allport drew this key distinction. The nomothetic approach seeks general laws and common dimensions on which everyone can be compared (the Big Five, Eysenck's PEN, most questionnaires). The idiographic approach studies the unique individual in depth, capturing the pattern that makes this one person unlike anyone else (case studies, personal documents, Kelly's rep grid).

FeatureNomotheticIdiographic
GoalGeneral laws, comparisonUnderstand the unique individual
MethodTests, statistics, large samplesCase study, interview, personal documents
ExampleBig Five, PENFreud's cases, Kelly's rep grid

Person-situation interactionism as the integrating idea

The debate Mischel opened resolved not into "traits win" or "situations win" but into interactionism: behaviour is a joint product of the person (traits, constructs, expectancies) and the situation, and the two continuously influence each other. People also select and shape the situations they enter, so person and situation are not independent. This interactionist stance now integrates the biological, cognitive, cultural, and trait approaches into one framework: stable dispositions expressed through, and modified by, the situations a person meets and makes.

Recap: Biology supplies the substrate (Eysenck's arousal, Gray's BIS/BAS, heritable temperament, Type A hostility). Cognition supplies the interpreting mind (Kelly's constructs, Rotter's locus of control, Mischel's if-then CAPS). Indian models supply a dynamic, layered, constitutional self (triguna, panchakosha, tridosha). Nomothetic versus idiographic frames the method, and person-situation interactionism ties it all together.

The Trait Approach in Depth: From Words to Factors

The trait approach asks a deceptively simple question: what are the stable psychological qualities on which people reliably differ, and how many of them do we really need to describe a person? A trait is a relatively enduring disposition to think, feel, and behave in a consistent way across situations and over time. Trait theorists are usually nomothetic in ambition (they want a universal set of dimensions on which everyone can be scored), though the earliest of them, Gordon Allport, insisted the individual could never be fully captured by universal scores.

From scratch: A "type" sorts people into discrete boxes (you are an introvert or an extravert). A "trait" places everyone on a continuous dimension (you sit somewhere on a line from very introverted to very extraverted). Modern personality science is almost entirely trait-based, because real people cluster in the middle of dimensions, not neatly at the poles.

Gordon Allport: cardinal, central, and secondary traits

Gordon Allport (working from the 1930s) is often called the father of trait psychology in America. With Henry Odbert he combed an English dictionary and extracted roughly 18,000 words that describe persons: this word count is the origin of the lexical hypothesis, the idea that the important individual differences in human life become encoded as single terms in every natural language. Allport then proposed a hierarchy of trait importance within one person:

  • Cardinal traits: a single, dominating disposition that organises almost the whole of a life. Rare. Allport gave eponymous examples: a person so ambitious we call them "Napoleonic", or so self-sacrificing we call them "a Mother Teresa". Most people have no cardinal trait at all.
  • Central traits: the five to ten core characteristics that a person who knows you would list in a reference letter (honest, anxious, warm). These are the workhorses of personality description.
  • Secondary traits: narrow, situation-bound preferences and attitudes (gets irritable only in traffic, likes spicy food). They surface only in specific contexts.

Allport also gave the field functional autonomy: the principle that a motive can become independent of its original cause. A young man may first take up carpentry to earn money, but decades later he saws wood for its own sake, long after the financial motive is gone. The activity has become "functionally autonomous" of the drive that started it. This idea let Allport reject the Freudian claim that all adult motives are merely disguised childhood ones.

Who → what: Gordon Allport → trait hierarchy (cardinal/central/secondary), functional autonomy, the lexical hypothesis, and a strong idiographic emphasis on the unique individual.

Raymond Cattell: surface traits, source traits, and the 16PF

Raymond Cattell wanted to do for personality what chemistry's periodic table did for matter: find the underlying elements. He took Allport's word list, reduced it, and then applied the statistical technique of factor analysis (which finds clusters of items that rise and fall together) to distinguish two levels of trait:

  • Surface traits: clusters of behaviours that obviously go together and that any observer can notice (being talkative, cheerful, and sociable). These are symptoms, not causes.
  • Source traits: the deeper, factor-analytically derived dimensions that generate the surface clusters. Cattell argued there are 16 such source traits, and he built the 16PF (Sixteen Personality Factor) Questionnaire to measure them (warmth, reasoning, emotional stability, dominance, and so on).

Cattell also insisted a good theory must triangulate across three data sources, which he labelled with letters:

Data typeWhat it isExample
L-data (Life)Records of real-life behaviour and ratings by othersSchool reports, number of car accidents
Q-data (Questionnaire)Self-report answers on a testAnswers on the 16PF itself
T-data (Test)Objective tests where the person cannot easily guess what is measuredReaction-time or behavioural tasks
Trap: Do not swap the pair. Surface traits are the visible clusters; source traits are the hidden factors that cause them. And it is Cattell (16 factors), not Eysenck (3), who gives the larger number.

From sixteen to five: Costa, McCrae, and the Big Five (OCEAN)

Cattell's 16 factors proved hard for other researchers to reproduce. Decades of lexical and factor-analytic work converged instead on five broad dimensions, now the dominant model in personality psychology. Paul Costa and Robert McCrae developed the leading measure, the NEO-PI-R (which scores each of the five domains plus six narrower facets each). The five, easily recalled by the mnemonic OCEAN, are:

  • Openness to experience: imaginative, curious, artistic vs conventional and practical.
  • Conscientiousness: organised, disciplined, dependable vs careless and impulsive.
  • Extraversion: sociable, energetic, assertive vs reserved and quiet.
  • Agreeableness: trusting, cooperative, warm vs antagonistic and suspicious.
  • Neuroticism: anxious, emotionally reactive vs calm and stable (the healthy pole is sometimes called Emotional Stability).

The Big Five is descriptive rather than explanatory: it tells us which dimensions matter and predicts life outcomes well (Conscientiousness predicts job performance and longevity; Neuroticism predicts psychopathology risk), but on its own it does not explain why those dimensions exist. Costa and McCrae's Five-Factor Theory adds that the five traits are strongly heritable "basic tendencies" that are relatively stable across adulthood.

Rule: Learn the trait ladder as a shrinking number: Cattell 16 → Big Five 5 → Eysenck 3. All three are hierarchical factor models; they differ mainly in how broadly they slice.

HEXACO: adding a sixth factor

Kibeom Ashton and Michael Lee, running lexical studies in several languages, found that a sixth factor kept appearing that the Big Five folded away. Their HEXACO model keeps five familiar dimensions but adds H for Honesty-Humility (sincerity, fairness, modesty, and lack of greed). The six are Honesty-Humility, Emotionality, eXtraversion, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, and Openness. The Honesty-Humility factor predicts things the Big Five handles poorly, such as exploitation, workplace deviance, and the "dark" traits (narcissism, Machiavellianism, psychopathy).

RML tilt: Expect a straight recall item on "which model adds Honesty-Humility?" (HEXACO, Ashton and Lee) and on "who built the NEO-PI-R?" (Costa and McCrae). Memorise the OCEAN expansion cold; it is the single most tested personality fact.

Psychodynamic Theories in Depth

Sigmund Freud: the two maps of the mind and the defences

Sigmund Freud gave personality two overlapping models. The topographic model divides the mind by accessibility to awareness: the conscious (what you are aware of now), the preconscious (what you can readily call up, like your phone number), and the unconscious (the vast reservoir of repressed wishes and memories that drive behaviour but cannot be reached directly). The structural model divides the mind by function:

  • Id: present from birth, entirely unconscious, runs on the pleasure principle (immediate gratification), the seat of the drives.
  • Ego: the executive that runs on the reality principle, negotiating between the id's demands and the outside world.
  • Superego: the internalised moral standards of parents and society, the conscience and the ego-ideal.

When the ego cannot reconcile these forces, anxiety rises, and the ego deploys defence mechanisms, unconscious strategies (elaborated by his daughter Anna Freud) that distort reality to reduce distress:

DefenceWhat happens
RepressionPushing a threatening thought out of awareness (the master defence)
DenialRefusing to accept an external reality
ProjectionAttributing your own unacceptable impulse to someone else
Reaction formationBehaving as the opposite of your true feeling
DisplacementRedirecting an impulse onto a safer target
SublimationChannelling an impulse into a socially valued activity (the only "mature" defence)
RegressionReverting to an earlier developmental stage
RationalisationInventing an acceptable reason for an unacceptable act

These structures are laid down through the psychosexual stages (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital). Fixation at a stage, from over- or under-gratification, shapes the adult character (an "anal-retentive" person who is orderly and stubborn, for example).

Trap: Sublimation and displacement both redirect an impulse. Displacement swaps the target (yell at the dog instead of the boss); sublimation swaps the impulse into something socially valued (channel aggression into becoming a surgeon).

Carl Jung: analytical psychology

Carl Jung broke from Freud over the nature of the unconscious. Where Freud saw a personal store of repressed material, Jung added a deeper layer, the collective unconscious, inherited by all humans and shared across cultures. Its contents are archetypes: universal, inherited templates that shape how we perceive and react. Key archetypes include the persona (the social mask we present), the shadow (the dark, disowned side), the anima/animus (the feminine image in a man / the masculine image in a woman), and the Self (the archetype of wholeness and the goal of development).

Jung also coined introversion and extraversion as the two basic attitudes (energy turned inward vs outward), later crossed with four functions (thinking, feeling, sensation, intuition), a scheme the Myers-Briggs Type Indicator later popularised. The lifelong process of integrating all these parts into a balanced whole he called individuation.

Who → what: Carl Jung → analytical psychology, collective unconscious, archetypes (persona, shadow, anima/animus, Self), introversion/extraversion, individuation.

Alfred Adler: individual psychology

Alfred Adler placed a single striving at the centre of personality. Every child begins small, weak, and dependent, and so develops feelings of inferiority. Healthy development turns these feelings into a lifelong striving for superiority (better understood as striving for competence and self-improvement), while an overwhelming, unresolved version becomes an inferiority complex. Each person pursues these goals through a characteristic style of life laid down in early childhood. Adler stressed social interest (Gemeinschaftsgefuhl), the innate potential to cooperate and care for the community, as the mark of mental health, and he famously argued that birth order (first-born, middle, youngest, only child) shapes personality, with the dethroned first-born and the pampered youngest facing distinctive challenges.

Karen Horney: basic anxiety and neurotic needs

Karen Horney rejected Freud's biological emphasis (and his "penis envy", which she reframed as women's envy of men's social power). For Horney, personality grows out of the child's relationship to the parents. A child who feels unsafe develops basic anxiety: a feeling of being isolated and helpless in a potentially hostile world. To cope, the person adopts one of three interpersonal orientations, and the neurotic rigidly overuses one:

  • Moving toward people (the compliant type): seeking approval, affection, and a partner to lean on.
  • Moving against people (the aggressive type): seeking control, power, and to exploit others.
  • Moving away from people (the detached type): seeking self-sufficiency and withdrawal.

Horney catalogued ten neurotic needs (for affection, for a partner, for power, for prestige, and so on), which are the exaggerated forms of ordinary needs.

Trap: Horney's three "movements" (toward / against / away) are frequently confused with attachment styles. They are her own interpersonal typology of coping with basic anxiety, not Bowlby or Ainsworth's categories.

Harry Stack Sullivan: the interpersonal theory

Harry Stack Sullivan argued that personality can never be studied in isolation, because it exists only in the web of relationships: personality is "the relatively enduring pattern of recurrent interpersonal situations". The self-system develops to manage anxiety, sorting experiences of the self into the good-me, the bad-me, and the dissociated, anxiety-laden not-me. Sullivan mapped development across interpersonal epochs and highlighted the pre-adolescent need for a same-sex "chum" as crucial for later intimacy.

Object relations and self psychology

The object relations school shifted the psychodynamic focus from drives to relationships, specifically the internalised images ("objects", usually people) that the infant carries inside. Melanie Klein described how the infant first relates to part-objects (the good breast and bad breast) and moves from a paranoid-schizoid position (splitting the world into all-good and all-bad) toward the depressive position (recognising that the loved and hated figure are one and the same person). Donald Winnicott introduced the good-enough mother (the caregiver need not be perfect, only reliably adequate) and the transitional object (the teddy bear or blanket that bridges the infant's inner world and outer reality). Heinz Kohut founded self psychology, arguing that a cohesive self forms through empathic selfobject relationships, and that narcissistic disturbance follows from early empathic failures.

Who → what: Klein → part-objects, paranoid-schizoid and depressive positions; Winnicott → good-enough mother, transitional object; Kohut → self psychology, selfobject, empathic failure.

Erich Fromm and the Erikson link

Erich Fromm, a humanistic psychoanalyst, argued that human beings are torn by the burden of freedom: liberation from instinct and tradition brings isolation, and people may flee this anxiety through submission or conformity (his book Escape from Freedom). He described several character orientations (receptive, exploitative, hoarding, marketing, and the healthy productive orientation). Erik Erikson (covered fully under development) belongs here too: he took Freud's stages, extended them across the whole lifespan, and reframed them as psychosocial crises, making him the bridge between classical psychoanalysis and social theories of personality.

Psychodynamic thread in one line: Freud (drives and structures) → Jung and Adler (breakaway, meaning and striving) → Horney, Sullivan, Fromm (interpersonal and cultural) → Klein, Winnicott, Kohut (relationships and the self).

Humanistic Theories in Depth

Carl Rogers: the self and conditions for growth

Carl Rogers built a person-centred theory around the self-concept: the organised set of beliefs a person holds about who they are. He distinguished the real self (who you actually are) from the ideal self (who you feel you ought to be). When the two are close, the person experiences congruence; a wide gap produces incongruence, anxiety, and defensiveness.

The engine of healthy growth, Rogers argued, is unconditional positive regard: being valued and accepted without strings attached. When love is instead made conditional, the child internalises conditions of worth ("I am lovable only if I achieve"), distorting the self. A therapist supplies the missing unconditional positive regard, along with empathy and genuineness. The end point is the fully functioning person: someone open to experience, living in the present, trusting their own judgement, and free to choose.

Rule: "Unconditional positive regard" is Rogers, and only Rogers. Pair it automatically with self-concept, congruence, and the fully functioning person.

Abraham Maslow: self-actualisation

Abraham Maslow arranged human motives into a hierarchy of needs (physiological, safety, belonging and love, esteem, and, at the peak, self-actualisation, the drive to fulfil one's unique potential). He separated deficiency needs (D-needs, the lower four, felt as a lack) from the being need (B-need) of self-actualisation, which grows the more it is fed. Studying people he judged self-actualised (Lincoln, Einstein), Maslow listed their common characteristics: accurate perception of reality, acceptance of self and others, spontaneity, problem-centredness, a need for privacy, autonomy, freshness of appreciation, deep interpersonal relations, democratic values, a philosophical sense of humour, creativity, and above all frequent peak experiences (moments of intense joy, wonder, and transcendence).

Behavioural and Social-Cognitive Theories in Depth

B. F. Skinner: personality as a learning history

B. F. Skinner, a radical behaviourist, denied that "personality" names any inner structure at all. For him, what we call personality is simply an individual's accumulated learning history: a repertoire of behaviours shaped by their consequences through operant conditioning. Behaviour followed by reinforcement becomes more likely; behaviour followed by punishment becomes less likely. Consistency of "personality" is just the consistency of the environments that keep reinforcing the same responses. Skinner rejected explanations that appeal to hidden mental causes as unscientific.

Albert Bandura: reciprocal determinism and self-efficacy

Albert Bandura's social-cognitive theory softened strict behaviourism by putting cognition back in. His central principle is reciprocal determinism: the person (P), their behaviour (B), and the environment (E) all continuously influence one another in a three-way loop, so the individual is both product and producer of their situation. Two further ideas are heavily tested:

  • Self-efficacy: a person's belief in their own capacity to carry out the actions needed to reach a goal. High self-efficacy leads people to attempt harder tasks and persist through failure; it is built from mastery experiences, vicarious experience (watching others succeed), verbal persuasion, and physiological states.
  • Self-regulation: people set their own standards and then monitor, judge, and reward or punish their own behaviour against those standards, rather than relying only on outside consequences.

Bandura's famous Bobo doll studies demonstrated observational learning (modelling): children imitate aggression they merely watched, without any direct reinforcement of their own.

Trap: Self-efficacy (Bandura) is domain-specific belief in "can I do this task?" Do not confuse it with self-esteem (global sense of self-worth) or with Rotter's locus of control (where do outcomes come from?).

Julian Rotter and Walter Mischel: a deeper look

Julian Rotter framed behaviour as a product of expectancy (how likely a behaviour is to produce a given reinforcement) and reinforcement value, and is best known for locus of control: whether a person believes outcomes flow from their own actions (internal) or from luck, fate, and powerful others (external). Walter Mischel lit the person-situation debate in 1968 by showing that trait scores predict behaviour only modestly across situations, and later resolved it with his Cognitive-Affective Personality System (CAPS): people are consistent not in raw behaviour but in if-then signatures ("if teased, then she withdraws; if praised, then she leads"). His preschool marshmallow test of delay of gratification showed that the ability to wait for a larger later reward predicts later academic and life outcomes.

RML tilt: Match-the-column items love pairing theorist to key term: Bandura→self-efficacy/reciprocal determinism, Rotter→locus of control, Mischel→person-situation debate/delay of gratification, Kelly→personal constructs. Fix these four pairings and you bank easy marks.

George Kelly's Personal Construct Theory: A Closer Reading

George Kelly proposed that every person acts as a "scientist" who builds and tests personal theories about the world. The units of these theories are personal constructs: bipolar dimensions of meaning (friendly vs hostile, safe vs dangerous) that we use to interpret and predict events. His single governing idea, the fundamental postulate, is that a person's processes are "psychologically channelised by the ways in which they anticipate events": we behave so as to make our world predictable. To assess a person's private construct system, Kelly built the Role Construct Repertory Test (the Rep Grid), in which the client compares significant people in their life ("in what way are two of these alike and different from the third?") to surface the constructs they actually use. Psychological distress, for Kelly, is a construct system that keeps failing to predict events.

Biological and Temperament Approaches: Extensions

Jeffrey Gray's BIS and BAS revisited

Jeffrey Gray reworked Eysenck's arousal theory into Reinforcement Sensitivity Theory, proposing two brain systems as the biological roots of personality. The Behavioural Inhibition System (BIS) is sensitive to punishment and non-reward; it drives anxiety, caution, and freezing, and an overactive BIS underlies the trait of anxiety. The Behavioural Activation System (BAS) is sensitive to reward and drives approach, impulsivity, and positive emotion. Gray argued these two systems, not Eysenck's introversion and neuroticism axes directly, are the "true" biological dimensions, rotated about 30 degrees within Eysenck's space.

Marvin Zuckerman: sensation seeking

Marvin Zuckerman identified sensation seeking: a trait defined as the need for varied, novel, complex, and intense sensations and experiences, together with a willingness to take physical, social, and financial risks to get them. He built the Sensation Seeking Scale and broke the trait into four components: thrill and adventure seeking (risky sports), experience seeking (new sights, travel, art), disinhibition (parties, drink, novelty in social life), and boredom susceptibility (intolerance of routine). Zuckerman linked high sensation seeking to biological markers such as low levels of the enzyme monoamine oxidase (MAO) and to dopaminergic reward activity, framing it as a strongly biologically based dimension.

Who → what: Gray → BIS/BAS (Reinforcement Sensitivity Theory); Zuckerman → sensation seeking and its four facets, MAO and dopamine links.

Recap: Nomothetic vs Idiographic, and the Indian Thread

Two lenses run through everything above. The nomothetic approach seeks universal laws and common dimensions on which everyone is scored (Cattell, Eysenck, the Big Five, HEXACO). The idiographic approach seeks to understand the unique, whole individual on their own terms (Allport's insistence on the single person, Kelly's Rep Grid, the case study). The two are complementary, not rival: good clinical work scores a client on universal traits and then reads their singular life story. Indian thought adds its own trait-like scheme in the triguna (sattva, rajas, tamas), three fundamental qualities whose changing proportions describe temperament, offering a distinctly nomothetic-yet-value-laden Indian counterpart to Western trait models.

RML tilt: A recurring conceptual item asks you to classify a named theory as nomothetic or idiographic. Rule of thumb: if it produces a score on a shared dimension it is nomothetic; if it aims to capture one person's uniqueness it is idiographic. Allport straddles both, which is exactly why examiners like him.

7 Abnormal Psychology (ICD-deep)

From scratch: Abnormal psychology studies patterns of thought, feeling and behaviour that are dysfunctional and distressing. There is no single fact that makes something "abnormal"; instead clinicians triangulate several markers, then match the picture to a named category in a classification system. For the RML paper you must know two systems (WHO's ICD and APA's DSM), the descriptive vocabulary used to describe symptoms before you name a disorder, and the theories of why disorders arise.

7.1 Defining abnormality, the 4 Ds

The most examinable framework is the Four Ds:

  • Deviance, behaviour that departs from social/cultural norms. A person who stops speaking to avoid "contaminating" others with their thoughts.
  • Distress, subjective suffering (though not universal: a person in a manic episode may feel euphoric).
  • Dysfunction, impaired ability to carry out daily roles (work, self-care, relationships).
  • Danger, risk to self or others (suicidality, aggression). Least common, most over-weighted by the public.

Competing models of abnormality: the statistical model (abnormal = statistically rare, i.e. far from the mean; weakness: genius and rare virtue are also "abnormal"); the social-norm / cultural-relativism model (abnormal = norm-violating; weakness: norms shift and can pathologise dissent); and Jerome Wakefield's harmful-dysfunction model (a condition is a disorder only if it is both a failure of an evolved mental function and harmful to the person by social standards).

Key. No single D is sufficient. Diagnosis requires a syndrome (a cluster of signs and symptoms) plus clinically significant distress or impairment, that impairment clause appears in almost every DSM-5-TR = Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, 5th ed., Text Revision (APA) criteria set.

7.2 Classification, DSM-5-TR vs ICD-11

Two authoritative systems exist. DSM-5-TR is APA's, used mainly in the USA and in research. ICD-11 = International Classification of Diseases 11th revision (WHO) is the global standard and the official system in India for morbidity coding and government/hospital reporting, which is why RML leans ICD.

FeatureDSM-5-TR (APA)ICD-11 (WHO)
ScopeMental disorders onlyAll diseases; mental disorders are Chapter 06
StructureExplicit operational criteria, counts and thresholdsFlexible prototypic descriptions ("essential features")
ApproachLargely categoricalCategorical with more dimensional elements (esp. personality)
AccessCopyrighted, purchasedFreely available; global public-health tool
Use in IndiaResearch, some clinicsDefault clinical/official system
RML tilt: Expect a direct "which system does India use / which is by WHO" item. ICD-11 = WHO; DSM-5-TR = APA. ICD-11 removed the old multiaxial idea, dropped separate "neurosis" chapters, and, unlike DSM, retains a fully dimensional personality-disorder model (severity + trait domains) rather than ten named types.
RML tilt: ICD-11 keeps categories DSM has restructured or renamed, e.g. ICD-11 has a distinct Complex PTSD and a category of Dissociative neurological symptom disorder (old "conversion"). Know that the older DSM-IV multiaxial system (Axis I–V, GAF) is abolished in DSM-5-TR, a classic trap.

7.3 Descriptive psychopathology, signs & symptoms (Fish)

From scratch: Before naming a disorder, clinicians describe the phenomena. This descriptive tradition (Karl Jaspers, Frank Fish) is what RML fishes for. A symptom is reported by the patient; a sign is observed.

Disorders of thought

Split into form (how thoughts connect) and content (what they are about).

  • Content, delusions (fixed false beliefs, not culturally sanctioned, held with certainty): persecutory, grandiose, delusion of reference, nihilistic, somatic/hypochondriacal, delusion of control (passivity), delusion of jealousy, erotomania. A primary (autochthonous) delusion arises fully formed out of nowhere.
  • Form, formal thought disorder: loosening of associations, tangentiality, circumstantiality, flight of ideas (mania), perseveration, neologisms, word salad, and Knight's move thinking (illogical jumps between unrelated ideas, classic in schizophrenia).
  • Thought alienation / passivity: thought insertion, thought withdrawal, thought broadcasting.
Key. Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia: (1) audible thoughts / thought echo, (2) voices arguing or discussing in the third person, (3) voices commenting on one's actions (running commentary), (4) somatic passivity, (5) thought insertion, (6) thought withdrawal, (7) thought broadcasting, (8) made feelings, (9) made impulses, (10) made volitional acts, and (11) delusional perception. Attributed to Kurt Schneider.
RML tilt: First-rank symptoms are highly suggestive but not pathognomonic (they occur in mania/organic states too), and both DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 have demoted them, they no longer carry extra diagnostic weight. RML still asks who described them: Kurt Schneider.

Disorders of perception

  • Illusion, misperception of a real external stimulus.
  • Hallucination, perception without a stimulus, experienced as real and in external space. Types: auditory, visual, olfactory, gustatory, tactile. Special: hypnagogic (falling asleep), hypnopompic (waking), functional, reflex, extracampine (outside the sensory field).
  • Pseudohallucination, experienced in inner subjective space, insight partly retained.

Mood, affect, speech, memory, consciousness

  • Mood/affect: anhedonia, blunted/flat affect, incongruity of affect, lability, alexithymia, and La belle indifférence (bland unconcern about a serious deficit, classic in conversion/dissociative disorder).
  • Speech: pressure of speech (mania), poverty of speech (alogia), echolalia (echoing others' words), palilalia, mutism.
  • Memory: confabulation (gap-filling with false memory, e.g. Korsakoff), déjà vu, jamais vu.
  • Consciousness/motor: delirium (clouding), stupor, and catatonic signs, waxy flexibility (cerea flexibilitas), echopraxia (mimicking movements), negativism, posturing.

Named signs & eponyms (the RML favourites)

Sign / syndromeCore feature
Capgras syndromeBelief a familiar person is replaced by an identical impostor
Fregoli syndromeBelief a stranger is a familiar persecutor in disguise
Cotard syndromeNihilistic delusion of being dead / not existing / rotting
Othello syndromeDelusional jealousy of an unfaithful partner
Ekbom syndromeDelusional parasitosis, insects crawling under the skin
De Clérambault syndromeErotomania, delusion that someone (often of status) is in love with you
Folie à deuxShared delusion transmitted between two closely related people
Ganser syndromeApproximate answers (vorbeireden), clouding, a dissociative state
Couvade syndromeSympathetic pregnancy symptoms in the partner
Echolalia / EchopraxiaAutomatic echoing of speech / movements
RML tilt: Almost every eponym above (Capgras, Fregoli, Cotard, Ganser, folie à deux) is absent as a named diagnosis in DSM-5-TR, DSM folds them into "delusional disorder" or "shared/other psychotic disorder." RML deliberately fishes for these older descriptive labels, so learn the eponym–feature pairing cold.
Trap: Capgras (impostor replacing a familiar face) vs Fregoli (a stranger is secretly one familiar persecutor) are routinely swapped in MCQs. Capgras = "duplication"; Fregoli = "disguise."

7.4 The disorder families (ICD-11 & DSM-5-TR framing)

Schizophrenia & other primary psychotic disorders

Positive symptoms: delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech/behaviour. Negative symptoms: affective flattening, alogia, avolition, anhedonia, asociality (the "5 A's"). Classic subtypes, paranoid, hebephrenic/disorganised, catatonic, simple, residual.

RML tilt: DSM-5-TR abolished the subtypes (paranoid, catatonic etc.) and rates symptom dimensions instead. ICD-11 also dropped them as formal types but keeps a symptom-domain qualifier. If the option lists "paranoid schizophrenia" as a current DSM diagnosis, it's a trap, it is the older ICD-10/descriptive tradition.

Mood (affective) disorders

Major depressive disorder (SIG-E-CAPS: sleep, interest, guilt, energy, concentration, appetite, psychomotor, suicidality), persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia), bipolar I (mania) and bipolar II (hypomania + depression), cyclothymia. ICD-11 requires a manic/hypomanic episode for a bipolar diagnosis, same as DSM.

Anxiety, OCD-related, trauma-related, dissociative, somatic

  • Anxiety: generalized anxiety disorder (free-floating worry), panic disorder (recurrent panic attacks), agoraphobia, specific phobia, social anxiety disorder.
  • Obsessive-compulsive & related: OCD, body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding, trichotillomania, hypochondriasis (ICD-11 places health anxiety here).
  • Trauma & stressor-related: PTSD, acute stress reaction, adjustment disorder, and ICD-11's unique Complex PTSD.
  • Dissociative: dissociative amnesia, dissociative identity disorder, depersonalization/derealization.
  • Somatic: somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder, and conversion disorder (the old hysteria; ICD-11 = dissociative neurological symptom disorder).
RML tilt: Since DSM-5, OCD and PTSD are NOT anxiety disorders, OCD sits in its own Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders chapter and PTSD in Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders. Both ICD-11 and DSM-5-TR make this split. A question asking "which is an anxiety disorder?" listing OCD/PTSD is testing exactly this.
Trap: The word hysteria is obsolete but RML still uses it, it maps to today's conversion / dissociative disorders, not to a single modern label. La belle indifférence is its hallmark sign.

Feeding & eating, personality, neurodevelopmental, substance, neurocognitive

  • Feeding & eating: anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, binge-eating disorder.
  • Neurodevelopmental: intellectual developmental disorder, autism spectrum disorder, ADHD, specific learning disorder.
  • Substance-use: dependence, withdrawal (ICD-11 uses "disorders due to substance use").
  • Neurocognitive: delirium, major & mild neurocognitive disorder (old "dementia"), e.g. Alzheimer's.

Personality disorders, DSM-5-TR keeps ten types in three clusters:

ClusterThemeDisorders
AOdd / eccentricParanoid, Schizoid, Schizotypal
BDramatic / emotional / erraticAntisocial, Borderline, Histrionic, Narcissistic
CAnxious / fearfulAvoidant, Dependent, Obsessive-Compulsive PD
RML tilt: ICD-11 has abolished the ten-type / Cluster A-B-C system entirely, replacing it with a single Personality Disorder rated by severity (mild/moderate/severe) plus trait domains (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia) and an optional Borderline pattern qualifier. Know both, the clusters are DSM/older; the dimensional model is the ICD-11 novelty.

7.5 Etiology models

  • Biological: genetics, neurotransmitters (dopamine in psychosis, serotonin in depression/OCD), brain structure, endocrine.
  • Psychodynamic (Freud): symptoms as expressions of unconscious conflict and defence mechanisms.
  • Cognitive-behavioural: learning (classical/operant), and Beck's cognitive triad / maladaptive schemas.
  • Diathesis-stress model: a predisposing vulnerability (diathesis) + environmental stress together precipitate disorder, the integrative model most examiners endorse.
Recap: Abnormality = the 4 Ds plus clinically significant impairment, judged against statistical, social-norm, and harmful-dysfunction models. Two systems: DSM-5-TR (APA, criteria-based, categorical) and ICD-11 (WHO, prototypic, used in India), RML leans ICD, which uniquely keeps Complex PTSD, dissociative neurological symptom disorder, and a fully dimensional personality model while both systems have dropped schizophrenia subtypes and demoted Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms. Master the descriptive phenomenology (form vs content, illusion vs hallucination) and the eponyms DSM dropped (Capgras, Fregoli, Cotard, Ganser, folie à deux, Othello, Ekbom, La belle indifférence, Knight's move, waxy flexibility). Remember OCD and PTSD are no longer anxiety disorders, "hysteria" = conversion/dissociative, and the diathesis-stress model integrates biology with environment.

7.6 History & Evolving Models of Abnormality

From scratch: How a society explains madness decides how it treats the mad. The story of abnormal psychology is a swing between supernatural, biological (somatogenic) and psychological (psychogenic) explanations, and the same three currents still shape today's debates.

Ancient & humoral models

The Greek physician Hippocrates (c. 460–377 BCE) rejected demonic explanations and located mental illness in the body, specifically an imbalance of the four humours: blood (sanguine), yellow bile (choleric), black bile (melancholic) and phlegm (phlegmatic). Excess black bile (Greek melan chole) produced melancholia, the ancestor of our word for depression. Galen later systematised this humoral theory, which dominated Western medicine for over a thousand years.

The demonological / supernatural era

In the medieval and early-modern West, disturbed behaviour was widely read as possession, witchcraft or divine punishment. Treatments included exorcism, and at the extreme, trials and executions codified in texts like the Malleus Maleficarum (1486). Care such as it was fell to asylums like Bethlem ("Bedlam") in London, which became bywords for neglect.

Moral treatment & asylum reform

Who → what: Philippe Pinel (France) → unchained patients at the Bicetre and Salpetriere hospitals and championed traitement moral, humane care with routine, dignity and conversation. William Tuke (England) → founded the York Retreat (1796), a Quaker institution built on kindness rather than restraint. Dorothea Dix (USA) → a schoolteacher whose 19th-century campaigns exposed the abuse of the mentally ill in jails and poorhouses and drove the building of dozens of state hospitals.

Kraepelin & the birth of nosology

Who → what: Emil Kraepelin (Germany) → the father of modern psychiatric classification (nosology). By grouping symptoms that clustered and ran a common course, he split the major psychoses into dementia praecox (later renamed schizophrenia by Eugen Bleuler) and manic-depressive insanity (today's bipolar/mood disorders). His descriptive, course-and-outcome method is the direct ancestor of DSM and ICD.

Biological vs psychological models & deinstitutionalisation

The 20th century saw the somatogenic tradition strengthen (the discovery that general paresis was caused by syphilis proved a "mad" condition could be an infection) alongside the psychogenic tradition of Sigmund Freud and later behaviourism and cognitivism. From the 1950s, the arrival of chlorpromazine and other antipsychotics, plus civil-rights concern over warehousing, drove deinstitutionalisation: the mass discharge of patients from large asylums into (often under-resourced) community care.
