Trap: Do not credit Kraepelin with unchaining patients (that is Pinel) or with naming schizophrenia (that is Bleuler). Kraepelin's contribution is classification.

7.7 Culture-Bound Syndromes & Cultural Concepts of Distress

From scratch: A culture-bound syndrome is a recurring pattern of distressing symptoms recognised as an illness within a specific culture, but not neatly mapped onto a standard diagnosis. Modern classifications prefer the umbrella term cultural concepts of distress, which DSM-5-TR breaks into cultural syndromes, cultural idioms of distress (shared ways of talking about suffering) and cultural explanations (perceived causes).

RML tilt: This is one of the highest-yield areas for an Indian entrance. Know Dhat cold, know which syndrome belongs to which region, and know that DSM-5-TR replaced the older DSM-IV "culture-bound syndromes" glossary with the Cultural Formulation Interview and the "cultural concepts of distress" framework.

SyndromeRegion / cultureCore features
DhatIndian subcontinentAnxiety and hypochondriacal concern over loss of semen (in urine), fatigue, weakness, guilt over the "vital fluid"
KoroSouth & East Asia (China, Malaysia)Sudden intense fear that the genitals (penis, or nipples/vulva) are retracting into the body and that this will cause death; can occur in epidemics
AmokMalaysia, IndonesiaA dissociative episode of brooding followed by a violent, homicidal outburst ("running amok"), then amnesia and exhaustion
LatahMalaysia, IndonesiaExaggerated startle response with echolalia, echopraxia, automatic obedience and trance-like behaviour, usually in older women
PibloktoArctic (Inuit)"Arctic hysteria": abrupt agitation, tearing off clothes, running into snow, then amnesia
SustoLatin AmericaIllness attributed to a frightening event that causes the soul to leave the body; insomnia, appetite loss, sadness
Taijin kyofushoJapanIntense fear that one's body, appearance or odour offends or embarrasses others (an other-focused social anxiety)

Dhat syndrome (in depth)

Dhat (from Sanskrit dhatu, meaning the elixir that constitutes the body) is the classic South Asian culture-bound syndrome. Young men present with vague somatic and anxious complaints (weakness, palpitations, poor concentration, sexual worry) that they attribute to the passage of semen in urine or through nocturnal emission. It reflects the folk belief that semen is a precious vital essence whose loss depletes the body. Management is chiefly psychoeducation, correcting the belief, and treating any comorbid depression or anxiety.

Possession & dissociative trance

Across India and many cultures, distress may present as spirit possession. ICD-11 lists trance disorder and possession trance disorder under dissociative disorders: a temporary, marked alteration of identity and awareness, experienced as being taken over by a spirit, power or other person, that is unwanted and causes distress or impairment. It is only a disorder when it is involuntary and dysfunctional, not when it is a sanctioned part of religious or ritual practice.

Key. Both ICD-11 and DSM-5-TR keep culture central: DSM-5-TR uses the Cultural Formulation Interview and "cultural concepts of distress"; ICD-11 threads cultural context through its clinical descriptions. A behaviour is only pathological when it exceeds cultural norms and causes genuine distress or dysfunction.

7.8 Sleep-Wake Disorders

From scratch: Sleep disorders are grouped into problems of getting/staying asleep, excessive sleepiness, and abnormal events during sleep (parasomnias).

  • Insomnia disorder: difficulty initiating or maintaining sleep, or early waking, at least 3 nights a week for 3 months, with daytime impairment.
  • Hypersomnolence disorder: excessive sleepiness despite a main sleep period of 7 hours or more.
  • Narcolepsy: irresistible sleep attacks, often with cataplexy (sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion), sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations; linked to loss of hypocretin (orexin) neurons.
  • Obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea: repeated upper-airway collapse causing breathing pauses, snoring and daytime fatigue.
  • Parasomnias – NREM (deep-sleep) arousal disorders: sleepwalking (somnambulism) and sleep terrors, with amnesia for the event, typical in children.
  • Nightmare disorder: repeated frightening dreams in REM sleep, with full recall on waking.
  • REM sleep behaviour disorder: loss of normal REM muscle paralysis so the person acts out dreams; a marker of later Parkinson's and Lewy body disease.
  • Restless legs syndrome: an urge to move the legs with unpleasant sensations, worse at rest and at night.

Trap: Sleep terrors (NREM, no dream recall, child screams but is not truly awake) are not the same as nightmares (REM, vivid recall).

7.9 Sexual Dysfunctions, Paraphilic Disorders & Gender Incongruence

Sexual dysfunctions are persistent problems in desire, arousal, orgasm or pain: examples include hypoactive sexual desire, erectile dysfunction, female orgasmic dysfunction, premature (early) ejaculation and genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder (dyspareunia, vaginismus).

Paraphilic disorders: a paraphilia (an atypical object or focus of arousal) becomes a disorder only when it causes distress, harm or involves non-consenting persons. Examples: exhibitionistic, voyeuristic, frotteuristic, paedophilic, sexual sadism and masochism, and fetishistic disorders.

Key. ICD-11 renamed "gender identity disorder" to gender incongruence and moved it out of the mental-disorders chapter into a new chapter on sexual health, explicitly de-pathologising being transgender. DSM-5-TR retains gender dysphoria, where the diagnosis rests on the distress, not on the identity itself.

7.10 Impulse-Control, Disruptive, Conduct & Elimination Disorders

  • Oppositional defiant disorder: a pattern of angry, argumentative, defiant behaviour toward authority.
  • Conduct disorder: repeated violation of others' rights and social norms (aggression, destruction, deceit, serious rule-breaking); a childhood risk pathway to antisocial personality disorder.
  • Intermittent explosive disorder: recurrent, disproportionate aggressive outbursts.
  • Pyromania (deliberate fire-setting for pleasure/relief) and kleptomania (failure to resist stealing objects not needed for use or value).
  • Elimination disorders: enuresis (repeated urination into clothes/bed after age 5) and encopresis (passing faeces in inappropriate places after age 4), each by developmental age.

7.11 Suicide & Risk Assessment

From scratch: Suicidal behaviour runs along a continuum: ideation → plan → attempt → completed suicide. Assessment aims to gauge risk and mobilise safety, not to predict with certainty.

Risk factors: previous attempt (the single strongest predictor), depression and other mental illness, substance use, hopelessness, chronic pain or illness, access to lethal means, recent loss, and social isolation. Protective factors: strong social support, religious or cultural prohibitions, responsibility for children, reasons for living and access to care.

Trap: The belief that "asking about suicide plants the idea" is a myth; direct, calm questioning does not increase risk and is essential. Another myth: people who talk about suicide do not do it, whereas most who die by suicide gave prior warning.

RML tilt: India carries one of the world's highest absolute suicide burdens, with young adults and, notably, the young-female and farmer sub-groups over-represented. Ingestion of pesticides is a leading method, so means restriction is a key public-health lever.

7.12 Epidemiology Basics for Psychopathology

From scratch: Epidemiology studies how often disorders occur in populations.

  • Prevalence: the proportion of a population with a condition at a point (point prevalence) or over a period (lifetime prevalence); it reflects existing cases.
  • Incidence: the rate of new cases arising over a period; it reflects onset/risk.

Key. Chronic disorders (like schizophrenia) can have low incidence but high prevalence because cases accumulate; brief disorders show the reverse. Mental and substance-use disorders are a leading global cause of years lived with disability. India's National Mental Health Survey estimated a lifetime prevalence of mental disorders near 13.7% and a wide treatment gap (most cases untreated).

Recap: Abnormality has been explained by turns as humoral, demonic, biological and psychological; Pinel and Tuke humanised care, Dix reformed asylums, and Kraepelin founded classification. Culture-bound concepts (Dhat, Koro, Amok, Latah, Piblokto, possession trance) are high-yield and now framed as cultural concepts of distress. Round out the chapter with sleep-wake disorders, sexual and gender presentations, impulse-control and elimination disorders, suicide risk assessment, and the prevalence/incidence backbone of epidemiology.

Anxiety and fear-related disorders (ICD-11 grouping)

ICD-11 gathers the disorders whose core feature is excessive fear or anxiety into one block. Fear is the alarm response to a present or perceived-imminent threat (fight, flight, freeze); anxiety is anticipatory apprehension about a future threat. A crucial ICD-11 move is that each disorder is defined mainly by the focus of apprehension (what the person is afraid of), which is what lets you tell them apart in a viva.

From scratch: Anxiety becomes a disorder only when it is out of proportion to real danger, persists for weeks, and impairs functioning. The physiology (racing heart, sweating, trembling, hyperventilation) comes from sympathetic and hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis over-activation.

Generalised anxiety disorder (GAD)

Core features: pervasive, free-floating "apprehensive expectation" (worry about multiple everyday domains such as family, finances, health, work) that is difficult to control, plus autonomic and muscle-tension symptoms (restlessness, fatigue, poor concentration, irritability, muscle tension, sleep disturbance). ICD-11 requires the worry or apprehension to persist for several months, more days than not. DSM-5-TR sets a 6-month minimum and requires 3 of 6 associated symptoms. Onset/course: typically insidious, often in the 20s to 30s, chronic and fluctuating with stress. Epidemiology: lifetime prevalence around 5 to 6 percent, roughly twice as common in women. Etiology: heritable temperamental trait of neuroticism, GABA and serotonin dysregulation, and an intolerance-of-uncertainty cognitive style.

Trap: GAD worry is about multiple real-life domains and is future-oriented. If the fear is confined to one object or situation, it is a phobia, not GAD.

Panic disorder

Core features: recurrent unexpected panic attacks (abrupt surges of intense fear peaking within minutes: palpitations, chest pain, dyspnoea, choking, dizziness, derealisation, paraesthesia, fear of dying or "going mad") followed by persistent concern about further attacks or a maladaptive change in behaviour to avoid them. The attacks are the alarm; the disorder is the fear of the alarm (anticipatory anxiety). Onset: late adolescence to mid-30s. Epidemiology: around 2 to 3 percent, women more affected. Etiology: Clark's cognitive model of catastrophic misinterpretation of benign bodily sensations, plus a hypersensitive brainstem "suffocation alarm" (Klein) and noradrenergic locus coeruleus over-firing.

Agoraphobia

Core features: marked fear or anxiety about situations from which escape is difficult or help unavailable should panic-like symptoms occur: crowds, public transport, queues, open spaces, being far from home alone. Situations are avoided, endured with dread, or require a companion. In ICD-11 agoraphobia is a standalone diagnosis and does not require a prior panic disorder. Course: can become housebound in severe cases. Etiology: conditioning of avoidance that is negatively reinforced by relief.

Specific phobia and social anxiety disorder

Specific phobia: marked, disproportionate fear of a circumscribed object or situation (animals, heights, blood-injection-injury, flying, enclosed spaces) with immediate anxiety on exposure and active avoidance. The blood-injection-injury type is unique in provoking a vasovagal biphasic response (brief tachycardia then a drop in heart rate and blood pressure causing fainting), unlike other phobias which sustain arousal. Social anxiety disorder (social phobia): fear of scrutiny and negative evaluation in social or performance situations, driven by fear of showing anxiety symptoms (blushing, trembling, sweating). Onset typically early adolescence. Etiology: Mowrer's two-factor theory (classical conditioning acquires the fear, operant avoidance maintains it), biological preparedness for evolutionarily relevant threats (Seligman), and modelling.

Separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism

Separation anxiety disorder: developmentally excessive fear about separation from attachment figures (worry about harm befalling them, refusal to go to school or sleep alone, nightmares of separation, somatic complaints on separation). ICD-11 explicitly recognises it can persist into or begin in adulthood (a change from older systems that limited it to childhood). Selective mutism: consistent failure to speak in specific social situations (typically school) despite speaking normally elsewhere, not explained by a language disorder; conceptualised as an anxiety-driven condition. Both cluster here in ICD-11 rather than under childhood disorders.

Obsessive-compulsive and related disorders

ICD-11 creates a dedicated OCD-related grouping unified by repetitive thoughts and behaviours. A key ICD-11 decision is placing hypochondriasis (health anxiety) here rather than with anxiety or somatic disorders, and grouping the body-focused repetitive behaviours together.

Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD)

Core features: obsessions (recurrent intrusive, unwanted thoughts, images, or urges that cause marked anxiety, e.g. contamination, harm, symmetry, forbidden thoughts) and compulsions (repetitive behaviours or mental acts performed to neutralise the obsession or prevent a feared outcome, e.g. washing, checking, counting, ordering). The person usually recognises them as their own and excessive (though ICD-11 codes an insight specifier: fair-to-good vs poor-to-absent). Onset: bimodal, childhood/adolescence and early adulthood; earlier onset in males. Epidemiology: around 1 to 2 percent. Etiology: cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical (CSTC) loop hyperactivity, serotonergic dysfunction (hence SSRI response at high doses), and the cognitive model of inflated responsibility and thought-action fusion. First-line treatment is exposure and response prevention (ERP).

Trap: Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD) is ego-syntonic perfectionism and rigidity with no true obsessions or compulsions. OCD is ego-dystonic and anxiety-driven. Do not confuse them.

Body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding, and body-focused repetitive behaviours

Body dysmorphic disorder (BDD): preoccupation with one or more perceived defects in appearance that are unnoticeable or slight to others, with repetitive behaviours (mirror-checking, camouflaging, reassurance-seeking, skin-picking) or comparing; high risk of cosmetic-surgery seeking and suicidality. Hoarding disorder: persistent difficulty discarding possessions due to a perceived need to save them and distress at discarding, leading to accumulation that congests living spaces; egosyntonic and often with poor insight. Trichotillomania (hair-pulling) and excoriation (skin-picking) disorder are the body-focused repetitive behaviours: recurrent pulling or picking causing lesions, with repeated attempts to stop; these are driven by tension-relief rather than by an obsession.

Olfactory reference disorder and hypochondriasis

Olfactory reference disorder: persistent preoccupation with the belief that one emits a foul or offensive body odour or breath that others notice, with repetitive checking, camouflaging, or social avoidance; a distinct ICD-11 entity. Hypochondriasis (health anxiety disorder): persistent preoccupation with or fear of having a serious illness, fuelled by misinterpreting normal bodily sensations, with repetitive checking or reassurance-seeking (or maladaptive avoidance of doctors). ICD-11 files it under OCD-related disorders because the repetitive checking/reassurance behaviour mirrors compulsions.

RML tilt: Know exactly which disorders ICD-11 moved compared with DSM-5-TR: hypochondriasis to OCD-related (DSM splits it into illness anxiety disorder and somatic symptom disorder under somatic), and body-focused behaviours grouped together. These "where does it sit in ICD-11" items are recurring MCQs.

Disorders specifically associated with stress

This ICD-11 grouping requires an identifiable stressor or trauma as a necessary cause. ICD-11 narrowed PTSD's symptom set and added two new diagnoses (complex PTSD and prolonged grief disorder).

PTSD and complex PTSD

PTSD core features (ICD-11): after exposure to an extremely threatening or horrific event, three clusters must all be present: (1) re-experiencing in the here-and-now (intrusive memories, flashbacks, nightmares with a sense of reliving), (2) avoidance of reminders, and (3) a persistent sense of current threat (hypervigilance, exaggerated startle). ICD-11 deliberately tightened PTSD to these core features (DSM-5-TR keeps a broader 4-cluster set including negative cognitions and mood). Complex PTSD (CPTSD) is an ICD-11 innovation: PTSD plus three "disturbances in self-organisation": affect dysregulation, negative self-concept (worthlessness, shame, guilt), and disturbances in relationships. It typically follows prolonged or repeated trauma from which escape is difficult (childhood abuse, captivity, domestic violence). Etiology: fear conditioning with failure of extinction, amygdala hyper-reactivity, hippocampal and prefrontal hypofunction, and dysregulated cortisol.

Acute stress reaction, adjustment disorder, prolonged grief, reactive attachment

Acute stress reaction: in ICD-11 this is regarded as a normal reaction to overwhelming stress (a transient response within hours to days), and is placed among "factors influencing health status," not as a mental disorder. Adjustment disorder: a maladaptive reaction to an identifiable psychosocial stressor (e.g. divorce, job loss) featuring preoccupation with the stressor and failure to adapt, emerging within a month and typically resolving within six months of the stressor ending. Prolonged grief disorder: a new ICD-11 (and DSM-5-TR) diagnosis: persistent, pervasive longing for or preoccupation with the deceased beyond the expected social norm (at least 6 months) with intense emotional pain that impairs functioning. Reactive attachment disorder (grossly inhibited, emotionally withdrawn attachment following pathological care) and disinhibited social engagement disorder (indiscriminate over-familiarity with strangers) arise from severe early neglect.

Mood (affective) disorders in depth

From scratch: ICD-11 defines a mood episode (depressive, manic, hypomanic, mixed) as a building block and then builds disorders from the pattern of episodes. A single low mood is not a disorder; the syndrome (a cluster lasting a set duration with impairment) is.

Depressive disorders

Depressive episode features: ICD-11 lists affective (depressed mood, anhedonia), cognitive-behavioural (hopelessness, worthlessness/guilt, poor concentration, suicidal ideation), and neurovegetative (sleep and appetite change, fatigue, psychomotor change) clusters, present most of the day nearly every day for at least 2 weeks. Single episode depressive disorder vs recurrent depressive disorder (two or more episodes separated by months of remission, with no history of mania, which would make it bipolar). Dysthymic disorder is persistent low-grade depression for at least 2 years without meeting full episode criteria most of the time. Epidemiology: lifetime prevalence around 15 to 20 percent, roughly twice as common in women. Etiology: monoamine hypothesis (serotonin, noradrenaline, dopamine), HPA-axis hypercortisolism, Beck's negative cognitive triad (self, world, future), and Seligman's learned helplessness reformulated as a depressive attributional style.

Bipolar and related disorders

Mania: at least a week (or any duration if hospitalised) of elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased activity/energy plus inflated self-esteem/grandiosity, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, flight of ideas, distractibility, and risky goal-directed behaviour; psychotic features may occur. Hypomania: the same quality but milder, several days, no marked impairment and no psychosis. Bipolar type I requires at least one manic (or mixed) episode; bipolar type II requires at least one hypomanic and one depressive episode with no full mania. Cyclothymic disorder: chronic (at least 2 years) instability with numerous hypomanic and mild depressive periods below threshold. Etiology: high heritability (among the most genetic of psychiatric disorders), dopamine dysregulation, and kindling. Lithium is the classic mood stabiliser.

Specifiers: postpartum, seasonal, melancholic

Peripartum onset (within about 6 weeks of delivery in ICD-11) marks depressive or bipolar episodes tied to childbirth; postpartum psychosis is a psychiatric emergency. Seasonal pattern describes recurrent episodes at characteristic times of year (classically winter depression, linked to light and melatonin/circadian shifts, treated with bright-light therapy). Melancholia denotes a severe biological pattern (pervasive anhedonia, early-morning waking, diurnal variation worse in mornings, weight loss, marked psychomotor change).

Trap: A single hypomanic episode with past depression makes it bipolar II, not recurrent depression. Missing a past hypomania is the commonest misdiagnosis in mood disorders.

Schizophrenia and other primary psychotic disorders

Symptom dimensions and first-rank symptoms

Positive symptoms (added to normal experience): delusions, hallucinations, disorganised thinking/speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour. Negative symptoms (loss of function): affective flattening, alogia (poverty of speech), avolition, anhedonia, asociality. ICD-11 describes schizophrenia through symptom-domain specifiers (positive, negative, depressive, manic, psychomotor, cognitive) rather than the old subtypes.

Who → what: Kurt Schneider proposed first-rank symptoms: thought insertion, thought withdrawal, thought broadcasting; delusions of control/passivity; made feelings/impulses; somatic passivity; third-person auditory hallucinations (voices commenting or arguing); and delusional perception. Once thought pathognomonic, now seen as suggestive, not definitive.

Course, epidemiology, and the dopamine hypothesis

Onset: late teens to early 30s, earlier in men. ICD-11 requires symptoms for at least one month (DSM-5-TR requires 6 months of disturbance including prodrome). Epidemiology: lifetime risk near 1 percent worldwide. Course is variable: roughly a third improve substantially, a third fluctuate, a third are chronic. Dopamine hypothesis: excess mesolimbic dopamine drives positive symptoms (antipsychotics block D2 receptors) while mesocortical dopamine deficit underlies negative and cognitive symptoms; the revised model adds glutamate (NMDA hypofunction). Other factors: high heritability, obstetric complications, cannabis use, and the neurodevelopmental hypothesis.

Historical models and other psychotic disorders

Who → what: Emil Kraepelin split the functional psychoses into dementia praecox (early deteriorating course) and manic-depressive insanity. Eugen Bleuler renamed it schizophrenia ("splitting of the mind") and defined the 4 A's: loosening of Associations, blunted Affect, Ambivalence, Autism. The old Kraepelinian subtypes (paranoid, hebephrenic, catatonic) were dropped in ICD-11.

Schizoaffective disorder: a mood episode (depressive or manic) and schizophrenia symptoms are prominent within the same episode, simultaneously or within a few days. Delusional disorder: one or more persistent delusions for at least 3 months with otherwise preserved functioning and no other psychotic features (subtypes: persecutory, grandiose, jealous, erotomanic, somatic). Brief/acute and transient psychotic disorder: sudden-onset, rapidly shifting psychotic symptoms lasting less than about 3 months, often after acute stress, with full recovery.

Personality disorders: the ICD-11 dimensional shift

Rule: ICD-11 abolished the categorical subtypes (except retaining an optional "borderline pattern" specifier). Diagnosis now runs in two steps: first rate severity (mild, moderate, severe personality disorder, or "personality difficulty"), then add any of five trait domain qualifiers.

General criteria: an enduring disturbance in how one experiences and thinks about self and others, manifest in maladaptive patterns of cognition, emotion, and behaviour that are pervasive across situations, stable over time (typically evident from adolescence), and cause significant distress or impairment. ICD-11 trait domains: Negative Affectivity, Detachment, Dissociality, Disinhibition, and Anankastia (rigid perfectionism). DSM-5-TR by contrast keeps the ten categorical PDs in three clusters: A (odd/eccentric), B (dramatic/erratic), C (anxious/fearful).

Cluster (DSM)PDHallmark
A: oddParanoidpervasive distrust, reads malice into others
A: oddSchizoiddetachment, indifference to relationships
A: oddSchizotypaleccentricity, magical thinking, perceptual oddities
B: dramaticAntisocialdisregard for and violation of others' rights, deceit
B: dramaticBorderlineinstability of affect, identity, relationships; impulsivity, self-harm
B: dramaticHistrionicexcessive emotionality and attention-seeking
B: dramaticNarcissisticgrandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy
C: anxiousAvoidantsocial inhibition, hypersensitivity to negative evaluation
C: anxiousDependentexcessive need to be cared for, submissiveness
C: anxiousObsessive-compulsiverigid perfectionism, control, orderliness (ego-syntonic)
RML tilt: Be able to map DSM clusters onto ICD-11 domains, e.g. schizoid/schizotypal to Detachment, antisocial to Dissociality, borderline to Negative Affectivity plus Disinhibition, obsessive-compulsive to Anankastia. The "ICD-11 has no categories except a borderline pattern qualifier" fact is a favourite.

Dissociative and bodily distress disorders

Dissociative disorders

Dissociation is a disruption of the normal integration of consciousness, memory, identity, perception, or bodily control. Dissociative amnesia (inability to recall important autobiographical, usually traumatic, information, sometimes with a fugue journey). Dissociative identity disorder (two or more distinct personality states with recurrent gaps in recall). Depersonalisation-derealisation disorder (persistent detachment from one's self or surroundings with intact reality testing). ICD-11 also lists trance and possession trance disorders and dissociative neurological symptom disorder (the ICD-11 relocation of conversion disorder).

Bodily distress disorder

ICD-11 term: the older somatoform cluster is replaced by bodily distress disorder: bodily symptoms that are distressing and to which excessive attention is directed (repeated contact with health services), defined by the response to symptoms rather than by whether they are "medically unexplained." DSM-5-TR's parallel is somatic symptom disorder, which similarly drops the "unexplained" requirement. This positive-criterion shift (defining by distress and excessive focus, not by absence of a medical cause) is a key modern change.

Feeding and eating disorders

Anorexia nervosa: significantly low body weight (ICD-11 uses BMI under 18.5 in adults) that is self-induced, an over-valuation of thinness with intense fear of weight gain, and behaviours to reduce weight (restriction, purging, exercise); restricting and binge-purge patterns are specified. Medical sequelae include amenorrhoea, bradycardia, osteoporosis, lanugo. Bulimia nervosa: recurrent binge eating with compensatory behaviours (self-induced vomiting, laxatives, fasting) in someone usually of normal weight, with self-evaluation unduly influenced by shape/weight; signs include Russell's sign (knuckle callus) and parotid swelling. Binge-eating disorder: recurrent binges with a sense of loss of control but no regular compensatory behaviour. Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID): avoidance/restriction based on sensory aversion, lack of interest, or fear of aversive consequences (choking) without body-image disturbance, causing weight loss or nutritional deficiency. Epidemiology: onset in adolescence, strongly female-predominant; anorexia has the highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder.

Neurodevelopmental disorders

Onset in the developmental period, producing deficits in personal, social, or academic functioning.

Intellectual, ADHD, and autism

Disorders of intellectual development: significantly below-average intellectual functioning and adaptive behaviour, with onset during development; ICD-11 grades severity by standardised tests and adaptive behaviour (mild, moderate, severe, profound). ADHD: a persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that is developmentally inappropriate and present across settings; ICD-11 folds it into neurodevelopmental disorders and describes predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, and combined presentations. Autism spectrum disorder: persistent deficits in social communication and reciprocal interaction, plus restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests, or activities (insistence on sameness, stereotypies, sensory sensitivities); ICD-11 uses one spectrum with specifiers for intellectual and language impairment.

Learning, communication, motor, and tic disorders

Developmental learning disorder: specific deficits in reading (dyslexia), written expression, or mathematics (dyscalculia) despite adequate schooling and intelligence. Developmental speech or language disorders and developmental motor coordination disorder (clumsiness beyond age expectation). Tic disorders / Tourette syndrome: sudden, rapid, recurrent motor and/or vocal tics; Tourette requires multiple motor tics and at least one vocal tic for over a year, onset in childhood; ICD-11 cross-lists tics under both neurological and neurodevelopmental groupings and they often co-occur with OCD and ADHD.

Neurocognitive disorders

Delirium

Core features: an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness developing over hours to days, with disorganised thinking, altered sleep-wake cycle, and often perceptual disturbances, caused by a medical condition, substance, or withdrawal. It is reversible if the cause is treated, which is the key contrast with dementia.

Trap: Delirium = acute, fluctuating, clouded consciousness/attention. Dementia = chronic, progressive, clear consciousness with declining memory/cognition. Examiners love the delirium-vs-dementia distinction.

The dementias and amnestic disorder

Alzheimer disease: the commonest dementia; insidious, progressive decline led by episodic memory loss, later language, visuospatial, and executive decline; pathology is beta-amyloid plaques and tau neurofibrillary tangles with hippocampal atrophy. Vascular dementia: stepwise deterioration after cerebrovascular events, with patchy deficits and preserved insight early. Dementia with Lewy bodies: fluctuating cognition, recurrent visual hallucinations, spontaneous parkinsonism, and neuroleptic sensitivity; alpha-synuclein Lewy bodies. Frontotemporal dementia: early personality and behaviour change or language decline with relatively preserved memory, younger onset. Amnestic disorder: prominent memory impairment (especially new learning) without the broader cognitive collapse of dementia; the classic cause is thiamine deficiency (Korsakoff syndrome, often with confabulation).

Substance use and addictive behaviours

ICD-11 separates harmful pattern of use and substance dependence. Dependence is defined by a core triad: (1) impaired control over use, (2) increasing priority given to use over other activities, and (3) physiological features (tolerance, withdrawal, or use to relieve withdrawal). A single occasion of harmful use is also codeable, and ICD-11 recognises substance intoxication and withdrawal states per substance class.

Who → what: ICD-11's landmark change is adding disorders due to addictive behaviours as a class: gambling disorder and, newly, gaming disorder (impaired control over gaming, escalating priority, continuation despite harm, for at least 12 months). This recognises behavioural (non-substance) addiction as diagnostically parallel to drug dependence.

Sexual, gender, and paraphilic conditions (ICD-11 relocation, in brief)

A defining ICD-11 reform is depathologising being transgender: gender incongruence was moved out of "mental disorders" into a new chapter on conditions related to sexual health, so that access to gender-affirming care is preserved without the stigma of a mental-illness label. Sexual dysfunctions likewise sit in the sexual-health chapter, integrating physical and psychological factors. Paraphilic disorders remain among mental disorders but are diagnosed only when the pattern involves non-consenting others or causes marked distress or risk, not merely for atypical arousal. These are treated in depth in the sexual-health section; the point to retain here is the deliberate ICD-11 move away from labelling identity as pathology.

Anxiety disorders are told apart by the focus of fear; ICD-11 made agoraphobia and adult separation anxiety standalone. OCD-related now houses hypochondriasis and the body-focused behaviours. ICD-11 added complex PTSD and prolonged grief, and treats acute stress reaction as normal. Mood disorders are built from episodes: watch for a single hypomania flipping a case to bipolar II. Schizophrenia dropped subtypes for symptom-domain specifiers; know Schneider's first-rank symptoms and Bleuler's 4 A's. Personality disorders went fully dimensional (severity + five trait domains, borderline pattern optional). Somatoform became bodily distress disorder (defined by excessive focus, not "unexplained"). ICD-11 added gaming disorder and moved gender incongruence out of mental disorders.

8 Clinical Psychology, scope, history & method

From scratch: Psychology is the science of behaviour and mental processes. Clinical psychology is the branch that applies that science to people who are actually suffering: it studies, assesses, and treats psychological distress and mental disorders. So the word "clinical" (from a Greek root meaning "at the bedside") signals a practical, patient-facing job, not just a laboratory one.

1. What clinical psychology is

Clinical psychology is the field concerned with the assessment, diagnosis, formulation, treatment, and prevention of psychological disorders and distress across the lifespan. A clinical psychologist studies how disorders arise, measures them with tests and interviews, builds an explanation of the individual case, and delivers psychological interventions (chiefly psychotherapy). Its scope spans mental illness (depression, anxiety, psychosis), developmental and neuropsychological problems, addictions, and everyday adjustment difficulties, in hospitals, clinics, schools, courts, and private practice.

Key. The four verbs to memorise: assess, formulate, diagnose, treat (plus prevent and research). Assessment + psychotherapy are the two signature skills that distinguish clinical psychology from its neighbours.

How it differs from neighbouring fields

FieldCore trainingDistinctive feature
Clinical psychologyPsychology (M.Phil / RCI MA)Assessment + psychotherapy for the more severe and complex disorders
PsychiatryMedicine (MBBS then MD)A medical doctor; can prescribe medication and admit patients
Counselling psychologyPsychologyFocus on adjustment, wellness, and milder problems; less on severe pathology
Clinical / psychiatric social workSocial work (MSW / M.Phil PSW)Family, community, and rehabilitation focus; links patient to social resources
Trap: A psychiatrist is a medical doctor and can prescribe drugs; a clinical psychologist is not and (in India) generally cannot. Do not treat the two as interchangeable, and note that psychiatric social work is a separate RCI-recognised profession, not a synonym for clinical psychology.

Training models

Two philosophies define how clinical psychologists are trained:

  • Scientist-practitioner (Boulder) model (1949): the clinician is both a researcher and a therapist, trained to produce research and to use evidence in practice. This is the dominant model.
  • Scholar-practitioner (Vail) model (1973): emphasises practice over research, awarding the PsyD (Doctor of Psychology) for those who mainly want to deliver clinical services.

2. History

Who → what: Lightner Witmer → founded the world's first psychological clinic at the University of Pennsylvania in 1896 and coined the term "clinical psychology." He treated a boy with a spelling difficulty, marking the field's birth in applied, individual case work.

Key milestones:

  • 1896, Lightner Witmer: first clinic; the term "clinical psychology" is born.
  • World War I & II, the assessment boom: the army needed to screen and place millions of recruits. Psychologists built the Army Alpha (group test for literate recruits) and Army Beta (nonverbal test for illiterate or non-English recruits). After WWII, huge numbers of veterans with psychological trauma created demand for treatment, pushing psychologists from testing into psychotherapy.
  • Boulder Conference, 1949: formalised the scientist-practitioner model as the standard for doctoral training.
  • Rise of psychotherapy: through the second half of the 20th century clinical psychology grew from a mainly diagnostic role into a treatment profession.
RML tilt: Expect direct factual items: Who founded the first psychological clinic? (Witmer), Where and when? (University of Pennsylvania, 1896), Which conference established the scientist-practitioner model? (Boulder, 1949), and the Army Alpha/Beta pairing.

Clinical psychology in India

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI), a statutory body under the RCI Act, 1992, regulates training and maintains the register of qualified clinical psychologists. Historically, the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology (a two-year RCI-recognised course after a master's) was the licensing route to practise. This is now being complemented and replaced by the RCI-recognised MA in Clinical Psychology, a direct two-year professional degree that itself confers RCI registration. Premier training centres include NIMHANS (National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences, Bengaluru), CIP (Central Institute of Psychiatry, Ranchi), and IHBAS (Institute of Human Behaviour and Allied Sciences, Delhi).

3. The clinical method (the process)

Clinical work follows an ordered sequence from first contact to review:

  1. Referral: the patient arrives via a doctor, family, court, or self-referral, with a presenting complaint.
  2. Clinical interview: the central assessment tool. Three types:
    • Structured: fixed questions in fixed order (high reliability; used in research and formal diagnosis).
    • Semi-structured: a core set of questions the clinician can probe and expand (the common practical middle ground).
    • Unstructured: free-flowing, follows the patient's lead (flexible but less reliable).
  3. Mental status examination (MSE): a structured snapshot of the patient's current functioning. Its components: appearance and behaviour, speech, mood and affect, thought (form and content, e.g. delusions), perception (e.g. hallucinations), cognition (orientation, attention, memory), insight, and judgement. A patient who denies being unwell shows poor insight.
  4. Case history: a full developmental, family, medical, and social account of how the problem arose.
  5. Psychological assessment: standardised tests of intelligence, personality, and neuropsychological function to complement the interview.
  6. Case formulation: a working explanation of this person's problem, organised by the 4 Ps.
  7. Diagnosis: matching the picture to a classification system (ICD or DSM).
  8. Intervention: the treatment plan (usually psychotherapy).
  9. Evaluation: reviewing whether the intervention worked and revising as needed.
Key, the 4 Ps of formulation. Predisposing (long-standing vulnerabilities, e.g. genetics, early trauma), Precipitating (the recent trigger, e.g. a job loss), Perpetuating (what keeps it going, e.g. avoidance), Protective (strengths and supports, e.g. a stable family). Formulation explains the case; diagnosis merely labels it.

4. Models of abnormality (paradigms)

ModelCause of disorderTreatment focus
Biological (medical)Genes, brain chemistry, neurotransmittersMedication, physical treatments
PsychodynamicUnconscious conflict, early experience (Freud)Insight, making the unconscious conscious
BehaviouralFaulty learning (conditioning)Relearning, exposure, reinforcement
CognitiveDistorted, maladaptive thinkingRestructuring beliefs (as in CBT)
Humanistic-existentialBlocked growth, loss of meaningAcceptance, self-actualisation, authenticity
SocioculturalFamily, culture, poverty, stigmaFamily and community interventions
From scratch: No single model explains everything, so modern practice integrates them. The biopsychosocial model holds that biological, psychological, and social factors all contribute. The diathesis-stress model refines this: a person carries a diathesis (a predisposition or vulnerability) that only produces disorder when enough stress is applied. Vulnerability plus trigger equals disorder.

5. Ethics and professional issues

  • Confidentiality: keep the patient's information private, with narrow exceptions (serious risk of harm to self or others).
  • Informed consent: the patient must understand and freely agree to assessment and treatment.
  • Competence: practise only within one's training; refer on when a case exceeds it.
  • RCI code of conduct: registered clinical psychologists in India are bound by the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) professional and ethical code.
  • Scope of practice: work within the legal boundaries of the profession (for example, not prescribing medication).
Recap: Clinical psychology assesses, formulates, diagnoses, and treats psychological disorders; it is distinct from psychiatry (a medical, prescribing profession), counselling, and psychiatric social work. Lightner Witmer founded the first psychological clinic (University of Pennsylvania, 1896) and coined the term; the World Wars drove the assessment boom (Army Alpha and Beta); the Boulder Conference (1949) set the scientist-practitioner model, with the Vail scholar-practitioner model as the alternative. In India the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) regulates the field, with the M.Phil giving way to the RCI MA, trained at NIMHANS, CIP, and IHBAS. The clinical method runs referral to interview (structured / semi-structured / unstructured) to MSE (mental status examination) to case history to assessment to formulation (the 4 Ps) to diagnosis to intervention to evaluation. Abnormality is read through biological, psychodynamic, behavioural, cognitive, humanistic-existential, and sociocultural models, integrated by the biopsychosocial and diathesis-stress frameworks, all bounded by confidentiality, informed consent, competence, and the RCI code.

6. The Clinical Interview & the Mental Status Examination (MSE)

Before any test is scored, the clinician's first and most powerful instrument is a structured conversation. The clinical interview is the purposeful, face-to-face exchange through which the psychologist gathers information, builds rapport, observes behaviour, and forms early hypotheses about a client's problem.

From scratch: An interview is not a chat. It has an aim (assessment, diagnosis, therapy planning, or research), a structure the clinician controls, and a running observation of how the person answers, not only what they say.

6.1 Purposes of the clinical interview

  • Rapport: establishing a working alliance so the client discloses freely.
  • Information gathering: presenting complaint, history, symptoms, functioning.
  • Diagnosis: mapping reported symptoms onto ICD-11 / DSM-5 criteria.
  • Observation: live sampling of behaviour, speech, and affect (the MSE).
  • Planning: deciding on further testing, formulation, and management.

6.2 Types of interview by structure

TypeFormatStrength / weakness
StructuredFixed questions, fixed order, standardised (e.g. SCID, MINI)High reliability, comparable; but rigid, low rapport
Semi-structuredCore questions plus freedom to probeBalances reliability and flexibility (most clinical use)
UnstructuredOpen, guided by the clinician's judgementRich rapport and depth; low reliability, hard to compare

Interviews are also classified by aim: intake, diagnostic, crisis, and mental status interviews.

6.3 The Mental Status Examination (MSE)

The Mental Status Examination is a systematic, cross-sectional snapshot of a person's psychological functioning at the moment of examination. It is to psychiatry what the physical examination is to medicine: partly observed, partly elicited. Learn its components in order.

Who → what: Adolf Meyer is credited with formalising the systematic mental status examination in early twentieth-century American psychiatry.
#ComponentWhat is assessed / example terms
1Appearance & behaviourGrooming, dress, eye contact, posture, psychomotor activity (retardation / agitation), rapport, cooperation, mannerisms
2SpeechRate, volume, tone, quantity, fluency (pressured, poverty of speech, mutism, dysarthria)
3Mood & affectMood = sustained subjective emotion ("How have you felt?"); affect = observed, moment-to-moment expression (range, congruence, blunted / flat / labile)
4Thought – form / streamHow thinking flows: circumstantiality, tangentiality, flight of ideas, loosening of associations, thought block, perseveration, neologisms
5Thought – contentWhat is thought: delusions (persecutory, grandiose, nihilistic), obsessions, overvalued ideas, phobias, suicidal / homicidal ideation
6PerceptionHallucinations (auditory, visual, tactile), illusions, depersonalisation, derealisation
7Cognition / sensoriumConsciousness, orientation (time, place, person), attention & concentration, memory (immediate, recent, remote), abstraction
8InsightAwareness that one is ill and that symptoms are part of illness (graded absent → partial → full)
9JudgementCapacity to make sound decisions; tested socially, and by response to hypothetical situations
Key. Mood is what the patient reports (subjective, sustained); affect is what the clinician observes (objective, momentary). This mood-versus-affect distinction is a favourite exam item.
Trap: Thought form (how ideas connect) is not thought content (what the ideas are). Flight of ideas is a form disturbance; a persecutory delusion is a content disturbance. Do not merge them.
Trap: An illusion is a misperception of a real stimulus; a hallucination is a perception without any stimulus. Only the latter signals more severe pathology.
RML tilt: The MSE is the single most testable clinical-skills topic. Memorise the nine headings in sequence and the mood/affect and illusion/hallucination pairs; one-word "which MSE domain?" items appear often.

7. Case History, History-taking & Case Formulation

Where the MSE is a snapshot, the case history is the longitudinal story: how the problem arose and evolved over the person's life.

7.1 Components of history-taking

  • Identifying data and chief / presenting complaint (in the client's words).
  • History of present illness (onset, course, duration, precipitants).
  • Past psychiatric and medical history; treatment history.
  • Family history; personal history (birth, development, education, occupation, marriage).
  • Premorbid personality; substance use; forensic history.

7.2 The biopsychosocial model

Proposed by George Engel (1977), the biopsychosocial model holds that health and illness arise from the interaction of biological (genes, neurochemistry), psychological (thoughts, coping, personality), and social (family, culture, poverty) factors. It replaced the purely biomedical model and underpins modern formulation.

7.3 The 5 Ps of case formulation

PMeaningExample
PresentingThe current problem / symptomsLow mood, hopelessness
PredisposingEarly vulnerabilities that set the stageFamily history, early loss
PrecipitatingRecent triggers of onsetJob loss, bereavement
PerpetuatingFactors maintaining the problemAvoidance, rumination
ProtectiveStrengths and resourcesSupportive spouse, insight
Key. A diagnostic formulation assigns a category (an ICD-11 / DSM-5 label). A case formulation is an individualised, explanatory story of why this person has this problem now, integrating the 5 Ps and the biopsychosocial model. Diagnosis classifies; formulation explains.

8. Psychological Report Writing

The report translates assessment findings into a clear, useful document for a referral source (a psychiatrist, court, school, or the client).

  • Identifying data and reason for referral.
  • Tests administered and behavioural observations during testing.
  • Relevant history and background.
  • Results and interpretation (integrated, not a list of raw scores).
  • Summary, diagnosis / impression, and recommendations.
Trap: A good report is referral-question driven and jargon-light; simply dumping scores without integration and recommendations is poor practice.

9. Community Psychology & Prevention

Community psychology shifts the focus from the individual in the clinic to systems, settings, and social change, stressing prevention over cure and a sense of community and empowerment (helping people gain control over their own lives).

Who → what: Gerald Caplan gave the classic three levels of prevention, and the U.S. Community Mental Health Act (1963) launched the community mental health movement, moving care out of asylums into local centres.
LevelTargetExample
PrimaryPrevent onset in the healthy populationMental-health education, reducing stressors
SecondaryEarly detection and prompt treatmentScreening, crisis intervention
TertiaryReduce disability from established illnessRehabilitation, relapse prevention
Trap: Primary prevention reduces incidence (new cases); secondary reduces prevalence by shortening episodes; tertiary reduces disability, not case numbers.

10. Health Psychology & Behavioural Medicine

Health psychology applies psychology to physical health, illness, and the health-care system, using the biopsychosocial model to explain how mind and behaviour shape bodily illness (e.g. how stress affects cardiovascular disease).

Who → what: Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman proposed the transactional model of stress: stress is the outcome of primary appraisal (Is this a threat?) and secondary appraisal (Can I cope?). Coping is then problem-focused (change the stressor) or emotion-focused (manage the distress).
  • Behavioural medicine: interdisciplinary field integrating behavioural and biomedical knowledge for prevention and treatment.
  • Adherence (compliance): the extent to which patients follow medical advice; poor adherence is a major cause of treatment failure.
Key. In the Lazarus and Folkman model, appraisal (not the event itself) determines the stress response, and coping style is chosen accordingly.

11. Rehabilitation Psychology (RCI-relevant)

Rehabilitation psychology helps people with disability or chronic illness achieve optimal functioning and participation. The World Health Organization distinguishes three levels:

TermLevelMeaning
ImpairmentBody / organLoss of a structure or function (e.g. damaged cochlea)
DisabilityPerson / activityRestricted ability to perform an activity (e.g. cannot hear)
HandicapSocial roleSocial disadvantage arising from disability (e.g. cannot get a job)
  • Community-based rehabilitation (CBR): a WHO strategy delivering rehabilitation within the person's own community using local resources, rather than in institutions.
  • Disability certification: under the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act (2016), a medical board issues a certificate quantifying percentage of disability, needed for benefits.
RML tilt: The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) licenses clinical psychologists; only an RCI-registered practitioner may legally use the title "clinical psychologist," conduct clinical practice, and sign disability certifications. Impairment → disability → handicap is a stock ordering item.

12. Forensic & Clinical-Legal Basics

Forensic psychology sits at the interface of clinical practice and the law.

  • Competency / fitness to stand trial: whether an accused can understand the proceedings and assist in their own defence; distinct from the insanity defence, which concerns the mental state at the time of the offence.
  • Expert testimony: the psychologist gives the court an opinion within their competence, based on assessment, while remaining objective and staying inside their expertise.
Who → what: The Mental Healthcare Act (MHCA) 2017 reframed Indian mental-health law around rights: it recognises a person's right to access care, the advance directive, the nominated representative, and it effectively decriminalised suicide (presuming severe stress) and bars most inhuman treatments.
Trap: Fitness to stand trial (present ability to participate) is not the same as criminal responsibility / insanity (mental state during the crime). Exams love to swap these.
Recap: The interview and MSE capture the present; history-taking and the 5 Ps explain the past-to-present; the report communicates it; community, health, and rehabilitation psychology extend clinical work into prevention, physical illness, and disability; and forensic practice plus the MHCA 2017 and RCI licensing set its legal frame.

13. The Clinical Assessment Process, End to End

Assessment in clinical psychology is not a single test; it is a staged decision process that starts the moment a person is referred and ends only when the clinician can answer the referral question with enough evidence to act. Understanding this pipeline as a sequence is more useful than memorising a list of tools, because each stage narrows the hypotheses carried forward into the next.

13.1 Referral and intake

A referral is the request that starts the case: it may come from a psychiatrist, a general physician, a school, a court, a family member, or the person themselves (self-referral). A good clinician first clarifies the referral question ("Is this depression or a thyroid problem?", "Is this child's reading failure due to low ability or a specific learning disability?"), because the whole assessment is designed to answer it. The intake is the first formal contact: demographic details, presenting complaints in the client's own words, consent, fees or eligibility, and a triage decision about urgency (for example, whether there is active suicidal risk that overrides the usual booking queue).

From scratch: "Presenting complaint" means the problem as the client states it, recorded verbatim. It is distinguished from the clinical problem, which is the clinician's reframing after assessment. A client may present with "I can't sleep"; the clinical problem may turn out to be a major depressive episode.

13.2 The clinical interview: three levels of structure

The interview is the single most important assessment tool. It varies along a dimension of structure, meaning how fixed the questions and their order are.

TypeHow it worksStrengthWeakness
UnstructuredClinician follows the client's lead; no fixed questionsRich rapport, flexibility, unexpected material surfacesLow reliability; two clinicians may reach different conclusions
Semi-structuredCore questions are fixed but the clinician can probe and reorderBalances coverage with clinical judgementNeeds trained interviewers
StructuredExact questions, order and scoring are prescribedHigh reliability; usable by less-experienced ratersRigid; can feel like an interrogation, misses the idiosyncratic
Trap: Do not equate "structured" with "better". Structured interviews maximise reliability (agreement between raters) but can reduce the rapport and idiographic richness that an unstructured interview yields. Choice depends on purpose: research and diagnosis favour structure; a first therapy session often favours flexibility.

13.3 Behavioural assessment

Behavioural assessment asks not "what does the client have?" but "what does the client do, and under what conditions?". Its central tool is functional analysis, which identifies the controlling variables of a behaviour rather than labelling it. The most-taught frameworks are:

  • ABC analysis: Antecedent (what happens before), Behaviour (the observable act), Consequence (what follows and thereby maintains it). Example: Antecedent = teacher gives worksheet; Behaviour = child throws it; Consequence = child is sent out and escapes the task. The escape reinforces the throwing.
  • SORC model: an extension adding the organism. Stimulus to Organism (the person's biology, cognitions, learning history) to Response to Consequence. The "O" acknowledges that the same stimulus produces different responses in different people.
Who → what: Frederick Kanfer and George Saslow developed the SORC-style behavioural-analytic framework as an alternative to psychiatric diagnosis, arguing the clinician should specify the functional relations maintaining a behaviour.

13.4 Psychophysiological assessment and self-monitoring

Psychophysiological assessment measures bodily correlates of psychological states: heart rate, galvanic skin response (electrodermal activity), muscle tension via electromyography (EMG), and cortical activity via electroencephalography (EEG). It is used in anxiety disorders, biofeedback, and research where self-report is unreliable. Self-monitoring asks the client to record their own behaviour in real time (a sleep diary, a thought record, a count of panic attacks). It has a double value: it yields data with less retrospective distortion, and the act of monitoring itself often changes the behaviour (this is reactivity, sometimes therapeutic).

13.5 Choosing and combining sources: the assessment funnel

No single method is trusted alone. Good practice triangulates across multiple sources (client, family, teacher, records), multiple methods (interview, test, observation, physiological measure), and multiple settings (clinic, home, school). Where sources disagree, the disagreement is itself data: a child rated as anxious by a parent but calm by a teacher points to a context-specific problem rather than a pervasive trait. This convergence logic is why a clinician builds a picture, rather than reading a diagnosis off one questionnaire.

Trap: A high score on a self-report inventory (for example the Beck Depression Inventory) is a flag, not a diagnosis. Self-report can be inflated by a wish for help or deflated by defensiveness; it must be integrated with interview and observation before any conclusion.
Recap: referral defines the question, intake triages, the interview gathers the core picture (choose the level of structure to fit the purpose), behavioural assessment maps antecedents and consequences (ABC, SORC) rather than labels, and psychophysiological measures plus self-monitoring add objective and real-time data, all triangulated across sources, methods and settings before any conclusion.

14. Structured Diagnostic Interviews and Rating Scales

When the goal is a reliable psychiatric diagnosis (especially in research or medico-legal work), clinicians use standardised diagnostic interviews that map directly onto DSM or ICD criteria. These reduce the variability of clinical judgement.

InstrumentFull nameNote
SCIDStructured Clinical Interview for DSMAdministered by a trained clinician; the reference standard for DSM diagnoses in research
MINIMini International Neuropsychiatric InterviewShort (about 15 minutes), widely used for quick, reliable screening across major disorders
SCANSchedules for Clinical Assessment in NeuropsychiatryDeveloped under the World Health Organization; semi-structured, ICD-oriented
CIDIComposite International Diagnostic InterviewWHO instrument designed to be given by trained lay interviewers in large epidemiological surveys
Rule: Keep the distinction between a diagnostic interview and a rating scale clear. A diagnostic interview (SCID, MINI) decides whether a disorder is present against defined criteria. A rating scale (for example the Hamilton Depression Rating Scale or the Beck Depression Inventory) measures severity once a problem is identified; it does not by itself make a diagnosis.
Trap: The CIDI is the one designed for lay interviewers and epidemiology; the SCID requires a trained clinician. Exams like to swap these.

15. Case Formulation Approaches Compared

A case formulation is the clinician's working explanation of why this person has these problems now: a hypothesis that links the presenting difficulties to their causes and guides treatment. Different paradigms formulate differently. (The 5 Ps and the biopsychosocial model are treated in section 7 above; here the aim is to contrast the school-specific approaches.)

15.1 Behavioural formulation

Explains the problem in terms of learning: what was classically conditioned, what is operantly reinforced, what was modelled. It rests on the functional analysis (ABC, SORC of section 13.3) and asks what maintains the behaviour now, not only what caused it.

15.2 Cognitive formulation (Beck)

Aaron Temkin Beck's cognitive model organises a case into three layers. Automatic thoughts (the moment-to-moment stream, for example "I'll fail"), dysfunctional assumptions (conditional rules, for example "If I am not perfect, I am worthless"), and core beliefs / schemas (absolute beliefs about self, world and future, for example "I am unlovable"). A cognitive formulation traces the current symptoms up to the schemas that generate them and identifies the maintaining vicious cycles.

Who → what: George Libman Engel proposed the biopsychosocial model in 1977, arguing that biology, psychology and social context must all be integrated rather than reducing illness to biology alone.
RML tilt: Be able to place a case in more than one formulation. A single vignette ("a young man with panic after his father's heart attack") can be read behaviourally (avoidance reinforced by relief), cognitively (catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations, in the style of David M. Clark), and biopsychosocially (family cardiac history + health anxiety + a recent stressor).

16. The Major Paradigms of Abnormality, In Depth

Section 4 introduced the models; here each is drawn out so it can be compared on a common set of features: its view of causes, its treatment logic, and its main criticism.

ParadigmCore claim about causeTreatment logicMain criticism
BiologicalDisorder is bodily: genes, neurotransmitters, brain structureMedication, ECT, physical interventionsReductionist; can neglect meaning and context
PsychodynamicUnconscious conflict, often from early experienceInsight, making the unconscious consciousHard to test; long and costly
BehaviouralMaladaptive learning (conditioning, reinforcement)Relearning: exposure, contingency changeIgnores cognition and meaning (early versions)
CognitiveDistorted thinking and beliefsIdentify and modify thoughts and schemasMay treat thoughts as cause when they are symptom
HumanisticBlocked growth, incongruence, thwarted self-actualisationProvide conditions for growth (empathy, unconditional positive regard)Vague, hard to operationalise
SocioculturalSocial forces: poverty, discrimination, family, cultureChange systems; family and community interventionUnderplays individual biology

16.1 The two integrative models

Two frameworks explicitly refuse a single cause. The diathesis-stress model says disorder arises when a predisposing vulnerability (the diathesis, which may be genetic, biological or psychological) meets a triggering stress; neither alone is sufficient. The biopsychosocial model (Engel) is broader still, holding that biological, psychological and social factors interact at every point of onset, course and recovery. Diathesis-stress is best seen as one well-specified mechanism inside the larger biopsychosocial view.

From scratch: A "diathesis" is a predisposition or vulnerability, not the illness itself. Two people can carry the same diathesis; only the one who also meets sufficient stress crosses into disorder. This is why identical twins are not 100 percent concordant for most disorders.

17. Evidence-Based Practice and the Question of What Works

17.1 The three-legged stool

The American Psychological Association defines evidence-based practice in psychology (EBPP) as the integration of three sources: (1) the best available research evidence, (2) clinical expertise, and (3) the patient's characteristics, culture, values and preferences. It is often pictured as a three-legged stool: remove any leg and practice becomes unbalanced (pure research ignores the person; pure expertise becomes idiosyncratic).

17.2 Empirically supported treatments

Empirically supported treatments (ESTs), an idea advanced through APA Division 12 (Clinical Psychology), are specific treatments shown to work for specific disorders in controlled trials (for example, exposure and response prevention for obsessive-compulsive disorder). EST is a narrower idea than EBPP: EST is about the treatment's research support, whereas EBPP is the broader decision process that also weighs expertise and the patient.

17.3 The common factors debate and the Dodo bird verdict

Against the emphasis on specific techniques stands the common factors position: that most of the benefit of therapy comes from factors shared across all therapies (the therapeutic alliance, empathy, expectancy of improvement, a coherent rationale) rather than the unique ingredients of any one school. Its slogan is the Dodo bird verdict, taken from Alice in Wonderland ("Everybody has won, and all must have prizes"), first applied to psychotherapy by Saul Rosenzweig in 1936 and later associated with the meta-analyses of Lester Luborsky: when bona fide therapies are compared, outcome differences are small.

Trap: The Dodo bird verdict does not say "therapy does not work". It says the differences between established therapies are smaller than their shared effects. It is a claim about relative, not absolute, efficacy, and it is contested for disorders (like specific phobia) where a particular technique clearly outperforms.

18. Clinical Training and Regulation in India

This is high-yield for the RML CET-166 candidate because the exam concerns entry into a regulated profession.

18.1 The Rehabilitation Council of India

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) is the statutory body, set up under the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992, that regulates the training of rehabilitation professionals (including clinical psychologists) and maintains a Central Rehabilitation Register (CRR). A clinical psychologist must hold an RCI-recognised qualification and be registered to practise legally; the registration yields a CRR number that functions as the practising licence.

18.2 Two routes to becoming a clinical psychologist

Historically the recognised professional qualification was the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology (a two-year post-master's programme, RCI-approved, with heavy supervised clinical work). More recently, RCI has approved a Professional MA / MSc in Clinical Psychology (CET-166 route) as an alternative professional entry qualification. Both lead to CRR registration; the difference is the level and length of training, and candidates must confirm which programme a given institution offers.

Rule: Only an RCI-registered person may call themselves a Clinical Psychologist and practise clinical psychology in India. A general MA in Psychology (non-RCI) does not confer this licence, however academically strong.

18.3 Scope of practice and role differences

ProfessionalTrainingCan prescribe?Distinctive role
Clinical psychologistPsychology degree + RCI clinical trainingNoPsychological assessment, diagnosis, psychotherapy
PsychiatristMedical degree (MBBS) + MD PsychiatryYesMedical diagnosis, medication, ECT
Psychiatric social workerMaster of Social Work (psychiatric specialisation)NoFamily, social and community intervention, linkage to resources
Trap: The single sharpest distinction is prescribing: in India the psychiatrist (a physician) prescribes medication; the clinical psychologist does not. Do not blur "psychologist" and "psychiatrist" on the exam.

19. Sub-fields and Specialties of Clinical Psychology

Clinical psychology fans out into specialties defined by population, setting, or problem type.

  • Clinical neuropsychology: the relationship between brain and behaviour; assessment of memory, attention, language and executive function after injury, stroke or dementia (using batteries such as those associated with Alexander Luria and standardised tests like the Halstead-Reitan battery).
  • Child and pediatric clinical psychology: developmental disorders, learning disabilities, and the emotional impact of physical illness in children.
  • Geriatric (clinical geropsychology): dementia, late-life depression, and adjustment to ageing and loss.
  • Health psychology: psychological factors in physical illness and its prevention (treated in section 10).
  • Forensic psychology: the interface with the legal system, including competence, risk and custody (treated in section 12).
  • Community psychology: prevention and system-level change (treated in section 9).

19.1 Consultation-liaison

Consultation-liaison psychiatry (and psychology) is the practice of providing mental-health input to patients on medical wards: for example, assessing depression in a cancer patient or delirium after surgery. The clinician "consults" to the medical team and "liaises" between the psychological and physical care of the patient. It is the clearest institutional expression of the biopsychosocial model.

20. Professional Ethics, In Depth

Section 5 introduced ethics; the following principles recur in exam vignettes and deserve exact treatment.

20.1 Confidentiality and its limits

Confidentiality is the duty not to disclose what a client reveals. It is strong but not absolute. Recognised limits include risk of serious harm to self or others, suspected abuse of a child or vulnerable adult, and legal compulsion (a court order). The clinician should explain these limits at the outset, not spring them later.

20.2 Informed consent

Informed consent requires that the client agrees to assessment or treatment with adequate information (what it involves, risks, alternatives, fees, limits of confidentiality) and does so voluntarily and with the capacity to decide. For children and those lacking capacity, assent from the person plus consent from a guardian is sought.

20.3 Dual relationships and competence

A dual (or multiple) relationship exists when the clinician has a second role with the client (friend, employer, business partner). These are discouraged because they impair objectivity and risk exploitation. Competence is the duty to practise only within the boundaries of one's training and to refer on when a case exceeds it.

20.4 The duty to warn (Tarasoff)

Who → what: The Tarasoff ruling (Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California, 1976) established the therapist's duty to protect an identifiable third party from a serious threat made by a client. The court's memorable line was that "protective privilege ends where the public peril begins." It is the classic case where confidentiality yields to the safety of others.
Trap: Tarasoff is often called "duty to warn", but the later refinement broadened it to a duty to protect (which may be met by warning the victim, notifying police, or hospitalising the client). Exams reward the "protect" phrasing.

21. Contemporary Directions: Digital, Cultural and Positive

21.1 Telepsychology and digital mental health

Telepsychology is the delivery of psychological services through telecommunication (video, phone, chat). It expands access, especially in a country with wide rural-urban gaps, but raises new issues: verifying identity, ensuring privacy and data security, managing crises at a distance, and confirming that the clinician is licensed to practise where the client is located. Digital mental health extends this to apps, chatbots and internet-delivered CBT, which offer scale but require caution about evidence and safety.

21.2 Cultural competence and cultural formulation

Cultural competence is the clinician's ability to work effectively across cultural differences: awareness of one's own biases, knowledge of the client's cultural context, and skills to adapt assessment and treatment. The DSM-5 provides a Cultural Formulation Interview (CFI) to elicit how the client understands their problem within their culture, its causes, and their expectations of help.

From scratch: Symptoms are shaped by culture. Distress that one culture expresses as low mood another may express through bodily complaints (somatisation) or through idioms like "tension" or "gas". Ignoring this leads to misdiagnosis.

21.3 Positive clinical psychology, recovery and stigma

Positive clinical psychology argues that well-being is more than the absence of symptoms and that assessment should measure strengths, resilience and positive functioning alongside pathology. The recovery model, prominent in serious mental illness, redefines the goal from "cure of symptoms" to a life of meaning, hope and agency defined by the person themselves, even where some symptoms persist. Both stand against stigma, the negative labelling and social exclusion of people with mental illness, which is itself a barrier to seeking care (self-stigma) and to social inclusion (public and structural stigma).

Recap: modern clinical psychology adds delivery through technology (with its privacy and licensure caveats), an explicit commitment to culture (the Cultural Formulation Interview), and a shift of emphasis from deficit-only thinking toward strengths, recovery, and the reduction of stigma.

9 Counselling & Psychotherapy (who developed what)

From scratch: A psychotherapy is a planned, structured psychological treatment in which a trained clinician uses a relationship and a set of techniques to relieve distress and change thinking, feeling or behaviour. Each therapy grew out of a particular school (a theory of what goes wrong and how change happens), and almost every school is tied to one or two named founders. The RML CET-166 paper loves exactly this fact. It rarely asks you to do therapy; it asks who developed which therapy and what does the acronym stand for. So learn each pairing until it is reflexive.
RML tilt: Expect stems like "The ABC model belongs to ___", "EMDR was developed by ___", "Person-Centred Therapy was founded by ___", "DBT was developed by ___". Master the two tables at the end and you have banked a cluster of marks.

1. Psychoanalytic & psychodynamic therapies

Who → what: Sigmund Freud → Psychoanalysis, the original insight therapy. Core techniques: free association (say whatever comes to mind), dream analysis (dreams as "the royal road to the unconscious"), analysis of transference (the patient projects feelings for early figures onto the analyst), resistance, and interpretation of unconscious conflict.
From scratch: Freud held that symptoms come from unconscious conflict, often rooted in childhood and sexuality. The cure is to make the unconscious conscious, so the ego can master what was repressed. This is insight-oriented and long-term.
Who → what: Carl Gustav Jung → Analytical Psychology (collective unconscious, archetypes, individuation). Alfred Adler → Individual Psychology (inferiority/superiority, style of life, social interest).
Who → what: Melanie Klein → object relations/play therapy; Donald Winnicott → "good-enough mother", transitional object; Heinz Kohut → self psychology; David Malan and Peter Sifneos → brief/short-term psychodynamic therapy. Modern psychodynamic therapy keeps interpretation and transference but is shorter and more focused.
Key. Freud = free association + dream analysis + transference + interpretation. That four-word chain is the single most examined item in this school.

2. Behaviour therapy

From scratch: Behaviour therapy assumes maladaptive behaviour is learned and can be unlearned using classical and operant conditioning. It targets observable behaviour, not the unconscious.
Who → what: Joseph Wolpe → Systematic Desensitization, built on his principle of reciprocal inhibition (a fear response cannot occur at the same time as relaxation, so pairing a feared image with deep relaxation, moving up a graded fear hierarchy, extinguishes the anxiety). A snake-phobic learns relaxation, then imagines snakes from a distant photo up to holding one.
Who → what: Mary Cover Jones → the "mother of behaviour therapy"; she deconditioned little Peter's fear of rabbits (early counter-conditioning), the practical ancestor of desensitization.
Who → what: Burrhus Frederic (B.F.) Skinner → operant conditioning, and its clinical child the token economy (earned tokens exchanged for privileges reinforce target behaviour, classically on inpatient wards).
Who → what: Exposure therapies (in-vivo, imaginal, flooding, graded exposure, exposure-and-response-prevention for OCD) → grounded in extinction; aversion therapy pairs an unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus. Flooding = full-intensity exposure with no gradual hierarchy, the opposite of Wolpe's graded method.
Trap: Systematic desensitization is graded and paired with relaxation (Wolpe); flooding is maximal and un-graded. Do not swap them. Also: Mary Cover Jones (not Watson) did the counter-conditioning of fear; Watson only created a fear (Little Albert).

3. Cognitive therapy & CBT

From scratch: The cognitive model says it is not events but our interpretations of events that drive emotion. Change the thinking and the feeling follows. Two founders anchor this, and RML routinely pits them against each other.
Who → what: Aaron Temkin Beck → Cognitive Therapy (CT). Key constructs: the negative cognitive triad (negative view of the self, world and future, central to depression), automatic thoughts, schemas, and cognitive distortions (e.g. all-or-nothing thinking, overgeneralization, catastrophizing, personalization). Beck's method is collaborative empiricism, testing thoughts like hypotheses.
Who → what: Albert Ellis → Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy (REBT) and the ABC model: A = Activating event, B = Belief (especially irrational beliefs), C = Consequence (emotion/behaviour). Ellis added D = Disputing the irrational belief and E = Effective new belief. REBT is the earliest cognitive therapy (1955).
Trap: ABC model + REBT + "irrational beliefs" → Albert Ellis. Cognitive triad + cognitive distortions → Aaron Beck. These two are the most commonly confused pair on the paper. Ellis disputes; Beck tests.
Who → what: Donald Meichenbaum → Self-Instructional Training and Stress Inoculation Training (SIT) (teaching coping self-talk in a "conceptualization → skills → application" sequence, a cognitive-behavioural way of vaccinating against stress).

4. Humanistic & existential therapies
