From scratch: The "third force" rejects both the deterministic unconscious and mechanical conditioning. People are seen as growth-seeking; therapy provides conditions for that self-actualizing tendency to work.
Who → what: Carl Rogers → Person-Centred Therapy (also called Client-Centred Therapy). The three core conditions the therapist must offer are empathy (accurate empathic understanding), unconditional positive regard (non-judgemental warmth) and congruence (genuineness/authenticity). These three words are examined verbatim.
Who → what: Fritz (Frederick) Perls → Gestalt Therapy; here-and-now awareness, "unfinished business", and the famous empty chair technique.
Who → what: Viktor Frankl → Logotherapy (therapy through meaning; "will to meaning", from his concentration-camp experience in Man's Search for Meaning). Techniques: paradoxical intention, dereflection.
Who → what: Rollo May (father of American existential therapy) and Irvin Yalom → Existential Therapy. Yalom's four "ultimate concerns": death, freedom, isolation, meaninglessness.
Key. Empathy + Unconditional Positive Regard + Congruence = Carl Rogers' core conditions. Empty chair = Perls / Gestalt. Meaning = Frankl / Logotherapy.

5. Family & systemic therapies

From scratch: Systemic therapy treats the family or system as the client. The identified patient's symptom is understood as serving, or being maintained by, the family's patterns.
Who → what: Salvador Minuchin → Structural Family Therapy (family structure, subsystems, boundaries that are rigid, clear or diffuse, enactment).
Who → what: Murray Bowen → Bowenian / Intergenerational Family Therapy; key ideas differentiation of self, triangulation, and the genogram (family-tree map).
Who → what: Virginia Satir → Experiential / Conjoint (Communication) Family Therapy; growth, self-esteem, communication stances (placater, blamer, etc.), family sculpting.
Who → what: Jay Haley (with Cloe Madanes) → Strategic Family Therapy (directives, paradox, changing the problem-maintaining sequence). Mara Selvini Palazzoli → the Milan systemic approach.
Trap: Genogram + differentiation → Bowen. Boundaries + structure → Minuchin. Communication stances / sculpting → Satir. Directives / strategic → Haley. The four family names are a classic matching item.

6. Third-wave & modern therapies (acronym central)

From scratch: "Third-wave" CBTs shift from changing thoughts to changing one's relationship to thoughts, using acceptance, mindfulness and values. This is where nearly all the acronyms live, so this is RML's favourite hunting ground.
Who → what: Marsha Linehan → Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT), developed for borderline personality disorder and chronic suicidality; balances acceptance and change; four skill modules: mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, interpersonal effectiveness.
Who → what: Steven C. Hayes → Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT); psychological flexibility, acceptance, defusion, and committed values-based action.
Who → what: William Miller and Stephen Rollnick → Motivational Interviewing (MI); a collaborative, client-centred method for resolving ambivalence, rolling with resistance and eliciting "change talk" (big in addictions).
Who → what: Francine Shapiro → Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing (EMDR); bilateral stimulation (guided eye movements) while recalling trauma, used for PTSD.
Who → what: Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman → Interpersonal Therapy (IPT); a time-limited therapy for depression targeting grief, role disputes, role transitions and interpersonal deficits.
Who → what: Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg → Solution-Focused Brief Therapy (SFBT); the miracle question, exception-finding and scaling questions, focus on solutions not causes.
Who → what: Jon Kabat-Zinn → Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR), an 8-week secular mindfulness programme. Building on it, Zindel Segal, Mark Williams and John Teasdale → Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy (MBCT), which adds cognitive therapy to prevent depressive relapse.
RML tilt: The single most likely acronym questions are DBT→Linehan, ACT→Hayes, REBT→Ellis, EMDR→Shapiro and MBSR→Kabat-Zinn. Over-learn those five.

7. Common (curative) factors & the therapeutic alliance

From scratch: Beyond any one school, research (the "Dodo bird verdict": all therapies work about equally) suggests much of the benefit comes from factors shared across therapies rather than the specific technique.
Who → what: Jerome Frank (Persuasion and Healing) → the classic common-factors account: an emotionally charged confiding relationship, a healing setting, a rationale/myth, and a ritual/procedure, all instilling hope.
Key. Common factors: the therapeutic alliance (bond + agreement on goals and tasks, per Edward Bordin), therapist empathy (Carl Rogers again), client expectancy/hope, and the healing ritual. These outweigh brand-name technique for many outcomes.

Master table 1, Therapy → Founder → Core idea

Trap: Do not confuse the two "mindfulness" acronyms: MBSR (stress reduction) is Kabat-Zinn; MBCT (cognitive therapy for relapse prevention) is Segal, Williams & Teasdale and is built on top of MBSR. And DBT (Linehan) is not ACT (Hayes), though both are third-wave.
Recap: Freud = psychoanalysis (free association, transference, dream analysis). Wolpe = systematic desensitization via reciprocal inhibition; Skinner = token economy; Mary Cover Jones = mother of behaviour therapy. Beck = cognitive triad/distortions; Ellis = REBT + ABC model; Meichenbaum = stress inoculation. Rogers = Person-Centred + core conditions (empathy, UPR, congruence); Perls = Gestalt/empty chair; Frankl = Logotherapy; May & Yalom = existential. Family four: Minuchin (structural/boundaries), Bowen (genogram/differentiation), Satir (experiential), Haley (strategic). Acronyms: DBT = Linehan, ACT = Hayes, MI = Miller & Rollnick, EMDR = Shapiro, IPT = Klerman & Weissman, SFBT = de Shazer & Berg, MBSR = Kabat-Zinn, MBCT = Segal/Williams/Teasdale, REBT = Ellis. Jerome Frank = common factors; the therapeutic alliance carries much of the outcome.

8. Group Therapy: Irvin Yalom's Therapeutic Factors

From scratch: Group therapy treats several clients together in a facilitated group (usually 6 to 12 members), on the premise that the group itself is the healing agent. The person often cited for the modern theory of group psychotherapy is Irvin Yalom, an American existential psychiatrist, whose textbook The Theory and Practice of Group Psychotherapy is the standard reference.

Who → what: Irvin Yalom → the 11 therapeutic (curative) factors of group therapy, and the modern interpersonal-existential model of group work.

Yalom's 11 therapeutic factors

  • Instillation of hope: seeing others improve gives faith in recovery.
  • Universality: realising "I am not the only one" (my problems are shared).
  • Imparting information: teaching, advice, and psychoeducation.
  • Altruism: helping fellow members raises self-worth.
  • Corrective recapitulation of the primary family group: the group re-enacts the family of origin so old conflicts can be worked through.
  • Development of socialising techniques: learning basic social skills.
  • Imitative behaviour (modelling): copying healthier members and the therapist.
  • Interpersonal learning: gaining insight from feedback about how one relates to others.
  • Group cohesiveness: the sense of belonging and acceptance (Yalom's analogue of the therapeutic alliance, considered the most important factor).
  • Catharsis: the ventilation of strong emotion within a safe group.
  • Existential factors: accepting responsibility, mortality, and the limits of life.

RML tilt: The count "11" and the definitions of universality, instillation of hope, and cohesiveness are the classic single-word items. Cohesiveness is the group parallel of the alliance.

Types of groups and process

  • Types: psychoeducational, support/self-help, process (interpersonal), psychodynamic, cognitive-behavioural, and encounter/T-groups. Alcoholics Anonymous is a self-help group.
  • Group stages (Bruce Tuckman): forming → storming → norming → performing → adjourning.

Trap: Tuckman gave the stages of group development (forming, storming, norming, performing); Yalom gave the therapeutic factors. Do not swap them.

9. Play Therapy

From scratch: Play therapy uses play, a child's natural medium of expression, as the vehicle for therapy because young children cannot verbalise conflicts the way adults can. There are two broad traditions: psychoanalytic and non-directive/humanistic.

Who → what: Virginia Axline → non-directive (child-centred) play therapy and its 8 principles; Melanie Klein → psychoanalytic play technique (play as free association); Anna Freud → play used to build the therapeutic alliance before analysis; Margaret Lowenfeld → the "World Technique" that became sandplay (later developed by Dora Kalff).

Virginia Axline's non-directive play therapy

Axline applied Carl Rogers' client-centred principles to children: the child leads the play, the therapist follows. Her 8 basic principles are the exam favourite: (1) build a warm relationship, (2) accept the child exactly as they are, (3) establish permissiveness so the child expresses freely, (4) recognise and reflect feelings, (5) respect the child's ability to solve their own problems, (6) let the child lead (do not direct), (7) do not hurry the process, and (8) set only necessary limits.

Psychoanalytic play and sandplay

  • Melanie Klein: treated the child's play as the equivalent of adult free association and interpreted it directly, often around early aggressive and depressive anxieties.
  • Anna Freud: more cautious; used play to form a relationship and strengthen the ego before interpreting.
  • Sandplay: the child builds scenes with miniatures in a tray of sand; rooted in Lowenfeld's World Technique and formalised by Dora Kalff on a Jungian basis.

Trap: Axline = non-directive/child-centred (Rogerian). Klein = directive interpretation. The Klein vs Anna Freud debate is about whether the child's play can be interpreted like free association (Klein: yes; Anna Freud: not immediately).

10. Behavioural Techniques in Depth

Who → what: Joseph Wolpe → systematic desensitization and reciprocal inhibition; Thomas Stampfl → implosive therapy; Edmund Jacobson → progressive muscular relaxation; B. F. Skinner → operant basis of token economy; Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin → the first token economy; Albert Bandura → modelling (observational learning); Neal Miller → early biofeedback demonstrations.

Anxiety-reduction techniques

  • Systematic desensitization (Wolpe): pair deep relaxation with a graded anxiety hierarchy so the feared stimulus stops evoking anxiety. Its mechanism is reciprocal inhibition (relaxation and anxiety cannot coexist, so relaxation inhibits anxiety).
  • Flooding: in-vivo exposure to the full-intensity feared stimulus with no gradation, held until anxiety extinguishes.
  • Implosive therapy (Stampfl): like flooding but imaginal and psychodynamically themed; the client imagines the worst-case scene.
  • Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP): the frontline behavioural treatment for OCD; the client faces the trigger and is prevented from performing the compulsion.
  • Progressive muscular relaxation (Jacobson): tense then release muscle groups in sequence to produce deep relaxation.

Trap: Systematic desensitization is graded and uses relaxation; flooding/implosion are ungraded and use maximum intensity. Reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's term, not extinction.

Operant and learning-based techniques

  • Token economy (Ayllon & Azrin, on Skinner's operant principles): desired behaviours earn tokens that are exchanged for backup reinforcers; used on inpatient and developmental-disability wards.
  • Aversion therapy: pairs an unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus (classical conditioning). Antabuse (disulfiram) for alcohol; covert sensitization uses imagined aversive scenes.
  • Modelling (Bandura): the client learns by watching a model; includes participant modelling for phobias.
  • Biofeedback: electronic feedback of a physiological signal (heart rate, muscle tension) lets the client learn voluntary control.
  • Contingency management: systematically rewarding target behaviours (and withholding reward for others); widely used in substance-use treatment.

11. Motivational Interviewing and the Stages of Change

Who → what: William Miller and Stephen Rollnick → Motivational Interviewing (MI); James Prochaska and Carlo DiClemente → the Transtheoretical Model (Stages of Change).

From scratch: Motivational Interviewing is a collaborative, client-centred style for strengthening a person's own motivation to change, developed for addictions. Its spirit is often summarised as PACE (Partnership, Acceptance, Compassion, Evocation), and it works by rolling with resistance and eliciting "change talk" rather than confronting.

The Transtheoretical Stages of Change

StageWhere the person is
PrecontemplationNot yet considering change; may deny a problem.
ContemplationAmbivalent; weighing pros and cons.
PreparationIntends to act soon; making a plan.
ActionActively modifying behaviour.
MaintenanceSustaining change and preventing relapse.

RML tilt: The 5 stages in order and the pairing (Prochaska & DiClemente = stages; Miller & Rollnick = MI) are the exam points. Relapse is treated as a possible loop back, not a formal sixth stage in the classic list.

12. Interpersonal Therapy (IPT)

Who → what: Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman → Interpersonal Therapy (IPT).

From scratch: IPT is a time-limited, evidence-based therapy developed for depression that links mood symptoms to current relationships. It works on four problem areas: (1) grief (complicated bereavement), (2) interpersonal role disputes, (3) role transitions, and (4) interpersonal deficits (loneliness/isolation). It focuses on the here-and-now, not childhood.

Trap: IPT (Klerman & Weissman) is not the same as CBT (Beck) or psychoanalysis; it is interpersonal and present-focused, and its four foci are the give-away detail.

13. Solution-Focused and Narrative Therapies

Who → what: Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg → Solution-Focused Brief Therapy (SFBT); Michael White and David Epston → Narrative Therapy.

Solution-Focused Brief Therapy

SFBT (de Shazer & Berg, Milwaukee) ignores the origins of the problem and builds on exceptions and future solutions. Signature techniques: the miracle question ("if you woke and the problem were gone, what would be different?"), scaling questions (rate 0 to 10), and exception questions (times the problem was absent).

Narrative Therapy

Narrative Therapy (White & Epston) sees problems as stories that dominate a person's identity. Its core move is externalisation ("the person is not the problem; the problem is the problem"), followed by finding unique outcomes and re-authoring a preferred story.

Trap: Miracle question → de Shazer/Berg (SFBT). Externalisation → White/Epston (Narrative). Both are postmodern/social-constructionist, but the techniques distinguish them.

14. Psychodrama

Who → what: Jacob Levy Moreno → Psychodrama (and sociometry).

From scratch: Psychodrama is a group method in which clients act out situations from their lives rather than only talking about them. Key elements: the protagonist (whose story is enacted), auxiliary egos (members who play other people), the director (therapist), the stage, and the audience. Signature techniques include role reversal, doubling, mirroring, and soliloquy. Moreno also coined "group psychotherapy" and invented sociometry.

15. Crisis Intervention, Supportive Therapy, and Evidence-Based Practice

  • Crisis intervention: short, immediate, present-focused help to restore functioning after an acute crisis (bereavement, disaster, suicidality); Gerald Caplan's crisis theory and models such as Albert Roberts' seven-stage model are the reference points. The aim is stabilisation, not personality change.
  • Supportive psychotherapy: strengthens existing defences and coping through reassurance, encouragement, and advice rather than uncovering the unconscious; used when insight-oriented work is unsuitable (for example, in severe or acute illness).
  • Evidence-based practice (EBP): integrates the best available research evidence, clinical expertise, and patient values/preferences (the three-legged stool, from the American Psychological Association's 2006 policy).

RML tilt: Distinguish crisis intervention (acute, time-limited, restore prior functioning) from supportive therapy (ongoing, bolster coping, no deep uncovering).

Recap: Yalom = 11 group factors (cohesiveness = the alliance); Axline = 8 non-directive play principles; Wolpe = systematic desensitization via reciprocal inhibition; Ayllon & Azrin = token economy; Bandura = modelling; Jacobson = progressive muscular relaxation; Miller & Rollnick = Motivational Interviewing; Prochaska & DiClemente = 5 Stages of Change; Klerman & Weissman = IPT (4 problem areas); de Shazer & Berg = SFBT (miracle question); White & Epston = Narrative (externalisation); Moreno = Psychodrama.

16. REBT (Albert Ellis) versus Beck's cognitive therapy: the full distinction

Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy (REBT) was created by Albert Ellis in 1955, which makes it the first of the cognitive therapies (predating Beck by roughly a decade). Ellis argued that events do not disturb people; rather, people disturb themselves by the rigid, absolutistic beliefs they hold about events.

Who → what: Albert Ellis → REBT (originally "Rational Therapy", then "Rational Emotive Therapy", finally REBT in 1993). Aaron T. Beck → Cognitive Therapy (CT), the parent of modern CBT.

The ABCDE model

The core assessment frame is the ABC model, extended in treatment to ABCDE:

  • A – Activating event: the trigger, real or imagined.
  • B – Belief: the evaluation of A, which may be rational or irrational.
  • C – Consequence: the emotional and behavioural result. Crucially, C flows from B, not from A.
  • D – Disputing: the therapist actively, forcefully challenges the irrational belief.
  • E – Effective new philosophy (or Effect): a rational belief and a healthier emotional consequence.

Ellis grouped irrational beliefs under the word "musturbation" (his coinage): rigid musts and shoulds directed at self, others, and the world. From these flow four derivatives: awfulising ("it is awful/terrible"), low frustration tolerance or "I can't stand it"-itis, and global rating / damnation of self or others ("I am worthless").

How Beck differs

Beck's cognitive therapy shares the premise that thinking drives emotion but diverges sharply in method and vocabulary.

DimensionEllis (REBT)Beck (CT)
Target unitIrrational beliefs (evaluative "musts")Automatic thoughts → intermediate beliefs → core schemas
Therapist stanceDirective, confrontational, philosophical disputationCollaborative empiricism (a shared "let us test this")
MethodPersuasion, logic, humour, forceful disputingGuided discovery, Socratic questioning, behavioural experiments
View of the beliefBeliefs are irrational and to be replacedThoughts are distorted hypotheses to be tested against evidence
Signature conceptMusturbation, LFT, awfulisingCognitive distortions, negative cognitive triad, downward arrow
Trap: Do not attribute the "cognitive triad" (negative view of self, world, and future) to Ellis. That is Beck. Ellis's signature term is musturbation. Also, Socratic questioning is Beck's tone; forceful disputing is Ellis's.
RML tilt: A stem may ask "who introduced the ABC model of emotional disturbance?" (Ellis) or "collaborative empiricism is associated with?" (Beck). Learn the pairing, not just the names.

Beck's cognitive distortions (the named list)

Because RML frequently asks for a specific distortion by name, learn Beck's catalogue: all-or-nothing (dichotomous) thinking, overgeneralisation, mental filter (dwelling on one negative), disqualifying the positive, jumping to conclusions (mind reading and fortune telling), magnification/minimisation (including catastrophising), emotional reasoning ("I feel it, so it is true"), should statements, labelling, and personalisation. The downward arrow technique drills from a surface automatic thought to the underlying core belief by repeatedly asking "if that were true, what would it mean?"

17. Exposure therapy: the full family of variants

All exposure methods rest on the principle that avoidance maintains anxiety, and that confronting the feared stimulus allows habituation and, in modern accounts, inhibitory (new-safety) learning to occur.

Systematic desensitization

Joseph Wolpe (1958) developed systematic desensitization on the principle of reciprocal inhibition: an anxiety response cannot coexist with an incompatible relaxation response. Three steps: (1) train deep muscle relaxation, (2) build an anxiety hierarchy from least to most feared, (3) pair relaxation with each step, moving up gradually. It is graded and counterconditioning-based.

Flooding and implosion

Flooding exposes the client to the most feared stimulus at full intensity, in vivo, with no relaxation and no escape, until anxiety extinguishes. Implosive therapy (Thomas Stampfl) does the same in imagination, often with psychodynamically themed cues.

Graded exposure and ERP

Graded exposure climbs a hierarchy in vivo without the relaxation component (distinguishing it from desensitization). Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP), the first-line behavioural treatment for OCD, exposes the client to the obsessional trigger (for example, touching a contaminated surface) while preventing the compulsion (hand-washing), so the client learns the feared outcome does not occur and anxiety falls without the ritual. ERP is strongly linked to Victor Meyer (first application) and Edna Foa's later refinement.

Interoceptive and virtual-reality exposure

Interoceptive exposure deliberately provokes feared bodily sensations (spinning to induce dizziness, breathing through a straw for breathlessness) and is central to panic disorder treatment, where the fear is of the sensations themselves. Virtual reality (VR) exposure uses computer-generated environments for phobias (flying, heights) when in-vivo exposure is impractical.

Rule: Desensitization = relaxation + graded hierarchy (counterconditioning). Flooding = maximum intensity, no relaxation. ERP = exposure + blocking the ritual (OCD). Interoceptive = feared body sensations (panic).
Who → what: Joseph Wolpe → systematic desensitization and reciprocal inhibition; Thomas Stampfl → implosive therapy; Victor Meyer / Edna Foa → ERP for OCD.
From scratch: The older explanation of why exposure works was habituation (repeated, prolonged contact makes the fear response fade). The current explanation is inhibitory learning (Michelle Craske): the old fear memory is not erased but is over-written by a new "this is safe" memory, so exposure aims to maximise the mismatch between what the client expects and what actually happens. This is why brief, escape-driven exposures fail: the client never learns the feared outcome does not occur.

18. Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (Marsha Linehan)

Marsha Linehan developed DBT in the late 1980s, originally for chronically suicidal women and borderline personality disorder (BPD). "Dialectical" refers to holding two opposites together, above all acceptance and change: the therapist radically accepts the client as they are and pushes for change.

The biosocial theory

Linehan's biosocial model holds that BPD arises from a biological emotional vulnerability (high sensitivity, high reactivity, slow return to baseline) transacting with a chronically invalidating environment that dismisses or punishes the person's emotional experience.

The four skills modules

  • Mindfulness (the core skill): observing, describing, participating; "wise mind" balancing emotion mind and reasonable mind.
  • Distress tolerance: surviving crises without making them worse (TIPP, radical acceptance, distraction).
  • Emotion regulation: identifying, reducing vulnerability to, and changing emotions (opposite action).
  • Interpersonal effectiveness: asking for what one needs and saying no while keeping the relationship and self-respect (DEAR MAN, GIVE, FAST).
Trap: The two "acceptance" modules are mindfulness and distress tolerance; the two "change" modules are emotion regulation and interpersonal effectiveness. DBT delivery combines individual therapy, group skills training, phone coaching, and a therapist consultation team.

19. Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (Steven Hayes)

Steven C. Hayes developed ACT (said as one word, "act") within the framework of Relational Frame Theory. The goal is not symptom reduction but psychological flexibility: contacting the present moment fully and, based on chosen values, changing or persisting in behaviour.

The hexaflex: six core processes

  • Acceptance (versus experiential avoidance).
  • Cognitive defusion (seeing thoughts as thoughts, not literal truths).
  • Contact with the present moment (mindful awareness).
  • Self-as-context (the observing self).
  • Values (chosen life directions).
  • Committed action (values-consistent behaviour).
From scratch: ACT does not try to make bad thoughts go away. It changes the client's relationship to those thoughts so they no longer control behaviour. This is what "third wave" means: the target shifts from thought content to the context and function of thinking.

The six processes are often reduced to three pillars: Open (acceptance and defusion), Aware (present moment and self-as-context), and Engaged (values and committed action). The opposite of flexibility, the pathology target, is experiential avoidance plus cognitive fusion. A common ACT metaphor is "passengers on the bus": unwanted thoughts are passengers the driver need not obey.

Trap: ACT belongs to the same "third wave" as DBT, MBCT, and Marsha Linehan's work, all of which fold in mindfulness and acceptance rather than direct cognitive restructuring. Do not classify ACT as classical (second-wave) CBT.

20. Schema, mindfulness-based, and EMDR approaches

Schema therapy (Jeffrey Young)

Jeffrey Young developed schema therapy by extending Beck's CT to entrenched personality patterns. It targets early maladaptive schemas (for example, abandonment, defectiveness, mistrust) formed from unmet childhood needs, and works through schema modes using limited reparenting and imagery rescripting.

MBCT and MBSR

Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy (MBCT) was created by Zindel Segal, Mark Williams, and John Teasdale to prevent relapse in recurrent depression by teaching decentring from ruminative thought. It grafted mindfulness onto CT and drew directly on Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR), the 8-week programme developed by Jon Kabat-Zinn at the University of Massachusetts for chronic pain and stress.

EMDR (Francine Shapiro)

Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing, developed by Francine Shapiro (1987), is used for trauma and PTSD. The client holds a distressing memory while tracking bilateral stimulation (typically the therapist's moving finger), across an 8-phase protocol built on the Adaptive Information Processing model.

Who → what: Young → schema therapy; Segal, Williams & Teasdale → MBCT (depression relapse); Kabat-Zinn → MBSR; Shapiro → EMDR (trauma).
Trap: MBSR is Kabat-Zinn and is not a therapy for depression relapse; MBCT (Segal/Williams/Teasdale) is the depression-relapse programme. Do not swap them.

21. Gestalt, reality therapy, and transactional analysis

Gestalt therapy (Fritz Perls)

Fritz (Frederick) Perls, with Laura Perls and Paul Goodman, founded Gestalt therapy. It emphasises the here and now, awareness, and taking responsibility for one's experience. Signature techniques: the empty chair and two-chair dialogues, working with the "top dog / under dog" split, and completing "unfinished business".

Reality therapy and choice theory (William Glasser)

William Glasser developed Reality Therapy, grounded in Choice Theory: behaviour is a chosen attempt to meet five basic needs (survival, love/belonging, power, freedom, fun). The therapist focuses on present behaviour and personal responsibility, summarised in the WDEP system (Wants, Doing, Evaluation, Planning). It rejects the "mental illness" and blaming-the-past framings.

Transactional Analysis (Eric Berne)

Eric Berne founded Transactional Analysis (TA). Its building blocks:

  • Ego states: the Parent, Adult, Child (the PAC model), analysed through transactions between two people (complementary, crossed, ulterior).
  • Strokes: units of recognition; people need them and will seek negative strokes over none.
  • Games: repetitive, ulterior-motive interactions with a predictable payoff, catalogued in Games People Play.
  • Life script: an unconscious life plan formed in childhood; life positions such as "I'm OK, You're OK".
Trap: Berne's Parent-Adult-Child ego states are not Freud's superego-ego-id. TA is a distinct social model; the resemblance is superficial.

22. Person-centred and existential therapies (recap and depth)

Rogers' core conditions

Carl Rogers' person-centred therapy holds that clients grow when the therapist provides three necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (genuineness), unconditional positive regard, and accurate empathic understanding. Growth is driven by the client's own actualising tendency; the therapist is non-directive.

The existential therapists

  • Viktor Frankl → Logotherapy: the "will to meaning" is the primary human drive. Techniques include paradoxical intention and dereflection. Rooted in his concentration-camp experience (Man's Search for Meaning).
  • Irvin Yalom: the four ultimate concerns or givens of existence: death, freedom (and responsibility), isolation, and meaninglessness.
  • Rollo May: introduced existential psychology to America; wrote on anxiety, the daimonic, and the courage to create.
Who → what: Frankl → logotherapy / paradoxical intention; Yalom → four existential concerns; May → American existential psychology; Rogers → person-centred core conditions.

23. Family and systemic therapies in depth

Bowen's intergenerational (natural systems) therapy

Murray Bowen viewed the family as an emotional unit across generations. Key concepts: differentiation of self (separating thinking from feeling, and self from the family's emotional fusion); the triangle (a two-person system pulls in a third to lower tension); the nuclear family emotional system; the family projection process; the multigenerational transmission process; and societal emotional process. Bowen popularised the genogram as a mapping tool.

Minuchin's structural family therapy

Salvador Minuchin focused on the family's structure: boundaries (rigid, clear, or diffuse), subsystems (spousal, parental, sibling), and hierarchy. Pathology appears as enmeshment or disengagement. Techniques: joining, enactment, and boundary making to restructure the family.

Haley's strategic therapy and the Milan approach

Jay Haley (with the influence of Milton Erickson and the MRI group) developed strategic therapy: the therapist designs a specific strategy or directive for the presenting problem, including paradoxical directives and prescribing the symptom. The Milan systemic team (Mara Selvini Palazzoli and colleagues) added circular questioning, hypothesising, neutrality, and positive connotation of the symptom.

Satir's experiential (communication) approach

Virginia Satir emphasised communication, self-esteem, and emotional experience. She described five communication stances under stress: placater, blamer, computer (super-reasonable), distractor (irrelevant), and leveller (the one congruent stance). She used family sculpting to make relational patterns visible.

ApproachFounderSignature concept
IntergenerationalMurray BowenDifferentiation, triangles, genogram
StructuralSalvador MinuchinBoundaries, subsystems, enactment
StrategicJay HaleyDirectives, prescribing the symptom
Milan systemicSelvini PalazzoliCircular questioning, positive connotation
ExperientialVirginia SatirCommunication stances, sculpting
RML tilt: "Family sculpting" → Satir; "genogram" → Bowen; "enactment" → Minuchin; "prescribing the symptom" → strategic/Haley. These pairings are frequent objective items.

24. Brief psychodynamic and interpersonal therapies

Short-term dynamic psychotherapy

David Malan formalised brief dynamic therapy around two triangles: the triangle of conflict (impulse/feeling, anxiety, defence) and the triangle of person (other, therapist/transference, parent/past). Habib Davanloo developed Intensive Short-Term Dynamic Psychotherapy (ISTDP), actively challenging defences to reach warded-off feelings. Peter Sifneos (anxiety-provoking brief therapy) and James Mann (time-limited, 12 sessions) are related pioneers.

Interpersonal therapy (brief recap)

IPT (Klerman and Weissman) is a time-limited treatment for depression that targets one of four problem areas: grief, role disputes, role transitions, and interpersonal deficits. It attends to current relationships, not intrapsychic conflict or the past.

25. Couple, sex, relaxation, and expressive therapies

Couple therapy (John Gottman)

John Gottman, from longitudinal observation of couples, identified the Four Horsemen that predict relationship breakdown: criticism, contempt, defensiveness, and stonewalling (contempt being the strongest predictor of divorce). His Sound Relationship House model builds "love maps" and a positive-to-negative interaction ratio.

Sex therapy (Masters and Johnson)

William Masters and Virginia Johnson pioneered direct behavioural sex therapy. Their signature technique, sensate focus, removes performance demand through graded, non-genital then genital touching exercises. They also mapped the four-stage human sexual response cycle (excitement, plateau, orgasm, resolution).

Relaxation and biofeedback

  • Edmund Jacobson → Progressive Muscle Relaxation (PMR): systematic tensing then releasing of muscle groups.
  • Johannes Schultz → Autogenic Training: self-suggestion of warmth and heaviness in the limbs.
  • Biofeedback: instruments feed back physiological signals (EMG, GSR, EEG, heart rate) so the client learns voluntary control; used for tension headache, hypertension, and anxiety.

Expressive (creative arts) therapies

These use a creative medium as the primary channel: art therapy (Margaret Naumburg, the "mother of art therapy", and Edith Kramer), music therapy, dance/movement therapy (Marian Chace), and drama therapy. They suit clients for whom verbal expression is limited.

Who → what: Gottman → Four Horsemen; Masters & Johnson → sensate focus; Jacobson → PMR; Schultz → autogenic training; Naumburg → art therapy.

26. The counselling process and core skills

Egan's Skilled Helper model

Gerard Egan's three-stage problem-management model: Stage 1 – Current picture ("What is going on?"), Stage 2 – Preferred picture ("What do I want instead?"), Stage 3 – The way forward ("How do I get there?").

Ivey's microskills

Allen Ivey organised counselling behaviours into a microskills hierarchy, built on attending behaviour and rising through open/closed questions, reflection of feeling, paraphrasing, summarising, and the influencing skills. The base is the basic listening sequence.

Process phenomena

  • Transference: the client redirects feelings from past figures onto the therapist. Countertransference: the therapist's reactions to the client.
  • Resistance: forces within the client opposing change or the work.
  • Termination: the planned ending phase, consolidating gains and processing loss.
Egan = three stages (current picture, preferred picture, way forward). Ivey = microskills hierarchy on attending behaviour. Transference is the client's; countertransference is the therapist's.

Rogers' facilitative conditions in the counselling skill set

Beyond the three core conditions, counselling training operationalises Rogers into observable skills: SOLER (Egan's attending posture: Squarely face, Open posture, Lean forward, Eye contact, Relaxed), active listening, reflection of feeling, paraphrasing (reflecting content), immediacy (commenting on the here-and-now relationship), and confrontation (gently pointing to discrepancies). The overarching aim is to move the client from an external locus of evaluation toward an internal one.

28. Master summary: founders, techniques, and indications

RML tilt: The single most reliable way to bank marks in this section is the founder-to-technique pairing. If you can answer "who → what → for whom" for every row above, you have covered the bulk of RML's counselling and psychotherapy objective items.

27. Psychotherapy efficacy and outcome research

Eysenck's 1952 challenge

Hans Eysenck (1952) reviewed outcome data and claimed that neurotic patients improved at about the same rate with or without psychotherapy, citing spontaneous remission. This provocative critique forced the field to gather rigorous outcome evidence.

Smith and Glass meta-analysis

Mary Lee Smith and Gene Glass (1977) conducted the landmark meta-analysis (Glass coined the term) of hundreds of psychotherapy studies and found a large average effect size (about 0.85), meaning the treated client was better off than roughly 80% of untreated controls. This effectively answered Eysenck.

The common-factors versus specific-factors debate

The finding that different bona fide therapies produce broadly similar outcomes is called the Dodo bird verdict ("everyone has won, all must have prizes"), a phrase introduced by Saul Rosenzweig. This supports common factors (the alliance, empathy, expectancy, the healing setting) as major drivers, against specific factors (the unique techniques of each model). Both matter; the alliance is among the most robust predictors of outcome.

Trap: The Dodo bird verdict term is credited to Rosenzweig (1936), not to Smith and Glass. Eysenck = the 1952 critique; Smith and Glass = the 1977 meta-analysis that rebutted it.
RML tilt: A single item can chain all three: "Who claimed spontaneous remission equalled therapy outcome?" (Eysenck), "the first large meta-analysis of psychotherapy?" (Smith and Glass), "the Dodo bird verdict refers to?" (equivalence of therapies / common factors).

10 Psychological Assessment & Tests

A psychological test is a standardized sample of behaviour, scored against norms, used to make an inference about a person. For the RML (Dr. Ram Manohar Lohia Hospital) CET-166 paper the questions are almost always reflexive: name the test, name its author (full name), say what it measures, and list its subtests. Train yourself to fire in that exact order.

RML tilt: Test → author → what it measures → subtests. If you can rattle off all four for every test below, you have covered the bulk of the assessment marks.

1. Psychometric foundations

From scratch: Before a test can be trusted, two properties must be shown. Reliability = consistency (does it give the same answer twice?). Validity = truth (does it measure what it claims?). A test can be reliable but not valid (a bathroom scale that is always 5 kg heavy), but it cannot be valid without being reliable.

Reliability types:

  • Test-retest reliability, same test given twice, correlate the scores; measures stability over time.
  • Split-half reliability, split the items into two halves (e.g. odd vs even), correlate; the Spearman-Brown formula corrects for the halving.
  • Internal consistency, do the items hang together? Indexed by Cronbach's alpha (α), the average of all possible split-halves; α > 0.7 is acceptable. Kuder-Richardson (KR-20) is its dichotomous cousin.
  • Inter-rater reliability, agreement between two scorers (crucial for projectives); indexed by Cohen's kappa.

Validity types:

  • Content validity, do the items cover the whole domain? (judged by experts).
  • Criterion validity, does the test predict an external criterion? Two flavours: concurrent (criterion measured now) and predictive (criterion measured later, an entrance test predicting first-year marks).
  • Construct validity, does it measure the theoretical trait? Shown by convergent validity (correlates with tests of the same trait) and discriminant validity (does not correlate with unrelated traits).

Norms are the reference scores of a representative sample; standardization means fixing the administration, scoring and norms so every testee is treated identically. Standard scores re-express a raw score relative to the norm: the z-score (mean 0, standard deviation 1), the T-score (mean 50, standard deviation 10, used on the MMPI), and the deviation IQ (mean 100, standard deviation 15, used on modern intelligence tests).

2. Intelligence tests

Who → what: Alfred Binet & Théodore Simon built the first practical intelligence scale (1905, Paris) to identify schoolchildren needing help; Lewis Terman of Stanford revised it into the Stanford-Binet and imported the Intelligence Quotient (IQ) = (mental age / chronological age) × 100, using mental age.

David Wechsler rejected a single ratio IQ and built a family of tests giving a deviation IQ plus index scores:

  • WAIS (Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale), ages 16+.
  • WISC (Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children), roughly ages 6–16.
  • WPPSI (Wechsler Preschool and Primary Scale of Intelligence), preschool/early childhood.

Modern Wechsler tests yield four index scores: VCI (Verbal Comprehension Index), PRI (Perceptual Reasoning Index, called VSI, Visual Spatial Index, in the newest editions), WMI (Working Memory Index) and PSI (Processing Speed Index).

Raven's Progressive Matrices (John C. Raven) is a culture-fair, nonverbal test of abstract reasoning: the testee picks the missing piece of a visual pattern. It is taken as a purified measure of Spearman's general factor, g.

Indian intelligence tests are RML favourites:

  • Bhatia Battery (Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence, C. M. Bhatia, 1955), a performance battery of five subtests: Koh's Block Design, Alexander's Pass-Along, Pattern Drawing, Immediate Memory, and Picture Construction; designed for the largely non-literate Indian population.
  • MISIC (Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children, Arthur J. Malin), the Indian adaptation of the WISC, giving Verbal and Performance IQs.
  • Seguin Form Board (Édouard Séguin), a speeded placing of shaped blocks into matching recesses; a performance test of visuo-motor speed used in children and in intellectual-disability screening.
  • DST (Developmental Screening Test, J. Bharat Raj), screens developmental level / gives a developmental quotient.
Trap: The Stanford-Binet introduced the ratio IQ; the Wechsler scales use the deviation IQ (mean 100, SD 15). Do not swap them.

3. Personality tests, objective / self-report

These use fixed items and objective scoring, usually true/false or Likert:

  • MMPI (Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory, Starke Hathaway & J. C. McKinley), the dominant clinical inventory; 10 clinical scales plus validity scales L (Lie), F (Infrequency) and K (Correction/defensiveness) that detect faking; scored as T-scores.
  • 16PF (Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire, Raymond B. Cattell), measures 16 source traits derived by factor analysis.
  • NEO-PI-R (Revised NEO Personality Inventory, Paul Costa & Robert McCrae), measures the Big Five: Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, each with six facets.
  • EPQ (Eysenck Personality Questionnaire, Hans Eysenck), measures three dimensions: Extraversion, Neuroticism and Psychoticism, plus a Lie scale.
  • CPI (California Psychological Inventory, Harrison Gough), a "sane person's MMPI" measuring normal interpersonal traits (dominance, sociability, responsibility).

4. Projective tests

Who → what: The projective hypothesis was named by Lawrence K. Frank (1939): faced with an ambiguous stimulus, a person projects unconscious needs, conflicts and structure onto it, revealing personality that a self-report cannot reach.
  • Rorschach Inkblot Test (Hermann Rorschach), 10 symmetrical inkblots; the modern standardized scoring is John Exner's Comprehensive System, coding location, determinants, form quality and content.
  • TAT (Thematic Apperception Test, Henry Murray & Christiana Morgan), ambiguous interpersonal picture cards; the testee tells a story, which is analysed for needs and press (Murray's need-press theory).
  • Sentence Completion tests (e.g. the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank), the testee finishes stems like "My greatest fear…"; semi-structured, easy to quantify.
  • DAP (Draw-A-Person Test, Karen Machover), interpret the human figure drawing as a projection of the self-image and body image.
  • HTP (House-Tree-Person Test, John Buck), the testee draws a house, a tree and a person, tapping self-perception and family/environment attitudes.
  • CAT (Children's Apperception Test, Leopold Bellak), the child version of the TAT, using animal figures in the original form.

5. Neuropsychological batteries

These localize and quantify brain dysfunction:

  • Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery (Ward Halstead & Ralph Reitan), a fixed battery (Category Test, Tactual Performance, Trail Making, etc.) giving an Impairment Index.
  • Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery (based on Alexander Luria, standardized by Charles Golden), scales for motor, rhythm, tactile, visual, receptive/expressive speech, reading, writing, arithmetic, memory and intellectual processes.
  • Bender-Gestalt Test (Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt Test, Lauretta Bender), copying nine geometric designs; screens visuo-motor / constructional impairment and organicity.
  • NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery (National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences, Bangalore), an Indian-normed adult battery across attention, executive function, learning, memory and comprehension.
  • MMSE (Mini-Mental State Examination, Marshal Folstein), a brief 30-point bedside screen for cognitive impairment/dementia (orientation, registration, attention, recall, language).

6. Objective vs projective, the distinction

Key. Objective tests use structured items and fixed scoring keys, are high in reliability, and are vulnerable to faking (hence MMPI L/F/K scales). Projective tests use ambiguous stimuli, rest on the projective hypothesis, are hard to fake but weaker in reliability and validity, and lean heavily on inter-rater reliability.

Master table, test → author → what it measures → key detail

Trap: The Rorschach was created by Hermann Rorschach, but the standardized scoring is Exner's Comprehensive System, RML loves separating the inventor from the scorer. Likewise, CAT is Bellak's child version of Murray's TAT.
Recap: Reliability = consistency (test-retest, split-half, Cronbach's alpha, inter-rater); validity = truth (content, criterion = concurrent/predictive, construct = convergent/discriminant); standard scores z / T / deviation IQ (100/15). Intelligence: Binet-Simon → Terman's Stanford-Binet (ratio IQ, mental age); Wechsler's WAIS/WISC/WPPSI (deviation IQ, indices VCI/PRI/WMI/PSI); Raven (culture-fair g); Indian, Bhatia, MISIC, Seguin, DST. Objective personality: MMPI (L/F/K), 16PF, NEO-PI-R (Big Five), EPQ, CPI. Projective (Frank's projective hypothesis): Rorschach (Exner), TAT (Murray & Morgan), Sentence Completion, DAP (Machover), HTP (Buck), CAT (Bellak). Neuropsych: Halstead-Reitan, Luria-Nebraska, Bender-Gestalt, NIMHANS battery, MMSE (Folstein). Always answer: test → author → what it measures → subtests.

7. Test Construction & Standardization

A psychological test is not written in one sitting; it is engineered through a fixed sequence so that the final scores mean the same thing across people. Knowing these steps and the two item-analysis indices is high-yield.

7.1 Steps in building a test

  • Planning: define the construct (what trait or ability), the target population, and the purpose (screening, diagnosis, selection).
  • Item writing: generate an item pool larger than the final test. Rules: one idea per item, simple language, avoid double negatives, avoid double-barrelled items, balance keyed direction to reduce acquiescence.
  • Item analysis: try items on a sample, keep the good ones (see below).
  • Standardization: fix administration, scoring, and time so conditions are identical for all.
  • Norming: administer to a large representative sample to build reference tables.
  • Reliability & validity checks, then publish the manual.

7.2 Item analysis: the two indices

IndexWhat it tells youRule of thumb
Item difficulty index (p)Proportion who answered correctly. p = 0.00 (nobody) to 1.00 (everybody).High p = easy item. Best around 0.50 for maximum discrimination; keep a spread.
Item discrimination index (D)How well an item separates high scorers from low scorers (upper vs lower group).D ranges −1 to +1; keep items above ~0.30. Negative D = flawed item, discard.
Key. Difficulty = how many got it right; Discrimination = whether the right people got it right. A high p can still be a bad item if D is low or negative.

7.3 Item Response Theory (IRT), briefly

Classical Test Theory treats difficulty as a property of the sample. Item Response Theory instead models the probability of a correct answer as a function of the person's latent ability and item parameters (difficulty, discrimination, guessing), plotted as an item characteristic curve. IRT underlies computer-adaptive testing, where the next item is chosen from the last response.

7.4 Norming, standardization & cross-cultural adaptation

Norms convert a raw score into a relative one (percentile, standard score, deviation IQ) by comparison with the standardization sample. A test imported from the West cannot be used as-is; it needs cross-cultural adaptation. The standard method is translation and back-translation: one bilingual translates into the target language, a second independently translates back to the source, and discrepancies are reconciled so meaning (not just words) is preserved. Re-norming on the local population is essential.

Trap: Merely translating a test is not adaptation. Without local re-norming and cultural item review, an American IQ table applied to Indian children inflates or deflates scores.

8. Ethics in Psychological Testing

  • Informed consent: the testee (or guardian) must understand the purpose, use, and limits before testing.
  • Confidentiality: results are shared only with authorised persons; records are protected.
  • Test security: items, manuals, and scoring keys are restricted so the test is not compromised; access is limited to qualified users.
  • Competence: a tester uses only instruments they are trained and qualified to administer and interpret.
  • Cultural fairness / bias: avoid items that penalise a group for reasons unrelated to the construct; report limits of the norms used.
RML tilt: In India the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) regulates who may practise clinical psychology and use clinical tests; the American Psychological Association (APA) Standards for Educational and Psychological Testing are the classic reference for test quality and ethics.

9. Indian Tests & Adaptations

This is the most examined cluster. Learn each as author → what it measures. Many are non-verbal or performance based precisely to reduce language and cultural bias in Indian settings.

9.1 Intelligence tests standardised in India

TestAuthor / adapterWhat it measures
Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests of IntelligenceChandra Mohan Bhatia (C.M. Bhatia, 1955)Non-verbal intelligence via 5 subtests (Koh's Block Design, Alexander's Pass-Along, Pattern Drawing, Immediate Memory, Picture Construction); built for the largely non-literate Indian population.
Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children (MISIC)Arthur J. Malin (1969)Indian adaptation of the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC); verbal + performance subtests, ages ~6–15, yields verbal, performance and full-scale IQ.
Binet-Kamat Test of Intelligence (BKT)Vishwanath V. Kamat (V.V. Kamat, 1934)Indian adaptation of the Stanford-Binet; yields mental age and IQ; long the standard Indian Binet for children.
Seguin Form Board (SFB)Edouard Seguin (Indian norms available)Speed of fitting shaped blocks into slots; quick non-verbal estimate of intelligence and eye-hand coordination, widely used for children and intellectual disability screening.

9.2 Developmental, social-maturity & adaptive scales

TestAuthor / adapterWhat it measures
Developmental Screening Test (DST)Bharat Raj (1977)Screens developmental level from birth to ~15 years; yields a developmental quotient; quick screen for delay.
Vineland Social Maturity Scale (VSMS), Indian adaptationOriginal by Edgar Doll; Indian adaptation by A.J. MalinSocial competence / self-help across 8 areas by informant report; yields a social age and social quotient; core in intellectual disability assessment.

9.3 Indian neuropsychological batteries

BatterySourceWhat it measures
NIMHANS Neuropsychological BatteryNIMHANS, BengaluruIndian-normed battery for attention, executive function, memory, learning and comprehension across age and education bands; detects brain dysfunction.
PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction (PGI-BBD)Dwarka Prasad (with colleagues, PGIMER Chandigarh)Screens organic brain dysfunction; incorporates the PGI Memory Scale and Bhatia performance tests; gives a dysfunction rating.
PGI Memory Scale (PGI-MS)Dwarka Prasad & N.N. Wig, PGIMERIndian memory scale across 10 subtests (recall, recognition, attention, retention); a Wechsler-Memory-style local instrument.
AIIMS / other Indian adaptationsAIIMS and Indian centresLocalised versions and comprehension batteries adapted for Indian language and literacy conditions.
Who → what: Bhatia → performance IQ battery; Malin → MISIC (WISC) and Vineland Indian adaptation; Kamat → Binet-Kamat; Bharat Raj → DST; Dwarka Prasad → PGI-BBD and PGI Memory Scale.
Trap: MISIC is adapted from the WISC, not the Stanford-Binet; the Binet line in India is the Binet-Kamat. Do not swap them.

10. Clinical Rating Scales You Must Recognise

These are symptom-severity or diagnostic instruments used in clinical settings. Learn the disorder each targets and whether it is self-report or clinician-rated.

ScaleAuthorTargets / type
Beck Depression Inventory (BDI)Aaron T. BeckDepression severity; 21-item self-report.
Beck Anxiety Inventory (BAI)Aaron T. BeckAnxiety severity; 21-item self-report; designed to separate anxiety from depression.
Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression (HAM-D / HDRS)Max HamiltonDepression severity; clinician-rated interview.
Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (HAM-A)Max HamiltonAnxiety severity; clinician-rated.
Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (Y-BOCS)Goodman and colleagues (Yale)Severity of obsessions and compulsions in OCD; clinician-rated.
Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale (PANSS)Kay, Fiszbein & OplerPositive, negative and general symptoms of schizophrenia; clinician-rated.
Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS)Overall & GorhamBroad psychiatric symptom severity (psychosis, mood); clinician-rated.
Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE)Marshal Folstein, Susan Folstein & McHughCognitive screening for dementia / impairment; scored out of 30.
General Health Questionnaire (GHQ)David GoldbergScreening for general (non-psychotic) psychiatric morbidity in community settings; self-report.
Structured Clinical Interview for DSM (SCID)First, Spitzer and colleaguesStructured diagnostic interview mapping symptoms to DSM criteria; clinician-administered.
Key. Beck scales are self-report; the Hamilton scales, Y-BOCS, PANSS and BPRS are clinician-rated. MMSE = cognition/dementia screen, GHQ = general morbidity screen, SCID = structured diagnostic interview (not a severity rating scale).
Trap: HAM-D is rated by the clinician; BDI is filled by the patient. A common item asks which of a pair is self-report versus interviewer-rated.

11. Aptitude, Interest & Achievement Tests, and Adaptive-Behaviour Scales

  • Aptitude tests predict future capacity to learn a specific skill (for example the Differential Aptitude Tests, General Aptitude Test Battery); used in guidance and selection.
  • Interest inventories map preferences to occupations (for example the Strong Interest Inventory, Kuder Preference Record); vocational, not ability, measures.
  • Achievement tests measure what has already been learned (present attainment), unlike aptitude which forecasts future learning.
  • Adaptive-behaviour scales assess everyday functioning (self-care, communication, social skills): the Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales and the AAIDD Adaptive Behavior Scales, central to diagnosing intellectual disability alongside an IQ test.
From scratch: aptitude looks forward (can they learn it?), achievement looks back (have they learned it?), interest asks what they like, and adaptive behaviour asks how well they cope with daily life. Intellectual disability needs deficits in both IQ and adaptive behaviour, which is why these scales pair with intelligence tests.
Recap: Tests are engineered (item difficulty p, discrimination D, IRT, norming, translation-back-translation) and used ethically under RCI and APA standards. India's high-yield instruments are Bhatia's battery, MISIC (Malin, from WISC), Binet-Kamat (Kamat), Seguin Form Board, DST (Bharat Raj), the Vineland Indian adaptation (Malin), and the NIMHANS and PGI batteries (PGI-BBD, PGI Memory Scale, Dwarka Prasad). Recognise the Beck, Hamilton, Y-BOCS, PANSS, BPRS, MMSE, GHQ and SCID by disorder and by self-report versus clinician-rated.

A. Reliability in full: every method, one at a time

Reliability means consistency. If a test is reliable, it gives you nearly the same score when the same person is measured again under the same conditions. Think of a bathroom scale: if it reads 60 kg, then 64 kg, then 58 kg for the same person stepping on within one minute, it is unreliable. In psychometrics reliability is expressed as a coefficient between 0 and 1, and for most clinical tools we want it at 0.80 or higher.

From scratch: Every observed score is made of two parts: the true score (the person's real standing on the trait) plus error (random noise: fatigue, guessing, a noisy room, a misread item). Reliability is literally the proportion of the observed-score variance that is true-score variance. A reliability of 0.90 means 90 percent of the differences between people's scores reflect real differences, and 10 percent is noise.

Test-retest reliability (temporal stability)

Give the same test to the same people twice, separated by a gap (often two to four weeks), then correlate the two sets of scores. A high correlation means the test is stable over time. The main threat is the carryover or practice effect: people remember items or get better with practice, which falsely inflates the correlation. If the gap is too long, real change in the trait can lower it. Test-retest suits stable traits (intelligence, personality) and is inappropriate for states like mood that genuinely fluctuate.

Parallel-forms (alternate-forms / equivalent-forms) reliability

Build two versions of the test that measure the same thing with different items (Form A and Form B), give both, and correlate. This sidesteps memory of specific items. It is the most demanding standard because it captures error from both time and content sampling. The difficulty is practical: constructing two truly equivalent forms is expensive and hard.

Split-half reliability and the Spearman-Brown correction

Administer the test once, then split it into two halves (usually odd items versus even items, never first-half versus second-half because of fatigue and difficulty drift), and correlate the two half-scores. Problem: you have just correlated two half-length tests, and shorter tests are less reliable. The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula corrects for this by estimating what the reliability would be for the full-length test. The same formula predicts how reliability rises if you add more items.

Internal consistency: Cronbach's alpha and KR-20

Internal consistency asks whether all the items hang together and measure the same construct. Cronbach's alpha (Lee Cronbach, 1951) is the average of all possible split-half correlations; it works for items scored on a scale (for example 1 to 5 Likert). Kuder-Richardson 20 (KR-20) (Kuder and Richardson, 1937) is the special case for dichotomous items (right/wrong, yes/no); KR-21 is a quicker approximation that assumes equal item difficulty. Alpha rises with more items and with more homogeneous items.

Trap: A very high alpha (say above 0.95) is not automatically good. It can signal redundancy, several items asking essentially the same thing in slightly different words. High alpha also does not prove the scale is one-dimensional; you need factor analysis for that.

Inter-rater reliability and Cohen's kappa

When scoring depends on a judge (rating a Rorschach response, coding an interview, grading an essay), we need agreement between raters. A simple percent-agreement overstates it because raters agree by chance sometimes. Cohen's kappa (Jacob Cohen, 1960) corrects for chance agreement for two raters on categorical judgements. Values around 0.61 to 0.80 are substantial and above 0.80 are almost perfect. For continuous ratings the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) is used instead.

Standard error of measurement (SEM)

Reliability at the level of the individual score is expressed by the SEM. It tells you how much an individual's observed score is likely to bounce around the true score due to error. SEM is smallest when reliability is high. Practically, it lets us build a confidence band around a score: an IQ of 100 with an SEM of 3 means we are about 68 percent confident the true IQ lies between 97 and 103. This is why good reports give ranges, not single numbers.

Rule: As reliability goes up, SEM goes down. When reliability equals 1 (impossible in practice), SEM equals 0.

B. Validity in full: does it measure what it claims?

Validity is whether the test actually measures the construct it says it measures, and whether the inferences drawn from scores are sound. A test can be highly reliable yet invalid (a scale that always reads 5 kg heavy is perfectly consistent but wrong). Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity.

Face validity

Whether the test simply looks like it measures the trait, to the test-taker or a layperson. It is not real evidence of validity, but it matters for cooperation and credibility. Sometimes low face validity is deliberate, so respondents cannot fake answers.

Content validity

Whether the items adequately sample the whole domain being measured. A statistics exam that only tests one chapter has poor content validity. It is judged by subject experts, not by a number. The related danger of leaving out part of the domain is construct underrepresentation; including irrelevant content is construct-irrelevant variance.

Criterion-related validity: concurrent and predictive

Whether test scores relate to an external criterion (an outcome we care about). Two timings: concurrent validity correlates the test with a criterion measured at the same time (a new depression scale against clinician diagnosis today). Predictive validity correlates the test with a criterion measured in the future (an entrance exam predicting first-year marks). The correlation itself is the validity coefficient.

Construct validity: the master concept

Construct validity is the degree to which the test measures the theoretical construct (anxiety, extraversion, intelligence) it is meant to measure. Modern testing treats it as the overarching form that content and criterion validity feed into. It is gathered through two complementary kinds of evidence:

  • Convergent validity: the test correlates highly with other measures of the same construct (a new anxiety scale correlates with an established one).
  • Discriminant (divergent) validity: the test correlates weakly with measures of unrelated constructs (an anxiety scale should not correlate strongly with a reading-speed test).

The multitrait-multimethod matrix (Campbell and Fiske, 1959)

Campbell and Fiske gave psychology a formal way to demonstrate convergent and discriminant validity together. You measure several traits using several methods, then inspect the correlation matrix. Same trait measured by different methods should correlate strongly (convergent). Different traits measured by the same method should correlate weakly. If two different traits correlate highly only because they share a method, that shared inflation is called method variance, a threat the MTMM matrix is built to expose.

Who → what: Campbell & Fiske (1959) → the multitrait-multimethod (MTMM) matrix for convergent and discriminant validity; Lee Cronbach & Paul Meehl (1955) → the concept of construct validity and the nomological network.

Factors that affect validity

  • Restriction of range: if a sample is homogeneous (only top scorers were admitted), correlations shrink and validity looks lower than it is.
  • Criterion contamination: the person judging the criterion knows the test score, biasing the outcome.
  • Reliability ceiling: a test cannot correlate with a criterion more than the square root of the product of their reliabilities; unreliable tests cannot be highly valid.
  • Unrepresentative sample, faking, and inappropriate norms all lower validity in practice.

C. Classical Test Theory versus Item Response Theory

Classical Test Theory (CTT) is the framework behind most of the above: observed score equals true score plus error, and statistics like item difficulty are properties of the whole test in a particular sample. Its weakness is that item statistics are sample-dependent: the same item looks "easy" in a bright group and "hard" in a weak group.

Item difficulty and discrimination (recap in depth)

The difficulty index (p) is the proportion who answered an item correctly; despite the name, a high p means an easy item. Values near 0.5 give the most information and best spread. The discrimination index (D) asks whether high scorers pass the item more often than low scorers; it ranges from -1 to +1, and a negative D is a red flag that the item is keyed wrong or misleading. The point-biserial correlation is the more precise version of discrimination.

Distractor analysis

In multiple-choice items, we examine each wrong option (distractor). A good distractor is chosen by some low scorers and almost no high scorers. A distractor chosen by nobody is dead weight; a distractor chosen more by high scorers signals a flawed or ambiguous item.

Item Response Theory and the Rasch model

Item Response Theory (IRT) models the probability of a correct response as a function of the person's ability and the item's properties, plotted as an item characteristic curve. Its great advantage over CTT is invariance: item parameters do not depend on the particular sample, and ability estimates do not depend on the particular items. IRT parameters can include difficulty (b), discrimination (a), and guessing (c), giving one-, two-, and three-parameter models. The Rasch model (Georg Rasch, 1960) is the one-parameter model: it uses difficulty only and assumes all items discriminate equally. IRT underlies computer-adaptive testing, where the next item shown depends on how you answered the last one.

D. Norms and standard scores, worked out

A raw score is meaningless until compared with a reference group, the norm group. Standardization means administering the test to a large, representative sample and building tables that convert raw scores into interpretable units.

Score typeMeanSDMeaning
z-score01Number of SDs above/below mean
T-score5010Used on MMPI and many clinical scales
IQ (deviation)10015Wechsler scales (Stanford-Binet historically used SD 16)
Stanine5~2Single-digit scale 1–9
Sten5.52Scale 1–10, used on 16PF
Percentile rank––Percent of group scoring at or below
Trap: A percentile is not a percentage correct. The 84th percentile means you did better than 84 percent of the norm group, roughly one SD above the mean. Percentiles are bunched in the middle and stretched at the extremes, so equal raw-score gaps do not give equal percentile gaps.

Age norms and grade norms express a score as the age or grade level at which it is typical. The old ratio IQ (mental age divided by chronological age, times 100, from Stern and Terman) has been replaced by the deviation IQ, which compares you to your own age group, because mental age stops rising in adulthood while chronological age keeps climbing.

E. Intelligence tests, expanded roster

Who → what: Lewis Terman (1916) → Stanford-Binet, the American revision of Binet-Simon that introduced the term IQ into wide use; David Wechsler (from 1939) → the Wechsler family with separate verbal and performance sides.

Stanford-Binet (Terman)

Terman's 1916 Stanford revision of the Binet-Simon scale made intelligence testing standard in the United States. It is now in its fifth edition (SB5, 2003) and yields a Full-Scale IQ plus five factor scores (Fluid Reasoning, Knowledge, Quantitative Reasoning, Visual-Spatial, Working Memory), each split into verbal and nonverbal domains. Historically it used an SD of 16, which is why older Stanford-Binet IQs are not exactly interchangeable with Wechsler IQs.

The Wechsler scales: WAIS-IV, WISC-V, WPPSI

David Wechsler built adult, child, and preschool versions. WAIS-IV (adults 16 to 90) gives a Full-Scale IQ and four index scores: Verbal Comprehension, Perceptual Reasoning, Working Memory, and Processing Speed. WISC-V (children 6 to 16) uses five indices (Verbal Comprehension, Visual Spatial, Fluid Reasoning, Working Memory, Processing Speed). WPPSI covers preschool and early primary (roughly 2.5 to 7 years). Core subtests include Similarities, Vocabulary, Block Design, Matrix Reasoning, Digit Span, and Coding. Wechsler pioneered the deviation IQ (mean 100, SD 15).

Raven's Progressive Matrices

John C. Raven (1938) built a nonverbal, culture-reduced test of abstract reasoning: the person picks the missing piece that completes a visual pattern. Because it needs no reading and little language, it is widely used cross-culturally and is a strong measure of Spearman's g and fluid intelligence. Versions include Standard, Coloured (for children and older adults), and Advanced (for high-ability adults).

Kaufman (K-ABC) and Das-Naglieri CAS

The Kaufman Assessment Battery for Children (K-ABC) (Alan and Nadeen Kaufman, 1983) separates sequential from simultaneous processing and tries to minimise cultural bias. The Das-Naglieri Cognitive Assessment System (CAS) (J.P. Das and Jack Naglieri, 1997) is built on the PASS theory (Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive processing), which grows out of Luria's model of brain function; J.P. Das worked extensively in India, so the PASS/CAS line is examiner-favourite for RML.

Indian intelligence and developmental tests

  • Bhatia Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence (C.M. Bhatia, 1955): five performance subtests (Koh's Block Design, Alexander's Pass-Along, Pattern Drawing, Immediate Memory, Picture Construction), designed for use with illiterate and rural Indian populations because it needs no reading.
  • Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children (MISIC) (Arthur J. Malin, 1969): the Indian adaptation of WISC for ages 6 to 15, with verbal and performance sections.
  • Binet-Kamat Test (BKT) (V.V. Kamat, 1934): the Indian adaptation of Stanford-Binet, standardised on Kannada- and Marathi-speaking children, giving mental age and IQ.
  • Seguin Form Board Test (Edouard Seguin): a speed-of-performance test where the child fits shapes into recesses; widely used in India as a quick nonverbal screen and to gauge eye-hand coordination and visual perception.
  • Developmental Screening Test (DST) (Bharat Raj, 1977): an Indian tool that estimates a developmental age and developmental quotient from birth to about 15 years, based on an informant interview.
RML tilt: Indian-adapted tools are high-yield. Remember the author-tool pairs cold: Bhatia (performance battery), Malin (MISIC), Kamat (Binet-Kamat), Bharat Raj (DST and also VSMS Indian adaptation), Seguin (form board). Examiners love asking "which test is used for illiterate rural adults" (Bhatia) and "Indian adaptation of WISC" (MISIC).

F. Objective personality inventories, expanded

MMPI-2 and its validity indices

The Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (Hathaway and McKinley, original 1943; MMPI-2 in 1989) is the most widely used objective personality and psychopathology inventory: 567 true/false items on the MMPI-2. It was built by empirical criterion keying (items were kept if they distinguished clinical groups from normals, regardless of face meaning). It has ten clinical scales (Hypochondriasis, Depression, Hysteria, Psychopathic Deviate, Masculinity-Femininity, Paranoia, Psychasthenia, Schizophrenia, Hypomania, Social Introversion) and, crucially, validity scales:

  • L (Lie): detects naive attempts to look unrealistically virtuous.
  • F (Infrequency): detects exaggeration, faking bad, or random responding.
  • K (Correction/defensiveness): detects a subtler guardedness; used to statistically correct some clinical scales.
Trap: The MMPI is scored in T-scores (mean 50, SD 10), and a T of 65 or above is the usual clinical cutoff on MMPI-2. Do not confuse the validity scales L, F, K with the clinical scales; the validity scales say whether to trust the profile at all.

16PF (Cattell), EPQ (Eysenck), NEO-PI-R, CPI

  • 16PF (Raymond Cattell, 1949): measures sixteen primary source traits derived by factor analysis; scored in stens (1 to 10).
  • EPQ, Eysenck Personality Questionnaire (Hans and Sybil Eysenck, 1975): three dimensions, Extraversion, Neuroticism, Psychoticism, plus a Lie scale. (The earlier EPI measured only E and N.)
  • NEO-PI-R (Costa and McCrae, 1992): measures the Big Five, Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, Neuroticism (OCEAN), each with six facets.
  • California Psychological Inventory (CPI) (Harrison Gough, 1957): measures normal-range interpersonal traits (dominance, sociability, responsibility) in nonclinical populations, sometimes called "the sane person's MMPI."

G. Projective tests, expanded

From scratch: Projective tests present ambiguous stimuli on the assumption that people "project" their inner needs, conflicts, and personality onto the vague material. They are rooted in psychodynamic theory. Their weakness is lower reliability and validity and heavy dependence on the scorer, which is why standardized scoring systems were developed.
TestAuthor & yearStimulus / task
Rorschach Inkblot TestHermann Rorschach, 1921Ten symmetrical inkblots; "what might this be?"
Thematic Apperception Test (TAT)Henry Murray & Christiana Morgan, 1935Ambiguous scenes; tell a story with past, present, future
Children's Apperception Test (CAT)Leopold Bellak, 1949Animal (later human) scenes for children 3–10
Rotter Incomplete Sentences BlankJulian Rotter, 1950Finish sentence stems ("I wish...")
Draw-A-Person (DAP)Karen Machover, 1949Draw a person; body-image interpretation
House-Tree-Person (HTP)John Buck, 1948Draw a house, a tree, and a person
Kinetic Family Drawing (KFD)Burns & Kaufman, 1970Draw your family "doing something"
Word Association TestCarl Jung (developed the method)Respond to stimulus words; reaction time reveals complexes

The Rorschach and the Exner Comprehensive System

The ten inkblots are shown one at a time and responses are coded for location (whole blot, common detail, unusual detail), determinants (form, colour, movement, shading), content, and popularity. Early scoring was chaotic across schools (Beck, Klopfer, Rapaport). John Exner integrated the best of these into the Comprehensive System (1974), giving standardized administration, coding, and norms; this is what made the Rorschach defensible as a psychometric instrument.

TAT (Murray and Morgan)

Henry Murray and Christiana Morgan created the TAT at the Harvard Psychological Clinic. The person tells a story for each card; the assumption is that the hero of the story represents the respondent, and the analysis reads off the hero's needs and the environmental press acting on him, straight out of Murray's own personology theory (need-press).

H. Neuropsychological assessment, expanded

Battery / testAuthor & yearWhat it assesses
Halstead-Reitan BatteryHalstead & Reitan (1940s–50s)Broad brain damage: category, tactual performance, rhythm, etc.
Luria-Nebraska BatteryGolden, from Luria's work, 1980Standardized version of Luria's functional-system exam
NIMHANS Neuropsychological BatteryRao, Subbakrishna & Gopukumar, 2004Indian-normed battery: attention, executive fn, memory, language
Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt TestLauretta Bender, 1938Visuomotor integration; screens organicity in children/adults
PGI Memory ScalePershad & Wig, 1977Indian memory battery (10 subtests); PGI Chandigarh
AIIMS Comprehensive Neuropsych BatteryMukundan / AIIMSIndian adult neuropsychological screening
RML tilt: Pair the Indian batteries with their home institutions: NIMHANS battery (Rao et al.), PGI Memory Scale (Pershad and Wig, PGI Chandigarh), AIIMS battery. Bender-Gestalt is the classic "organicity screen" answer.

I. Clinical rating scales you must recognise

ScaleAuthor & yearMeasures
Beck Depression Inventory (BDI / BDI-II)Aaron Beck, 1961 / 1996Self-rated depression severity (21 items)
Beck Anxiety Inventory (BAI)Aaron Beck, 1988Self-rated anxiety, minimizes overlap with depression
Hamilton Depression Rating Scale (HAM-D / HDRS)Max Hamilton, 1960Clinician-rated depression severity
Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (HAM-A)Max Hamilton, 1959Clinician-rated anxiety severity
Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (Y-BOCS)Goodman et al., 1989OCD symptom severity (obsessions + compulsions)
Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale (PANSS)Kay, Fiszbein & Opler, 1987Schizophrenia symptoms (positive, negative, general)
Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS)Overall & Gorham, 1962General psychiatric symptom severity
Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE)Folstein & Folstein, 1975Cognitive screen for dementia (out of 30)
Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA)Nasreddine, 2005Cognitive screen, more sensitive to mild impairment

Vineland Social Maturity Scale (VSMS)Edgar Doll, 1935; Indian adaptation Malin/Bharat RajSocial competence via social age & social quotient
Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales (VABS)Sparrow, Balla & Cicchetti, 1984Adaptive behaviour (communication, daily living, socialization, motor)
From scratch: A self-report scale is filled in by the patient (BDI, BAI, GHQ); a clinician-rated scale is scored by the interviewer (HAM-D, HAM-A, PANSS, Y-BOCS). This distinction is a favourite one-mark question. The VSMS gives a Social Age and a Social Quotient (social age divided by chronological age times 100), and its Indian adaptation is essential for assessing intellectual disability alongside an IQ test.

J. Aptitude, interest and achievement tests

From scratch: An aptitude test predicts future capacity to learn a skill; an achievement test measures what has already been learned; an interest inventory measures preferences to guide career choice. Aptitude looks forward, achievement looks back.
TestAuthor & yearType / measures
Differential Aptitude Tests (DAT)Bennett, Seashore & Wesman, 1947Aptitude battery (verbal, numerical, abstract, spatial, mechanical, etc.)
General Aptitude Test Battery (GATB)US Employment Service, 1947Aptitudes for occupational placement
