  • Tamas (tamas = inertia, darkness, dullness): heavy, lethargic, confused, sleepy, procrastinating, ignorant. Tamas is needed for rest and sleep, but in excess it produces stagnation, delusion and depression-like states.

Each guna colours cognition, emotion, food preference, work style and even the kind of faith a person holds. The Bhagavad Gita (chapters 14, 17 and 18) systematically classifies knowledge, action, doer, intellect, resolve, happiness and even charity into sattvic, rajasic and tamasic types. This is one of the world's earliest fully worked-out personality typologies, and RML has asked "which guna corresponds to X" style items.

GunaMental stateTypical behaviourModern rough analogue
SattvaClarity, peaceBalanced, altruistic, self-controlledWell-being, high conscientiousness + low neuroticism
RajasCraving, agitationAmbitious, competitive, easily angeredHigh drive + high neuroticism / Type-A
TamasDullness, delusionInert, avoidant, exploitativeLow activation, apathy, some dark-triad features

The Vedic Personality Inventory (VPI)

Contemporary Indian and Western psychologists have tried to measure the gunas so the model can be tested empirically. The best-known instrument is the Vedic Personality Inventory (VPI) developed by David Wolf (1998, 1999). It is a self-report scale with items loading onto three subscales, one for each guna, and it has shown that sattva correlates positively with mental health and life satisfaction, while tamas correlates with poorer adjustment and higher symptoms. Other guna scales exist (for example the Gita Inventory of Personality and various Indian-authored triguna scales), but the VPI is the one most likely to be named in an exam.

RML tilt: Learn the pairing "Wolf → Vedic Personality Inventory → measures the three gunas." Also remember the direction: sattva up = good adjustment; tamas up = poor adjustment. Gunas are dimensions of prakriti, not of purusha, which stays untouched by them.

12. Purusharthas, Ashramas, and Karma as a Psychology of Motivation

Western motivation theory asks "what energises and directs behaviour?" Indian thought answered this long ago with a structured account of legitimate human goals, a developmental sequence for pursuing them, and a moral law that keeps the whole system going across lifetimes. Together these form an indigenous theory of motivation and lifespan development.

The four purusharthas (aims of life)

Purushartha means "that which is sought by a person," the sanctioned goals of human life. There are four, and they are meant to be pursued in balance, not one at the expense of the others.

  • Dharma (duty, righteousness, ethical order): living in accordance with one's role and moral law. It is placed first because it regulates the pursuit of the other three.
  • Artha (wealth, security, resources, worldly success): the material and economic base of life.
  • Kama (pleasure, desire, aesthetic and sensual enjoyment, love): legitimate enjoyment, not condemned but to be kept within dharma.
  • Moksha (liberation, release from the cycle of birth and death): the ultimate goal, freedom of the self.
Example: A householder earning a living (artha) and raising a family (kama) does so within ethical limits (dharma), while never losing sight of eventual liberation (moksha). Compare this with Maslow's hierarchy: artha and kama map loosely onto lower needs, dharma onto esteem and self-actualisation, and moksha onto self-transcendence, which Maslow added late in life.

The four ashramas (stages of life)

Ashrama means a stage or station of life. The classical scheme divides the ideal lifespan into four stages, giving each its own developmental task, a striking parallel to Erikson's psychosocial stages.

  • Brahmacharya (student stage): celibate discipline, learning, skill acquisition under a teacher.
  • Grihastha (householder stage): marriage, work, raising a family, pursuing artha and kama within dharma.
  • Vanaprastha (forest-dweller / retirement stage): gradual withdrawal from worldly duties, handing over responsibility, turning inward.
  • Sannyasa (renunciate stage): full renunciation, single-minded pursuit of moksha.
Rule: Purusharthas = what to pursue (the goals). Ashramas = when to pursue it (the stages). The householder stage is the one where artha and kama are legitimately maximised; moksha dominates the later stages.

Karma and rebirth as motivation

Karma literally means "action," but as a doctrine it is the moral law that every intentional action leaves an imprint (samskara) that shapes future experience, in this life or a later one, through the cycle of rebirth (samsara). Psychologically this functions as a powerful internal locus of control and a delay-of-gratification framework: because consequences are guaranteed even if not immediate, the doctrine motivates ethical restraint and long-horizon effort. It reframes suffering as intelligible rather than random, which can aid coping, though critics note it can also breed fatalism if misread. The Gita's answer to this risk is nishkama karma, action performed as duty without attachment to its fruits, which keeps the person active while dissolving anxiety about outcomes.

Trap: Karma is not "fate." Fate implies you are passive; karma insists the future is being authored by present action. It is closer to a lawful cause-effect view of moral behaviour than to predestination.

Classical texts further sort karma into three kinds, which is worth knowing because it turns the doctrine into a clean theory of how past learning constrains present choice: sanchita karma (the whole accumulated store of past actions not yet ripened), prarabdha karma (the portion already "activated" and playing out as the circumstances of this life), and agami / kriyamana karma (fresh action being generated now, which feeds the store). Psychologically this is a layered model of learning history: prarabdha is the given you cannot change (like temperament and early conditioning), while agami is the space of present freedom. The therapeutic reading is that a person is never wholly determined; there is always a live margin of new, wilful action through which the pattern can be redirected.

13. The Self in Indian Psychology: Atman, Ahamkara, and the Inner Instrument

Where does the sense of "I" come from, and is it the real self? Indian psychology draws a sharp line between the true self and the mistaken self, and it maps the inner apparatus that produces ordinary experience. This is arguably the most philosophically distinctive Indian contribution and a favourite exam area.

Atman versus ahamkara (self versus ego)

Atman is the true Self: pure consciousness, unchanging, the witness behind all experience. Ahamkara (aham = "I" + kara = "maker," literally "the I-maker") is the ego, the manufactured sense of a separate self that says "I am the doer, this is mine." In Vedanta, suffering arises precisely from confusing the two: mistaking the changing ego and body for the changeless Atman. Liberation is the correct discrimination between them (viveka).

The antahkarana (the inner instrument)

Antahkarana means "inner instrument," the fourfold inner apparatus through which consciousness knows and acts. Learn the four parts and their division of labour:

ComponentMeaningFunction
ManasMind (lower)Sensory-processing mind: receives impressions, doubts, wavers, coordinates the senses. The "monkey mind."
BuddhiIntellectDiscriminating faculty: decides, judges, discerns true from false. The highest, most sattvic instrument.
ChittaMemory / mind-stuffStorehouse of impressions (samskaras), the substrate on which experiences are recorded.
AhamkaraEgoThe I-sense that appropriates experience as "mine."
From scratch: A simple worked example. You hear a sound at night. Manas registers the raw sensation and wavers ("what was that?"). Buddhi discriminates ("it is the wind, not a thief"). Chitta stores the memory of the fright. Ahamkara claims it: "I was afraid." The pure Atman merely witnesses all four operating.

The panchakosha: five sheaths of the self

The Taittiriya Upanishad gives a layered model of the person as five concentric koshas (sheaths or coverings) wrapped around the Atman, from grossest to subtlest. This is an early holistic, multi-level model of the human being and maps neatly onto a biopsychosocial-spiritual view.

  • Annamaya kosha (anna = food): the physical body, sustained by food. The gross, outermost sheath.
  • Pranamaya kosha (prana = vital energy / breath): the energy or life-force body governing physiological vitality.
  • Manomaya kosha (manas = mind): the mental-emotional sheath of thoughts, feelings and sensory processing.
  • Vijnanamaya kosha (vijnana = knowing): the sheath of intellect, discrimination and wisdom (buddhi).
  • Anandamaya kosha (ananda = bliss): the innermost blissful sheath, closest to the Atman.
RML tilt: Order the koshas grossest to subtlest: Anna → Prana → Mano → Vijnana → Ananda ("A Person May Value Ananda"). Modern integrative and yoga-based therapies (for example the panchakosha model used at some Indian centres) assess a client across all five layers rather than only the psychological one.

14. Sattvavajaya and Prajnaparadha: Ayurveda's Cognitive Therapy

Ayurveda is usually remembered only for the three body humours (vata, pitta, kapha). But it also contains a genuine mental-health framework that reads like an ancient forerunner of cognitive therapy, and this is under-taught, so it is worth knowing well.

Manas and the mental gunas

Ayurveda treats manas (mind) as having its own qualities. Alongside the physical doshas it names rajas and tamas as the two mental doshas (manasika doshas), the disturbers of mental health, with sattva as the balanced ideal. Mental disorder is imbalance of these mental gunas, not only of the bodily humours.

Prajnaparadha (crimes against wisdom)

Prajnaparadha (prajna = wisdom + aparadha = offence, literally "the failure or crime of wisdom") is Ayurveda's concept for acting against one's better judgement: knowing what is good yet doing the opposite, because intellect, restraint and memory have faltered. It is held to be a root cause of both physical and mental illness. Psychologically it is remarkably close to the idea of maladaptive choices driven by faulty cognition, an ancient statement that distorted thinking makes us ill.

Sattvavajaya (mind control therapy)

Sattvavajaya (sattva = mind/purity + avajaya = conquest, literally "conquest of the mind") is Ayurveda's psychotherapy: the restraint of the mind from unwholesome objects and the cultivation of sattva. Charaka describes it as controlling the mind, redirecting it away from harmful desires, and strengthening knowledge, self-control and memory. Because it works by correcting the client's thinking and self-regulation, many contemporary Indian authors explicitly describe sattvavajaya as an indigenous cognitive-behavioural therapy.

Who → what: Charaka (Charaka Samhita) → classical descriptions of sattvavajaya, prajnaparadha and the mental doshas rajas/tamas. Learn these three terms together as the "Ayurvedic mind" cluster.

The three lines of treatment

Ayurveda's mental-health management is classically organised into three approaches, and sattvavajaya is the psychological one of the three: daivavyapashraya (spiritual or faith-based measures such as mantra, ritual and religious observance), yuktivyapashraya (rational, physical measures such as diet, herbs and cleansing therapies aimed at the doshas), and sattvavajaya (non-pharmacological control of the mind). The presence of a distinct psychological line of treatment, separate from the bodily and the spiritual, shows that classical Indian medicine already recognised mind-directed therapy as a category in its own right. In addition, Ayurveda offers a temperament typology: it describes seven sattvic, six rajasic and three tamasic mental constitution types (the manasa prakriti), an early classification of personality by dominant mental guna.

15. Indigenous and Asian Therapies: Yoga, Meditation, Morita and Naikan

RML likes to test whether you can place Indian practices alongside other Asian indigenous therapies and against modern Western therapy. Treat this as a comparison table in your head.

Yoga and meditation as therapy

Beyond philosophy, yoga (postures, breath regulation or pranayama, and meditation) and meditative practices are used therapeutically in India for anxiety, depression, stress, hypertension and psychosomatic conditions. Their mechanism is understood as increasing sattva and reducing chitta-vritti (mental fluctuations), producing calmer autonomic arousal. This is the lineage that fed into modern mindfulness-based interventions: Buddhist sati (mindful awareness) was secularised by Jon Kabat-Zinn into MBSR (Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction) and later into MBCT (Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy) for relapse prevention in depression.

Morita and Naikan (Japanese therapies)

Two Japanese therapies rooted in Zen Buddhist thought are the standard comparison points, and they are frequently confused, so pin them down:

  • Morita therapy (Shoma Morita, 1919): originally for shinkeishitsu (anxiety-based, obsessive, perfectionistic neurosis). Core idea: stop fighting your symptoms; accept feelings as they are and act on purpose anyway ("arugamama," acceptance of things as they are). Classic version uses phases of bed rest followed by graded light then heavy work. The lesson is character-building through purposeful action, not symptom elimination.
  • Naikan therapy (Ishin Yoshimoto, mid-20th century, from Jodo Shinshu Buddhism): structured, guided self-reflection. The client meditates on three questions about significant people (usually starting with the mother): What did this person do for me? What did I do for them? What troubles and difficulties did I cause them? The aim is gratitude, realistic self-appraisal and repair of relationships.
TherapyOriginCore mechanismGoal
Yoga / meditationIndia (Hindu / Buddhist)Reduce mental fluctuation, raise sattva, regulate arousalCalm, insight, self-mastery
MoritaJapan (Zen)Accept feelings, commit to purposeful actionFunction despite symptoms; character
NaikanJapan (Jodo Shinshu)Structured reflection on three questionsGratitude, self-honesty, mended relationships
Trap: Do not swap Morita and Naikan. Morita = action and acceptance of symptoms; Naikan = reflection and gratitude. Memory hook: Naikan starts with "Nai/in" = looking inward at what others did for you.

16. The Growth and Indigenization of Psychology in India

The contemporary section already names the leading psychologists. This part goes into the debate that shapes the discipline and adds a few concepts likely to be tested as short items.

The indigenization debate

Psychology in India began as an imported discipline (the first laboratory was set up at Calcutta University in 1915 under N. N. Sengupta, and much early work simply replicated Western studies). By the 1970s, Indian psychologists argued this borrowed psychology did not fit Indian realities. This launched the indigenization movement, usually described in three phases:

  • Indigenization from without: importing Western concepts and testing them on Indian samples (early, imitative phase).
  • Indigenization from within: building concepts, theories and methods out of Indian culture, texts and lived experience (the guna model, purusharthas, and so on).
  • An integrated / cross-cultural psychology: combining both so that Indian psychology contributes to a genuinely universal discipline rather than a merely local one.
Who → what:
Durganand Sinha → pioneer of the critique of imported psychology; classic work on deprivation and the culture-rooted growth of Indian psychology.
J. B. P. Sinha → the Nurturant-Task (NT) leadership model: an effective Indian leader is both caring/nurturant toward subordinates and firmly task-oriented, contingent on the subordinate's maturity.
Girishwar Misra → edited major surveys of Indian psychology; champion of culture-sensitive, indigenous methods.
Anand C. Paranjpe → bridged Indian thought and Western self/identity theory (self and identity across the two traditions).
K. Ramakrishna Rao and Kiran Kumar Salagame → systematised "Indian psychology" as consciousness studies and well-being.

Institutions and the present

The discipline's growth in India has been carried by key institutions: the Indian Council of Social Science Research (ICSSR) and surveys of psychological research, NCERT and UGC shaping curricula (including moves to include Indian psychology in syllabi), and clinical and neuroscientific work led by NIMHANS (Bengaluru) and the ICMR. Applied strands now include health psychology, organisational behaviour rooted in Indian values, yoga-and-meditation research, and positive psychology drawing on classical concepts of well-being (sukha) and flourishing.

RML tilt: High-probability pairings to memorise: Sengupta → first Indian psychology lab (Calcutta, 1915); Durganand Sinha → indigenization critique + deprivation; J. B. P. Sinha → Nurturant-Task leadership; Wolf → Vedic Personality Inventory; Paranjpe → self and identity. Know the three phases of indigenization by name.
Recap: Personality in Indian psychology is the balance of three gunas (sattva, rajas, tamas), measurable via Wolf's Vedic Personality Inventory. Motivation and development are framed by the four purusharthas (dharma, artha, kama, moksha), the four ashramas, and the karma-rebirth law softened by nishkama karma. The self is analysed as true Atman versus the manufactured ahamkara, worked through the fourfold antahkarana (manas, buddhi, chitta, ahamkara) and the five koshas from food-body to bliss. Ayurveda adds an indigenous cognitive therapy: prajnaparadha (crimes against wisdom) as cause, sattvavajaya (mind conquest) as cure, with rajas and tamas as the mental doshas. Indigenous therapy sits beside Japan's Morita (accept feelings, act) and Naikan (reflect and give thanks), and beside modern MBSR/MBCT descended from Buddhist mindfulness. Finally, Indian psychology moved from imported replication to the three-phase indigenization movement led by Durganand Sinha, J. B. P. Sinha, Misra, Paranjpe, Rao and Salagame, supported by ICSSR, UGC/NCERT, NIMHANS and ICMR.

How to attack it from here

  1. Read the five carrying domains first, in this order: General & basic psychology (2), Assessment & tests (10), Research + Statistics (11), Abnormal (7), Biopsychology (3). The six-year PYQ map shows these are 65% of the paper. That is where the marks actually live.
  2. Make the founder pairs and acronyms reflexive from Counselling & Psychotherapy (9): who developed which therapy, and what every acronym stands for. This is RML's single most repeated question type.
  3. Over-prepare Indian Psychology (12), both ends: the modern history of the discipline in India and the classical frameworks. This is the recurring leak.
  4. Go ICD-deep on Abnormal (7): know the ICD-11 side and the older signs and eponyms, not DSM alone.
  5. Thin pass on Section A (1): a couple of timed reasoning sets plus the mental-health Acts and institutes. It is about 20 marks, do not over-invest.
  6. Then sit the six real PYQ mocks untimed (2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025), the same 570 questions the PYQ map is built from, and any weak spot they expose maps straight back to the section above.
